= Souther Language Specification
:toc:
:sectnums:
:sectnumlevels: 3
:xrefstyle: short
:section-refsig: §

[#purpose]
== Purpose

Souther is a JVM-targeted language for transcribing the *specification DSL* of Specification Model-Driven Development (SMDD) into an implementation model with minimal translation.

The spec DSL expresses business rules using only `+data+` (AND / OR / List / `+?+`) and `+behavior+` (`+->+` / `+>->+`). Constraints on the values themselves ("amount is non-negative", "the approver is the applicant's manager"), the confinement of construction paths, and dependency on the outside world are not written there. The spec DSL deliberately leaves these three in comments.

Souther's role is to make those three executable.

[cols="1,1",options="header"]
|===
| What the spec DSL leaves in comments | How Souther makes it executable

| Value constraints (`+// at least 0+`, `+// the approver is the applicant's manager+`) | `+invariant+`

| The invariant "this value has been validated" | Confinement of construction paths (`+decoder+` / `+constructs+`)

| `+// depends: productCatalogue+` / `+// side effect: sendingMail+` | A `+behavior+` with no `+let+` at a leaf; `+depends on+` (an injected Java implementation) at the call site

|===

The structure of the spec DSL (distinction, requiredness, multiplicity, state transition, totality) maps directly onto Souther's types. What the spec DSL cannot guard it divides up: structure by types, value constraints by tests, part of requiredness by the types an implementation is written against. Souther takes that remainder into the implementation model — a value constraint becomes an `+invariant+`, and an outside-world dependency an injected behavior.

[#mapping-principle]
=== Mapping principle

Each construct of the spec DSL maps to Souther as follows.

[cols="1,1",options="header"]
|===
| Spec DSL | Souther

| `+data Amount = Integer // at least 0+` | `+data Amount = Int invariant value >= 0+` (newtype; <<newtype>>)

| `+data X = A OR B+` (A, B are named data) | `+data X = A \| B+` (sum; cases are existing named data)

| `+data Reimbursement = Unit+` | `+data Reimbursement+` (data with no fields; a case named in a sum or a behavior output needs no declaration, <<unit-data>>)

| `+data X = A AND b+` (A is a record, b is an added item) | `+data X = { ...A, b: B }+` (field composition)

| `+List<T>+` / `+T?+` | `+List<T>+` / `+T?+`

| `+behavior f = In -> Out+` (Out has multiple success states) | `+behavior f : (...) -> Out1 \| Out2+` (bare sum)

| `+behavior f = In -> Out OR Failed+` | `+behavior f : (...) -> Out \| Rejected+` (a failure is just data; departure from the mainline is decided by composition)

| `+f >-> g+` | `+f >-> g+`

| `+// depends+` / `+// side effect+` | A `+behavior+` with no `+let+`

| `+// precondition+` | `+guard ... else+` (a business branch, not a contract; <<violation-destination>>)

|===

[#boundary]
=== The boundary with the outside world

Souther does not handle the outside world directly. The boundary is expressed with primitive values, collections, and Decoders / Encoders.

[,text]
----
outside world
  ↓
primitive values / collections
  ↓ Decoder
internal data
  ↓ behavior
internal data
  ↓ Encoder
primitive values / collections
  ↓
outside world
----

The JVM is used as the distribution platform that makes the generated types and behaviors available to Java programs.

[#design-principles]
== Design principles

[#closed-construction]
=== Construction paths of data are closed

A value of a given `+data T+` MAY be constructed only through one of the following.

* the Decoder of `+T+`
* the implementation of a behavior declaring `+constructs T+` (a Souther `+let+`, or an injected Java implementation)
* compiler-generated code

A behavior that merely writes `+T+` as its return type MUST NOT be able to construct `+T+`. The authority to construct belongs to the behavior, not to its implementation. Therefore `+constructs+` is written on the behavior (<<constructs>>); the `+let+` and the Java implementation receive the authority from the same declaration.

A helper `+let+` with no corresponding behavior (<<fn-declaration>>) MAY also construct data. In that case the constructed data MUST appear in the `+constructs+` of the behavior that calls that `+let+`.

The type need not be declared in the same module. A behavior constructs what its `+constructs+` names, and a type another module exposes is built through the same checked entry as any other, so its invariant runs either way. A type a module does not expose cannot be built elsewhere — there is no name for it, and no entry — which is also why arithmetic that would build one is a compile error (<<newtype-arithmetic>>).

[#invariant-on-construct]
=== Invariants are checked on every construction

Every invariant declared on a `+data+` MUST be checked on every construction path. A value that violates an invariant MUST NOT be returned.

The destination of a violation differs inside and outside the boundary: in a Decoder it becomes a failure of Raoh's `+Result+`; inside the domain it aborts (<<violation-destination>>).

[#separate-representation]
=== External representation and internal data are separated

A Decoder constructs internal data from an external representation; an Encoder converts internal data to an external representation. An external representation MUST NOT be cast directly to internal data.

[#behavior-composition-unit]
=== behavior is the unit of composition

Internal computation, outside-world dependency, validation, and conversion are all expressed as behaviors and composed sequentially with `+>->+`. A behavior is a top-level input/output relation with no owning class.

As a type, a behavior states *only the specification*. A simple behavior has no implementation; the implementation is written as a `+let+` (<<fn>>). If none is written, it is injected from Java (<<injected-behavior>>). The exception is `+>->+` composition, which is itself the implementation.

Only behaviors that appear in the spec DSL are behaviors; implementation helpers remain `+let+`. This keeps the list of behaviors aligned with the spec DSL.

[#no-impl-for-outside]
=== No implementation is written for outside-world dependencies

Outside-world dependencies — DB, HTTP, files, clock, ID generation — MUST NOT be implemented in the language. These correspond to the `+// depends:+` and `+// side effect:+` notes in the spec DSL. Only the type is declared in the language.

[,souther]
----
behavior findMember : (id: MemberId) -> Member | NoSuchMember // no let
----

The implementation is injected from Java. Writing no implementation (`+let+`) is what "provided from outside" means (<<injected-behavior>>). A caller writes that name in its `+depends on+` (<<depends-on>>). The distinction between dependency (only reads the outside) and side effect (changes the outside) is documentation of intent; it does not affect the rules of value composition.

[#unmarked-sum]
=== Business results are expressed as bare sums

Every business result is returned as a sum of domain data. A *business* "failure" is not a first-class concept in the language. A behavior's output is a bare sum. The case that a downstream stage does not consume in a `+>->+` composition is what departs from the mainline (<<composition>>). *Unexpected* failures such as out-of-memory or a network drop MUST NOT be listed in the type.

An invariant violation MUST NOT appear here. It is not a business result but a model bug, and its destination is an abort (<<algebraic-types>>).

Multiple independent validation errors are accumulated inside the Decoder (<<case-propagation>>).

[#asymmetric-interop]
=== Java interop is asymmetric

Java MAY use the generated artifacts. Souther MUST NOT call arbitrary Java APIs directly.

[,text]
----
Java   -> Souther artifacts     allowed
Souther -> arbitrary Java API    forbidden (touch the outside only via a behavior with no implementation)
----

[#out-of-scope]
== Out of scope

Souther does not have the following. These are design decisions, not features to be added later.

* [[no-arbitrary-jvm-calls]]arbitrary Java API calls
* mutable state
* exceptions (see below)
* [[no-null]]null
* threads
* asynchronous processing in the language
* inheritance
* structural intersection types (`+A & B+`; see <<union-intersection>>)
* a function stored in a data field (it has no external representation, so it would break decoder / encoder derivation; <<decoder>>, <<encoder>>, <<blocks>>)
* reflection
* macros
* type classes
* higher kinds
* dependent types
* proof by SMT
* a termination prover a model writes measures for — recursion is proven total by size-change analysis, and what that cannot show is opted out with `+partial+` (<<fn-rules>>)
* a custom VM
* a package manager
* a REPL

Because the implementation of outside-world dependencies is delegated to Java, Souther itself has no DB, HTTP, or file APIs.

*On exceptions.* What is forbidden is *using exceptions to control business flow*. Business results are returned as cases of a sum (<<unmarked-sum>>); neither `+throw+` nor `+catch+` can be written.

The reporting path for model bugs, however, is not blocked. An invariant violation *aborts* (<<algebraic-types>>). An abort is not a Souther expression and has no catching syntax, so it cannot be used to control business flow.

A Java Binding at the boundary (the Java implementation of a behavior with no implementation; <<injected-behavior>>) is different. Against a *platform failure* (DB down, network drop, host not responding — anything that is not a domain outcome) it MAY throw an exception; Souther does not fold it into a case but passes it through to the boundary. The language (`+.sou+`) has no exceptions, but the Java side that touches the outside world may throw. The distinction is *domain outcome vs. platform failure*: a business-meaningful result such as "not found" or "insufficient stock" is a case; "infrastructure unavailable" or "not responding" is an exception (<<java-impl-rules>>).

[#modules]
== Source files and modules

The extension is `+.sou+`. Each file defines one module.

Two hold of the names inside it:

* [[a-declaration-is-made-once]]a name is written once where one is expected — a `+let+`, a data, a field of one data, a field of one construction, an entry of one clause list;
* [[a-spelling-answers-one-thing-where-a-value-is-written]]a spelling has one answer where a value is written. A data is written as a value — a unit data is one, a newtype is applied to what it wraps — so a `+let+` and a data of one name would be one spelling with two answers.

[[a-binding-does-not-shadow-a-builtin]]A binding MUST NOT take the name of a built-in value. A binding is in force over a region and everything outside it is unaffected (<<blocks>>), which is what makes shadowing a model's own names harmless; a built-in has no declaration to read instead, so a binding over one takes it away with nothing to point at.

[[a-name-is-in-scope]]A name written in an expression, a type position or a pipeline MUST be in scope there. The nearest name that is gets suggested, since a name out of scope and a name misspelled read the same.

[[a-name-held-as-a-value-is-a-value]]A name held as a value MUST be one. A behavior, a module qualifier and a type are names of other things, and writing one where a value goes is naming the thing rather than a value of it.

[[a-standard-library-function-is-called-qualified]]A standard-library function is called through its qualifier — `+List.map+`, not `+map+`. The qualifier is the only spelling that reaches the library (<<stdlib>>).

Three things hold of a module before anything in it is read:

* [[a-module-is-declared-in-one-place]]a module is declared once. Two files declaring one name, or a file declaring a module that is also on the path, leave two answers to what that name means;
* [[a-core-name-is-not-taken]]a module MUST NOT take a name the core has — the reserved `+souther+` namespace, or a standard-library qualifier such as `+List+` (<<reserved-namespace>>). A data MUST NOT take a qualifier's name either: the qualifier is the only spelling that reaches the library, so a declaration of that name hides it;
* [[every-module-reached-is-on-the-path]]every module a compile reaches — imported, named in a qualified reference, or needed by one that is — MUST be among the modules being compiled or on the path.

[,souther]
----
module example.businesstrip
----

A single file compiled on its own MAY omit the `+module+` header and start directly with a `+data+`
/ `+behavior+` / `+let+`; the module is then named after the file (the file-name stem), or `+Main+`
when compiled from a source string with no file name. A module compiled together with others and reached by
`+import+` MUST write the header — an unnamed module cannot be the target of an `+import+`, so
omitting it there is a compile error.

Keywords and the standard library are in English, but user-defined identifiers (types, fields, behaviors) MAY use Unicode. Because the spec DSL is written in Japanese, business vocabulary can be used as identifiers directly. Identifiers SHALL be *NFC-normalized* before binding and comparison; in Japanese a precomposed character and a combining sequence can look identical, so without normalization names that look the same would diverge in binding. Source is UTF-8.

[#identifier]
=== What a name is made of

An identifier is UAX #31's default form:

....
identifier = XID_Start XID_Continue*
....

The properties are Unicode's, read from `+DerivedCoreProperties.txt+` at the version below and not
re-derived from character categories. Every script's letters begin a name, so `+数量+` and
`+髙島屋+` are names, and so is one written with a character outside the basic multilingual plane —
the language reads a source file by code point, and `+𠮷+` is one character to it as it is one to
`+String.length+` (<<stdlib-string>>).

`+_+` continues a name and begins none: `+foo_bar+` is a name and `+_foo+` is not. On its own `+_+`
is not an identifier at all but a form of its own, written where a value arrives under no name:
inside a binding pattern (<<binding-patterns>>), in the departure arm answering the clauses that
carry no name (<<attempt-departures>>), and as a `+fake+` table's default row (<<example-fakes>>).

`+$+` is not written in a name. It is how the compiler spells the names it generates beside the ones
a model declares — a data's codecs, a behavior's implementation, the locals a desugaring introduces
— and the two sets have to be disjoint: a model able to declare the name a generated class already
has would take that class's place rather than collide with it, and the compile would succeed
(<<no-two-declarations-become-one-class>>).

The Unicode version is *17.0.0*, fixed by the language rather than taken from whatever platform a
compile runs on. Which characters may spell a name decides which sources read at all, and a
published module carries its helpers' bodies as source for an importing compiler to read again
(<<exposed-values>>) — so a compiler answering from its host's tables would make one artifact mean
different things on two machines. Moving to a later Unicode version admits names that were not names
before, and moves the boundary version with it (<<exposed-values>>).

[,souther]
----
data TravelRequest = Drafting | Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval | PreApproved
----

Public elements are listed with `+exposing+`.

[,souther]
----
module example.businesstrip exposing ( TravelRequest, submitTravelRequest, preApprove )
----

`+exposing+` exposes at type granularity. A public data's `+decoder()+` / `+encoder()+` is always public API (<<jvm-codec>>), so member-level narrowing such as `+TravelRequest.decoder+` is not written — there is nothing to narrow.


When an exposed behavior is a `+>->+` composition, its output SHALL be written in `+exposing+` in the form `+name : cases+` (<<declared-composition-output>>). A composition's output is inferred from its stages (<<composition-with-requirements>>), but at the public boundary a signature is required rather than inference, so that the contract used by another module or by Java does not change silently when an upstream stage changes. Omitting it is a compile error (E1605).

[,souther]
----
module example.order exposing ( placeOrder : PlacedOrder | OutOfStock )
----

`+exposing+` is the module's interface description: implementations are inferred, only the boundary is stated explicitly.

[#exposed-values]
==== A value and a helper are published

[[exposing-lists-this-modules-own-definitions]]`+exposing+` lists what this module declares. A name it does not declare is not published by writing it here, and neither is an imported one: a module re-exporting another's vocabulary would put one declaration behind two surfaces.

[[exposing-is-type-granular]]`+exposing+` is granular to the type. A data's derived `+decoder+` and `+encoder+` are published with it and are not named separately — they are how the type crosses a boundary, and a type that crosses one carries them (<<decoder>>, <<encoder>>).

`+exposing+` MAY name a *value* — a `+let+` with no parameter list (<<fn-declaration>>) — and a *helper* — a `+let+` with them — alongside the module's data and behaviors. A limit a rule is written against, the representative record its examples are stated with, and the rule itself are part of what the module offers, and a reader names them instead of writing them out again.

[,text]
----
module example.pipeline exposing ( negotiatingDeal, taxIncluded, Deal, ... )

let negotiatingDeal = Negotiating { ... }
let taxIncluded (amount: Amount) = Amount(amount.value * 110 / 100)
----

An importer names the definition and nothing else: what it is built out of stays the declaring module's business, and does not join the reader's import list. What arrives is *closed* — the body with its own module's definitions already substituted into it — so a definition the importer happens to spell the same way is a different definition and changes nothing (<<qualified-reference>>).

A behavior's own `+let+` is not published, whatever its shape. What a reader of the module reaches is the behavior, which it calls; the `+let+` that satisfies it is an implementation, and the module publishes its specification.

A published definition MAY reach a *recursive* helper. Closing cannot remove one — it is lowered to a method rather than expanded (<<fn-rules>>) — so the helper travels with the body that calls it, under the name of the module that declares it, and so does every recursive helper it reaches in turn. The importing module emits them as its own methods, exactly as it does for a recursive standard-library helper it reaches. Which module declared a helper is its identity and not where its method goes: two modules may declare a recursive helper of one name, and one importer may end up emitting both.

A published value is substituted where it is named and a published helper expanded where it is called, exactly as one of the reader's own is (<<fn-value-semantics>>, <<fn-rules>>). What such a body builds is *not* the reader's to declare in `+constructs+`: publishing the definition is what states that origination, and the reader may have no name for the type. A type of some *third* module that the body happens to build is neither the reader's nor the publisher's to hand over, so that one stays the reader's to declare — which it can, since the module declaring it exposed it (<<closed-construction>>).

A published *value* rests on none of that. What a value builds belongs to its definition whatever module declares the type, so reading one across a boundary answers the same as reading one within a module (<<fn-value-semantics>>). Crossing the boundary is what the paragraph above is about, and a value never needed it.

A published body's source travels in the jar and is read back by the importing compiler (<<published-modules>>). What that source means is therefore part of what a compiled module promises, and is recorded under the same boundary version as the rest of it.

[#exposed-surface]
==== What reaches out may not rest on what is kept

[[an-exposed-signature-names-only-exposed-types]]What a module reaches out with SHALL NOT rest on a type it keeps to itself. An exposed data's field types, and an exposed behavior's input and output, are exposed by the module that declares them. A type carried inside a collection counts — `+List<UserId>+` names `+UserId+` as surely as a bare `+UserId+` does.

An exposed value SHALL NOT stand for a type the module keeps to itself, and an exposed helper SHALL NOT return one or take one — a reader would hold a value it has no name for, or be unable to write the argument it has to pass. What a definition stands for is its *type*, settled from its body (<<fn-rules>>), and not the constructions the body makes on the way: a definition may name a type it never builds (`+let published (n: Int) : List<Hidden> = []+`), and a type its body reached for on the way is its own workings — requiring those would put every inner type back in the reader's import list, which is what publishing the definition was for.

The same holds for an injected behavior — one with no `+let+` — whether or not it is in `+exposing+`, because the abstract base it generates is public regardless (<<java-base-class>>) and the implementation writes both types where it overrides `+apply+`.

Otherwise a reader is handed a value it has no name for, and the generated classes carry a public surface over classes it may not touch. It is reported where the module decides it, not at each reader that runs into it.

This does not reach the *cases* of a sum, or of an output written as more than one case. Those are generated as a union interface that is public regardless (<<jvm-anonymous-union>>), and a case reaches a reader through the decoder or through the `+protected+` factory rather than by being named — which is what E1305's unit-data allowance rests on. A module keeping a case to itself therefore stays a decision about the union, not about this rule.

[#imports]
Imports SHALL be listed explicitly. Wildcard imports are forbidden.

[[module-dependencies-are-acyclic]]A module depends on every module it imports and on every module it names in a qualified reference (<<qualified-reference>>). Those dependencies MUST have no cycle.

[[imports-are-used]]An entry on an import list MUST be written bare somewhere below it. What another module declares is reachable qualified whether or not it is imported, so an entry earns its place only by being used under the name it brings in.

[,souther]
----
import example.employee ( Employee, EmployeeId )
----

The standard library is imported in the same form: the qualifier — `+List+`, `+Map+`, `+Set+`, `+String+`, `+Bool+`, `+Int+`, `+Decimal+`, `+Date+`, `+DateTime+`, `+Option+` (<<reserved-namespace>>) — as the name, and the functions used listed. Once imported, they MAY be written unqualified (<<stdlib>>).

[,souther]
----
import List ( map, filter, fold )
import String ( trim, split )
----

Qualified access (`+List.map+`) always works without importing, so `+import+` only elides the qualifier. Importing the same bare name from two modules is a compile error, reported at the second import line: writing both `+import String (length )+` and `+import List (length )+` says `+length+` is exposed from both. Import one and qualify the other call (`+String.length(...)+`). As with the wildcard prohibition, no name is delivered implicitly. A name the library does not have is a compile error at the import line, not at the first use.

An import says which names MAY be written without their qualifier. It does not say what one of them means where it is written: a binding in force means what it binds. So `+import List ( map )+` and a parameter named `+map+` may sit in one module, and inside that parameter's scope `+map+` is the parameter. What a name means is settled once, wherever it is written — in a body, in an `+invariant+`, in an `+example+` row, in a `+fake+` — and an import is the last thing consulted.

A declaration is not a binding in force and does not shadow an import. Importing a name the module declares itself — a `+data+`, a `+let+`, or a behavior written with that name — is a compile error reported at the import line, not a shadowing: one spelling would stand for two things, and nothing at a call site could choose between them. Rename the declaration, drop it, or drop the import and write the library name qualified. Qualified access is always available, so a module MAY declare `+let not+` and still call `+Bool.not(...)+`; it is `+import Bool ( not )+` beside that declaration that is refused.

[#published-modules]
=== Importing a module another project compiled

[[a-published-module-agrees-with-this-compiler]]A module read from compiled output MUST have been compiled by a Souther that agrees with this one about what an importing module may reach. What it published is read back as declarations, so a disagreement about the surface is a disagreement about the text of this compile.

[[a-published-module-carries-what-it-says-it-declares]]A published module MUST carry every declaration it says it publishes. What it publishes is read back as declarations, and a declaration with no class behind it is not one.

[[a-published-module-can-be-read-back]]A module used from compiled output MUST be readable back into the published surface this compiler understands. This is the boundary condition an importing project depends on, and the rules above are among the ways an artifact fails to meet it — a class carrying metadata this compiler cannot read, a boundary revision that does not agree, declarations of a module other than the one they were published under, a declaration whose class is absent, published text this compiler does not parse, an `+import+` line the published surface needs and this compiler cannot read. An artifact that fails any of them is reported as one that cannot be read (<<e1509>>), with the reason said under that report; the rules themselves belong to the project that published it, and are not obligations of the project reading it.

Which import lines those are is settled by what the published surface needs, and not by what the artifact carries. A compiled module carries every `+import+` line it was written under. Reading one back takes only the lines the declarations it publishes name: a module's implementation does not cross, so an import only its bodies used names nothing an importing project reads, and taking it would make that project put a module on its path to read declarations that never mention it. A line left out for that reason is a line this compiler never reads, and so is never one it cannot read.

A module MAY be imported from the compiled output of another project, with no `+.sou+` of it in this
one. A compiled module carries what it declares — its `+data+` definitions, its behavior
signatures, its `+exposing+` line and the imports it was written under — on the classes it generated,
as the source that declared it. An `+import+` naming no module among the sources being compiled is
resolved against the compiled modules on the class path.

Nothing is declared beyond the dependency itself: under the annotation processor the class path is
the compile classpath, and the CLI takes `+-cp+` / `+--class-path+`.

[,text]
----
souther compile app/order.sou -d out-app -cp out-lib
----

What crosses that boundary is what crosses a boundary within one compilation: `+exposing+` is the
gate, and an imported type may not be a case of a sum declared here (<<e1606>>). An imported type in
a behavior's output union is unaffected — it joins through a bridge case this module emits
(<<bridge-case>>), so nothing of the imported module has to be regenerated for it. A module read this
way is not generated again — its classes and its examples belong to the build that produced them.

A compiled module records the version of what an importing module reaches. A module whose version is
not this compiler's is refused, naming the compiler that built it, rather than read and
misunderstood. A module compiled here SHALL NOT also be on the class path, and a module on the class
path that needs one absent from it is reported against the path.

A module on the class path needs another when what it declares names it — in a field's type, a
spread, an invariant, a helper an invariant calls, or an exposed composition's output. A `+let+` body
is not published, so an `+import+` only a `+let+` used is not one the importing project has to
satisfy: a dependency's implementation-only dependencies need not be on the class path.

[#qualified-reference]
=== Qualified reference

A type is also reachable through the module that declares it, written as the module name and the type name.

[[a-reached-name-is-declared-by-its-module]]A name reached across a module boundary MUST be one that module declares, and [[a-reached-name-is-exposed-by-its-module]]it MUST be one that module exposes. The two are separate: a name the module never declared is a mistake about what is there, and a name it declared and kept is a mistake about what it publishes, which is the module's own decision to make (<<exposed-surface>>). Both hold whether the name arrives by `+import+` or is written qualified.

[[an-imported-name-denotes-one-thing]]A name an `+import+` brings in MUST denote one thing here. Two modules exposing it, an alias already in use, or this module's own declaration of that spelling each leave the reader with a name and no single answer; reaching one of them qualified is what says which. Like the standard library's `+List.map+`, this needs no `+import+`: the `+import+` line only lets the name be written bare.

[,souther]
----
data Line =
    { quantity: example.counting.Amount
    , price: example.pricing.Amount
    }
----

This is what a module does when two it depends on declare the same type name — `+Amount+` in each of two bounded contexts, which is normal and not the integrator's to rename. A qualified reference MAY be written wherever a type is written or named (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>, <<binding-patterns>>, <<match>>). A sum's case list is the exception: its cases are declared with it (<<sum-data>>), so there is no other module to name.

Which spelling names a type is not something a check has an opinion about. A bare `+Amount+` an `+import+` brings in and a qualified `+example.pricing.Amount+` are one type, so a clause and a body that spell it differently agree, and a pattern opens the type it names however the field that holds it was written.

`+import ... as+` names a module locally, and the alias may then carry the reference. The alias holds only in the module that writes it, and it MUST be a name nothing else answers to there — not another alias, not a module of this compilation, not a standard-library qualifier.

[,souther]
----
import example.counting as Counting
import example.pricing as Pricing ( taxRate )

data Line =
    { quantity: Counting.Amount
    , price: Pricing.Amount
    }
----

Both parts of an `+import+` are optional: `+import example.pricing as Pricing+` only renames, and `+import example.pricing+` only writes the dependency down. What a qualified reference reaches is still what the declaring module exposes — qualifying a name that module keeps to itself is a compile error, as is qualifying with a name that is no module of this compilation.

A behavior of another module is named the same way, as a `+>->+` stage and as a `+depends on+`. What the qualifier says is which module the behavior comes from, which is what an `+import+` says, so a qualified stage or requirement needs no import line either.

[,souther]
----
behavior ship = inventory.allocate >-> makeInvoice
behavior notify : (order: PlacedOrder) -> Notified
    constructs Notified
    depends on messaging.sendMail
----

A behavior is the module that declares it and its name, so two modules declaring one name declare two behaviors. A qualified reference says which of them is meant, and a module may reach both — as a type of one name from two modules is reached, and for the same reason. A behavior this module declares is one of the two: `+inventory.allocate+` and an `+allocate+` written here are different stages, and each is held to its own declaration.

[,souther]
----
behavior fromPrimary = primary.allocate >-> confirm
behavior fromSecondary = secondary.allocate >-> confirm
----

What cannot be told apart is a bare spelling two `+import+` lines both claim: written here, the name has two answers and neither line is the one to prefer, which is a compile error (<<e1508>>). Qualifying one of them is what says which is meant, and is the fix that error names.

A behavior injected into a construction is held in a field of the generated class (<<jvm-behavior>>), and what that field is called is the class's own — not the behavior's name. So a composition may name two behaviors of one name as stages and require both, and the two are two fields.

A `+depends on+` clause is not written that way. What it names becomes the implementation's trailing parameter, under that name, so two entries of one name have one name to be written under and the second is a name listed twice (<<e1011>>). Naming the two apart is the module's to do — declaring a behavior of its own for each, which is what an injection target is for (<<injected-behavior>>) — or reaching one of them through a composition, where nothing is named again.

A dependency is a dependency however it is written, so a cycle closed by a qualified reference is the same compile error (<<e1501>>) as one closed by an `+import+`.

`+exposing+` lists what a module declares and does not re-export a name it imported. A module downstream of an integration module names an upstream type through the module that declares it, so the integration module translates a type it consumes rather than republishing it.

[#grammar]
== Grammar

Where each of the language's written forms is given — its concrete syntax, and where to look each part of it up.

A form is stated with the construct it belongs to, once, where a reader of that construct meets it. This is the index that reaches those statements and not a second copy of them: a form written here as well would be a second place to keep in step, and the two would disagree the first time one of them moved.

A row is a form, or a rule about one, together with what kind of thing it is and the section that gives it. The kinds divide in two. A token, a delimiter and an operator are written with what the compiler holds a set of, so what is listed here is checked against it: the reserved words against the words the lexer reserves, and the symbols of the three rows together against the spellings the syntax kinds carry. A production, a constraint and an association are the specification's own: no set the compiler enumerates says which `+then+` an `+else+` closes, and those rows are held by whatever states them.

Which of the three a symbol is listed as is the specification's own too. The compiler holds no set that says `+->+` joins two types where `{plus}` computes from two numbers, so nothing checks the column, and reading a row for what a symbol does is reading this document. An editor asks something else — which symbols a reader wants told apart from the names and the brackets around them — and answers it for itself, so what it paints as an operator is neither this column nor derived from it.

The full formal grammar (BNF) is deferred (<<future-work>>).

[cols="3,1,2",separator=!,role="syntax-inventory"]
!===
!Form or rule !Kind !Given in

!a name !lexical form !<<identifier>>
!`+_+` !token !<<identifier>>
!a string literal !lexical form !<<string-literal>>
!an integer and a `+Decimal+` literal !lexical form !<<numeric-literal>>
!a `+Date+` and a `+DateTime+` literal !lexical form !<<temporal-literal>>
!a type variable (`+'a+`) !lexical form !<<type-variables>>
!a comment !lexical form !<<comment>>
!the reserved words !token !<<reserved-words>>
!`+==+` `+/=+` `+<+` `+<=+` `+>+` `+>=+` !operator !<<reserved-words>>
!`+&&+` `+||+` !operator !<<reserved-words>>
!`{plus}` `+-+` `+*+` `+/+` !operator !<<reserved-words>>
!`{plus}{plus}` !operator !<<reserved-words>>
!`+{+` `+}+` `+(+` `+)+` `+[+` `+]+` !delimiter !<<delimiters>>
!`+:+` `+,+` !delimiter !<<delimiters>>
!`+=+` !delimiter !<<delimiters>>
!`+|+` !delimiter !<<delimiters>>
!`+->+` !delimiter !<<delimiters>>
!`+.+` and `+...+` !delimiter !<<record-literal>>
!`+?+` !delimiter !<<optional>>
!`+>->+` !delimiter !<<sequential-composition>>
!`+|>+` !delimiter !<<pipe>>
!`+<?>+` !token !<<example-answer-owed>>
!a module header !production !<<modules>>
!an `+import+` !production !<<imports>>
!an `+exposing+` clause !production !<<exposed-values>>
!a qualified name !production !<<qualified-reference>>
!a product `+data+` and its fields !production !<<product-data>>
!a field spread !production !<<field-spread>>
!a sum `+data+` !production !<<sum-data>>
!a unit `+data+` !production !<<unit-data>>
!a newtype !production !<<newtype>>
!an `+invariant+` clause !production !<<invariant-declaration>>
!a behavior's signature !production !<<behavior-io>>
!a `+constructs+` clause !production !<<constructs>>
!a `+depends on+` clause !production !<<depends-on>>
!an `+ensures+` clause !production !<<ensures>>
!a definition (`+let+` at the top level) !production !<<fn-declaration>>
!a binding (`+let+` in a block) !production !<<let>>
!a lambda and a block !production !<<blocks>>
!a record literal !production !<<record-literal>>
!a list literal and a guard comprehension !production !<<stdlib-list>>
!a tuple !production !<<tuple>>
!a type in a type position, a function type among them !production !<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>
!an `+if+` !production !<<if>>
!a `+match+` and its arms !production !<<match>>
!a `+guard+` !production !<<guard>>
!an attempted construction !production !<<attempted-construction>>
!a departure per clause !production !<<attempt-departures>>
!an `+unreachable+` !production !<<unreachable>>
!a binding pattern !production !<<binding-patterns>>
!a `+>->+` composition !production !<<sequential-composition>>
!an `+example+` and its rows !production !<<examples>>
!a `+fake+` !production !<<example-fakes>>
!an `+examples for+` file !production !<<example-placement>>
!an `+if+` is written with both of its branches !constraint !<<if>>
!a string literal ends on the line it began on !constraint !<<string-literal>>
!a `+,+`-separated list MAY carry a trailing `+,+` !constraint !<<delimiters>>
!an anonymous union is not written in a narrow type position !constraint !<<an-anonymous-union-is-not-written-in-a-narrow-type-position>>
!a postfix `+?+` is not written inside another type !constraint !<<an-optional-is-not-written-inside-another-type>>
!`+|+` binds more loosely than `+->+` !association !<<delimiters>>
!`+->+` is right-associative !association !<<delimiters>>
!`+|>+` binds most loosely, and to the left !association !<<pipe>>
!`+>->+` is associative !association !<<type-routing>>
!which `+match+` an arm belongs to !association !<<match>>
!===

[#reserved-words]
== Reserved words

[,text]
----
module   import    exposing
data     invariant
behavior constructs depends  ensures
match    with      as
let      if        then     else
guard    unreachable
true     false
----

Operators and symbols: `+=+` `+|+` `+>->+` `+|>+` `+?+` `+->+` `+.+` `+...+` `+[+` `+]+`

Comparison, logic, arithmetic: `+==+` `+/=+` `+<+` `+<=+` `+>+` `+>=+` `+&&+` `+||+` `{plus}` `+-+` `+*+` `+/+` (`+/+` is division; `+/=+` is inequality; negation is not a prefix operator but `+not+` in `+souther.bool+`; <<stdlib-bool>>).

Lists: `+[+` `+]+` (literal and guard comprehension), `{plus}{plus}` (concatenation, of two lists or two strings; <<stdlib-list>>).

`+>->+` is associative (<<type-routing>>). `+|>+` is the value pipe, threading a value through functions — `+e |> f(a)+` is `+f(a, e)+` (<<pipe>>).

`+->+` is the arrow. It joins types — in a behavior declaration (<<behavior>>) and in a function type, wherever a type is written (<<delimiters>>) — and it introduces an anonymous body — in a `+match+` case (<<match>>) and a lambda (<<blocks>>). There is one kind of arrow. `+=+` binds a name to a right-hand side (whether the right-hand side is a type or a term is decided by the rules of <<behavior>>).

`+depends on+` is two words, and the `+on+` is not a reserved word. It is read as the second word of the keyword only where it follows `+depends+`, as the `+for+` of `+examples for+` is read after `+examples+` (<<example-placement>>), so a field, a parameter or a behavior MAY still be named `+on+`.

`+decoder+` / `+encoder+` / `+discriminate+` are not reserved words: the mapping to external representation is not syntax but is derived from a data's shape (<<decoder>>, <<encoder>>). `+error+` / `+success+` / `+failure+` are not reserved words either: a *business* failure is neither a special type nor syntax but ordinary domain data, and whether it departs from the mainline is decided by composition (<<unmarked-sum>>, <<behavior>>, <<composition>>).

There is no type name that carries an invariant violation. A violation inside the domain aborts, so no value needs to be carried (<<algebraic-types>>, <<violation-destination>>).

[#comment]
=== Comments

A comment begins at `+//+` and runs to the end of the line. The line ends it, and nothing else does.

[,text]
----
let a = 1   // the rest of this line is a comment
----

There is no block comment. `+/*+` is not a form: the characters read as the operators they are, and a text written that way does not compile. A `+//+` inside a string literal is part of the literal (<<string-literal>>), the literal having begun first.

[#delimiters]
=== Delimiters and disambiguation

The full formal grammar (BNF) is deferred (<<future-work>>). The delimiters and precedence needed for reading and writing are fixed here.

What a text MUST be for the compiler to read it is stated with each construct, and a text that is not is refused where the reading stops. Where the reading can stop is kept apart, because a reader looking one up is asking about a different part of the language, and each place names where the forms it is about are given (<<grammar>> indexes them all):

[role="reading-stops"]
* [[declaration-syntax]]a declaration MUST be written in the form given for it: the module header (<<modules>>), a top-level `+data+` (<<product-data>>, <<sum-data>>, <<unit-data>>, <<newtype>>), a behavior's signature (<<behavior-io>>), a top-level `+let+` (<<fn-declaration>>), a `+constructs+` clause (<<constructs>>), a `+depends on+` clause (<<depends-on>>), an `+ensures+` clause (<<ensures>>);
* [[expression-syntax]]an expression MUST be written in the form given for it, and a block MUST end in one (<<blocks>>, <<block-expression>>): a literal below, a record literal (<<record-literal>>), a list (<<stdlib-list>>), a tuple (<<tuple>>), an `+if+` (<<if>>), a `+match+` (<<match>>), a `+guard+` (<<guard>>), an `+unreachable+` (<<unreachable>>), an application (<<fn>>), a pipe (<<pipe>>);
* [[pattern-syntax]]a pattern MUST be written in the form given for the thing it destructures (<<binding-patterns>>, <<match>>);
* [[examples-syntax]]an `+example+`, a `+fake+` and an `+examples for+` file MUST be written in the form given for them (<<examples>>, <<example-fakes>>, <<example-placement>>);
* [[literal-syntax]]a literal MUST be written in the form given for its type: an integer and a `+Decimal+` (<<numeric-literal>>), a `+String+` (<<string-literal>>), a `+Date+` and a `+DateTime+` (<<temporal-literal>>);
* [[an-anonymous-union-is-not-written-in-a-narrow-type-position]]an anonymous union MUST NOT be written as a data field's type, a type argument, or a tuple's member. Those three read the narrower type production, so a `+|+` following the type is recognized in order to be refused and is not built as part of the type standing there — which is what lets the refusal name the form rather than the delimiter the form displaced. Where an anonymous union *is* read, and the one position that reads one and refuses it, are <<union-intersection>>;
* [[an-optional-is-not-written-inside-another-type]]a postfix `+?+` MUST NOT be written inside another type — as a type argument or a tuple's member. `+?+` marks where an optional is *made*, which is a data field being given its value (<<optional>>), and no position inside a type is one. This is not a privilege the core keeps (<<core-privileges-stay-in-the-core>>): `+List<T?>+` is written nowhere, and an optional a collection holds is named `+List<Option<T>>+`;
* [[the-source-is-made-of-tokens]]the text MUST be made of tokens the language has (<<reserved-words>>, <<type-variables>>). This one is answered before anything is parsed, so it says nothing about where in the language the text was — a character that begins nothing, and a `+'+` with no name after it, are the two ways to reach it.

* Both `+data+` declaration fields and record-literal `+TypeName { ... }+` fields are `+,+`-separated (<<product-data>>, <<record-literal>>). A declaration gives a type, so `+name: type+`; a literal binds a value, so `+name = value+` (`+:+` = has this type, `+=+` = is defined as this). A `+...+` spread is an element that composes types but appears in the same comma sequence as fields (<<field-spread>>). An `+invariant+` is a clause placed after the type body (`+{ ... }+` or `+= Y+`) and is not part of the field list (<<invariant>>). A clause's name follows the same rule as everything else named with `+=+` — it is defined as that rule, so `+invariant nonEmpty = List.length(value) >= 1+` (<<invariant-clause-name>>).
* Argument lists and the enumerations in `+constructs+` / `+depends on+` are `+,+`-separated (<<behavior-io>>, <<constructs>>, <<depends-on>>).
* Every `+,+`-separated list — declaration and record-literal fields, parameter and argument lists, tuple elements, list literals, and the `+constructs+` / `+depends on+` name lists — MAY carry a single trailing `+,+` before its closing delimiter. It is optional and has no meaning. Where this spec breaks such a list across lines, a `+data+` product body opens each line after the first with its `+,+` (<<product-data>>) and every other list closes each line but the last with its own; neither writes the trailing `+,+`. The convention is the construct's, not the document's.
* `+|+` is the case separator of a sum and binds more loosely than `+->+`. `+(x: T) -> A | B+` reads as `+(x: T) -> (A | B)+` (the whole output is a sum). Where an anonymous `+|+` is read as a type is the type position's answer (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>), and a behavior's parameter refuses one it reads (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>).
* `+>->+` is the stage separator of a pipeline: not a binary operator but a flat n-ary sequence. It is associative (<<type-routing>>). Only a behavior name MAY appear in a stage position; parentheses such as `+(f >-> g)+` MUST NOT be written.
* `+|>+` is the value pipe, binding most loosely and left-associatively. `+a |> f |> g+` reads as `+g(f(a))+`. The left side enters the last argument of the right-hand call and is expanded to a call at parse time (<<pipe>>). This is distinct from the `+>->+` that composes behaviors; it threads the value of an expression.
* `+?+` is a type postfix (`+EmployeeId?+`) and desugars to `+Option<EmployeeId>+` (<<optional>>).
* `+.+` is field reference; `+...+` is spread (<<record-literal>>).
* [[a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position]]A type is written in a *type position*, and these are the positions: a data field and a newtype's base; a behavior's parameter and its output, and the output declared for a `+>->+` composition, written either on the composition or in `+exposing+` (<<declared-composition-output>>); a helper's parameter and its declared return; a local binding's annotation; and, inside another type, a type argument, a tuple's member, and a function type's parameter and its result. A lambda's parameter is a pattern and takes no type (<<blocks>>). A type is also *named* in three places that are not type positions — a `+match+` arm's case, a `+constructs+` entry, and a constructor pattern — and each of those takes the name of a declared type rather than a type form.
* What a type position reads is not the same in every position. Where a *whole* type is read — a behavior's parameter and its output, a composition's declared output, a helper's parameter and its declared return, a local binding's annotation, and a function type's parameter and its result — the forms are a named type, a stdlib generic type (such as `+List<T>+`), a tuple type, a function type `+(A) -> B+`, an anonymous output sum (`+A | B+`), and a postfix `+?+`. A data field reads all of those but the anonymous sum (<<an-anonymous-union-is-not-written-in-a-narrow-type-position>>). A type argument and a tuple's member read neither the anonymous sum nor the postfix `+?+`, so `+List<A | B>+` and `+List<T?>+` are not written at all (<<an-optional-is-not-written-inside-another-type>>). A newtype's base is narrower again, below. What a position *reads* is what it builds as the type standing there; a form it does not read is still recognized where it is written, and named, so that what the author reached for is what they are answered about. Recognizing a form in order to refuse it is neither admitting it nor building it. A function type's parameters and result are themselves whole types, so `+->+` is right-associative — `+(A) -> (B) -> C+` returns a function and `+((A) -> B) -> C+` takes one — and `+(A) -> B | C+` still reads as `+(A) -> (B | C)+` by the `+|+` rule above. `+(A, B) -> C+` takes two parameters and `+((A, B)) -> C+` takes one tuple, as a lambda's parameter list does. A parenthesised single type is grouping.
* Writing a type in a position is not being admitted by it. What a position requires of a type is asked of the type: a data field, a newtype base and a behavior's input and output require an *external representation*, so none of them takes a type that carries at any depth something with none (`+List<(Int) -> Bool>+` included; <<what-has-no-external-representation>>); `+==+` / `+/=+`, a `+Set+`'s element and a `+Map+`'s key require *equality*; `+sort+` and a `+sortBy+` key require an *ordering* (<<equality>>).
* A newtype's base (`+data X = Y+`) is a type position narrowed to what `+X+` can take as its own external representation: no tuple, no function type, and no `+?+` (<<newtype>>). A sum's cases are names of declared data, so a case is never generic: `+data X = List<A> | B+` is not a sum (<<sum-data>>).

[#external-representation]
== External representation

What crosses a decoder/encoder boundary is decided by representation, and these obligations fall out of it:

* [[what-crosses-the-boundary-has-an-external-representation]]a value that crosses MUST have an external representation (<<what-has-no-external-representation>>). What has none cannot be a behavior's parameter or output, a data's field, or anything a codec would be derived from;
* [[a-parameter-names-one-type]]a *behavior's* parameter MUST name a single type. An anonymous union is the shape of one behavior's answer and nobody names it (<<union-member>>), so a caller has no name to write and the input takes a declared one. This is a rule about what crosses, and not about where the form is read. A helper's parameter is not a boundary and takes one (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>);
* [[an-optional-does-not-stand-in-a-boundary]]an optional MUST NOT stand anywhere in a behavior's boundary shape — neither as a parameter nor as the output, and at any depth inside a collection either. A boundary carries the model's own vocabulary, and absence there is owned by the data that holds it, on a `+?+` field, or by a sum the model names (<<optional>>). The shape is read down to a named type and no further: a data carrying a `+?+` field crosses as that data;
* [[a-map-that-crosses-is-keyed-by-a-text-key]]a `+Map+` that crosses MUST be keyed by something a key can be written as — `+String+`, a temporal (`+Date+` / `+Time+` / `+DateTime+` / `+Instant+`), an enumeration (a sum every one of whose cases is a unit data), or a newtype over any of those, at any depth (<<collections>>). The boundary writes a key as text, and a key with no text is a key nothing can address;
* [[a-boundary-carries-the-models-own-vocabulary]]a named type that crosses MUST be one a model declares. The language declares vocabulary of its own — the case a division by zero answers with, the rounding mode a `+Decimal+` operation takes, the reserved `+Raw+` — and those name what an operation can answer or take, not what a behavior publishes. A model that has such a case to report declares its own and names it (`+-> Int | Undivided+`), which is what keeps the behavior's protocol independent of how the language accounts for itself;
* [[a-collection-member-supports-equality]]a `+Set+`'s element and a `+Map+`'s key MUST support equality. Both ask whether two of them are the same, and a function has no value to compare (<<equality>>).

The external representation handled by Decoders and Encoders is not Souther's own types but the neutral value model of the boundary library Raoh (a tree of scalar / List / Map). Souther owns the domain's types, invariants, and behaviors; the external representation and its parsing and generation are delegated to the peripheral library Raoh (<<separate-representation>>, <<decoder>>).

From a data's shape, Souther derives the decode side per input source (bare values / Map = raoh-core, JSON `+JsonNode+` = raoh-json, jOOQ `+Record+` = raoh-jooq) and the encode side to a neutral Map (raoh-core). The decode skeleton (fields, keys, discriminator) is common across sources; only the terminal field access varies per source. JSON output serializes the Map at the boundary (`+objectMapper.valueToTree+`, etc.; <<codec-generation>>).

An external representation may contain null, but internal data forbids null. null exists only at the boundary (Raoh's input) and MUST NOT pass through a Decoder into the domain.

[[absence-is-written-as-null]]`+null+` is what a boundary writes for an absent value, wherever the value would have stood: `+None+` is written `+null+` and read back from `+null+`, and `+Some(v)+` is written the way `+v+` is written. An object's field has a second form for holding nothing, which is not being there at all, and a field takes that one — `+None+` omits the key, and a missing key reads back as `+None+` the same way `+null+` does (<<decoder-derivation>>, <<encoder-derivation>>). A collection's element and a `+Map+`'s value take `+null+`. A map's value does not omit, because a boundary map's keys are data rather than a declaration: dropping a key drops the entry and changes which keys there are, where omitting a field's key changes nothing about which fields the data has.

[[what-has-no-external-representation]]Three things have no external representation, and a position that requires one refuses each of them at any depth — inside a `+List+` or a `+Map+` written there as much as standing on its own. A *tuple*, whose members have no names to be written under (<<tuple>>). A *function*, which is code and has no value on the wire (<<fn>>). And an *optional whose own value is an optional*: absence has one form wherever it stands, so `+None+`, `+Some(None)+` and `+Some(Some(v))+` are three values facing two forms, and a field's omitted key is not a third — it is what absence looks like at a field and says what `+null+` says (<<optional>>). This is the list the boundary and the derived codec both read, and it is where a rule about one of them is written down.

[#builtin-types]
== Built-in types


[[a-value-has-the-type-its-position-requires]]A value MUST have the type its position requires (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>). This is the rule most others here are a special case of, and it is what a diagnostic falls back to where no narrower one applies.

[[a-lists-elements-have-one-type]]A list literal's elements MUST all have one type. A list is `+List<T>+` for a single `+T+`; a sequence of several types is a list of a sum they are cases of.

[[an-operator-takes-the-types-it-is-defined-for]]An operator MUST be given the types it is defined for: `+++++` joins two lists or two strings, unary minus takes an `+Int+` or a `+Decimal+`, a comparison takes two ordered values of one type, and `+==+` takes values that have something to compare — a function does not (<<equality>>).

[[a-condition-stops-when-its-answer-is-settled]]`+&&+` and `+||+` evaluate their operands left to right and stop as soon as the answer is settled: a false left operand is the answer of a `+&&+` and a true one is the answer of a `+||+`, and in neither case is the right operand evaluated. Which operands run is part of what the operators mean and not a choice a backend makes — `+/+` aborts on a zero divisor and `+Int+` overflows (<<stdlib-int>>), so `+x /= 0 && 100 / x > 1+` is how a condition narrows what its right side may compute, and evaluated eagerly it would abort at the value it exists to exclude.

[[a-tuple-pattern-matches-its-tuple]]A tuple pattern MUST be written on a tuple, and MUST bind as many names as the tuple has elements.

[[a-field-read-is-declared-by-the-value]]A field read MUST be a field the value has. A sum has no fields of its own — its cases do — and it exposes one only where every case spreads the data declaring it (<<sum-data>>); which case a value is is not known until it is opened.

[[a-temporal-value-is-written-as-a-literal]]A temporal literal MUST have its text spelled out (<<temporal-literal>>), so the text can be read at compile time and refused there rather than at run time. This says how a temporal is *written*; it does not say how one is *reached*. A temporal built from values a model holds comes from `+Date.fromParts+` / `+Time.fromParts+`, which answer a case where the parts name no such moment (<<a-temporal-built-from-parts-is-refused-where-the-parts-name-none>>).

[[a-temporal-built-from-parts-is-refused-where-the-parts-name-none]]`+Date.fromParts+` and `+Time.fromParts+` MUST answer `+NotADate+` / `+NotATime+` where the Ints they are given name no day and no time of day, and MUST NOT normalise. February the 30th is not the 2nd of March here, and the 25th hour is not one o'clock the next day. They are the inverses of the part readers, so `+Date.fromParts(Date.year(d), Date.month(d), Date.day(d)) == d+` holds of every `+Date+` and `+Time.fromParts(Time.hour(t), Time.minute(t), Time.second(t)) == t+` of every `+Time+`. This is where a refusal belongs once the parts are values rather than text: the arithmetic normalises, because shifting a date by a month has to land somewhere, so a model that reaches an impossible day by adding gets a real one back and no sign that it asked for a day the calendar does not have.

[[a-matches-pattern-is-a-literal-regular-expression]]`+String.matches+` takes a pattern that is a string literal and a valid regular expression. Both are read at compile time, which is the point of writing it out: a pattern computed at run time could not be checked at all.

[#primitives]
=== Primitive types

[,text]
----
Bool
Int          // signed 64-bit integer
Decimal      // java.math.BigDecimal
String
Date         // date. ISO 8601. java.time.LocalDate
Time         // time of day, to the second. ISO 8601. java.time.LocalTime
DateTime     // date-time, to the second. ISO 8601. java.time.LocalDateTime
Instant      // a moment on the timeline, to the nanosecond. ISO 8601 in UTC. java.time.Instant
----

`+Date+` / `+DateTime+` correspond to the spec DSL's `+Date+` / `+DateTime+`. The current time is not obtained in the language; it is declared as a behavior with no implementation (<<injected-behavior>>).

[[a-date-time-is-a-date-and-a-time]]A `+DateTime+` is a `+Date+` and a `+Time+`, and MUST be readable and writable as the two: `+DateTime.toDate+` / `+DateTime.toTime+` take it apart and `+DateTime.fromDateAndTime+` puts it back (<<stdlib-datetime>>). The last of those cannot fail — whatever could have been wrong about the parts was settled when the `+Date+` and the `+Time+` were built.

[[a-local-temporal-is-held-to-the-second]]`+Date+`, `+Time+` and `+DateTime+` are *local*: they say what a calendar and a clock read, and carry no zone. `+Time+` and `+DateTime+` are held to the second and MUST NOT carry a fraction of one — written text that has one is <<e1322>>, and text arriving at a boundary that has one is a decode failure at that path (<<decoder-error>>). Neither drops it: a value that cannot be read back is a value nothing downstream can tell from the one that was sent.

[[an-instant-carries-what-a-timestamp-said]]`+Instant+` is a moment on the timeline rather than a reading of a clock, and is held to the nanosecond, which is what an outside timestamp says. It has no year, no month and no hour: naming one needs a zone, and the language names none. So `+Instant+` has *no calendar decomposition* (<<stdlib-instant>>), and this is a rule rather than a gap — there is nothing to read a part with and nothing to build one from parts either, so the two directions are absent together. That its module is empty is the smaller fact: what the missing zone rules out is the calendar, and an operation that stays on the timeline would need none. What a model does with one it does without a library: two compare (<<primitives>>), one keys a `+Map+` (<<collections>>), one is written as a literal (<<temporal-literal>>), and turning one into a `+DateTime+` is a behavior with no implementation, the zone being supplied from outside as the current time is (<<injected-behavior>>). A boundary takes an `+Instant+` written with a numeric offset and answers the moment it names, which is the same moment the `+Z+` form of it names; source takes the `+Z+` form only (<<temporal-literal>>). Neither is a zone.

[#numeric-literal]
==== Numeric literals

An integer literal is written as `+500+` and is `+Int+`. A `+Decimal+` literal takes an `+m+` suffix — `+500m+`, `+1.5m+`, `+0.5m+`. A fractional literal without `+m+`, such as `+1.5+`, is an error. Souther has no binary floating-point type and defaults fractions to `+Decimal+` (exact decimal), so `+Int+` and `+Decimal+` MUST be made explicit at the literal.

[#string-literal]
==== String literals

A `+String+` literal is written between `+"+` and `+"+`, and it ends on the line it began on. A literal not closed before the end of the line is refused there (<<e2305>>). A newline in a value is written `+\n+`; the language has no multi-line literal form.

A backslash is read before these characters, and before nothing else:

[,text]
----
\n   a newline
\t   a tab
\r   a carriage return
\"   a quote, which does not close the literal
\\   a backslash
----

A backslash written before anything else is refused (<<e2305>>). It is not read as the character alone: doing that would take a character the author wrote out of the value and say nothing about it, so `+"\d"+` would be the text `+d+`. A regular expression's class is therefore written with the backslash doubled — `+"\\d{3}"+` is the text `+\d{3}+` (<<stdlib-string>>).

A literal's value is normalized to Unicode NFC. A source file is text from an editor, which is the other place text arrives from outside — the first being a decoder, which canonicalizes for the same reason (<<decoder>>). Two canonically equivalent forms are the same text by Unicode's definition and different values to a comparison by code units, so a literal left as written would compare unequal to the same text that came in through a boundary. Which form an editor writes is not something the author chose. The folding is NFC and not NFKC: compatibility folding turns ① into 1 and a half-width kana into a full-width one, which is a different claim about the text than "these are the same characters".

[#equality]
==== Equality

`+==+` and `+/=+` are *value equality*, not reference identity. Souther data are immutable values, so whether two are the same is decided by whether their fields are the same.

[,text]
----
guard approverId == request.applicant.managerId      // two EmployeeId built separately are equal if their values are equal
----

* primitives compare values
* data compare *fields recursively*, including fields taken in by a `+...+` spread
* sums are equal when they are the same case and their contents are equal
* `+List<T>+` compares elements in order; `+Map<String,T>+` compares keys and values
* `+T?+` is equal when both are `+None+`, or both are `+Some+` with equal contents
* a tuple compares its elements left to right (<<tuple>>)

Only *values of the same type* may be compared. Comparing different types is a compile error. A sum may also be compared with one of its *cases* (`+Rank == Staff+`): a case value is a value of its sum (case→sum is transparent, <<sum-data>>), so this is a sum-vs-sum comparison — true when the sum value is that case. Pure case membership is written this way for a case with no fields (a unit data, the usual state / role enum); a field-bearing case compared with `+==+` compares its fields too.

Ordering (`+<+` `+<=+` `+>+` `+>=+`) is defined for `+Int+` (numeric), `+String+` (lexicographic), `+Decimal+` (by value, ignoring scale), and `+Date+` / `+Time+` / `+DateTime+` / `+Instant+` (chronological — `+Instant+` to the nanosecond, the other three to the second, which is all they hold). A product data has no ordering, and neither does a sum with a field-bearing case. An *enumeration* — a sum every one of whose cases is a unit data (<<sum-discrimination>>) — is ordered too, by the order its cases are declared in, which is the order a state moves through (`+Prospecting < Won+`). A single-value newtype (`+data X = Y+`; <<newtype>>) over anything ordered *is* ordered — by the value it wraps — and that includes an enumeration: `+data StageN = Stage+` compares on `+Stage+`'s declaration order, and `+sort+` takes a `+List<StageN>+`. The newtype rule is about the value underneath and not about which kind it is, so excepting one kind from it would be a rule of its own.

The order belongs to the sum, not to the case value: the same unit data may be a case of two sums, which place it differently. A case value therefore takes its order from the sum it is compared with or held in (`+stage < Won+`, a `+List<Stage>+`), and a bare case value that two enumerations both list has no order of its own. Nor does a newtype over such a case: a name wrapped round a value carries the order that value has, and there is none to carry. Where one enumeration lists the case the value does have an order, and a newtype over it is ordered by that enumeration, as one over the *sum* is — what the name asks is whether the value it wraps is ordered, and it asks nothing else.

[#newtype-comparison]
===== Comparing a newtype

A single-value newtype is compared by the value it wraps, for both equality and ordering, so `+.value+` is not written — `+value+` is the newtype's implicit inner name, not something a caller spells out. The nominal boundary is kept:

* Two of the *same* newtype compare their wrapped values: `+Amount <= Amount+`, `+Amount == Amount+`.
* A *bare literal* of the wrapped type takes that newtype from the other operand: `+Amount <= 100+`, `+Amount == 0+` (the `+100+` is an `+Amount+`). This mirrors how `+[]+` takes its element type from context (<<algebraic-types>>).
* Two *different* newtypes over the same base do not compare: `+Amount <= Quantity+` is a compile error, even though both wrap `+Int+`.
* A non-literal value of the wrapped type is not taken implicitly: `+Amount <= n+` (where `+n+` is an `+Int+`) is a compile error — write `+Amount <= Amount(n)+`.

The wrapped type must itself be ordered for `+<+` / `+<=+` / `+>+` / `+>=+`; equality works over any wrapped type. Arithmetic on a newtype is `+{plus}+` / `+-+` only (<<newtype-arithmetic>>).

`+Decimal+` ignores scale. `+1.0+` and `+1.00+` are equal.

[,souther]
----
data Amount = Decimal
// read as 1.00 from a DB column, 1.0 from JSON. Treated as the same amount.
----

Scale is not lost from the value: it is carried, and Java reading the value sees it (<<jvm-product>>). What an Encoder writes is the *amount* — `+1.50+` and `+1.5+` are written `+1.5+`, and `+100.00+` is written `+100+` — because scale records how a number was written and not how much it is, and a boundary that wrote it would write two equal values two ways (<<encode-law>>). An exponent is spelt out into at most a thousand digits and is otherwise written as it stands (`+1E+1000000+` stays `+1E+1000000+`): what is bounded is the *spelling out*, not the number — an amount that already carries a thousand digits is written with all of them. Without a bound, `+1E+1000000+` would be eleven characters asking for a million and one. Which side of the bound an amount falls is decided by the amount, so two writings of one amount are still one number. The round trip (<<round-trip>>) is unaffected: identity ignores scale, so what is read back is the same value. If the number of rounding digits is a domain concern, write it as an invariant or a separate field, not as `+Decimal+` scale — a rounding call decides an amount, and the digits it rounded to are not what crossing the boundary shows.

The `+hashCode+` of a data containing `+Decimal+` is computed after dropping scale (`+stripTrailingZeros+`). If equality ignores scale, the hash MUST ignore it too, or using the value as a `+Map+` key breaks. Java sees the same value equality: a generated data class implements `+equals+` / `+hashCode+` from all fields and can be used as a `+Map+` key directly (<<jvm-product>>). This is distinct from Java's `+==+` (reference) and `+BigDecimal.equals+` (scale-sensitive).

[#newtype-arithmetic]
===== Arithmetic on a newtype


[[newtype-arithmetic-follows-the-units]]Arithmetic on newtypes MUST follow the units. Two newtypes over one base add and subtract only where they are the same newtype; a product and a quotient answer with what the units make, and where that is not a type the model declares there is nothing for the result to be. The refusal names the rule it is: which of them was broken is what an author needs, and it is not readable from the operand types alone.

A single-value newtype over `+Int+` or `+Decimal+` supports `+{plus}+` and `+-+`, and the result stays in the newtype: `+Money {plus} Money+` and `+Money - Money+` are `+Money+`. The operator opens each operand to its wrapped value, computes on the base, and re-wraps the result — so the result's invariant is re-checked at construction, exactly as any other construction is (<<invariant-mvp>>). A subtraction that leaves the invariant (a non-negative `+Money+` going below zero) *aborts* inside the domain (<<violation-destination>>). To make that a business result, guard it with `+guard ... else+` (<<guard>>); the compiler then confirms the guard discharges the construction (<<invariant-discharge>>).

Operand pairing is the same as comparison (<<newtype-comparison>>): the *same* newtype, or a newtype with a bare literal of its base (`+Money {plus} 100+`, the `+100+` taken as `+Money+`). Two *different* newtypes do not combine — `+Money - Quantity+` is a compile error even though both wrap `+Int+` — and a non-literal value of the wrapped type is not taken implicitly.

Arithmetic builds a value, so it is subject to <<closed-construction>>: a newtype of another module is built through the entry that module opens by exposing it, and a value of a type its module does *not* expose does not reach here at all: a module may not expose a data whose field would carry one out (<<exposed-surface>>). So the arithmetic and the entry it needs are always in the same place.

`+*+` and `+/+` *scale* a newtype by a plain number of its base and stay in the newtype (`+Money * 2+`, `+2 * Money+`, `+Money / 2+`) — the dimension is unchanged. The scalar is a bare `+Int+` / `+Decimal+` (literal or variable) of the newtype's base, not itself coerced to the newtype. A product or quotient of *two* newtypes *does* change dimension (`+UnitPrice * Quantity+` is a `+Money+`, not a `+UnitPrice+`), which the language does not model, so `+newtype * newtype+` (and `+Money * Money+`) is a compile error; compute those on `+.value+` and wrap the result explicitly. Only a newtype whose value is *directly* `+Int+` or `+Decimal+` has arithmetic — a newtype over another newtype does not.

Re-wrapping is a computation, not a fresh construction from raw data, so it needs no `+constructs+` (<<constructs>>) — as `+Int+` arithmetic does not "construct" an `+Int+`. A `+Money(...)+` literal construction (<<newtype>>) still does.

[#collections]
=== Collection types


[[a-generic-type-is-given-its-arguments]]A collection type is written with its arguments — `+List<Int>+`, `+Map<String, Int>+`, `+Set<String>+`. The bare qualifier names the module its functions are reached through (<<stdlib>>) and is not a type.

[,text]
----
List<T>
Map<K, V>
Set<T>
----

Collections are immutable. A `+List+` is ordered and may repeat; a `+Set+` is unordered with no duplicate elements, compared by value equality (<<primitives>>); a `+Map+` is keyed by `+K+`, so a key carries its domain type (`+Map<ProductId, Stock>+`). Inside a behavior body `+K+` is any value type — `+List.groupBy+` keys by whatever the projection returns. A map that *crosses the boundary* — a data field, a behavior's input or output, at any depth — is a JSON object, whose keys are strings, so there `+K+` MUST be `+String+`, a temporal (`+Date+` / `+Time+` / `+DateTime+` / `+Instant+`), an enumeration — a sum every one of whose cases is a unit data (<<sum-discrimination>>) — or a newtype over any of those. A newtype is written as what it wraps is written, so it is a key exactly when what it wraps is a key: `+data ProductId = String+` and `+data LoanDate = Date+` both key a boundary map, and so does a newtype over either of them, however many are written round it. A newtype key is decoded through its own decoder (its invariant enforced, and the invariant of anything it wraps, a bad key failing at that key's path) and encoded back through its own encoder; a temporal key is parsed from and written as its ISO 8601 form, the same representation a `+Date+` field has (`+{"2026-01-01": 300}+`); an enumeration key is the case's name, the same representation it has in a field (`+{"Won": 300}+`). An `+Int+`-backed newtype is not a key, because `+Int+` is not: a number is written as a number in a field, and a type's external form does not change with where it stands. A `+Set+`'s external representation is a JSON array, deduplicated on decode. The array is where the boundary has to put a value the language calls unordered, so no meaning is carried by the position an element ends up in: the order of the input array has no semantic significance and is not guaranteed to be preserved. What *is* written is fixed, and by the members rather than by the collection holding them — the array's members are written in ascending order of their own external representation, and a boundary map's members in ascending order of their rendered keys. So the order is decided by the members the collection turned out to hold: not by which of them share a hash, and not by whether the collection was decoded or built (<<stdlib-set>>, <<stdlib-map>>). Two collections that are equal therefore write the same JSON (<<encode-law>>). This is not the language's ordering (<<equality>>), which a `+Set+`'s element is not required to have; it is an order over what is written:

[,text]
----
null < false < true < number < string < array < object
  number : by amount — an amount is written one way (<<primitives>>), so two of one amount are one
  string : by UTF-16 code unit
  array  : element by element, the shorter one first where one runs out
  object : its members read in ascending key order, each key before its value
----

An element may itself be a collection — `+Map<String, List<ProductId>>+` is what bucketing rows by a key produces (`+List.groupBy+`, <<stdlib-list>>) — and it crosses the boundary as the nested JSON its shape implies, decoded and encoded the same way at every depth.

Only `+List+` is written as a literal. `+[ … ]+` is a `+List+` whatever the position it fills declares, so a `+Set+` field takes `+Set.fromList([ … ])+` and a `+Map+` field takes `+Map.fromList([ … ])+` or `+Map.empty+` — the conversion is written rather than implied, which is where a reader sees that duplicate elements collapse. The one thing context supplies is the element type of an empty collection, which `+[ ]+`, `+Map.empty+` and `+Set.empty+` have no argument to learn (<<stdlib-map>>). An `+example+` row is evaluated as this module's code like anything else, and its brackets are the one thing it writes that a body does not: there `+[ … ]+` is the collection the position it stands at declares (<<example-evaluable>>).

A collection MAY be what a newtype wraps (`+data TagSet = Set<Tag>+`, <<newtype>>), which names the collection itself and gives an invariant something to hold about it — that its elements are unique, that it is not empty. The newtype crosses the boundary as the collection's own representation, a JSON array or object, not as `+{"value": [...]}+`.

Collections are *covariant* in their element. A case `+A+` may be used as its sum `+S+` (<<sum-data>>), so `+List<A>+` may be passed where `+List<S>+` is expected, `+Set<A>+` where `+Set<S>+` is expected, `+Map<String, A>+` where `+Map<String, S>+` is expected, and `+A?+` where `+S?+` is expected. This is sound because collections are immutable and there is no room to write a sibling case later. Covariance of mutable arrays (Java) is unsound because writes are a hole; that is the difference. Covariance applies only in the case→sum direction; sibling cases (`+A+` and `+B+`) are unrelated.

[#algebraic-types]
=== Built-in algebraic and auxiliary types

[,text]
----
Option<T>    = Some(T) | None
Never                      // a type with no value: an impossible case (empty sum)
Unit                       // a type with exactly one value
----

Souther has no `+Result+` / `+Either+` type. A behavior's output is a bare domain sum with no two-slot type separating success and failure (<<behavior>>, <<composition>>).

`+Never+` and `+Unit+` are not type names writable at the surface. `+Never+` denotes an impossible case: a single-case output (`+-> A+`) can be read as having an empty failure row, and it is the type of `+unreachable+` (<<unreachable>>) — the one expression that answers no value, and so fits every position. `+Unit+` is a reading aid only; it corresponds to the spec DSL's `+Unit+`, but it is never provided as a way to write a field, and is always expressed as a "data with no fields" (<<sum-data>>).

The only polymorphic types are the stdlib ones (`+Option+`, `+List+`, `+Map+`, `+Set+`). There are no user-defined generics. Neither `+data+` nor `+behavior+` takes a type parameter.

[#invariant-abort]
==== An invariant violation aborts

When the construction of a data with an invariant violates it inside the domain (<<invariant-mvp>>), the computation *aborts*. It returns no value and no case.

[,souther]
----
behavior discount : (d: Quote) -> Discounted
    constructs Discounted
----

Writing a computation that breaks the invariant of `+Discounted+` does not change the output type; breaking it aborts. An abort is not a Souther expression and has no catching syntax, so it cannot control business flow (<<out-of-scope>>). At the boundary (<<java-example>>), Java catches it and maps it to 500, distinct from a business failure (400).
