If the author wants to return the violation, that is a business judgment and MUST be written explicitly with `+guard ... else+` (<<guard>>). An invariant is a declaration that "a value passing here is always so", not a branch designed to be broken.

That judgment MAY be written by *attempting* the construction (<<attempted-construction>>), which states the rule once instead of restating it in the guard. What the attempt answers is a branch, not a value: no failure value reaches the domain, and the name a business failure gets is still the one the author writes in `+else+`.

A decode-side violation does not abort; Raoh's `+Result+` failure carries it (<<decoder-error>>). The same invariant is handled differently inside and outside the boundary.

==== How Option appears

`+Option<T>+` is the name of the type a `+?+` field has. It is written in a type position like any other name (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>), and the positions that refuse it are these: the boundary, which is a behavior's parameter and its output (<<an-optional-does-not-stand-in-a-boundary>>); a newtype's base, which wraps a value that is always there (<<newtype>>); and, where an external representation is required, inside another optional, which leaves absence no way to say which of the two it is (<<what-has-no-external-representation>>). A helper's signature requires no representation and takes any of them (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>). On a data field `+T?+` means the same thing and is the shorter spelling. A recursive helper and a function-typed parameter MUST declare their types (<<fn-declaration>>), so for those the name is the only way to say it at all.

The mark `+?+` outside a field is written only in the shipped core, as a type variable is (<<stdlib>>): `+?+` marks where an optional is *made*, and the name says what the type is.

An optional is *made* in one place: a `+?+` field being given its value (<<optional>>). Writing the type does not make one — `+let p: Option<Int> = None+` is <<e1303>> — and neither does declaring it as a helper's return: a helper answers the optional it read. Where a helper's absence is its own to name, that is a case of the model's own sum.

[,souther]
----
data OrgMember =
    { manager: OrgMember?
    , level: Int
    }

let firstOver (n: Int, m: OrgMember): Option<OrgMember> =
    match m.manager with
        | Some b -> if b.level > n then m.manager else firstOver(n, b)
        | None -> m.manager // the absence it read, passed on
----

Where the boundary refuses it, what replaces it is a name the model owns. To state "might not be found" as a business result, that is a domain sum (<<unmarked-sum>>); an optional output is reported as itself rather than as a composition that does not fit (<<e1701>>).

[,souther]
----
behavior findMember : (id: MemberId) -> Member | NoSuchMember // write this
----

`+Some(T)+` is an exception to the rules of <<sum-data>> (a case is a reference to a named data and cannot carry a payload). Because `+Option+` is built in, users cannot imitate this form. Its contents are taken out with `+match+`; the `+v+` in `+| Some v+` is positionally bound to the wrapped value, not to `+Some+` (<<match>>).

[#optional]
=== Optional type

Optionality is expressed with `+?+`.

[,souther]
----
data TravelCompleted =
    { preApprover: EmployeeId?
    }
----

This desugars to:

[,text]
----
preApprover: Option<EmployeeId>
----

[[optional-marks-one-type]]`+?+` marks one type optional. It is written on a single type and not on a sum: what a sum of several cases is missing is a case of its own, not a mark on the whole.

[#option-cases-are-not-written]
To put a value in a `+?+` field, write an `+EmployeeId+` value directly and it is wrapped in `+Some+`; write `+None+` for empty. This is the only place either name may be written as a value: what puts it there is the field, not the type of the position — a model may name `+Option<T>+` where it reads one (<<algebraic-types>>), and an annotation saying so does not license making one. Users never write `+Some(...)+` to construct a value (as in <<algebraic-types>>, this form is built-in only); `+Some(...)+` appears only as a destructuring pattern in `+match+` (<<match>>).

[,text]
----
TravelCompleted { preApprover = approverId }   // wrapped in Some(approverId)
TravelCompleted { preApprover = None }         // empty
----

Wrapping in `+Some+` and `+None+` are not data construction, so `+constructs+` is not required (<<constructs>>). Extraction is done with `+match+`'s `+| Some v+` / `+| None+` (<<match>>).

Outside a field, `+?+` is written only in the shipped core (<<algebraic-types>>); a user model marks nothing else with it, and names the type where it reads one.

The Decoder's external type is (Raoh's Decoder):

[,text]
----
Decoder<T> = external representation -> Result<T>    // failure carries Raoh's Issues
----

[#data]
== data

`+data+` defines typed data of the internal world. All fields are immutable.

A declaration takes the form `+data <Name> = <right-hand side>+`. If the right-hand side is `+{ ... }+` it is a product; `+A | B+` a sum; a single type name `+Y+` a newtype wrapping `+Y+` (<<newtype>>); if the right-hand side is omitted, a unit. This mirrors the spec DSL's `+data <Name> = ...+`.

[#product-data]
=== Product data (AND)

The spec DSL's `+AND+` maps to a product. A field name is the business role; the type may be specified separately.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: data Employee = EmployeeId AND Rank AND ManagerId
data Employee =
    { id: EmployeeId
    , rank: Rank
    , managerId: EmployeeId // role name (managerId) differs from type (EmployeeId)
    }
----

Role and type may be written separately. In the spec DSL the same concept has a different name per layer: `+approverId+` (operation input) becomes `+preApproverId+` (stored state) becomes `+pre_approver_id+` (DB column). A field such as `+preApprover: EmployeeId+` writes the role in the field name and the concept in the type.

[#self-referential-data]
==== Self-referential data

Data MAY reference itself (directly or via another data). Structures such as an org chart or a category tree appear routinely in business.

[,souther]
----
data OrgMember =
    { manager: OrgMember?
    , name: String
    } // the manager is again an OrgMember (the chain ends at None)
data Department =
    { children: List<Department>
    , name: String
    } // a tree (a leaf has an empty children list)
----

The derived codecs follow this self-reference. A nested `+{"name":"a","manager":{"name":"b"}}+` decodes to an `+OrgMember+` whose `+manager+` is again an `+OrgMember+`. Traversing a self-reference *by computation* (climbing the manager chain to the root, counting tree depth) is written with a recursive helper `+let+` (<<fn-declaration>>). `+fold+` folds only a fixed-structure `+List+`, so a self-reference of unbounded depth can be traversed only with helper recursion.

A self-reference requires a *base case*: an occurrence on the cycle that has a value holding no further one. A cycle with none needs its own value to construct itself, which continues indefinitely — it is not constructible. Therefore *a self-referential cycle with no base case is a compile error*.

* `+manager: OrgMember+` (no `+?+`) is a cycle with no base case. It is a compile error, not a runtime stack overflow.
* `+manager: OrgMember?+` is a base case. `+None+` is a value of every optional and no rule can remove it: a newtype MUST NOT wrap an optional (<<e1009>>), so there is nowhere to write one.
* `+children: List<Department>+` is a base case where the empty list is a value the rules admit. The empty collection is not held by the language the way `+None+` is, and a rule on how much the collection holds removes it:
+
[,souther]
----
data Children = List<Department>
    invariant nonEmpty = List.length(value) >= 1
data Department =
    { children: Children
    , name: String
    } // no first value: the empty list is gone, so no Department bottoms out
----
+
Whether the collection may be empty is decided by whether any value satisfying the rules holds nothing, over every rule that reaches the occurrence — those written on its own type, through each name it wears, and those the record holding it writes about that field, including rules that reach it through another field. No spelling is privileged: a floor, an equality, a disequality from none and a ceiling below none all remove the empty collection, because each leaves nothing satisfying the rules that holds none. A rule stating only how many at most leaves it where it was.

Which occurrence a cycle stops at is a property of the occurrence and not of the type standing there. The same `+Children+` is a base case at `+children: Children?+` and is not at `+children: Children+`. A count read at an occurrence carries through the names that value wears, and stops at what a collection holds, which is a value of its own.

This decides a cycle and not inhabitance in general. A sum is an OR composition and is taken as a base case, because a cycle through one may bottom out in a case the cycle did not come through; a sum whose every case is on the cycle therefore has no value and is not reported.

[#field-spread]
=== Field composition (spread)

In the spec DSL, a state sharing common items is written as `+data Submitted = TravelRequestCommon AND submittedAt+`, meaning "has all fields of `+TravelRequestCommon+`, plus `+submittedAt+`". Souther expresses this with the `+...+` spread.

[,souther]
----
data TravelRequestCommon =
    { applicant: Employee
    , estimatedCost: Amount
    , destination: String
    , purpose: String
    , startsOn: Date
    , endsOn: Date
    }

// spec DSL: data Submitted = TravelRequestCommon AND SubmittedAt
data Submitted =
    { ...TravelRequestCommon // take in the common item's fields flatly
    , submittedAt: DateTime
    }
----

The `+...+` spread is defined as follows.

* The taken-in fields are laid out flatly. A `+Submitted+` value is referenced directly as `+x.applicant+` (not nested as `+x.common.applicant+`).
* The taken-in data's invariants are inherited too. The invariants of `+TravelRequestCommon+` are checked when `+Submitted+` is constructed. The spread carries not only fields but invariants.
* [[spread-fields-do-not-collide]]A field-name collision is a compile error.
* A spread is not inheritance. `+Submitted+` is not a subtype of `+TravelRequestCommon+`; the two are not assignment-compatible. They only share fields.
* A spread is an element of the same comma sequence as fields. It appears inside `+{+` separated by `+,+`, and multiple may be written. By convention it is placed first. It uses the same `+...+` as the value-level record-literal spread (<<record-literal>>).

To hold the common items as a single value rather than flattened, nest it as a normal field (`+common: TravelRequestCommon+`). The spec DSL's `+AND+` is a spread; explicit nesting is a normal field.

[#sum-data]
=== Sum data (OR)

The spec DSL's `+OR+` maps to a sum. *A case MAY reference an already-defined named data.*

[[a-sum-case-is-a-declared-data]]A case MUST be a data that is declared. [[a-sum-does-not-contain-itself]]A sum MUST NOT contain itself, through a case or through a chain of them: a sum's cases are the values it can be, and one of them being the sum is not a value. As in the business-trip model, where each state has its own fields, invariants, and transition behaviors and is bound together by `+OR+`, this structure is represented directly.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: data TravelRequest = Drafting OR Submitted OR AwaitingPreApproval OR PreApproved
data TravelRequest = Drafting | Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval | PreApproved
----

Nested sums MAY be written directly.

[,souther]
----
data CostBearer = OwnCost | ClientCost
data OwnCost = Reimbursement | Advance | CorporateCard
----

A case names a data; an inline record MUST NOT be written in a case position. A case that carries fields is declared separately, as the spec DSL declares it (for `+data Rank = Manager OR Staff+`, `+data Manager = { level: Level }+`). A case declared nowhere carries no fields, and the case list is its declaration (<<unit-data>>).

*A case value is a value of its sum.* As in functional languages, a value of a case type (e.g. `+Submitted+`) may be used transparently wherever its sum type (`+TravelRequest+`) is expected — field assignment, argument, or return value. Only the upward direction (case→sum) is allowed; the downward direction (sum→specific case) requires `+match+`. A nested sum (`+data OwnCost = Reimbursement | Advance+` being a case of `+CostBearer+`) is decided the same way, folding to the leaf case.

[[sum-cases-are-declared-with-the-sum]]A sum's cases are declared with it. A case a module imports MUST NOT be one of them: the sum is published as a sealed interface its own module closes, so another module's type cannot be a member of it. What crosses a boundary is matched and restated as a case of this module.

*What holds of every case is a property of the sum.* A sum has no fields of its own, so reading one is an error and the value must be opened with `+match+` — except for a field that *every* case spreads (<<field-spread>>): the sum exposes the fields of a data all of its cases include, and `+d.id+` reads it without opening the value. The same part is spread from the sum into a construction, so `+Filed { ...d, filedOn = on }+` writes at once what a `+match+` arm per case would write alike.

[,souther]
----
data DocumentCommon =
    { id: String
    }
data Draft =
    { ...DocumentCommon
    }
data Sent =
    { ...DocumentCommon
    , at: DateTime
    }
data Document = Draft | Sent
data Filed =
    { ...DocumentCommon
    , filedOn: Date
    }

let idOf (d: Document): String = d.id
let file (d: Document, on: Date): Filed = Filed { ...d, filedOn = on }
----

The shared part is what the author wrote: a spread is nominal (<<field-spread>>), so two cases that happen to declare a field of the same name have not shared it, and reading it stays an error. A spread reached through another spread counts, and a case that is itself a sum contributes its own leaves. When a case stops spreading the common data the field is no longer exposed, and the error appears at the read.

The read and the spread reach the same part and no more. What a single case carries of its own is not spread from the sum, so a construction wanting it is the missing-field error it would be from any other source (<<e1005>>), and a sum whose cases share no spread is not a spread source at all. A sum is still not assignment-compatible with the data its cases spread, and constructing one of its cases — deciding which case a value is — is still `+match+`.

[#unit-data]
=== Unit data

A data with no fields is declared with no body. It corresponds to the spec DSL's `+= Unit+`.

[[unit-data-is-written-without-a-body]]A unit data is written without a body. An empty body names a type with one value, which is what a unit data is, so the two spellings would be one declaration written two ways.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: data Reimbursement = Unit
data Reimbursement
data Advance
data CorporateCard
data ClientCost
data Staff
----

Unit data appear frequently as sum cases (such as `+Staff+` in `+data Rank = Manager | Staff+`).

A unit written where a module *introduces* a type needs no declaration of its own: the name alone declares it. Three positions introduce a type — a sum's case list (<<sum-data>>), a behavior's output (<<behavior-io>>), and the output declared for a `+>->+` composition, written either on the composition or in `+exposing+` (<<declared-composition-output>>).

[,souther]
----
data CostBearer = OwnCost | ClientCost
data OwnCost = Reimbursement | Advance | CorporateCard
// Reimbursement, Advance, CorporateCard and ClientCost are units; nothing else declares them

behavior quote : (c: Cart) -> PricedCart | EmptyCart // EmptyCart is a unit
----

Everywhere else a type is *referred to*, and a name nothing declares is unknown there and reported: a parameter type, a field's type, `+constructs+`, `+depends on+`, a `+match+` arm, a codec variant, a `+let+`'s declared return type.

Whether a name is declared is answered within the module — its own declarations and the names its `+import+` lines list. A qualified name (`+inventory.InsufficientStock+`) names another module's declaration, so it is a reference wherever it is written. A sum's cases are declared with the sum, so they are module-local in any event (<<e1606>>).

The explicit declaration stays available, and is how a case that carries a comment of its own is written.

[[a-unit-introduced-by-a-case-list-is-not-declared-again]]Where a position introduces the type, writing the declaration as well SHOULD be avoided: `+data Rank = Manager | Staff+` beside `+data Staff+` says one thing twice, and the second spelling is the one that goes stale — a case renamed in the list leaves the bare declaration behind as a type nothing refers to. The vocabulary a model reads by is the case list, so let it be the declaration. Write the separate `+data+` when the case has something of its own to carry: a comment, or an `+exposing+` entry the sum does not give it. This is a modelling convention rather than a consequence of <<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>; what the two amount to together is that for most units the case list is the only place the name is written at all.

A unit data MUST NOT carry an `+invariant+`: it has no field for one to observe and no value it could reject (<<invariant-declaration>>). Writing one is a compile error rather than a clause with no effect.

A data body MUST NOT be empty: `+data T = { }+` names a type with one value, which is what a unit data is, so it is a second spelling of one. The two are not interchangeable where it counts — an empty product is built as `+T {}+` and a unit by its name alone — so the declaration form silently decides how the value is written. Writing it is a compile error naming the unit form. A body of spreads alone (`+data T = { ...CommonItems }+`) is not empty: what it includes gives it its fields (<<field-spread>>).

A unit data value is constructed by *writing the name directly* (as a zero-argument constructor becomes a value in functional languages). An empty record such as `+T {}+` is not used.

[,text]
----
// construct a unit in an implementation (let). Requires the behavior's constructs
[ClientBorne | bearer == ClientCost]     // ClientBorne is a unit, constructed by name alone
----

This is a guard comprehension (<<stdlib-list>>): it yields one `+ClientBorne+` if the condition holds, an empty list otherwise.

A bare identifier in an expression resolves as: a variable if a bound local exists; otherwise a built-in value (such as `+None+`; <<algebraic-types>>); otherwise the construction of a unit data of the same name; otherwise undefined. Souther does not distinguish identifiers syntactically by case (so that Japanese business vocabulary can be used as identifiers), so this resolution is done in the symbol table.

Because a local shadowing a built-in value is always an error, using a *built-in value name* (`+None+`; <<algebraic-types>>) as a binding name — `+let+`, a `+match+` binding, or a lambda argument — is a compile error. This prevents a shadowed built-in from silently becoming unreachable. A unit data name is domain vocabulary and may be used as a binding, in which case the local takes precedence and constructing a unit data of that name is not writable in the same scope. The rounding-mode cases (<<stdlib-decimal>>) are ordinary unit data and follow this rule; a shadowed case has no qualified escape syntax.

[#field-visibility]
=== Field visibility

A field is read as `+e.field+`, where `+e+` is a name, a call, or a construction, and `+.a.b+` chains from the left. So `+amountOf(i).value+` opens a newtype on the call that builds it, with no `+let+` written to hold the result. This never competes with the bare `+.field+` getter of <<blocks>>: a getter starts an expression, a read follows one.

*Reading* a field is possible wherever that data's type is visible. Within the same module, any data of that module may be read. A data of another module may have its fields read if it is exposed with `+exposing+` — being importable means it is exposed. An exposed data has all fields readable (there is no per-field exposure). A behavior may read the fields of its input data even when they come from another module.

A value of a type its module keeps to itself never arrives in another module, because the field that would have carried it there cannot exist: what a module reaches out with may not rest on what it keeps (<<exposed-surface>>). So "the data's type is visible" is the whole rule, and there is no second one about what a reader may do with a value it has no name for.

What is constrained is *not reading but construction*. Reading a field and constructing a new value of that data are separate authorities, so allowing cross-boundary reads leaves the guarantee that no unvalidated value can be built (<<asymmetric-interop>>) intact. There is no need to define a getter-only behavior just to pass a value out (<<behavior-composition-unit>>).

On the JVM, a public read accessor is generated for every field of a data, and a data is a record over its fields (<<jvm-product>>). Because module = package, the accessor is what reads across the boundary; on a data the module does not expose, the class is package-private, so the accessors are out of reach from outside all the same. Java can read an exposed one, but that is the same exposure as the encoder already emitting all fields as JSON; what is guarded is construction. The constructor stays non-public, and to build a value Java must go through the invariant-checking decoder (<<jvm-construction-privacy>>).

[#union-intersection]
=== Union types and intersection types

Souther has union types. They appear in three forms.

* named sum `+data X = A | B+` (sealed; subject to exhaustiveness checking)
* anonymous union in a behavior output `+-> Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval+` (<<unmarked-output>>)
* anonymous union with failure cases in a behavior output `+-> Out | E1 | E2+` (<<type-routing>>)

An anonymous union is read as a type wherever a whole type is read (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>). Being read in a position is not being admitted by it, and one of those positions refuses the form: a behavior's parameter, which names what a caller has to write (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>). A behavior's output is the position the form is *for* — it is where a behavior's answer is shaped, and the two other forms above are written nowhere else — which is a different sentence from the only one it may stand in. To carry a sum across a behavior's input, make a named data (<<unmarked-output>>); naming it makes it sealed, with exhaustiveness checking and a JVM type (<<jvm-sum>>). Where the spec DSL writes `+(A OR B)+` in an input, the sum is named as a `+data+` and then referenced. A JVM type is generated for an output anonymous union (<<jvm-anonymous-union>>).

[#union-member]
[[a-union-member-is-nameable-in-an-arm]]A member of an anonymous union MUST be nominal and MUST tell itself apart from the other members at run time, and it MUST have a form a `+match+` arm can write. The primitives (`+Int+` / `+Bool+` / `+Decimal+` / `+String+` / `+Date+` / `+Time+` / `+DateTime+` / `+Instant+`) and named data types meet both, a data whether this module declares it or imports it. A collection meets neither: its type argument is erased, so `+List<Order>+` and `+List<Item>+` are one type at run time and no arm could choose between them, and no arm form names one. `+Option+` and a function type are outside for the same reason. What a member may be does not depend on where the union came from — a union written as a behavior's output and one a `+>->+` composition arrived at (<<type-routing>>) are the same union.

A named sum member contributes its cases and not itself, as it does in an external representation (<<sum-discrimination>>). The members a union is checked and generated from are therefore its *effective* members: every member written, with each named sum expanded to its leaves.

Each effective member goes by a name of its own. A `+match+` arm names a member by the name written for it, and so does the `+"type"+` discriminator (<<sum-discrimination>>), so one name cannot stand for two members. `+-> up.Yen | other.Yen+` is refused, and so is a `+Yen+` this module declares beside an imported one — including where one of them arrives as the leaf of a named sum member.

Structural intersection types (`+A & B+`, a type operator for "a value that is both A and B") are *not* provided. The spec DSL's `+AND+` means "has all of A's fields, plus more", not "is both A and B", and is fully expressed by the `+...+` spread of <<field-spread>> (take fields in flatly, inherit invariants, not a subtype).

[#newtype]
=== newtype (`+data X = Y+`)

When the right-hand side is a single type rather than a `+{ ... }+` product or a `+|+` sum, `+X+` is a newtype wrapping that type `+Y+`.

[[newtype-wraps-a-value-that-is-always-there]]What a newtype wraps MUST be there. A newtype is one value under another name and takes that value's representation, so an optional leaves it nothing to be when the value is absent; the absence belongs to the field that holds the newtype (<<optional>>).

[[newtype-wraps-an-external-representation]]What a newtype wraps MUST have an external representation of its own, since the newtype takes it as its own (<<external-representation>>). What has none cannot be wrapped (<<what-has-no-external-representation>>); a named data can be.

 `+Y+` is written the way a type is written anywhere else: a primitive (`+String+` / `+Int+` / `+Date+`, etc.), a named data, a sum, or a collection (`+List<T>+` / `+Set<T>+` / `+Map<K, V>+`, <<collections>>), including a collection of any of these. `+X+` is a distinct type from `+Y+` (nominally distinguished) with the same (bare) external representation as `+Y+`. The spec DSL's `+data X = Y+` (right-hand side a single name) is written directly.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: data EmployeeId = String // not empty
data EmployeeId = String
    invariant String.length(value) > 0

// spec DSL: data InsufficientAuthority = Rank
data InsufficientAuthority = Rank

// spec DSL: data CostLine = CostType (CostType is a sum)
data CostLine = CostType

// a collection the domain has a name for, with an invariant on the collection itself
data TagSet = Set<Tag>
data Stock = Map<ProductId, Int>
----

`+Y+` MUST have an external representation, since `+X+` takes it as its own: a tuple (<<tuple>>) and a function type (<<fn>>) are refused as a newtype's base, where they are written. `+Y+` MUST NOT be optional — neither `+data X = Y?+` nor `+data X = Option<Y>+` — because whether a value is present is a property of the place it is used, written there as `+f: X?+`. A `+Map+` that a newtype wraps crosses the boundary as a JSON object, so its key follows the same rule a field's does (<<collections>>).

`+data X = Y+` is equivalent to having a single field `+value: Y+`. Construction applies the value to the type name, `+X(v)+` (`+Amount(500)+`, `+MemberId("m-01")+`), equal to `+X { value = v }+`. Reference is `+x.value+`; an invariant refers to the inside as `+value+`. The difference from `+data X = { value: Y }+` (with braces) is *the external representation only*: `+data X = Y+` reads and writes `+Y+`'s representation bare (`+EmployeeId+` is `+"e-01"+`), while `+data X = { value: Y }+` becomes an Object `+{"value": "e-01"}+`.

The constructor `+X(v)+` is written with parentheses like function application. Juxtaposition `+X v+` is not adopted, because Souther's application is uniformly `+f(x)+` and because, absent a capitalization rule on identifiers, `+Amount -5+` would be ambiguous between construction and subtraction. An invariant violation aborts like any other data construction (<<violation-destination>>). A construction whose argument is a compile-time constant statically verified to satisfy the invariant cannot abort, so it may appear anywhere in the type body; a construction where a constant breaks the invariant, such as `+Amount(-5)+`, is a compile error. A construction passing a runtime value may appear wherever an expression may — a behavior's result, a non-tail `+let+`, a `+match+` arm — and aborts on violation.

Whether it is a newtype is *decided by this syntax*, not inferred from the single-field shape. Adding a field to `+data Amount = { value: Int }+` does not silently change its representation (`+{...}+` is always an Object, even with a single field). For the bare representation, write `+data Amount = Int+`.

Because there is no `+|+`, there is no room to read `+data InsufficientAuthority = Rank+` as a one-case sum. A sum always has `+|+` (<<sum-data>>). When `+Y+` is a sum, `+X+`'s external representation is `+Y+`'s discriminated representation, and if it becomes a case of a nested sum it is folded to the leaf and dispatched (<<sum-discrimination>>).

[#invariant]
== invariant

[#invariant-declaration]
=== Declaration

A value constraint written as a comment in the spec DSL is made executable as an `+invariant+`.

[[invariant-expression-is-bool]]An invariant clause states a condition, so its expression MUST have type `+Bool+`.

[[invariant-needs-a-value-to-constrain]]A type an invariant is declared on MUST have a value for it to observe. A unit data has one value and no field, so there is nothing for a clause to read and nothing it could reject.

[[an-invariant-is-declared-where-a-construction-owes-it]]An invariant MUST be declared on a type something constructs. A sum names which cases a value can be and is never itself built — `+T(...)+` is refused wherever it is written, and a value of `+T+` is written as one of its cases (<<a-sum-case-is-a-declared-data>>) — so a clause declared on one would be owed by no construction and checked at no boundary. Write it on the case, or on a newtype over the sum, which is built and carries its own check (<<newtype>>). Writing one on a sum is a compile error rather than a clause with no effect, for the reason the same rule is stated for a unit data: a clause that is dropped takes whatever is written inside it along with it.

[[an-invariant-observes-and-does-not-build]]An invariant observes the value being built and MUST NOT build one. A construction inside it would run the built type's own check inside this one, and the answer to whether this value holds would depend on whether another one did.

[[an-invariant-answers-on-every-path]]An invariant MUST answer on every path through it. It is checked on every construction and at every boundary (<<invariant-mvp>>), so it may not reach an `+unreachable+` and may not reach a `+partial+` helper, which publishes no termination guarantee (<<fn-rules>>).

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: data Amount = Integer // at least 0, in yen
data Amount = Int
    invariant value >= 0

data DateRange =
    { start: Date
    , end: Date
    }
    invariant start <= end
----

[#invariant-clause-name]
=== A clause MAY be named

A clause MAY be given a name, which is what tells it apart from the type's other clauses: `+invariant <name> = <expr>+`.

[[invariant-clause-names-are-distinct]]No two clauses of one type may carry the same name. The name is what an attempted construction and a boundary failure call one rule by, so two rules cannot answer to it.

[[invariant-clause-name-is-not-underscore]]A clause may not be named `+_+`. `+| _ -> …+` answers the clauses that carry no name (<<attempt-departures>>), so a clause named `+_+` could never be the one it answered. An attempted construction departs by the named clause that did not hold (<<attempted-construction>>), and a decode failure reports the name (<<decoder-error>>).

[,souther]
----
data QuoteLines = List<QuoteLine>
    invariant nonEmpty = List.length(value) >= 1
    invariant uniqueProducts = List.allDistinctBy(.product, value)
----

Two clauses that apply to one data MUST NOT share a name, a `+...+` spread's included clauses among them: a name that answered two rules would answer neither in particular. A clause MUST NOT be named `+_+`: that is what an attempt writes for the clauses carrying no name, so a clause named so could never be answered by name. A clause a spread takes in keeps the name its own declaration gave it.

A clause written without a name is enforced exactly as before one could be named: nothing tells it apart from the type's other unnamed clauses, so it aborts inside the domain and fails the decode outside it without saying which rule it was, and no arm can name it.

[#invariant-mvp]
=== Checking on construction

Invariants are checked at runtime. The compiler inserts an invariant check on every path that constructs a data. Invariants taken in by a `+...+` spread are checked on the same path.

The clauses are checked in the order they are declared — a `+...+` spread's first, then the declaration's own — and the first that does not hold is the one the failure names. Where several clauses fail, only the first is reported, so reordering the clauses of a published type changes what a caller is told and is a breaking change.

[#invariant-expressions]
=== Expressions allowed / forbidden in an invariant

Allowed: comparison, logical operations, arithmetic, field reference, built-in functions, blocks, and a `+let+` satisfying the three conditions below.

[,souther]
----
let isNonNegative (v: Int) = v >= 0

data Amount = Int
    invariant isNonNegative(value)
data Cart =
    { items: List<Line>
    }
    invariant List.all(x -> isNonNegative(x.quantity), items)
----

What may be called is a `+let+`, not a behavior (<<fn>>). An invariant is part of what a type is: it travels to an importing module as the source it was written as, together with the helper `+let+`s it names (<<published-modules>>). It names only what travels with it, so a behavior MUST NOT be called from an invariant whatever its requirement set — including one that depends on nothing and is callable from a `+let+` body (<<calling-a-behavior>>). To name a business rule, use a `+let+`.

A value or a helper another module publishes travels with it, so an invariant MAY name one, as a `+let+` body may (<<exposed-values>>). A limit or a format several modules' types are written against is therefore written once, in the module that owns it.

[,souther]
----
module limits exposing ( maxLength )

let maxLength = 20
----

[,souther]
----
module Member exposing ( MemberName )

import limits ( maxLength )

data MemberName = String
    invariant String.length(value) <= maxLength
----

The value is substituted and the helper expanded where they are named, closed by the module that declares them (<<exposed-values>>), so what the rule says does not change with where it is read. A project importing `+MemberName+` from a jar reads the invariant as it was written and resolves `+maxLength+` against `+limits+`, which is why that module is on its class path (<<published-modules>>).

A `+let+` may be called from an invariant when it satisfies all of the following, all statically decidable.

* the requirement set is empty (the corresponding behavior writes no `+depends on+`; <<depends-on>>)
* it constructs no data (the construction set of any helper `+let+` it calls is also empty; <<blocks>>)
* it reaches nothing `+partial+`

The first is decided by reading the declaration. `+let preApprove+` does not touch the outside world directly and so is pure in the sense of <<purity>>, but `+behavior preApprove+` writes `+depends on now+`, so its requirement set is non-empty and that `+let+` MUST NOT be called from an invariant.

A helper `+let+` always satisfies the first and second. To touch the outside world it can only receive a function as an argument (<<fn-declaration>>), and an invariant has no way to pass one. The third is about termination: an invariant is checked on every construction and must terminate. A `+partial+` helper carries no termination guarantee, so it cannot appear in an invariant — nor be reached from one through any chain of calls, which is how the condition is decided. Inlining leaves a recursive helper standing, so what a clause names is not all it runs, and the whole path is reported. A *total* helper is admissible even when it recurses — the compiler has proven it terminates (<<fn-declaration>>). This is what lets the list quantifiers `+List.all+` / `+List.any+` / `+List.contains+` and the uniqueness checks `+List.distinct+` / `+List.allDistinctBy+` appear in an invariant: each derives from the total stdlib fold `+List.foldFrom+`, which is bounded by the finite list.

Forbidden: Decoder, Encoder, data construction, `+partial+` recursion, `+unreachable+` (<<unreachable>>), non-deterministic processing. Data construction is barred throughout the invariant expression, including inside a quantifier's closure — an invariant observes the value being built, it does not build another. `+unreachable+` is barred for the neighbouring reason: an invariant answers whether the value holds, and it answers on every path through itself, so a path that aborts instead decides a construction by a premise the clause never stated. A behavior call is barred by the rule above, whether written in the invariant or reached through a `+let+` it calls; and a `+let+` that goes through a behavior touching the outside world has that much of a requirement set and is rejected by condition 1 as well. Only pure (empty-requirement-set) computation enters an invariant.

[#violation-destination]
=== A violation's destination differs inside and outside the boundary

The same invariant is handled differently depending on where it breaks.

* *Decoder (boundary)*: the violation enters a failure (`+Issue+`) of Raoh's `+Result+` (<<decoder-error>>). A value that came from outside was invalid — an *expected result*. Which field broke it can be stated via `+path+` and `+code+`.
* *behavior (inside the domain)*: it *aborts* (<<algebraic-types>>). A computation among validated values broke a rule — a *model bug*.

[,souther]
----
data Discounted =
    { amount: Int
    }
    invariant amount >= 0

behavior discount : (d: Quote) -> Discounted // no violation case needed
    constructs Discounted
----

*A behavior's output needs no slot for the violation.* Constructing an invariant-bearing data does not change the output type.

If the business decides "return a different result when the amount is negative", that is not an invariant violation but a business judgment and MUST be written explicitly with `+guard ... else+` (<<guard>>). These are different: an invariant declares "a value passing here is always so", while `+guard+` is a business branch of "if not so, return this". The former breaking is a bug; the latter not holding is an expected result.

[#invariant-discharge]
=== Discharging an invariant at compile time

An invariant is checked at run time on every construction (<<invariant-mvp>>), and inside the domain a violation aborts (<<violation-destination>>). The compiler *also* checks, per behavior, whether each construction's invariant is *established* where the value is built — so a construction that must abort is caught at compile time, and one a guard makes safe is confirmed.

[[a-construction-is-judged-by-what-it-builds]]A construction is judged by what it builds. What each field is given is what the check reads, whether the source wrote that field a value, took it from another value with a `+...+` spread (<<record-literal>>), or wrote no field at all — a newtype's constructor and the arithmetic that re-wraps one (<<newtype-arithmetic>>) build a value like any other and owe its invariant where they stand. Two ways of writing one construction are one construction: they owe the same clauses, over the same values, and come to the same verdict.

The check is intraprocedural. Within a behavior's `+let+` body it seeds what the input types guarantee — a newtype's own invariant, a product data's invariant over its fields — which is sound because an input of type `+T+` was built through `+T+`'s checked construction. It refines that along each `+guard+` / `+if+` guard (<<guard>>, <<if>>), and at every construction asks whether what is known there proves its invariant. A guard is one of those sources and not the whole of them, which is why what a report of this check says is what is known and not what a guard did.

* *Discharged* — what is known there proves the invariant. No diagnostic; the construction cannot abort here (overflow aside).
* [[construction-satisfies-its-invariant]]*Definitely violated* — what is known there proves the invariant false on a reachable path. A compile error — the path-sensitive generalization of the constant check `+Money(-5)+` (<<newtype>>).
* [[construction-invariant-is-discharged]]*Possibly violated* — expressible but not proven. A warning: a possible abort. Two answers and three spellings: where the failing inputs are ones the behavior takes, the failure is a business one — guard it with `+guard+`, or attempt the construction (<<attempted-construction>>), which states the invariant check once rather than restating it as a guard; where they are not inputs the model admits, the relation belongs to a data that owns it and is reified into that data's invariant (below), which means introducing the data where none holds the values the relation is about. There is no third answer that leaves the construction as written and accepts the abort.
* *Not expressible* — a clause outside the checked fragment. No diagnostic for it; the run-time check stands. The other clauses of the same invariant are answered on their own, so writing one the check cannot read beside one it can does not cost the second its report (<<invariant-discharge-capability>>).

Guards that cannot all hold describe a path the behavior does not take, and a construction standing on one is neither of the middle two: nothing is built there, so nothing is reported. That the guards contradict is itself derived over the same fragment — `+a <= b+` with `+b <= 0+` bounds `+a+` above without a word about `+a+` — so a path is dead whenever the relations say so, whatever order they were written in.

[,souther]
----
data Money = Decimal
    invariant value >= 0m
data WithdrawRequest =
    { balance: Money
    , amount: Money
    }

behavior withdraw : (req: WithdrawRequest) -> Money | InsufficientBalance
let withdraw (req) = {
    guard req.amount <= req.balance else InsufficientBalance
    req.balance - req.amount // discharged: the guard proves balance - amount >= 0
}
----

The checked fragment is bounded: the decision procedure derives over intervals and differences (`+value >= c+`, `+a <= b+`, and the like) with no external solver. A linear relation of neither shape — a sum of two lengths against a bound — is a premise, used to prove a clause where what is left once the premise is taken off is something the intervals and differences bound. It is also read one position at a time, with the rest of it bounded by that same question — the intervals, the differences, or one other relation of neither shape whose own residual the intervals and differences bound — so that a rule of that shape narrows each position it names rather than only answering questions put to it. Asking how far a sum reaches takes at most one such premise, which is what says none of them is added to another. What is derived is available to the next round, so longer chains come about through the ranges a round hands the next and not inside one question. So a guard *restating* an invariant discharges it, and so does a guard over a computed value the fragment relates to what the clause names: `+guard x - y <= req.amount.value+` discharges a clause about `+req.balance.value+` where `+WithdrawRequest+` declares `+amount <= balance+`, whatever `+x - y+` was computed from. The set of *flagged* constructions is tied to that fragment, so every flagged construction has a guard the procedure can verify — an invariant it cannot express (a non-linear one, or one over a value it cannot name) is left to the run-time check rather than reported as a possible violation no guard could silence.

An *attempted* construction (<<attempted-construction>>) is never possibly violated: a failing invariant takes its `+else+` branch, so there is no abort to warn about. It is what silences the warning for an invariant outside the fragment, where no guard could. A violation the procedure *decides* is still an error there, because with the outcome settled at compile time there was no branch. In the success branch the built value seeds the check with its own invariant, as an input of that type does.

[#invariant-discharge-terms]
==== What the procedure can name

A term it compares is the numeric content of a location — a parameter, a field chain, a newtype's wrapped value — or the size of a container or a string: `+List.length+`, `+String.length+`, `+Set.size+`, `+Map.size+` applied to such a location. A value the body reaches nowhere else is a location too, and carries what its type guarantees exactly as a parameter does: what a `+match+` arm binds, and what a standard-library operation hands its closure — an element, and a fold's accumulator. A size is named by the location it is taken of, so a guard and an invariant clause mean the same term exactly when they take the size of the same one. The size of a container is never negative, and the procedure knows that much without being told.

[,souther]
----
data Lines = List<Line>
    invariant List.length(value) >= 1

behavior build : (rows: List<Line>) -> Lines | NoRows
let build (rows) = {
    guard List.length(rows) >= 1 else NoRows
    Lines(rows) // discharged: the guard sizes the same list
}
----

A size taken of a container built by an operation is read through that operation, by the rules in <<invariant-discharge-preservation>>. A size taken of anything else — of what a behavior answered — is not a term, and a clause needing it is left to the run-time check.

Beyond those, a value is named by what computes it wherever that computation is a function of named parts. `+List.sum(xs)+` is a term, and so is `+a * b+`, which is not a form the fragment carries and is bounded by what its factors are bounded to (<<invariant-discharge-arithmetic>>), and so is a value chosen by an `+if+` or opened by a `+match+`, which is bounded by what its arms are bounded to (<<invariant-discharge-arithmetic>>), and one built by a construction. Naming a value says that two writings of it are one value, and says nothing else: nothing follows from `+List.sum+` being a sum, and nothing has to. A `+guard List.sum(xs) >= bill+` states a relation about that value, `+List.sum(xs) - bill+` is arithmetic over it, and the two meet because both mentions of the call are the same value. A term exists before anything is said about it, so the first guard to mention one states what it states, exactly as a later one does.

What is named and what is known of it are two questions, and the second is the one that has rules. A named value the procedure has no rule about is still a value a guard can be written about, and a construction from one is reported as unproven rather than left unsaid — the author is told that the invariant was read here and not established, which is a different thing from its not having been read. Giving the procedure a rule about an operation moves such a construction from unproven to discharged without anything being named differently.

[,souther]
----
data NonNeg = Int
    invariant value >= 0
data Fine

behavior settle : (xs: List<Int>, bill: Int) -> NonNeg | Fine
    constructs NonNeg
let settle (xs, bill) = {
    guard List.sum(xs) >= bill else Fine
    NonNeg(List.sum(xs) - bill) // discharged: the guard is about this value
}
----

A value the procedure cannot name all the way down is not a term. What names a call is what the call was resolved to: a `+let+` of this module or of another is pure and total (<<fn-rules>>), and the language's own operations are what the language defines, so a call to either answers one value wherever it is written with the same arguments. What a `+behavior+` answered is named by nothing, and neither is what applying a function value answered — a function a body was handed may be a behavior injected from outside (<<injected-behavior>>), whose implementation is not the language's and may answer differently each time it is asked. So neither is a size taken of one, nor arithmetic over one.

That a helper's body was written into the body that called it, rather than left as the call, is not one of these questions. A `+let+` that recurses cannot be written in, and answers the same value it would have answered written in; a clause about a construction from either is read the same way.

A value the procedure does not carry as a number is a term all the same where what computes it is one — a string a `+let+` answered is the term that call is. What it has none of is anything said about it: the procedure carries no strings, and there is no rule about the operation, so a clause is read against such a value only where a guard on the path has stated something about that very term. Until one has, a construction from it is left to the run-time check, and the silence is that nothing could be asked rather than that the value has no name.

[,souther]
----
data Q = Int
    invariant value >= 0
data Held =
    { q: Q
    }
data M = A | B
data A =
    { q: Q
    }

behavior viaMatch : (x: Held, m: M) -> Q
let viaMatch (x, m) =
    match m with
        | A as a -> x.q + a.q // discharged: `a` carries A's invariant, as `x` does
        | B -> x.q

behavior total : (xs: List<Held>) -> Q
let total (xs) = List.fold((acc, i) -> acc + i.q, Q(0), xs) // discharged: both are Q
----

A binding given a location *is* that location, so `+let c = cart+` makes `+c.quantity+` the term `+cart.quantity+`. A helper is expanded by binding each argument and reading the parameter through that binding (<<invariant-discharge-representation>>), which is why a construction moved into a helper still reads the terms the caller's guards and input types settled. A binding given anything else is the term that expression is, so a name and the expression it was given are read alike: a guard about either states the same thing about the same value, and a construction from either owes the same clauses.

[,souther]
----
data Qty = Int
    invariant value >= 1
data Cart =
    { quantity: Int
    }
    invariant quantity >= 1

let one (c: Cart): Qty = Qty(c.quantity) // `c` is `cart`, so this is discharged
behavior toQty : (cart: Cart) -> Qty
    constructs Qty
let toQty (cart) = one(cart)
----

[#invariant-discharge-representation]
==== What the check reads

The check reads a behavior's body and the invariants around it at the level the rules above are written at: the standard library's operations are operations, and a `+let+` a body or an invariant names is read as its body where its body can be written in (<<blocks>>). The language defines what `+List.map+` does to a length, and every module has that definition, so a call to it says something on its own. A `+let+` carries no such definition, so what there is to know about it is what its body does — the body of one another module published as readily as of one written here.

Reading a `+let+` as its body is how more is known, and not how it is named. A `+let+` that recurses is read as the call it is, and the value it answers is named all the same (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>): what is lost is the arithmetic inside it, so a clause is discharged there by a guard about the call rather than by the body's own steps. Nothing about a construction's verdict turns on which of the two a reading did.

A case split standing where a value is handed is read as the arms it answers one of: the body is read once with each arm standing there, under what choosing that arm settles, and what those readings find about one construction is said once. An `+if+` and a `+match+` are read the same way, so what may be discharged does not turn on which of the two an author wrote, and what an arm binds carries what its type guarantees exactly as it does in tail position (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>). A helper whose body is a `+match+` is this rule and not another one: reading the `+let+` as its body puts the `+match+` where the call stood.

How far the splits down one path are opened is bounded by how far they compound. Past that bound the reading is not copied into a context per arm, and what can be read without that refinement is read as it stands: a value under an unopened split is still the value its arms answer one of, and a range holding all of them holds it. Opening a split reads what stands under it once per arm, so nested splits multiply the readings below them, and it is that product the bound is over. It is not a number of splits, because an `+if+` has two arms and a `+match+` has one per case. What is asked of a split is what opening it would bring the path to, so the first one that has more than one arm is opened however wide it is: a sum's cases are the model's, and a rule that read fewer of them the more cases the model named would be a rule about the declaration rather than about the expression. A split with one arm multiplies nothing and is opened wherever it stands — an or-pattern naming every case of a sum is such a split, and where a value is written under one the reading is as precise there as anywhere.

[[provenance-does-not-decide-the-fragment]]Where a declaration was written does not decide what can be discharged against it. A type declared in this module, one imported from a module compiled in the same run, and one imported from a module compiled separately and reached through its published classes all state the same invariant, and a construction of any of them is read against the same clauses and comes to the same verdict (<<published-modules>>). How a published declaration travels is the compiler's business; that it says what its author wrote is the language's.

An operation the rules name is named as the call that survives to here, which is not every spelling an author can write: `+List.fold+` is the walk `+List.foldFrom+` from the head (<<stdlib-list>>) and is rewritten to it before any of this, so it is the second name the rules are written against. A rule under a name nothing here holds could not be reached, which is why the compiler's own tests hold each rule to a program that fires it.

[#invariant-discharge-preservation]
==== What an operation keeps of what it was built from

An invariant is stated of a value, and a value is often built from another by a standard-library operation. Whether the clause carries across that operation is a property of the operation, and is stated here rather than left to the checker: an author predicts which constructions are judged safe by reading this table.

Four shapes cover the operations.

[cols="1,3"]
|===
| shape | what the result is

| *permutes* | the same elements in another order — `+List.reverse+`, `+List.sort+`, `+List.sortBy+`
| *subset* | some of the same elements — `+List.filter+`, `+List.distinct+`, `+List.distinctBy+`, `+List.take+`, `+List.drop+`, `+Set.filter+`, `+Set.remove+`, `+Set.intersection+`, `+Set.difference+`, `+Map.filterEntries+`, `+Map.remove+`, `+Map.intersection+`, `+Map.difference+`
| *maps* | one new element for each — `+List.map+`, `+List.mapIndexed+`, `+Map.mapValues+`, `+Map.updateIfPresent+`
| *collapses* | at most one new element for each — `+List.filterMap+`, `+Set.map+`
|===

What each keeps:

[cols="1,1,1,1"]
|===
| shape | size | a property of every element | a particular element

| *permutes* | the same | kept | kept
| *subset* | no greater | kept | not kept
| *maps* | the same | not kept | not kept
| *collapses* | no greater | not kept | not kept
|===

So `+List.length(List.map(f, xs))+` *is* `+List.length(xs)+` — how the elements are made has no bearing on how many there are — and a guard on the length of `+xs+` discharges a construction from the mapped list. `+List.length(List.filter(p, xs))+` is its own term, no greater than `+List.length(xs)+`: an upper bound on the input bounds the result, and a lower bound on the input says nothing.

[,souther]
----
data Lines = List<Line>
    invariant List.length(value) >= 1

behavior build : (rows: List<Row>) -> Lines | NoRows
let build (rows) = {
    guard List.length(rows) >= 1 else NoRows
    Lines(List.map(toLine, rows)) // discharged: a mapping keeps the length
}
----

"A property of every element" is a clause like `+List.all(p, value)+` or `+List.allDistinctBy(f, value)+`: nothing new is in a sublist, so what held of every element still holds. "A particular element" is `+List.contains(x, value)+` or `+Set.contains(x, value)+`, which a selection may drop.

An operation not in the table keeps nothing that can be relied on, and a clause over what it built is left to the run-time check.

===== A construction whose result is no smaller than what it read

An operation given two containers, or given a container and something to put in it, establishes no element relation to a single source that any of the four shapes states. Appending and union read two sources, so neither of them alone is what the result was built from; an insert puts in an element or an entry the container it read did not hold. A property of every element does not survive either way. How many there are is bounded from below all the same, and that bound is a fact of its own.

[cols="2,3"]
|===
| what holds of the result | the operations

| no smaller than either container it was given | `+List.append+`, `+Set.union+`, `+Map.union+`
| no smaller than the container it was given | `+Set.insert+`, `+Map.insert+`
|===

`+List.append+` is written `+a ++ b+`, and a `+String+` is joined with the same operator and read the same way: `+String.length(a ++ b)+` is no less than `+String.length(a)+` and no less than `+String.length(b)+`.

So a lower bound on any source named above is a lower bound on the result, and a non-empty list appended to any list is non-empty.

[,souther]
----
data Lines = List<Line>
    invariant List.length(value) >= 1

behavior joined : (a: Lines, b: List<Line>) -> Lines
let joined (a, b) = Lines(a.value ++ b) // discharged: no shorter than `a`
----

Nothing is said the other way. A union answers one of what both sides hold, and an insert of something already there adds none, so the result is not the sum of what was read; an upper bound on the sources does not bound it.

[#invariant-discharge-projection]
===== A projection the closure copied

A mapping keeps no property of its elements in general, but a clause stated over a *projection* is a clause about one field, and a closure that copies that field across keeps it. Two mapped elements differ at a copied field exactly when the two they came from did, so uniqueness by that field carries.

[,text]
----
data QuoteLines = List<QuoteLine> invariant List.allDistinctBy(.product, value)

let toLine (r: LineRequest): QuoteLine =
    QuoteLine { product = r.product, quantity = Quantity(r.quantity), ... }

behavior buildQuote : (lines: List<LineRequest>, ...) -> Quote | DuplicateProduct | NoLines
let buildQuote (lines, ...) = {
    guard List.length(lines) >= 1
        else NoLines
    guard List.allDistinctBy(.product, lines)
        else DuplicateProduct
    Quote { lines = QuoteLines(List.map(toLine, lines)), ... }   // both clauses discharged
}
----

The field the closure copied need not be the one the clause names: when the closure writes `+code = r.sku+`, uniqueness of the result by `+.code+` is uniqueness of the input by `+.sku+`, and that is the guard to write.

This is bounded to a field *copied*. A field the closure computes from others — `+code = String.concat([r.sku, r.name])+` — is not this: two rows that differ can land on one value, and nothing about the result follows.

[#invariant-discharge-guarantees]
==== What an operation guarantees of what it answers

<<invariant-discharge-preservation>> says what carries across an operation from what it was built from. An operation also says something on its own: `+Int.clamp(0, 100, x)+` is between 0 and 100 whatever `+x+` is, and a construction standing over it owes nothing a guard would have to restate. What each operation guarantees is stated here, in the same way and for the same reason: an author predicts which constructions are judged safe by reading it.

A guarantee takes one of four forms.

*A bound on the result.* It holds wherever the call is written, and is read into the clause as it stands.

[cols="2,3"]
|===
| the operation | what holds of its result

| `+Int.abs+`, `+Decimal.abs+` | not negative
| `+Int.floorMod(x, k)+` | not negative, and below `+k+` — both only where `+k+` reads as a constant above zero
| `+Decimal.toInt(mode, d)+` | within one of `+d+`, whichever mode it is handed
|===

Neither end is known without the divisor: `+Int.floorMod+` takes the sign of its divisor, so a divisor that could be negative puts the result the other side of zero — `+Int.floorMod(1, 0 - 3)+` is `+-2+` — and one the check cannot read says nothing about either end. A name given a constant is that constant, so `+let k = 100+` and a written `+100+` are one divisor. A divisor that is above zero for some other reason — a parameter whose type says so — is not read here: what an operation guarantees holds wherever it is called, and that one holds where the caller happens to have such a value.

*One of the values it was given.* What such an operation answers depends on what it was given, so it is stated as the cases its definition is written in, and a clause holds of the result when it holds in every case.

[cols="2,3"]
|===
| the operation | the cases

| `+Int.min(a, b)+`, `+Decimal.min+` | `+a+` where `+a < b+`; `+b+` otherwise
| `+Int.max(a, b)+`, `+Decimal.max+` | `+a+` where `+a > b+`; `+b+` otherwise
| `+Int.clamp(lo, hi, n)+`, `+Decimal.clamp+` | `+lo+` where `+n < lo+`; `+hi+` where `+n >= lo+` and `+n > hi+`; `+n+` otherwise
|===

So a smaller of two non-negative numbers is non-negative, and a value clamped to `+0+` and `+100+` is a percentage. `+clamp+` does not ask that `+lo+` be below `+hi+`: written the other way round it answers `+lo+` for a low `+n+` and `+hi+` for a high one, and a clause that only the ordering would establish is not discharged.

[,souther]
----
data Pct = Int
    invariant value >= 0 && value <= 100

behavior score : (raw: Int) -> Pct
let score (raw) = Pct(Int.clamp(0, 100, raw)) // discharged: every case is inside both ends
----

A case is reached where its condition, the equality saying the call answered that argument, and what the path already establishes can hold at once. What the path knows can put a case out of reach: a value the guards put above four and below six is neither below zero nor above ten, so `+Int.clamp(0, 10, n)+` answers `+n+` and the clause is read there and nowhere else.

A clause reads one such operation. Where it names two — `+Int.min(a, b) + Int.min(c, d) >= 0+` — the cases are not multiplied out, and the clause is left to the run-time check however much is known of the four arguments.

Over the cases that are reached, the clause is established where every one of them establishes it and refused where every one of them refuses it — `+AboveTen(Int.min(0, x))+` is neither of two numbers above ten, and is reported as a violation the values decide. Where no case is reached at all the guards cannot all hold, and this says nothing: a value the program never builds establishes nothing, and what a guard said about the call itself is what answers there.

*A shift stated through a measure.* An operation that moves a value by an amount says how far through the operation that counts two such values apart, which is what an invariant over a pair of them is written in.

[cols="2,3"]
|===
| the operation | what holds

| `+Date.addDays(n, d)+` | `+Date.daysBetween(d, result)+` is `+n+`
| `+DateTime.addMinutes(n, dt)+` | `+DateTime.minutesBetween(dt, result)+` is `+n+`
| `+DateTime.addHours(n, dt)+` | `+DateTime.minutesBetween(dt, result)+` is `+60 * n+`
| `+DateTime.addDays(n, dt)+` | `+DateTime.minutesBetween(dt, result)+` is `+1440 * n+`
|===

`+Date.addMonths+` and `+Date.addYears+` state nothing: months and years hold different numbers of days, so neither moves a date by a count `+Date.daysBetween+` can name.

*A value it was already given.* `+Decimal.fromInt(n)+` answers the number `+n+` is, so a clause about the call and a guard about `+n+` are about one value rather than two.

An operation not named here guarantees nothing that can be relied on, and a clause over what it answered is left to the run-time check. What is known of the arguments at one call is a different question: it is what the path establishes, and it is read where the clause is.

[#invariant-discharge-arithmetic]
==== What a value outside the fragment lies between

A product of two values, a truncating division and a division rounded to a scale are not linear, so none of them is a form the procedure derives in and each names one value the guards may say nothing about (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>). A value chosen by an `+if+` or opened by a `+match+` is one for a reason of its own: nothing composes a choice, and it too is one value. What is known of such a value is read off what it was computed from, where the construction stands: a quantity times a unit price is not negative wherever both of them are, and there is no third `+guard+` about the product that an author could write instead.

This is not a rule about `+*+` or `+/+`, in the way <<invariant-discharge-guarantees>> states rules about operations. Nothing holds of a product wherever it is written. What holds is decided by what the path establishes about the two factors at the point the clause is read, and a construction under guards that say nothing about them is reported exactly as before.

*A product.* It lies between the least and the greatest of the four products of its factors' ends. A factor with an end nothing bounds leaves the product unbounded that way, except against a factor that is only ever zero, since every value times zero is zero. Both `+Int+` and `+Decimal+` are read: multiplication of either is exact, and an `+Int+` that overflows aborts rather than wrapping (<<primitives>>).

Where an end of the product is at zero, whether the product *reaches* it is a separate question from where the end is. A product is constant along the axis where a factor is zero, so zero is a value it takes wherever either factor holds zero at all — which a factor may do between its ends, or at an end the other factor's ends do not pair with. So `+a * b+` for an `+a+` in `+[0, 1]+` and a `+b+` above zero without reaching it is at or above zero *and reaches it*, at `+a = 0+`, whatever `+b+` is.

[,souther]
----
data NonNeg = Int
    invariant value >= 0

behavior total : (a: Int, b: Int) -> NonNeg | Bad
let total (a, b) = {
    guard a >= 0 else Bad
    guard b >= 0 else Bad
    NonNeg(a * b) // discharged: both factors are at or above zero, so the product is
}
----

*A truncating division.* Division of whole numbers truncates toward zero (<<stdlib-int>>). Which spelling it was written with does not enter into what is known of it: `+/+` aborts on a zero divisor and `+Int.divide+` answers one as a case, and where both answer they answer the same number, so the `+Int+` the second's value arm binds *is* the quotient the first computes and the two are one value (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>). What decides the rule is that the value is a truncating quotient of two operands, which both spellings have and neither owns; an author who writes the function because the divisor is not a literal they can argue about is told exactly what an author who writes the operator is told.

Where what is established at the construction holds every divisor it admits away from zero, the quotient lies within the hull of the quotients of the two ranges' ends — the four of them, as a product's are. A divisor held off zero is of one sign throughout, and over such a range the divide moves one way along each operand with the other held, in a direction that may turn with that other's sign; so what the rule is about is the corners and not an order the ends are taken in. Truncation toward zero never sends a larger value to a smaller one, so the corners put through it are still where the answer stops. A divisor the reading reads as a constant is this with both ends at one number, and a name given a constant is that constant (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>).

What is bounded is the values the operation produced. `+/+` does not answer everywhere — a zero divisor aborts, and so does the one pair whose quotient no `+Int+` holds (<<stdlib-int>>) — and a pair it aborts on leaves no value to bound. So what is stated here says nothing about whether every pair the construction admits is one the operator answers. Whether a divide can abort at all is a separate question with a separate answer (<<an-evaluation-that-answers-nothing>>), and where a model wants the abort ruled out it is the divisor's own type or a `+require+` that rules it out (<<primitive-arm>>).

What is derived is held to what the answer's own kind of number holds. The arithmetic a form is read through composes numbers of any size, so what a rule works out to can be a number the value never is: the quotient of the smallest `+Int+` by minus one is one past the whole-number range, and the operation that would have answered it aborts instead. An end past where the values stop is pulled back to where they stop. An end the arithmetic put nowhere stays nowhere: this corrects what a rule derived and states no range of its own.

Where the correction leaves no value at all, the rule states nothing — not an empty range. What the arithmetic worked out to is values the answer cannot take, so the operation answers on none of the inputs the reading admits. Stating that as a range with nothing in it would be a reading that proves every clause there is, so it is not stated as a range; it is stated as what it is, which is the operation answering nothing (<<an-evaluation-that-answers-nothing>>).

[[an-evaluation-that-answers-nothing]]
*An evaluation that answers nothing.* An operation whose arithmetic produces no value for any operands the reading admits, and which cannot come back as a case either, is one no run carries a value on from: it aborts wherever it is reached. What is written after such an evaluation is written after nothing, and it is not judged. So a construction under the `+Int+` arm of a `+match+` on `+Int.divide(a, -1)+` where `+a+` is the smallest `+Int+` is owed no clause and fails none, whether it is built from the quotient the arm binds or from anything else — the arm is entered by no run, and which value the author wrote there does not decide it.

Three questions and not one. Whether the arithmetic produced a number is what the rules above answer. Whether the operation answered *at all* takes its other cases in as well: `+Int.divide+` comes back as `+DivisionByZero+` where the divisor is zero, and an arm is reached there, so an operation answering no number is one that answers nothing only where no other case can come back. Whether a place is reached is about what comes after the evaluation, and it is not a verdict about a clause — a construction refused by the values and a construction nothing reaches are different findings and an author is owed the first and not the second.

A divide by nought is this and is not the same for both spellings. `+/+` aborts on a zero divisor and answers nothing, so nothing after it is reached; `+Int.divide+` comes back as `+DivisionByZero+`, which is a case an arm is reached at, so the run carries on. The divisor is the same and the answer differs, because what settles it is the operation's own cases. A scale outside the ones a `+Decimal+` division is performed at is the same shape again (<<stdlib-decimal>>).

Not known to abort is not unreachable. Where any operand pair the reading admits is one the operation answers, nothing here applies and what is asked of the construction is what it always was. The same holds of an operation this reading states no arithmetic for, and of operands it bounds too loosely to decide — a divisor a reading leaves holding nought among other values settles nothing, where one it holds to nought settles it.

What runs is what the operators say runs. `+&&+` and `+||+` stop as soon as the answer is settled (<<a-condition-stops-when-its-answer-is-settled>>), so the right operand is evaluated on the runs the left did not answer for and on no others: an operation there that answers nothing stops only those runs, and a construction there is built only on those runs. It is read exactly as the branch of an `+if+` written with the same condition is, and which of the two an author wrote does not decide anything.

A fork leaves what its ways leave. Where an operator, a branch or an arm has no way out that a run takes — every one of them answering nothing — what is written after it is written after nothing. Where it has exactly one, what that way established holds after it, because every run that got there came that way: `+x >= 0 && <something that answers nothing>+` leaves only the runs where `+x+` is below nought, and a construction after it is read knowing that. Where more than one way is left, what held before the fork is what still holds.

A closure a library operation applies is read where it runs, which is inside the operation and after every argument has answered. `+List.map+` takes the function before the container, and the function is *made* there rather than run — so a container that answers nothing means the operation is never entered and the closure body never runs, and what the other arguments answered holds where the body is read.

What runs before the abort still runs. A construction among the values an aborting evaluation is waiting on — a field declared before it, an argument written beside it — is one the program really builds, and it is judged. An evaluation nothing leaves is not a place nothing arrives at.

Nothing follows from this rule unless what is established at the construction keeps zero out of every divisor it admits. That is the condition for stating anything and not a claim about the divides that do answer — over the whole numbers a divisor between zero and five divides by one at the nearest, and where those quotients lie is simply not what this rule says. Nothing follows either where the divisor is left no value an `+Int+` is — the arithmetic a form is read through composes numbers of any size, and what the operator divides by is an `+Int+`, so `+x / (9223372036854775807 + 1)+` divides by a value this operator never has. Neither is a finding about the path. The construction is owed its clause, as it is owed over any other value nothing is known of.

*What a truncating division leaves.* A quotient bounded on its own says nothing about the value it was taken of, and what is left of that value — the thing every step of a change-making loop is built on — is where the two have to be related. Writing `+r+` for `+a - b * (a / b)+`, what holds is

----
-|b| < r < |b|
a >= 0  =>  0 <= r
a <= 0  =>  r <= 0
----

so for a dividend at or above nought and a divisor above it, `+0 <= r < b+`. Both halves are wanted. The sign is what a construction over the remainder owes, and the magnitude is what says the next denomination's quotient is bounded.

The divisor's own sign is not in either of them, which is why they are written this way. Truncation toward zero leaves what is divided on its own side of nought whichever side the divisor is on: `+7 / -10+` is nought and `+7 - -10 * 0+` is seven, so a rule stated as `+0 <= r < b+` in general would be a rule the values fail wherever the divisor is negative. What the divisor decides is how far from nought the remainder can get, and that is its magnitude.

Neither half is unconditional, and the two hold under different conditions. The sign is the dividend's: truncation toward zero leaves the remainder on the dividend's side of nought — `+-7 / 2+` is `+-3+` and `+-7 - 2 * -3+` is `+-1+` — so a rule stated without the sign facts would be wrong for every negative dividend. Which side the dividend is on is what the path establishes, and it is read where the clause is read, as a product's bound is; a reading that puts it on neither side gets neither half. The magnitude is below the divisor's, and that half is stated where what is established at the construction leaves the divisor a furthest distance from nought. What is left is smaller than the divisor it was divided by, and the divisor may be any value the path admits — so what a rule stated over ends can use is the further of them, which for a range open either way is no number at all. It is read off what the path holds the divisor to and not off how the divisor was written: a name given a constant is that constant, and so is a value a guard pins (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>).

`+Int.truncatingRemainder+` answers that value directly, and its value arm binds it. What holds of it is the same two halves under the same conditions. It is stated of that value rather than of `+a - a / b * b+` rewritten: the identity above relates the two answers of one division and is not a way of computing either, and reading it as one would make what a remainder is depend on `+/+` answering at all — which for one pair of `+Int+`s it does not (<<stdlib-int>>).

[,souther]
----
data 硬貨枚数 = Int
    invariant value >= 0

behavior 買う : (額: Int) -> 硬貨枚数
    constructs 硬貨枚数
let 買う (額) = {
    guard 額 >= 0 else 硬貨枚数(0)
    let k0 = 額 / 100
    let r0 = 額 - k0 * 100 // at or above nought, and below a hundred
    let k1 = r0 / 10 // so between nought and nine
    硬貨枚数(k0 + k1) // discharged
}
----

*A quotient rounded to a scale.* `+Decimal.divide(a, b, scale, mode)+` rounds at a scale the domain chose, and its value arm binds what it answered. Under the same condition on the divisor, that answer lies between the two points of the scale's grid the exact quotient lies between. The mode is not read: every mode the library declares picks one of those two points, so the pair holds all seven of them, and a rule per mode would be seven statements of one fact. The scale is read as a number and the rule is stated only where it is one — a scale the procedure cannot read leaves no grid to state — and only where that number is one the run time receives as the number it is (<<a-scale-is-used-as-the-number-written>>) and one the procedure will lay a grid out for. The first of those is not a bound this procedure chose: a scale outside it produces no quotient at all, so there is nothing for a range to be about, exactly as there is nothing where the divisor is zero. That last is a resource bound and not a statement about the arithmetic: a scale of a million places is a number a megabyte wide at every end of the divide, and how far a compilation will go is the compilation's to set.

Where the scale is not read, which side of nought the answer is on still is. Rounding to a grid never crosses zero, so a quotient at or above nought lands at or above nought at every scale.

`+/+` over `+Decimal+` is *not* read, and is not a coarser statement of the rule above. It rounds half away from zero at a significant-digit precision the run time sets (<<stdlib-decimal>>) rather than at a scale the domain wrote down, so there is no grid for an end to land on — how many places the answer keeps depends on how large it is.
