That is different arithmetic and not the same operation read less sharply. A clause over one is left to the run-time check.

*A choice.* A value that is one of several — chosen by an `+if+`, opened by a `+match+`, or answered by an attempted construction depending on whether its invariant held (<<attempted-construction>>) — is one of its arms, so it lies within the range holding every one of them: the lower of their lower ends and the higher of their higher ends, read where the construction stands. An arm the reading says nothing about leaves the whole choice unbounded that way, since the value may be that arm.

An arm is read under what choosing it settles. What settles it holds exactly where that arm is the answer, so an arm read that way says more than the arm alone: `+if n < 100 then n else 100+` is below a hundred by its condition and not by its arms, and a `+match+` arm's body reading what the arm bound reads a value only that arm has. What a condition states is read this way, and an arm whose statements cannot all hold is an arm the choice never answers, so it is left out of the join rather than widening it. What a case refines the scrutinee to, and what an attempt's construction guarantees, are not read yet: where they are what the arm rests on, the join stands as it is, which is sound — it is a range the value cannot leave — and wider than the range the value actually has, so what is recognised there is narrower than what a model can write. Where a case split stands in a value position the walk reads it a different way, once per arm and under what choosing the arm settles, and this rule is what answers where that reading stops: inside a closure a container operation applies, and past the bound on how far the splits down one path are opened. A reading that opened the split is not weakened by this one standing beside it — both are sound, and a clause either of them discharges is discharged.

[,souther]
----
data Money = Int
    invariant value >= 0

behavior pick : (a: Int, b: Int) -> Money | Bad
let pick (a, b) = {
    guard a >= 0 else Bad
    guard b >= 0 else Bad
    Money(if a > b then a else b) // discharged: both arms are at or above zero
}
----

A bound derived this way is not a fact about the value that is kept and read back afterwards. A construction is read twice — under what the guards established, and under what holds of the values whatever the guards did (<<invariant-discharge>>) — and each reading derives its own from what that reading holds. So a bound that rests on a guard is in the first and not the second, and one that follows from what the values' own types guarantee is in both: a product of two values a type puts at or above zero is at or above zero wherever it is built, and a construction the two refuse is refused wherever it is built rather than on a path an author is told to guard. And it refuses as readily as it discharges — a product the guards put on the other side of zero is a construction the value fails, which is an error (<<construction-satisfies-its-invariant>>) and not a warning.

What is derived is derived once, from what is known where the clause is read. Nothing derived here is read back to derive something further about the same value: a bound that tightened under its own answers would be a procedure that decides when to stop, which this is not. A value computed from another such value is read through — `+a * b / 100+` reads what the product was bounded to — because that is the expression's own structure and not a second round over the same one.

[#invariant-discharge-reduction]
==== What a walk from a seed answers

A `+List.fold+` walks a container and hands its step an accumulator and an element, over and over, and answers the accumulator it ends with. What it answers is a value the guards may say nothing about, in the way a product is (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>), and reading it needs neither the length of the container nor the walk written out.

A range holds the answer where two things hold: the seed is inside it, and the step, given an accumulator inside it and what the container guarantees of everything else it hands the step, answers inside it again. Each is asked once. Nothing here iterates towards an answer, nothing widens, and nothing depends on how many elements there are or on the order they arrive in — so the same rule reads a walk over a `+Set+` and over a `+Map+`.

What that establishes is conditional, and the condition is not this procedure's to discharge: *if the walk answers at all, its answer is in the range*. A step whose arithmetic aborts part way — an addition past what an `+Int+` holds (<<primitives>>) — answers nothing, and a range saying where an answer lies says nothing about a run that produces none. Whether the arithmetic can abort and whether the walk terminates are other questions, and neither is weakened by this and neither is assumed by it.

[,souther]
----
data Money = Int
    invariant value >= 0

data Line =
    { amount: NonNeg
    }

behavior total : (xs: List<Line>) -> Money
let total (xs) = Money(List.fold((sum, x) -> sum + x.amount.value, 0, xs))
// discharged: the seed is at zero, and an accumulator at or above zero
// plus an amount at or above zero is at or above zero again
----

The ranges tried are made from the seed and there are four of them: the seed's own range, the range open above it, the range open below it, and the seed's range together with what the step answers where nothing is assumed about the accumulator — which is the whole answer for a step that does not read the accumulator at all. Where more than one is established the answer is what they all say. Which ranges are tried is not a rule an author has to predict: one that cannot be established costs nothing and settles nothing, and a range established is established whichever others were tried beside it.

The seed and what the step answers must both be values the procedure names. A step over a value that is what a behavior answered is not read. A step whose answer is one the fragment cannot carry — a product of the accumulator and the element, or a choice between two answers — is read, and it is read *under* the range being checked: such a value is one nothing is known of until what it was computed from is, and what it was computed from is the accumulator the range is about. So a step that chooses is read by reading its arms: every arm handed an accumulator inside the range answers inside it, or the range is not established. An arm is read as the arms of any choice are (<<invariant-discharge-arithmetic>>), so a step whose arm stays inside the range only by what chose it gets no range rather than a wrong one.

[,souther]
----
data Positive = Int
    invariant value >= 1

behavior product : (xs: List<Positive>) -> AtLeastOne
let product (xs) = AtLeastOne(List.fold((acc, x) -> acc * x.value, 1, xs))
// discharged: one is at or above one, and something at or above one times
// something at or above one is at or above one
----

[,souther]
----
behavior total : (xs: List<Line>) -> Money
let total (xs) =
    // discharged: one arm is the accumulator and the other adds an amount at
    // or above zero, so every arm leaves the range where it was handed it
    Money(List.fold((sum, x) -> if x.amount.value > 0 then sum + x.amount.value else sum, 0, xs))

behavior other : (xs: List<Line>) -> Money
let other (xs) =
    // reported: the second arm is below the seed, and what chose it is not read
    Money(List.fold((sum, x) -> if x.amount.value > 0 then sum else 0 - 1, 0, xs))
----

What the container guarantees of what it hands the step is what a value of that type guarantees, and what a container written out holds. It is the same reading that seeds a parameter (<<invariant-discharge>>), so a rule written on a record bounds the field a step reads exactly as a rule written on a newtype the field wears does, and it is read of every parameter the step is handed but the accumulator — a `+Map.fold+` is read over its key as well as its value. A `+[1, 2]+` written on the line holds those two numbers and nothing else, so every element of it is between them.

A relation a guard states about *some* element is not this: it says nothing about the element the walk is at, and a walk is not established by it. Nor is a relation between two places under one parameter — `+end >= start+` written on the element's type is not carried, and a step that needs it gets no range rather than a wrong one. Nor is a container written out with nothing in it: what that would establish is that the step never runs, which is a fact about how many elements there are and not about what any of them is.

[,souther]
----
behavior total : () -> Money
let total = Money(List.fold((sum, x) -> sum + x, 0, [1, 2]))
// discharged: every element written there is above zero

behavior other : () -> Money
let other = Money(List.fold((sum, x) -> sum + x, 0, [1, 0 - 2]))
// reported: an element written there is below zero, so the answer may be
----

The operations read this way are `+List.fold+` and the `+List.foldFrom+` it is sugar for, `+List.foldRight+`, `+Set.fold+` and `+Map.fold+`. That an operation walks a container from a seed is not something its declaration says — an operation of the same shape could apply its closure once — so it is stated rather than read off, and every operation the library declares with that shape either is stated to be one or is written down as not being one. Where the seed and the accumulator are is read off the declaration: the seed is the argument of the type the operation answers, and the accumulator is the closure parameter of that type, which is why `+List.foldRight+` — whose step takes the element first — needs nothing written about it.

A bound established this way belongs to the reading that established it, as a derived bound does (<<invariant-discharge-arithmetic>>): what the step is read against is what the reading holds, and nothing established about the accumulator while the step is read is true anywhere else.

[#invariant-discharge-quantified]
==== A relation over every element, read at the element

`+List.all(p, xs)+` states `+p+` of each element of `+xs+`. Where a standard-library combinator hands its closure an element of `+xs+`, the check reads the relation again there, with the quantifier's own parameter standing for the element the closure was handed.

[,souther]
----
data Qty = Int
    invariant value >= 1
data Line =
    { quantity: Int
    }
data Cart =
    { items: List<Line>
    }
    invariant List.all(i -> i.quantity >= 1, items)

behavior toQty : (cart: Cart) -> List<Qty>
    constructs Qty
let toQty (cart) = List.map(i -> Qty(i.quantity), cart.items) // discharged
----

Both sources of a relation read alike: an input type's invariant clause, and a `+guard+` or `+if+` on the path stating the relation of the same container.

The container the closure walks need not be the one the relation was stated of. Where every element of it is an element of the one that was — a *permutes* or a *subset* construction (<<invariant-discharge-preservation>>) — the relation is read there too, so `+List.map(f, List.filter(q, xs))+` carries what was stated of `+xs+`. A *maps* or *collapses* construction makes elements the relation says nothing about.

A relation the clause in turn states of a container is read at that container's elements as well, so a clause over a container of containers reaches the innermost element.

`+List.any+` is not this: one element having a property says nothing about the rest. Neither is a `+List.all+` the path has *denied*, which says some element fails the predicate without naming which.

[#invariant-discharge-facts]
==== A clause that states no relation is settled, not derived

Not every invariant is a comparison. `+List.allDistinctBy(.product, value)+`, `+List.contains(value, codes)+` and `+String.matches("[A-Z]{2}-[0-9]{4}", value)+` state something about a value that no arithmetic relates to anything else. Such a clause is *settled* by a guard rather than derived: a `+guard+` states its condition where it does not depart and denies it where it does, and a construction under a guard stating the clause is discharged, one under a guard denying it a violation.

[,souther]
----
data Lines = List<Line>
    invariant List.allDistinctBy(.product, value)

behavior build : (rows: List<Line>) -> Lines | DuplicateProduct
let build (rows) = {
    guard List.allDistinctBy(.product, rows) else DuplicateProduct
    Lines(rows) // discharged: the guard states the clause
}
----

Two clauses are one thing exactly when they are the same expression over the same terms. Nothing relates two different ones — uniqueness by `+.product+` says nothing about uniqueness by `+.sku+`, and one pattern says nothing about another. What a binding is *called* is not part of the expression, so `+r -> r.product+`, `+row -> row.product+` and `+.product+` state one thing; a name the closure reads from outside it is part of the expression, so bounding by one field is not bounding by another. Which of the six comparisons an author reached for is not part of it either: `+a /= b+` is `+a == b+` denied, `+a >= b+` is `+a < b+` denied, and which side of an equality a term is written on says nothing — so a `+guard+` on `+a == b+` discharges a clause reading `+b /= a+` where it departs.

This is what a clause outside the derived fragment falls back to, whatever its shape. A comparison over a value the procedure cannot name is settled the same way, which is why `+guard+`-ing it verbatim discharges the construction.

[#invariant-discharge-capability]
==== A clause says how it can be discharged

Some clauses are statically dischargeable and others are not, and the same `+invariant+` keyword is written for both. Left implicit, an author believes there is a static guarantee where there is none. So the compiler answers, per clause, how that clause can be discharged. It is a query rather than a diagnostic — a correct program would carry one on every declaration, which is noise — and the LSP surfaces it on the clause.

[cols="1,3"]
|===
| | what discharges it

| *derivable* | a relation the procedure reasons over. Any guard that *implies* it discharges the construction: `+guard amount >= 10+` discharges `+invariant value >= 0+`.
| *exact match* | a term the procedure can name but not reason about. Only a guard establishing *the same* property discharges it, and nothing weaker: `+invariant List.allDistinctBy(.product, value)+` takes `+guard List.allDistinctBy(.product, lines)+`.
| *always holds* | the clause settles on its own. `+invariant 1 >= 0+` holds of every value, so the obligation is met without a guard.
| *never holds* | it settles the other way, so no guard establishes it and no value of the type can be built. The declaration is refused where the whole compiler runs (<<a-data-can-be-constructed>>); this says what the clause is, so an editor reading half-written source is not told that a guard would discharge it.
| *runtime only* | not representable as a static obligation. No guard discharges it, and the run-time check on construction is the whole of its enforcement.
|===

The first two are the *statically dischargeable* ones, and they are separate because they admit different guards — which is what an author needs to know before writing one. The last carries a reason, because there is more than one way to be outside the fragment and which one it is decides what can be done about it.

A clause that settles on its own is not one outside the fragment. Both leave the construction with no guard to write, and only one of them is something the model says: `+invariant 1 >= 0+` was read to the end and asks nothing, while a clause outside the fragment is one the reading could not carry. They are told apart the way the report tells *not derivable* from *not read* (<<example-report-vocabulary>>), and for the same reason — one is about the model and the other about the compiler.

And a reading that did not finish is *not determined* rather than any of these. Whether a clause is inside the fragment is exactly what such a reading did not find out, so answering it as `+runtime only+` would tell an author that no guard discharges their clause because this compiler fell over on it. Being a fact about the compiler on that run, it names nothing about the clause.

The classification is the clause's own, read with the construction assumed to name what it is given. A construction that names nothing the procedure can name discharges nothing whatever its clauses say (<<invariant-discharge-terms>>); that is a fact about the construction and not about the clause.

The answer carries the clause's name where it has one (<<invariant-clause-name>>), so what the LSP shows on a clause says both how it can be discharged and what an attempt departs by. The unit is the conjunct, as it is for the rest of the check, so a named clause written as `+a && b+` is classified twice under that one name — what discharges each half is what an author acts on, and the name is what a caller reads.

One conjunct has one answer, and the answer has a shape. What is written as one thing need not be read as one thing — a conjunct naming a helper is one thing to its author and is whatever that helper states to the checker — so a reading may find the conjunct to have *several parts*, and *every* part of it has to be established for the conjunct to be. A part may in turn have *several routes*, and *any one* of them establishes that part: a relation the procedure reasons over is often a term it can name as well, and either guard will do.

The two multiplicities are not the same and are not written as one. Read as a set of answers, a conjunct needing a bound in one part and a term in another said that either guard discharges it — so an author writes the one they prefer and finds the construction still refused. A part the checker made nothing of is `+runtime only+` and is a part like the others, since a conjunct is discharged only where every part of it is.

All of them are given at the one place the conjunct is written; the parts have no places of their own, having been written as one.

[#invariant-reify]
==== Reifying a cross-behavior relation

The check does not infer relations across behaviors: an implicit relation would be an implicit cross-unit contract, which the language declines — `+depends on+` and an exposed composition's output are always declared, not inferred (<<depends-on>>). The consistent way to carry a relation that fits in one value is to *reify it as a type invariant*, which the seeding then assumes downstream for free. A relation between a behavior's parameters and its answer, which cannot fit in the answer without changing the model's shape, is declared with `+ensures+` (<<ensures>>).

[,souther]
----
data WithdrawRequest =
    { balance: Money
    , amount: Money
    }
    invariant amount <= balance // the relation lives in the type

behavior withdraw : (req: WithdrawRequest) -> Money
let withdraw (req) = req.balance - req.amount // discharged with no guard here
----

So a possible-violation warning over the fields of one input data is a prompt to declare their relation as that data's invariant.

The prompt is the same where the values are separate parameters, and what it asks for is more. A relation between two parameters is a property of no one input, so there is no existing type to declare it on: writing it means introducing the data that holds both values and taking that as the input, as `+WithdrawRequest+` is here. `+(balance: Money, amount: Money)+` says the behavior accepts every pair of the two, which is what a signature says and is not qualified by anything written elsewhere; a behavior that accepts fewer pairs than that says so by taking a type that admits fewer. There is no clause on a behavior that narrows its own parameters — a relation that can really fail is a business branch (<<attempted-construction>>, <<guard>>), and one that cannot is what a data declares.

When a value's validity is guaranteed *outside* the language — a Java-side or injected contract stronger than any type — there is no type to reify onto, and that construction keeps its run-time check.

[#decoder]
== Decoder

[#decoder-role]
=== Role

A Decoder is the boundary that constructs internal data from external input. It implements the spec DSL's parse-don't-validate (validate raw data at the boundary and construct a domain type only on success). Souther derives a Raoh Decoder from a data's shape. The derived Decoder reads an external representation (<<external-representation>>) and, on success, constructs T; failure is represented by Raoh's `+Result+` (the failure side carrying `+Issues+`).

[,text]
----
MemberId.decoder() : Decoder<MemberId>     // external representation -> Result<MemberId> (failure = Raoh Issues)
----

A decode failure is not a Souther domain type. Malformed input is not a business result but a boundary concern (<<unmarked-sum>>), so it is not listed among domain sum cases but placed on the failure side of Raoh's `+Result+`. Decode is not a behavior but the edge of the boundary and is not a `+>->+` stage (<<sequential-composition>>). Composition in the domain world is done only with behaviors and `+>->+`.

A Decoder does not appear in Souther's syntax. A default Decoder is derived from a data's field names and types. A data definition writes only `+data+` / `+invariant+` / `+behavior+`. This keeps the mapping to external representation (key names, normalization, discriminators) out of domain definitions. When the default derivation is insufficient, a custom Decoder is written on the boundary side (Java / Raoh) (<<custom-codec>>).

[#decoder-derivation]
=== Derivation rules

The default Decoder is derived from a data's shape by the following conventions.

* A JSON key matches the field name. Each field of an Object is read from the key of the same name.
* A newtype (`+data X = Y+`; <<newtype>>) is read directly from `+Y+`'s representation, not an Object (a bare scalar if `+Y+` is primitive, the derived Decoder of `+Y+` if a named data or sum). `+{ ... }+` is read as an Object even with a single field.
* A nested named data is read recursively with that data's derived Decoder.
* `+List<T>+` is read element by element with `+T+`'s derived Decoder.
* `+T?+` (Option; <<optional>>) at a field is `+None+` if the key is absent or the value is `+null+`, and `+Some+`-wrapped after reading `+T+` if a value is present. Where an optional stands with no key of its own — a `+List+`'s or a `+Set+`'s element, a `+Map+`'s value — `+null+` is the whole of what `+None+` is read from (<<absence-is-written-as-null>>).
* Invariants are checked on construction; a violation becomes a failure (`+Issue+`) of Raoh's `+Result+`. Because decode always succeeds or fails, the destination of a violation always exists (<<violation-destination>>).
* Errors of independent fields are accumulated (Applicative; <<case-propagation>>). Accumulation is handled by Raoh combinators.

[,souther]
----
// a newtype reads from a bare integer (the data X = Y form)
data Amount = Int
    invariant value >= 0
// derived Decoder: Int(n) -> Amount { value: n } (with invariant check)

// a multi-field data reads from an Object, field name = key
data Employee =
    { id: EmployeeId
    , rank: Rank
    , managerId: EmployeeId
    }
// derived Decoder: reads { "id": ..., "rank": ..., "managerId": ... } with each field's derived Decoder
----

The invariant check on construction and spread (`+...+`) follow the same semantics as a record literal (<<field-spread>>, <<record-literal>>).

[#sum-discrimination]
=== Discrimination of sum data

The derived Decoder of a sum data reads the discriminator field `+"type"+` and dispatches to the case's derived Decoder. The tag is the case name.

[,souther]
----
data TravelRequest = Drafting | Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval | PreApproved
// derived Decoder: reads "type" and dispatches "Drafting" -> Drafting, "Submitted" -> Submitted, ...
----

A sum every one of whose cases is a unit data (folded to the leaves) is an *enumeration*: it carries nothing but which case it is, so it travels as that case's name — a bare string — and not as an object holding a discriminator alone. This is the form a fieldless enumeration has everywhere else, and it is what lets such a sum key a `+Map+` at the boundary (<<collections>>): a JSON object's key cannot be an object. A name no case answers to fails the decode at that value's path. One field-bearing case is enough to make the sum an object with `+"type"+` again.

[,souther]
----
data Stage = Prospecting | Won | Lost
// derived Decoder: reads "Prospecting" / "Won" / "Lost"
// derived Encoder: Won -> "Won", so a field is { "stage": "Won" } and a key is { "Won": 300 }
----

A nested sum is folded to the leaf case and dispatched.

[,souther]
----
data OwnCost = Reimbursement | Advance | CorporateCard
data CostBearer = OwnCost | ClientCost
// derived Decoder: the name is one of "Reimbursement" / "Advance" / "CorporateCard" / "ClientCost"
// (every case is a unit data, so this one is an enumeration).
// The tag "OwnCost" does not appear in the external representation.
----

Folding is done because only the leaf identifies the value. Tagging a direct case would break the round trip (<<round-trip>>). Once `+CostBearer+`'s encode writes `+"OwnCost"+`, which leaf it was is lost, and the subsequent decode of `+OwnCost+` would reject that name as not one of its own. Folding at the leaf lets the same external representation be read by any layer's decoder. This is the same as case→sum assignment folding to the leaf (<<sum-data>>). Exhaustiveness in `+match+` is decided over the same leaves (<<match>>), so what a codec tags and what a `+match+` must answer for are one set; which names a report says it in is a separate matter again (<<an-arms-alternatives-each-add-what-it-answers-for>>).

A case's contents are decided by the case's shape. A product case lays fields flatly in the same Object as `+"type"+` (`+{"type":"Submitted", "applicant":…, …}+`). A unit case writes only `+"type"+` (`+{"type":"Staff"}+`). A newtype case (`+data X = Y+`; <<newtype>>) cannot place `+"type"+` on a bare scalar, so it puts the contents under the `+"value"+` key.

[#discriminator-field]
[[a-case-does-not-declare-the-discriminator-field]]Because a product case lays its fields there, a case MUST NOT declare a field named `+"type"+` — the field and the tag would want one key, and whichever was written second would be all that was left. This is a compile error where the case is declared, not a value quietly written over where it is encoded. It holds of a member of a behavior's output union for the same reason (<<union-encoder>>), and of a field a case takes in by spread as much as one it writes. A data that is neither a case nor a union member may have the field: nothing writes a discriminator beside it.

[,souther]
----
data Rank = Manager | Staff
data Manager = Level // newtype
data Staff // unit
// derived: Manager -> {"type":"Manager","value":3}, Staff -> {"type":"Staff"}
----

This maps directly onto Raoh's `+discriminate("type", …)+`. The discriminator selects `+"Manager"+` and hands that case's decoder what is under `+"value"+` — the bare `+3+` — while a product case is handed the Object itself and reads its fields there. Each case's decoder reads what the case is on its own, and the sum's decoder takes the contents out of where membership put them. A standalone newtype (not a sum case) stays bare (`+3+`); the `+"value"+` wrapping is what membership in a sum adds, so a newtype's own representation does not change when some sum names it.

Folding to the leaf concerns nested *sums*; a newtype case stops there. A newtype is not a sum, so making `+data InsufficientAuthority = Rank+` (Rank is a sum) a case of `+PreApprovalReason+` yields `+{"type":"InsufficientAuthority","value":{"type":"Manager","value":3}}+`, where the inner Rank is discriminated independently under `+"value"+`.

When the discriminator key or tag is business-specific (reading `+"status"+` and dispatching to `+"draft"+` / `+"submitted"+`, or reading a persistence `+representation_version+` for versioning), that is a matter of the external representation and cannot be derived from a data's shape. It is done with a custom Decoder on the boundary side (<<custom-codec>>).

[#custom-codec]
=== Custom boundary codec

[[a-custom-codec-agrees-with-its-type]]A codec written by hand MUST be a codec for the type it is declared on: a decoder for `+T+` constructs a `+T+`, an encoder for a sum writes every case, an element encoder matches the element type, and a source-shaped encoder is given that shape. What is derived agrees with the type because it was read off it; what is written has to be checked against it.

[[a-codec-reached-for-exists]]`+T.decoder+` and `+T.encode+` MAY be written only where `+T+` has one. Every declaration of a program that compiles has one: a product's is derived from its declared shape, a sum's from what its cases are, and a unit is read and written as the single value it is.

When the default derivation is insufficient (a different key name, normalization, default filling, a business-specific discriminator, persistence version branching), write a Decoder on the Java side that assembles the external representation and calls the data's (invariant-checking) construction. The `+JdbcFindMember+` calling `+Member.decoder()+` in <<java-example>> is this form. External-representation conversion stays confined to the boundary and is not brought into domain definitions.

A business-specific sum tag does not mean rebuilding the case decoders. Souther generates a public per-case decoder (`+Submitted.decoder()+`) and a sum decoder that is only a `+discriminate("type", …)+` over them (<<sum-discrimination>>). The boundary keeps the per-case decoders and rewrites just that one layer with its own key and tags — reading `+"status"+` and dispatching `+"draft"+` / `+"submitted"+` is `+discriminate("status", variant("draft", Drafting.decoder()), variant("submitted", Submitted.decoder()))+`. Field assembly and invariant checks are reused; only the tag table is written. A newtype case is reached the same way through the key its contents sit under — `+variant("manager", field("value", Manager.decoder()).asDecoder())+` — since its decoder reads what the case is on its own. The encoder side is the mirror of it: a per-case encoder writes what the case is on its own, so a custom sum encoder writes the tag beside it, in the case's own Object where it has one and beside the contents where it does not.

[#decoder-error]
=== Decoder errors

A decode failure is carried by the failure side of Raoh's `+Result+`. An error is a Raoh `+Issue+` (with `+path+` / `+code+` / `+message+`), and failures of independent fields are accumulated (<<case-propagation>>). Souther does not own the *boundary's* failure vocabulary. No domain type such as `+DecodeFailed+` or `+DecodeError+` is introduced; the representation of boundary failure is delegated to the peripheral library Raoh.

This differs from a violation inside the domain (<<algebraic-types>>). A break at the boundary means a value from outside was invalid — an expected result, and which field broke it should be statable (`+path+` and `+code+`), which Raoh holds. A break inside the domain means a computation among validated values broke a rule — a model bug — so it aborts (<<violation-destination>>). The boundary's vocabulary is not brought into the domain, and the domain's vocabulary is not taken out to the boundary.

At the boundary (HTTP, etc.), the `+Result+` failure is received and mapped to an appropriate response (e.g. 400). The `+MemberController+` branching on the decode `+Result+` in <<java-example>> is this form.

==== Codes an invariant produces

A newtype's invariant reaches the boundary as the Raoh constraint that states the same rule, so the failure carries that constraint's `+code+`, its `+messageKey+` and its metadata: `+String.length(value) >= 1+` fails as `+too_short+` with `+min+`, `+String.matches(p, value)+` as `+invalid_format+`, a numeric bound as `+out_of_range+` with `+min+` / `+max+`, `+List.length(value) >= 1+` as `+too_small+` with `+min+` / `+actual+`, and `+List.allDistinctBy(x -> x, value)+` as `+duplicate_element+` with the duplicates it found. The vocabulary is Raoh's, so a `+MessageResolver+` resolves and localises these without Souther supplying message text. It is keyed on the issue rather than on the code alone: one code covers several constraints — `+out_of_range+` states a lower bound, an upper bound, or both, and `+value > 0+` and `+value >= 0+` carry the same bound — so which constraint rejected the value is what the `+messageKey+` says and the `+code+` cannot. Which type rejected it is told by `+path+`, as for any other decode failure.

Only a rule with an exact Raoh equivalent is mapped this way. Anything else — a quantifier over a list, a uniqueness under a projection, a check across two fields, an object's invariant — runs as the rule it is and fails with the code `+invariant_violation+`, carrying the rejecting type in the metadata — its declaring module as `+module+` and its name as `+type+` — and, where the clause was named (<<invariant-clause-name>>), that name as `+clause+`. The module is its own entry because a type is its module and its name (<<modules>>): two modules may each declare an `+Id+`, and a resolver keyed on the name alone would answer for either. So a boundary failure says which rule was broken and not only that the value was rejected. A clause's name never becomes the `+code+`: the code stays the shared one, so a resolver keyed on Raoh's vocabulary keeps working and the metadata is what it switches on.

The clauses are checked in the order they are declared, which is the order construction decides in (<<invariant-mvp>>) — so the boundary and an attempted construction name the same clause for the same value. A mapped clause declared after an unmapped one gives up its Raoh code to keep that order: Raoh answers a plain `+Decoder+` once a rule of its own has been chained on, and a typed constraint cannot follow. Construction re-checks every clause regardless (<<violation-destination>>): the constraints are how a failure is *reported*, not what makes a value valid.

[#codec-generation]
=== Generation method and Raoh

The derived Decoder, without going through an independent Decoder IR, is generated *directly as bytecode* by the ClassFile backend as a Raoh Decoder (`+net.unit8.raoh.decode.Decoder+` and combinators). The generated decoder reads from the neutral tree of the external representation (<<external-representation>>; Raoh's `+Object+` / `+Map+` input) and returns `+Result<T>+`.

[,text]
----
Source -> AST -> resolve -> (add decoder/encoder to each data by derivation) -> type check -> ClassFile backend -> .class (emits Raoh Decoders)
----

Souther's runtime (souther-runtime) does not depend on Raoh. Only the generated bytecode and the apps using it depend on Raoh. Primitive parsing, error accumulation (Applicative), and discriminator dispatch (<<sum-discrimination>>) are handled by Raoh combinators; Souther only maps a data's shape (keys, types, discriminator, case tags) onto Raoh decoder construction.

Decoders are generated per input source. Bare values / Map use raoh-core combinators, JSON `+JsonNode+` uses raoh-json, and jOOQ `+Record+` uses raoh-jooq. The skeleton (keys, types, discriminator, case tags) is common across sources; only terminal field access is swapped per source. For each exposed data, decoders are generated for the sources its shape supports. Bare values / Map and JSON are always generated. JSON temporal fields are read as ISO 8601 strings (`+string().date()+` / `+string().time()+` / `+string().dateTime()+` / `+string().iso8601()+`), and a `+Time+` and a `+DateTime+` are then held to the second: text carrying a fraction of one is a decode failure at that field's path, not a value rounded on the way in (<<a-local-temporal-is-held-to-the-second>>). jOOQ `+Record+` decoders are generated only for a flat shape with no nesting, List, or Map (scalar and newtype columns only) — a row is flat, so a nested object does not fit in one column. Unit data ignore input, so they support all sources.

[#encoder]
== Encoder

[#encoder-role]
=== Role

An Encoder is a total function converting internal data to an external representation (<<external-representation>>). Souther derives a Raoh Encoder from a data's shape.

[,text]
----
TravelRequest.encoder() : Encoder<TravelRequest>     // TravelRequest -> external representation (Raoh's neutral tree)
----

Like a Decoder, an Encoder does not appear in Souther's syntax. A default Encoder is derived from a data's field names and types (<<decoder-role>>). Encode is also an edge of the boundary and is not a `+>->+` stage.

[#encoder-derivation]
=== Derivation rules

The default Encoder is derived by rules symmetric to the Decoder.

* A newtype (`+data X = Y+`; <<newtype>>) writes directly to `+Y+`'s representation (a bare scalar if `+Y+` is primitive). `+{ ... }+` writes to an Object even with a single field.
* A multi-field data writes to an Object. The key is the field name; the value is converted by each field's derived Encoder. Fields taken in by a `+...+` spread are laid flatly too.
* A nested named data is converted by that data's derived Encoder.
* `+List<T>+` is converted element by element with `+T+`'s derived Encoder.
* `+T?+` (Option; <<optional>>) at a field: if `+Some(v)+`, write `+v+` at the key; if `+None+`, omit the key, which is read back as `+None+`. Where an optional stands with no key of its own — a `+List+`'s or a `+Set+`'s element, a `+Map+`'s value — `+Some(v)+` writes `+v+` and `+None+` writes `+null+`, which is read back as `+None+` (<<absence-is-written-as-null>>). The round-trip law (<<round-trip>>) holds either way.
* A sum data tags the discriminator field `+"type"+` with the case name and converts with the case's derived Encoder. A product case lays fields flatly, a unit case writes only the tag, a newtype case puts contents in `+"value"+` (<<sum-discrimination>>). A nested sum writes the leaf case's tag.

[,souther]
----
data Amount = Int
    invariant value >= 0
// derived Encoder: Amount -> Int(self.value) (bare integer)

data Submitted =
    { ...TravelRequestCommon
    , submittedAt: DateTime
    }
// derived Encoder: Submitted -> { "applicant": ..., "estimatedCost": ..., ..., "submittedAt": ... }
----

To match key names or representation to an external spec, write a custom Encoder on the boundary side (<<custom-codec>>).

[#round-trip]
=== Round-trip law

The following is recommended (not required).

[,text]
----
decode(encode(value)) == value
----

`+encode(decode(raw)) == raw+` is not required, because a Decoder may normalize, fill defaults, or drop surplus items.

[#encode-law]
=== What equal values write

At a boundary position — a field, a behavior's input or output — the type is declared, and that declaration fixes one derived Encoder. Under that Encoder, a value's *value* is all that is written about it:

[,text]
----
a == b  ⟹  E.encode(a) == E.encode(b)      // E the Encoder the position's declared type derives
----

Two values the language calls equal (<<equality>>) are written the same JSON, byte for byte. This is what lets a response body be compared, diffed, cached under its own bytes, or written into a golden test. It holds because everything a value carries that its identity does not is settled on the way out: a `+Decimal+` is written as its amount and not with the scale it was read at (<<primitives>>), and a `+Set+` and a `+Map+` are written in the order their members' own representations give and not in the order they were built (<<collections>>).

The position is part of the law and not a technicality about it. A case value *is* a value of its sum (case→sum is transparent, <<sum-data>>) and equals itself at either type, but the two positions do not write it alike: a case's own Encoder writes no discriminator and its sum's writes one (<<sum-discrimination>>). So `+Approved { id = 1 }+` is written `+{"id":1}+` where `+Approved+` is what is declared, and `+{"id":1,"type":"Approved"}+` where `+Approved | Rejected+` is. What is written is decided by the type the position declares, so the law is read one position at a time — it says that a position answers the same bytes for equal values, not that a value has one representation wherever it goes.

A *custom* Encoder (<<custom-codec>>) is written by hand and is bound by nothing here. The law is about the Encoders derived from a data's shape (<<encoder-derivation>>).

[#behavior]
== behavior

A behavior defines the *specification* of a behavior over internal data. It is a top-level input/output relation with no owner.

A declaration takes the form `+behavior name = right-hand side+`. There are two right-hand sides, and what is written and what is left undetermined are inverted between them.

If *an argument list and output* (<<behavior-io>>) are written, what is written is the type. The implementation is undetermined: writing a `+let+` makes it the implementation (<<fn>>); writing none injects it from Java (<<injected-behavior>>). A behavior of this form has no body. What the spec DSL notes as `+// depends:+` comes here.

If a `+>->+` composition (<<composition>>) is written, what is written is the assembly step of chaining stages in this order, and that is itself the implementation. So no `+let+` is needed and no injection occurs. The type is undetermined and computed by the rules of <<type-routing>>.

Both are the same form as the spec DSL's `+behavior+`. The DSL writes both with `+=+`.

[,text]
----
// spec DSL
behavior preApprove = AwaitingPreApproval AND ApproverId -> PreApproved OR NotAuthorized   // right side is a type
behavior placeOrder = validateOrder >-> priceOrder >-> allocateStock                      // right side is a composition
----

That a type or a term may come to the right of `+=+` is because the DSL writes it that way; it is not a distinction Souther introduced. The same two lines map to Souther as:

[,souther]
----
behavior preApprove : (
    request: AwaitingPreApproval,
    approverId: EmployeeId
) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
    constructs PreApproved // a type. Implementation is a let or Java injection

behavior placeOrder = validateOrder >-> priceOrder >-> allocateStock
// a composition. It is itself the implementation

// spec DSL: // depends: clock
behavior now : () -> DateTime // write the type, no let → injected from Java
----

Specification and implementation are separated not because `+data+` already does so, but *so that one line of the spec DSL remains as is* (<<non-functional>>). Just as `+invariant+` rides on `+data+`, `+constructs+` rides on `+behavior+`. Both are the part Souther makes executable of what the spec DSL left in comments (<<mapping-principle>>).

[#behavior-io]
=== Input and output

The spec DSL's input `+AND+` maps to an argument list. A parameter is always written `+name: type+`.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: behavior decidePreApproval = EstimatedCost AND Applicant AND CostBearer -> PreApprovalReasons
behavior decidePreApproval : (
    estimatedCost: Amount,
    applicant: Employee,
    costBearer: CostBearer
) -> List<PreApprovalReason>
----

A parameter name is for the reader and does not affect type identity. The implementation (`+let+`) gives its own names and corresponds by position. A type MUST NOT be written without a name: if `+(String)+` were writable, whether it meant "the type String" or "the name String" would be unreadable.

Zero arguments is written `+()+` (as in `+now+`). "No argument" and "takes one `+Unit+`" are different; `+Unit+` is not exposed at the surface (<<algebraic-types>>).

[#unmarked-output]
=== Output is a bare sum

The spec DSL's output `+OR+` maps directly to a bare sum. No success/failure distinction and no marker such as `+error+` is attached. A behavior is a function from input to one of the possible business results.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: behavior submitTravelRequest = Drafting -> Submitted OR AwaitingPreApproval
behavior submitTravelRequest : (request: Drafting) -> Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval
    constructs Submitted, AwaitingPreApproval

// spec DSL: behavior preApprove = AwaitingPreApproval AND ApproverId -> PreApproved OR NotAuthorized
// NotAuthorized is just data. Not an error, not a Result.
behavior preApprove : (
    request: AwaitingPreApproval,
    approverId: EmployeeId
) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
    constructs PreApproved
----

The commonest shape a model asks for is "the amount, or the reason there is none", and the value it answers with is usually a primitive or a type another module owns. Both are members (<<union-member>>), so the behavior answers with the value itself and no type is declared to hold it.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: behavior computeTaxableAmount = Remuneration AND UpliftPay AND SocialInsurance -> Yen OR DeductionsExceedGross
behavior computeTaxableAmount : (
    remuneration: Remuneration,
    upliftPay: Yen,
    socialInsurance: Yen
) -> Yen | DeductionsExceedGross // Yen is imported; it is the answer, not a wrapper around it
    constructs Yen, DeductionsExceedGross

behavior half : (n: Int) -> Int | NoAnswer // a primitive is a member the same way
    constructs NoAnswer
----

The value is read back with the arm that names it — `+| Yen as y+`, `+| Int as n+` (<<primitive-arm>>) — and binds bare.

Which case "departs as a failure" is not decided by this behavior alone. When composed with `+>->+`, the case the immediately following stage does not consume departs the mainline (<<composition>>). If a downstream stage takes `+NotAuthorized+` as input, that case is processed; if not, it departs and is carried to the output. The same value departs in one composition and stays on the mainline in another.

An input may be a sum (spec DSL `+(Submitted OR PreApproved) AND ...+`). A behavior's parameter takes a declared name rather than an anonymous one (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>), so declare the sum as a named data and write that name. The implementation opens the cases with `+match+`.

[,souther]
----
// name (Submitted OR PreApproved)
data CompletableRequest = Submitted | PreApproved

// spec DSL: behavior completeTravel : (Submitted OR PreApproved) AND ActualCost AND TravelReport -> TravelCompleted OR InvalidActualCost OR NoTravelReport
behavior completeTravel : (
    request: CompletableRequest,
    actualCost: Amount,
    travelReport: String
) -> TravelCompleted | InvalidActualCost | NoTravelReport
    constructs TravelCompleted, InvalidActualCost, NoTravelReport
----

A behavior's *input* type is always a single named type, including a named sum (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>); of the two positions a signature has, the *output* is the one an anonymous union is written in (<<union-intersection>>). Naming the input lets the reader fix the candidates by looking once at `+data CompletableRequest = Submitted | PreApproved+`, and on the JVM the input has a name so a Java implementation receives it as a single type (an anonymous union has no common supertype; <<asymmetric-interop>>, <<jvm-anonymous-union>>).

[#constructs]
=== Data construction authority (constructs)

A behavior that constructs data MAY write the constructed type in `+constructs+`. This is an internal construction authority; unlike a dependency (`+depends on+`), it is invisible to callers. So for a behavior with a `+let+` implementation it is *optional* — omit it and it is inferred from the body. If written, it MUST *exactly match* the body. An injected behavior has no body to infer from, so what it writes is what its implementation may build, and it MUST write every such data (<<java-base-class>>). A behavior whose construction set is empty writes no clause, injected or not: the unit cases of an injected behavior's output get their factories from the output type rather than from this clause (<<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>).

Exact agreement is two obligations, and a declaration can fail either one on its own:

* [[constructs-authority-complete]]every data in the behavior's construction set MUST appear in the clause (<<e1002>>);
* [[constructs-no-overdeclaration]]every data the clause names MUST be in that set (<<e1006>>).

What the set is made of is <<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>: a unit data is constructed but holds no construction authority, so it is not in the set and MUST NOT be named.

[,souther]
----
behavior applyChange : (input: ChangeEmailRequest) -> Member
    constructs Member
----

To construct multiple data, list them: `+constructs Member, AuditRecord+`.

An entry names a type the way a type is named anywhere else, so a type another module declares may be written through it (`+constructs up.Amount+`) as well as under the name an `+import+` brings in. Which spelling the clause uses and which the body uses are independent — both name one type, and that is what E1002 and E1006 compare. Naming one type twice, however spelled, is a duplicate entry.

The following count as data construction: a record literal (`+TypeName { ... }+`), a sum data constructor, a unit data construction, and constructing a collection containing new data as elements. Returning an existing value unchanged does not count as construction.

Construction and *membership of a behavior's construction set* are not the same question, and a unit data is where they part. [[constructs-excludes-unit-data]]A unit-data construction does not contribute to a behavior's construction set and requires no construction authority, and a unit data MUST NOT be named in `+constructs+` (<<e1026>>). Writing `+Domestic+` still constructs the `+Domestic+` value (<<unit-data>>); what it does not do is put `+Domestic+` in the set the clause declares.

A unit data has no fields, cannot declare an invariant (<<unit-data>>), and has exactly one value. So there is nothing for <<invariant-on-construct>> to guard, and no observable difference between minting another `+Domestic+` and passing an existing one through — which is the very distinction the clause exists to record. Authority is not what a unit needs either: a case is reachable through the decoder or the `+protected+` factory rather than by being named, which is what <<closed-construction>> already rests on for cases (<<jvm-anonymous-union>>).

This keeps the clause exact rather than optional. Were a unit merely allowed to be omitted, `+constructs Member+` and `+constructs Member, Missing+` would both be correct for one body and the exact match above would say nothing.

[,souther]
----
data Kind = Domestic | Overseas
data Missing

// `Domestic` is compared against, not declared: the clause names `Decision` alone
behavior classify : (r: Request) -> Decision
    constructs Decision
let classify (r) =
    if r.kind == Domestic then Decision { auto = true } else Decision { auto = false }

// and `Missing` is a unit, so it is not named either
behavior find : (id: MemberId) -> Member | Missing
    constructs Member
----

Every one of these constructs the unit value and none of them puts it in the set: `+Domestic+` written bare, `+f(Domestic)+`, `+[Domestic]+`, `+r.kind == Domestic+`, and `+guard allowed else Domestic+`. A helper `+let+` that mentions or returns a unit likewise carries none to its caller.

The built-in `+Some+` / `+None+` (<<algebraic-types>>) do not count as data construction. Option is not domain data but an auxiliary type and has no invariant, so <<invariant-on-construct>> has nothing to guard. So `+constructs+` is not required to put a value in a `+?+` field (<<optional>>) — and since Option cannot be written as a type, there is no way to declare `+constructs Some+` anyway (<<algebraic-types>>).

*If you write `+constructs+`, you may not omit anything in the behavior's construction set.* The departure value of `+guard ... else+` (<<guard>>) is no exception: `+else TooManyPending { limit = n }+` builds a record on the failure path and belongs in the clause, and what <<closed-construction>> closes is construction regardless of success or failure. A departure written as a bare unit case — `+else NotAuthorized+` — is the usual shape and is a unit-data construction, so it is not in the set and is not named (<<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>).

==== What constructs is for

`+constructs+` is not for guarding invariants — that is handled by `+__construct+` and the Decoder, both of which always check on construction (<<invariant-mvp>>). What `+constructs+` handles is *reading the model*: from the declaration, whether a behavior *creates* a new value or merely *passes through* an existing one. This is not visible from the output type.

[,souther]
----
behavior preApprove : (
    request: AwaitingPreApproval,
    approverId: EmployeeId
) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
    constructs PreApproved // creates new states

behavior checkCeiling : (a: Amount) -> Amount | OverCeiling
    constructs OverCeiling // Amount returns the input as is; not created
----

Both have `+Amount+` / `+PreApproved+` in the output, but one creates and the other passes through. This distinction does not appear in the output type. Writing it leaves it in the declaration and makes a check against reading and mis-taking. Omit it and it is inferred — write it (opt-in) if you want the reading and the check.

Creating a type that does not appear in the output also shows here. The `+constructs HighValueTravel, InsufficientAuthority+` of `+decidePreApproval = (...) -> List<PreApprovalReason>+` is information nowhere in the output type.

A helper `+let+` (with no corresponding behavior) may also construct data, but a helper `+let+` is not a specification statement and does not declare its own `+constructs+`. Its *construction set is inferred transitively* (folding in any helper `+let+` it calls). If the calling behavior writes `+constructs+`, it MUST include that set (E1002 if it does not); if omitted, that set is folded into the inference. A block is the same, constructing under the enclosing behavior's authority (<<blocks>>).

A *value* is the other case. It too is substituted where it is named (<<fn-value-semantics>>), but what it builds is built by its definition: a behavior stating a rule against a named limit originates none of it, and the limit does not belong in that behavior's `+constructs+`. This is what lets a threshold be named at all — the figure gets one place to live and one comment saying where it comes from, and the rules reading it go on saying what they do. Writing the entry anyway is accepted, the same answer a construction another module's published body carried in gets (<<exposed-values>>).

A dependency (`+depends on+`) is different and MUST *always* be written (<<depends-on>>). `+depends on+` is an outward contract to the caller and injector; leaving it to inference would let the contract change silently. `+constructs+` is an internal authority invisible to callers, so they diverge here. Thus `+depends on+` is a declaration and `+constructs+` (for a behavior with a `+let+` implementation) is optional. A `+>->+` composition infers both output and requirements (<<composition-with-requirements>>).

[#record-literal]
=== Record literals and spread

Construction is done with a record literal that writes the constructed type name.

[[a-construction-names-a-data]]What is constructed MUST be a data. [[a-construction-names-fields-of-its-type]]A field written in the literal MUST be one that data declares, and [[a-construction-is-written-where-one-may-be]]a construction MUST be written where one is allowed — not, for instance, inside an `+invariant+`, which states a condition over a value rather than building one (<<invariant-expressions>>).

[[a-data-can-be-constructed]]A data MUST be constructible at all, and it comes to have no value in more than one way. Rules that cannot all hold leave nothing satisfying them. A `+Set+` asked to hold more values that differ than its element has cannot be filled. A self-referential data reaches itself with nothing to bottom out on, so no first value of it can be built; the self-reference belongs at an occurrence that has a base case — an optional, or a collection the rules let be empty (<<self-referential-data>>). All of them are one count coming to nothing, and the refusal says which of them was shown.

[[rules-leaving-a-position-no-value-are-refused]]*Rules that leave a position no value its order holds are refused, on every ordered type.* A position bounded above a value it is bounded below of has none, and so does one bounded past where its order stops — above the last case an enumeration declares, below the empty string, outside the day a `+Time+` runs through — and so does one two equalities name two values of. This is one rule and it does not depend on what carries the value: `+value > 5 && value < 3+` over an `+Int+`, `+value > Date("2020-01-01") && value < Date("2010-01-01")+` over a `+Date+`, and `+c > Blue+` over an enumeration are one thing shown and are refused alike, in one sentence naming the position. A rule stated as one of several alternatives is read as one: a choice among alternatives none of which admits anything admits nothing.

This is the same list as the values a line is drawn on (<<a-line-is-drawn-where-the-values-can-carry-one>>). Deciding that a rule admits nothing needs the order and the values a literal names, and drawing a line needs a value to be written back at the position as well; every ordered type this language has can be written back at, so the two lists coincide.

[,text]
----
PreApproved {
    applicant     = request.applicant,
    estimatedCost = request.estimatedCost,
    submittedAt   = request.submittedAt,
    preApprovedAt = now(),
    preApprover   = approverId
    // ... list the rest of the common items too
}
----

To fill same-named fields together, use a spread `+...src+`. It copies `+src+`'s same-named fields and states the rest explicitly. Surplus fields of `+src+` are ignored; a field missing from `+TypeName+` is a compile error.

[,text]
----
// copy the common items and submittedAt of request (AwaitingPreApproval), and add two
PreApproved { ...request, preApprovedAt = now(), preApprover = approverId }
----

To replace only some fields while keeping the same type, also write the type name and use a spread.

[[a-spread-copies-a-product]]What is spread MUST be a product data, or a sum whose cases all spread one. There has to be a set of fields to copy, and a sum whose cases share nothing has none.

[[a-field-is-supplied-once]]A field is supplied once. A field the literal writes takes the value written, whatever any spread carries; where several spreads carry one field, the first of them supplies it and the rest carry it no more than they carry any other field the literal does not want. So what every field of the construction is given is settled by the literal alone, and it is the same answer wherever it is asked — what runs, what the field's type is checked against, and what the invariant check reads.

[[a-spread-supplies-what-the-fields-need]]What a spread provides MUST have the type the field it lands on needs. Copying by name is not copying by shape: two fields of one name and two types are two fields. If `+x+` has type `+Member+`, write `+Member { ...x, address = newAddress }+`. No dedicated update syntax is provided.

When a field value is a binding of the same name as the field, the value may be omitted. `+Member { address }+` is shorthand for `+Member { address = address }+`, filling from the same-named binding in scope.

A record literal and a spread both require construction authority (`+constructs+`). Because the type name appears at the construction point, the authority check and field check are done on the spot.

[[construction-supplies-every-field]]Every field the constructed type declares MUST be given a value — written out, filled from a same-named binding, or supplied by a spread. A spread supplies what the type it copies from declares and nothing else.

[#blocks]
=== Blocks

To apply processing to each element of a list, pass a block as an argument. A block is written `+<args> -> <expr>+`.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: data UnvalidatedOrder  = ... AND List<UnvalidatedOrderLine>
//           data ValidatedOrder = ... AND List<ValidatedOrderLine>
// validate and repack each detail. A block is needed here.
behavior validateLines : (xs: List<UnvalidatedOrderLine>) -> List<ValidatedOrderLine>

let validateLines (xs) = List.map(x -> validateLine(x), xs)
----

A block is an anonymous `+let+`. It is the same as a `+let+` (<<fn>>), only unnamed. A named one may be passed by name.

[,text]
----
let isValid (r: PreApprovalReason) = ...

List.all(r -> isValid(r), reasons)   // wrap in an anonymous block
List.all(isValid, reasons)           // pass by name directly. Same thing
----

A name that names a function *is* that function where a value goes, whoever declared it: a `+let+` of this module, a `+let+` another module published, and a standard-library function alike. `+String.trim+` written where a value goes is a `+(String) -> String+`, and `+String.trim(s)+` is that function applied to `+s+`. So the two lines above are one rule rather than two, and a function reaches every position a block reaches — an argument, a binding, a branch of an `+if+`, an element of a list a `+fold+` walks.

[,souther]
----
let step: (String) -> String = if flag then String.trim else String.uppercase
let steps: List<(String) -> String> = [String.trim, String.uppercase]
----

Application is written after what is applied, and what is applied is any expression: a name (`+f(x)+`), a parenthesised expression (`+(if flag then String.trim else String.uppercase)(s)+`), or the result of an application (`+adder(1)(x)+`). What is applied is evaluated once, and before the arguments. An argument list MUST begin on the line its callee ends on — a `+(+` that opens a line is a parenthesised expression, so a block whose result is a tuple written under a call reads as that block's result and not as an application of the line above.

A behavior's name handed over is the *behavior*, not the `+let+` that implements it: a Java implementation replaces the behavior, so passing one to `+List.map+` and calling it by name reach the same place (<<calling-a-behavior>>). A behavior with a requirement arrives bound through `+depends on+` and is a binding by the time it can be named, so nothing carries a requirement past that statement.

A block and a `+let+` may be passed as arguments, and a `+let+` may take one as an argument (<<requirement-propagation>>). *What is not allowed is a boundary that requires an external representation*: a data field, a newtype's base, and a behavior's input and output (<<fn-rules>>). A function has none, so placing one would create an exception to decoder / encoder derivation (<<decoder>>, <<encoder>>), and it is refused wherever it sits — directly, or inside a `+List+` or a `+Map+` written there. This is a representation constraint unrelated to side effects. The spec DSL itself holds a rule it wants to swap not as a function but as data (`+data fieldDefinition = ... AND validationRule?+`).

A block may also be bound to a name by a block's `+let+` and applied (`+let f = (x) -> ...+`, then `+f(a)+`); written at the top level the same line is a definition, and its parameters move to the left of `+=+` (<<fn-declaration>>). Like a named `+let+` (<<fn-declaration>>), as long as it does not escape — applied only, never leaving as a value — a block is expanded into its call/apply site and forms no closure. When it escapes — *returning* a function, or choosing at runtime (`+let f = if ... then (x) -> ... else ...+`) — a closure capturing free variables is generated. It is a synthesized class implementing the runtime `+Fn+`, holding the captured variables in fields. Where expandable it is expanded; only where necessary is it a closure. The *cannot place in a data field* constraint above is unchanged by closures: it is a representation constraint, separate from whether a closure exists.

A bare field access `+.field+` is a getter block: it desugars to `+(x) -> x.field+`, so a field is projected point-free — `+List.map(.value, xs)+` instead of `+List.map(x -> x.value, xs)+`, and with the pipe `+xs |> List.map(.value)+`. It is single-layer (`+.value+`, not `+.a.b+`; reach through a newtype over a newtype with the constructor pattern of <<binding-patterns>> or <<match>>) and works for any field, so a newtype's `+.value+` and a record's `+.a+` read the same way. It is a block like any other: it may sit in an argument, be bound with `+let+`, and — since it names its own parameter's type nowhere — be given one by the binding it is written on (`+let g: (Item) -> Int = .n+`). It is a lambda shorthand, not a first-class value, and never collides with the in-scope `+value+` an invariant refers to (<<invariant>>), which is a field name, not a getter.

A block and a helper `+let+` are the same in constructing data. A block constructs under the enclosing behavior's `+constructs+`, and a hoisted helper `+let+` constructs under the `+constructs+` of the behavior that calls it (the construction set is inferred transitively and reconciled; <<constructs>>). Either way the construction authority is on the behavior and does not disappear with naming. So hoisting a constructing `+[HighValueTravel { threshold = threshold } | …]+` (<<stdlib-list>>) into `+let makeHighValueTravel (threshold) = HighValueTravel { threshold = threshold }+` passes if the calling behavior declares `+constructs HighValueTravel+`.

[#binding-patterns]
=== Binding patterns

Where a name is bound — a block's `+let+`, a behavior implementation's parameter, a helper's parameter, a lambda's parameter — a *pattern* may be written instead, opening the value where it is named. The patterns admitted here are the ones every value of the type satisfies: a name, a tuple, a newtype opened by its constructor, a record's fields.

[,text]
----
let TagSet(ts) = set                    // open a newtype
let { name, quantity } = line           // read fields under their own names
let { quantity = n } = line             // ... or under another
let (k, v) = pair                       // open a tuple (<<tuple>>)
let (k, _) = pair                       // ... reading one of its members

let count (TagSet(ts)) = Set.size(ts)   // a helper's parameter

behavior size : (set: TagSet) -> Int
let size (TagSet(ts)) = Set.size(ts)    // a behavior implementation's parameter

List.map((TagSet(ts)) -> Set.size(ts), sets)   // a lambda's parameter
----

[[a-discard-stands-inside-a-pattern-for-what-is-not-read]]Inside a pattern, `+_+` stands for a part of the value the pattern does not read: a member of a tuple (`+let (_, out) = pair+`), the value a newtype's constructor opens, and a lambda's parameter. `+_+` is not a name (<<identifier>>), and nothing reads what it received — an expression writing `+_+` is refused — so a discarded part has no name anywhere.

A whole binding is not written `+_+`. `+let _ = e+`, a helper's or behavior's parameter, and the name a record pattern renames a field to (`+{ f = _ }+`) each take a name: a binding that reads nothing of what it binds is a line to delete rather than a form to write.

A pattern that some values of the type do not satisfy — a sum's case, `+Some+` — MUST NOT be written here; it belongs to `+match+` (<<match>>), which has the arm that catches what fails. A binding has one arm and nothing to fall to. Whether a name is refutable is decided by what it declares, so `+let X(v) = e+` is refused when `+X+` is not a newtype, and the report names `+match+`.

Outside `+match+` the name a constructor pattern writes is also checked against the value's own type: `+let TagSet(ts) = LabelSet+` is refused. In a `+match+` the arm's name is one of the scrutinee's cases and exhaustiveness has already settled it; a binding names a type on its own authority.

A helper's parameter written as a constructor pattern needs no type beside it — `+let count (TagSet(ts))+` already says the parameter is a `+TagSet+`. A tuple or record pattern names no type, so a helper whose parameter type its body does not determine still annotates it (`+let twice ({ quantity }: Line)+`).

On a behavior's implementation the pattern is not a type either way: the parameter types come from the behavior (<<fn-declaration>>), and a pattern only opens the input the behavior has already typed. The name the pattern writes is checked against that input, so `+behavior size : (set: TagSet) -> Int+` implemented as `+let size (LabelSet(ts))+` is refused. A `+depends on+` parameter names an injected behavior, so it takes a name, not a pattern.

A lambda's `+(a, b) -> ...+` is its parameter list, not a tuple pattern; a single tuple parameter is `+((a, b)) -> ...+`.

[#depends-on]
=== Dependencies (depends on)

A behavior that touches the outside world writes what it depends on in `+depends on+`.

[,souther]
----
behavior preApprove : (
    request: AwaitingPreApproval,
    approverId: EmployeeId
) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
    depends on now
    constructs PreApproved
----

What is written is the name of a behavior whose *requirement set is not empty* (<<requirement-propagation>>) — one with no implementation (<<injected-behavior>>), or one whose own `+let+` declares `+depends on+` — declared in this module or imported from another (<<modules>>). Write it if the `+let+` uses that name. Even when used indirectly by passing it to a helper `+let+`, the one passing is you, so you write it.

Two entries of one clause MUST go by different bare names, however they are written and whichever modules declare them (<<e1011>>). What the clause decides is the implementing `+let+`'s trailing parameters, and each is named after the entry it takes; `+depends on up.now, other.now+` names two behaviors and leaves no `+let+` that could be written for them. Which behaviors they are is settled by resolving the names, so the same pair under an alias is refused the same way.

A behavior with an empty requirement set is not written here. It depends on nothing, so there is nothing to inject, and it is called directly (<<calling-a-behavior>>). Writing one in `+depends on+` is rejected where the clause is written (<<e1607>>), as is a `+>->+` composition — whose requirements are inferred from its stages and are not written, so requiring one would make a caller declare a set that changes when an upstream stage changes — and as is a name that is no behavior in scope.

Only a simple behavior whose implementation is Souther's may have `+depends on+`. An injection target does not write it: it is the leaf of the dependency, and its name being listed is itself the statement of dependency. Writing the clause is what says the implementation is Souther's, so a behavior that writes it and has no `+let+` yet is one nobody has written rather than an injection target (<<unwritten-behavior>>). A `+>->+` composition does not write it either: it is decided by the union over stages, readable from the stages (<<composition-with-requirements>>).

==== Correspondence to the spec DSL

The DSL attaches `+// depends:+` / `+// side effect:+` to a *leaf*.

[,text]
----
// responsibility 2: save the business-trip application to the database
// depends: travelRequestDatabase
// side effect: save to the database
behavior saveTravelRequest = UnvalidatedTravelRequest AND PreApprovalNeeded -> SavedTravelRequest
----

In Souther this becomes a behavior with no implementation, so no `+depends on+` is needed. The same as the DSL attaching no note to a composition: it is readable from the stages.

The DSL holds only these two because it places side effects at the ends of a workflow. The form "use the outside world once in the middle of a pure computation" does not appear in a specification model. Souther allows it, so a position with no source in the DSL — a behavior with a `+let+` — arises. `+depends on+` lets the DSL's note be written on a non-leaf behavior too. The positions increase, but the content is the same "what it depends on" as the DSL; no lines are added.

==== depends on becomes a let argument

What is written in `+depends on+` appears as a `+let+` argument, after the business inputs, in declaration order.

[,souther]
----
let preApprove (request, approverId, now) = {
    guard approverId == request.applicant.managerId else NotAuthorized

    PreApproved { ...request, preApprovedAt = now(), preApprover = approverId }
}
----

The behavior has 2 arguments and the `+let+` has 3 because *the behavior's type is the type after partial application*.

[,text]
----
let:      (request, approverId, now) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
bind:     fix now                             f_a(x) = f(x, a)
behavior: (request, approverId)      -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
----

`+PreApprove.bind(new JdbcClock())+` (<<jvm-behavior>>) is this partial application. What remains after binding is the behavior's declared type, and Java calls `+apply(request, approverId)+` with 2 arguments. `+depends on+` does not appear in the caller's arguments.

This merely surfaces the "requirements are constructor arguments" of the end of <<blocks>>. In the `+let+`, `+now+` is just an argument, not an effect or a special type. So the `+let+` side needs no place to write `+depends on+`.

A helper `+let+` (with no corresponding behavior) fits the same form. To touch the outside world, it receives a function as an argument — a helper writes its own type (<<fn-declaration>>), so it writes it there.

[,text]
----
let record (x: Int, now: () -> DateTime) = ...       // helper. Receives it as an argument
----

The `+now+` appearing inside a `+let+` is an argument in either case. There is no path to call a behavior with no implementation directly as a top-level name. So a helper `+let+` having no place to write `+depends on+` is not a restriction. The passer writes it in its own `+depends on+` and passes it as an argument.

==== Checking

The set of behaviors with a non-empty requirement set that a `+let+` uses and the set written in `+depends on+` MUST match. As with `+constructs+`, matching is two obligations and either can fail alone. An over-declaration erring on the safe side is not admitted.

* [[depends-on-names-every-requirement]]every behavior the implementation reaches that requires something MUST appear in the clause (<<e1602>>);
* [[depends-on-names-no-more]]every behavior the clause names MUST actually be reached by the implementation (<<e1603>>).

[[depends-on-names-a-dependency]]An entry MUST name something there is to inject: a behavior in scope whose own requirement set is not empty (<<e1607>>). A behavior that requires nothing is reached by name, and a `+>->+` composition infers its requirements from its stages rather than being handed them.

[#ensures]
=== `ensures`

An `+ensures+` clause declares the relation between a behavior's named parameters and the value it answers. It is a clause of the behavior signature, beside `+constructs+` and `+depends on+`, and travels with the published declaration.

[,souther]
----
behavior findBuildings : (cond: SearchCriteria) -> List<Building>
    ensures inTheAreaAsked = List.all(b -> b.area == cond.area, value)

behavior findTodo : (id: TodoId) -> Todo | NotFound
    ensures Todo | NotFound -> value.id == id
----

The answer is named `+value+`.

A rule states a relation, so it refers to both sides of one. [[an-ensures-clause-names-the-answer]]A rule MUST refer to the answer, which it does through the arm it is written under or through `+value+`; where it names no arm there is nothing else to refer to the answer by, so its expression MUST name `+value+`. [[an-ensures-clause-names-a-parameter]]And it MUST name at least one parameter. A property of the answer alone belongs on the answer type's invariant; a property of the inputs alone is a precondition and is modelled as ADR-0003 decides. [[a-behavior-with-a-clause-has-no-parameter-named-value]]A behavior carrying a clause MUST NOT have a parameter named `+value+`.

An arm is a reference to the answer, so `+ensures Missing -> id == tag+` states a relation: where the answer is `+Missing+`, the inputs stood so. Asking for the word `+value+` in every expression would read the text for the relation rather than the rule, and would refuse a rule that states one.

What a rule refers to is not what it is about, and only the first is decided here. `+value == value && id > 0+` refers to the answer and says nothing about it, and an arm of an answer with one case holds of everything it answers. Telling those apart is a proof; what this rule holds is that a relation is written with both of its sides in it.

An answer that can be more than one thing has cases, and they are the ones a `+match+` over that answer opens: the members of an anonymous sum, or the cases declared with a named one. A case of a case is not one of them — a caller opens the outer case and only then what is inside it — so a rule naming one would be a rule the caller has no arm to assume it at. What `+value+` is read as in an arm is what that case holds, which for a case named by a primitive (<<unmarked-output>>) is that primitive.

Where the answer has cases the form is `+ensures [name =] Case { | Case } -> expression { | Case { | Case } -> expression }+`. [[an-ensures-clause-over-a-sum-names-arms]]Each expression MUST name one or more arms, and [[an-ensures-arm-is-an-output-case]]every named arm MUST be a case of the answer. Several cases MAY share an expression, and a case named twice under one arrow states one rule: what a clause states is a conjunction, so naming a case twice says what naming it once said. Where the answer has none the form is `+ensures [name =] expression+`, and [[an-ensures-clause-over-a-single-type-names-no-arm]]it MUST name no arm. A case no rule names carries no stated postcondition, and there is no wildcard arm.

Two arms of one clause MAY name the same case, and both of their rules then hold of it. That is the same reading as two clauses naming it, which is what makes an arm a statement rather than a choice.

A behavior MAY carry more than one `+ensures+`, and every rule of every one of them holds. Where the answer is a case, the rules whose arms name that case are the rules it is held to — all of them, and not the first — so writing two clauses and writing their rules under one are the same statement about the model. What differs is where a violation is reported: a clause is what a failure is reported by, and its name is what a reader is given. Where more than one does not hold, the first that does not is the one reported, as a data's clauses are (<<invariant-declaration>>).

Cases may overlap. A data may be a case of two sums declared in its module, so one answer can be named by an arm of each, and both of their rules hold of it. This is what makes a rule's arms a statement rather than a dispatch: `+match+` takes the first arm that answers, which is a control-flow reading, and a declaration has no order to read.

A clause MAY be named. [[ensures-clause-names-are-distinct]]Named clauses are distinct within the behavior, and [[an-ensures-clause-name-is-not-underscore]]`+_+` is not a clause name. A name here says which clause is meant and nothing more: unlike a data's clause name, it names nothing a departure arm can be written for (<<attempt-departures>>), an `+ensures+` having no such form. The expression fragment is the invariant fragment unchanged: it is total, constructs nothing and contains no `+unreachable+` (<<invariant-expressions>>).
