[[a-composition-carries-no-ensures]]A `+>->+` composition MUST NOT carry `+ensures+`: it writes no parameter list whose names a clause could relate to its answer, and a stage's relation is not inferred as the composition's.

A clause travels with the published declaration, as a data's does and for the same reason: a reader that cannot read it cannot assume it.

Where a value the model writes down states both sides of the relation — an `+example+` row, a row of a `+fake+` table — that value is held to the clause while the module is compiled (<<example-ensures>>).

==== What a caller may assume

Where a caller has reached the case a rule is about, that rule holds of the answer, and the checker takes it as holding — the same seeding an input's type gives (<<invariant-discharge>>). This is what declaring a relation buys: the answer does not carry the input, so before it a caller could only re-establish the relation with a `+guard+` it had no way to derive.

It is reached by matching that case and by nothing weaker. An arm answering for several cases says the answer is one of them, which is not that it is any one of them, so a rule about one of those cases is a rule about a value that arm may not have. A rule under no case applies to every answer and is reached wherever the answer is.

What is taken in is what the checker can read of the rule, conjunct by conjunct, as everywhere else a clause is read (<<ensures-discharge-capability>>). A rule one part of which it cannot read still says the part it can.

The answer has to be a value the checker can name, which a matched case's binding is. An answer no arm opens — a behavior whose output has no cases — is named by nothing the checker reasons about, so what is stated about such an answer is held when the behavior answers and reaches no caller.

[#ensures-discharge-capability]
==== A rule says how much of it the check reads

A rule is classified as a data's clause is (<<invariant-discharge-capability>>), by the same answers and for the same reason: what is written decides how much of it the checker can carry, and nothing in the source tells the two apart. The unit is the *rule* and, under a rule, the conjunct. A rule is already about one case — `+ensures Found | Missing -> value.id == id+` is a rule for `+Found+` and a rule for `+Missing+` — and what `+value+` is differs between them, so the same words can be read to different depths. One answer for the clause would be one of those readings shown as if it were the other's.

A rule has one answer, shaped as a data's conjunct's is (<<invariant-discharge-capability>>) — every part of it to be established, any route of a part to establish that part — and it is given at the one place the rule is written.

The classification says nothing about whether the rule is enforced. Every rule holds when the behavior answers, whatever this says of it; what it says is how much of the relation is written in a form the checker reads — `+derivable+` a relation the numeric domain reasons over, `+exact match+` a term it can name and compare and nothing weaker, `+always holds+` and `+never holds+` a rule that settles without the answer, `+runtime only+` nothing it can represent.

The rule is read as it is written. An operation whose meaning the language defines stands as that operation, as a data's clause is read (<<invariant-discharge-representation>>), so a rule naming one is classified by what the language says of it and not by the algorithm it is emitted as.

It is a query rather than a diagnostic, again as a data's is, and the LSP surfaces it on the clause. So is which of the answer's cases no rule names: nothing being stated about a case is what the declaration says (<<ensures>>), and a reader who wants to know asks rather than being told.

[#calling-a-behavior]
=== Calling a behavior

A behavior's `+let+` may call a behavior. Which ones it may call is decided by one question — the requirement set — and the answer does not depend on where the behavior was declared. A behavior this module declares and one it imported are called the same way.

[[behavior-does-not-reach-itself]]A behavior MUST NOT reach itself, whether through calls, `+depends on+` or `+>->+` stages, or a mixture of them. A `+depends on+` cycle leaves nothing to build first and a call cycle does not terminate; recursion belongs to a helper `+let+`, where it is proven total (<<fn-rules>>).

[[a-helper-does-not-call-a-behavior]]A helper `+let+` may not. A helper is expanded into what calls it, and an invariant that names one carries it to an importing module as source (<<published-modules>>); it names only what travels with it, which is what keeps an invariant's rule a single question about the requirement set rather than a second one about where a name came from. A behavior a helper wanted is called from the behavior's own `+let+` and its result passed in.

* An *empty* requirement set: call it by name. Nothing is injected and nothing is written in `+depends on+`.
* A *non-empty* requirement set: write it in `+depends on+` and call it through that name (<<depends-on>>). It arrives already bound, as any requirement does.

[,souther]
----
behavior preApprove : (request: AwaitingPreApproval) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
    depends on now

behavior rejectAndNotify : (request: AwaitingPreApproval) -> Notified | NotAuthorized
    depends on preApprove
let rejectAndNotify (request, preApprove) =
    match preApprove(request) with
        | PreApproved as p -> Notified { to = p.applicant }
        | NotAuthorized as r -> r
----

A call yields the behavior's declared output. Where that output is a sum, the caller opens it with `+match+`, and a case it does not handle is its own to declare in its output. Nothing departs on its own: departure is what `+>->+` does (<<type-routing>>), and a call is not a stage.

A behavior's `+constructs+` is its own and adds nothing to its caller's. A helper `+let+` is different — it is not a specification statement and declares no authority, so its construction set folds into the behavior that calls it (<<constructs>>).

Behaviors do not recurse. A behavior MUST NOT reach itself, through calls, through `+depends on+`, or through a mixture of the two (<<e1608>>). Recursion is a property of a named module helper (<<fn-rules>>).

[#tuple]
== Tuples

`+(a, b, ...)+` is a tuple bundling two or more values into one. It is an expression-level value, used only in the middle of a computation — a helper `+let+` returning multiple values and destructuring them — and MUST NOT be written in a data field or a behavior input/output. Like a function (<<blocks>>), it does not cross the boundary, so no codec is derived. Domain vocabulary stays as named data.

A tuple *type* `+(A, B, ...)+` is written in a type position like any other type (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>), and what refuses it is the positions requiring an external representation — a data field, a newtype's base, and a behavior's input and output — where a codec would have to be derived (<<what-crosses-the-boundary-has-an-external-representation>>). A signature is not a codec boundary, so a helper's or the standard library's takes one, `+List<(String, 'a)>+` included. A one-element `+(T)+` is not a tuple type.

A tuple is compared by its elements (<<equality>>), so two built separately from the same values are one value. It is therefore a `+Set+`'s element and a `+Map+`'s key like any other, which is how a rule over a composite key is written: `+allDistinctBy(t -> (t.from, t.event), transitions)+` says one row per `+(from, event)+`. A tuple has no *ordering*, so it is not a `+sort+`'s element or a `+sortBy+`'s key (<<stdlib-list>>) — order by one key and then the other instead.

[,text]
----
let stats (xs: List<Int>) = (List.sum(xs), List.length(xs))   // return multiple values

let average (xs) = {
    let (total, count) = stats(xs)      // positional destructuring bind
    ...
}
----

`+let (x, y) = t+` destructures a tuple positionally and binds. Names correspond to elements left to right. A one-element `+(e)+` is just a parenthesized expression and is not a tuple. A tuple may be held as is in a `+let+`, passed to a helper, or be an element of `+List<(a, b)>+` (a first-class value). Extraction is by destructuring only; there are no index functions such as `+fst+` / `+snd+`.

A lambda argument `+(a, b) -> ...+` is multiple arguments (<<blocks>>), distinct from a tuple. A single tuple argument is written with its own parentheses, `+((a, b)) -> ...+` (<<binding-patterns>>).

[#fn]
== let — implementation


What holds wherever something is applied:

* [[what-is-applied-is-a-function]]what is applied MUST be something that can be — a helper, a behavior, a newtype, a block, a function-typed parameter. A name the language gives is not one;
* [[an-application-gives-the-arguments-it-takes]]an application MUST give as many arguments as the thing takes. A newtype wraps one value, a block takes what it was written with, a helper takes its parameters;
* [[an-argument-has-the-type-its-parameter-takes]]an argument MUST have the type its parameter takes.

A block is written where a function is taken, and that position is part of what the standard library states:

* [[a-function-goes-where-a-function-is-taken]]a block or a function name goes in the argument that takes a function, and only there. Which argument that is belongs to the function being called (<<stdlib>>);
* [[a-block-answers-the-type-its-position-takes]]a block MUST answer with what its position takes — the accumulator of a `+fold+`, `+Bool+` for a predicate, the element type of what is being grown.

[[a-block-is-not-a-value]]A block is not a value. It may be passed as an argument, or bound with `+let+` and applied, and it MUST NOT be returned or stored in a field: a function has no external representation (<<external-representation>>), so nothing carries it across a boundary.

[[a-function-binding-has-one-type]]A binding holding a function has one type. Applied at two argument types, or given by two branches that produce different function types, it has no single one to be.

[[a-function-bindings-type-is-known]]That type MUST be readable where the binding is written — from its annotation, or from an application in the same scope. Nothing outside the scope is consulted for it.

[[an-annotation-on-a-function-binding-is-a-function-type]]An annotation on a binding that holds a function MUST be a function type. An ordinary type does not describe a function, so it settles nothing and disagrees with the value in the same breath. Leaving the annotation off is the other way to write the binding, and then the applications settle it.

A `+let+` is the implementation of a behavior. A behavior is the specification (<<behavior>>), and a `+let+` is what satisfies it.

[#fn-declaration]
=== Declaration and binding

A `+let+` is written `+let <name> (<args>) = <body>+`. If a behavior of the same name exists, it implements it.

Parameters go on the left of `+=+`, and a `+let+` written without them defines a *value*. The parameter list, not the shape of the body, is what tells a function from a value. An empty `+()+` is refused, since it would be a second spelling of the value form.

[,souther]
----
let ceiling = Amount(100000) // a value
let isOver (amount: Amount) = amount > ceiling // a function
----

A value is named where the value it stands for would be written, and it may be spread (`+Drafting { ...standardRequest, estimatedCost = Amount(50000) }+`). A behavior taking nothing is implemented by a value, since the parameter counts agree.

[,souther]
----
behavior preApprove : (
    request: AwaitingPreApproval,
    approverId: EmployeeId
) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
    depends on now
    constructs PreApproved

let preApprove (request, approverId, now) = {
    guard approverId == request.applicant.managerId else NotAuthorized

    PreApproved { ...request, preApprovedAt = now(), preApprover = approverId }
}
----

The type comes from the behavior. The `+let+` side only names its arguments, corresponding by position. The behavior-side names are for the reader; what binds is the `+let+`-side names. `+constructs+` is on the behavior, so it is not written on the `+let+` (<<constructs>>).

Arguments are the behavior's argument list followed by `+depends on+` in declaration order. A `+let+` is the pre-injection function and a behavior's type is the post-injection type, so a `+let+` has as many more arguments as there are `+depends on+` (<<depends-on>>). If `+depends on+` is empty, both have the same arguments.

A `+let+` with no corresponding behavior is a *helper*. Not being a domain behavior, it does not pollute the behavior list, cannot be placed in a `+>->+` stage, and does not appear in `+exposing+`. It states its own type, though a parameter whose type its body determines may leave it unwritten (below).

[,souther]
----
let isNonNegative (v: Int) = v >= 0
let applyAll (xs: List<Int>, p: (Int) -> Bool) = List.all(p, xs)
----

The return type is inferred. However, a *recursive helper* MUST declare its return type — `+let depth (s: OrgMember): Int = ...+` — because the result type cannot be inferred through the cycle, and only with a declaration can a self-call be type-checked. The declaration is required only for a recursive helper. A non-recursive helper is inferred as before, and a `+let+` with a same-named behavior cannot write a return type (the type comes from the behavior).

A written return type is a declaration into the body, and it stays one where the helper is expanded: it is pushed into the body at each call site, so a value only its context can type — an empty `+[]+` / `+Map.empty+` / `+Set.empty+`, or a `+fold+` over one — takes the declared element type even where the call site itself expects nothing concrete (a generic parameter such as `+Map.toList+`'s). This is the same direction a binding's `+let x: T = v+` has (<<block-expression>>).

The body is an expression. `+{ ... }+` is a block expression (<<block-expression>>), written to place a `+let+` or `+guard+`.

`+=+` introduces a named definition. The bodies of an anonymous block (<<blocks>>) and a `+match+` case are introduced by `+->+` — the same symbol as the type arrow. A lambda written on the right of `+=+` is the parameter-list form written the other way round: `+let f = (x) -> e+` and `+let f (x) = e+` define one thing, and which of the two a source writes changes nothing about what is defined. Only a lambda the source wrote is read this way. A `+.field+` getter is a block too (<<blocks>>), but its parameter is synthesized, so it stays a block — and a block is not a value, which is what a definition writing no parameters would need it to be. A definition that writes a function type is a *value* of that type, and the lambda on the right is that value rather than the definition's parameters. In `+let bump: (Int) -> Int = (x) -> next(x)+` the written type says what the definition is, and reading `+x+` as a parameter of `+bump+` would leave that type describing a definition answering a function rather than being one. A lambda's own parameters take no annotation, so this is where a function value states its type, as a binding does (<<block-expression>>).

[#fn-value-semantics]
=== A value is substituted where it is named

A value is not module state. Its expression is elaborated in the module that declares it and substituted at each reference, so nothing is held between references and the module's values come into being in no order.

* Its names are resolved once, where it is written (<<qualified-reference>>). What is substituted is the resolved definition, not text to be read again at the reference.
* It is evaluated at each reference. A value's body obeys a helper's rules — it cannot call an injected behavior (<<depends-on>>) — so it is pure and total, and re-evaluation is not observable.
* Two references may yield distinct values. Nothing observes that: there is no reference identity in the language, and comparison is structural (<<newtype-comparison>>).
* An invariant a value breaks is reported at the value, whether or not anything names it.
* What a value constructs is the value's own. A behavior that names one reads something already made, so the construction stays with the definition and is not required in that behavior's `+constructs+` (<<constructs>>). A spread of a value reads one the same way. This is where a value parts from a helper, whose construction set does fold into its caller.
* A spread names a value like any other position: `+Drafting { ...standardRequest, estimatedCost = Amount(50000) }+` reads the same in a behavior's body and in an `+example+` row. A binding in force wins there too, so a parameter or a `+let+` sharing a value's name is what the spread copies.
* [[a-value-does-not-reach-itself]]A value MUST NOT reach itself, through naming another value, spreading one, or calling a helper that does. A helper on a call cycle is lowered to a method and recurses (<<fn-rules>>); a value has no such form, so a cycle through one is a compile error naming the path it goes round. This is reported apart from the recursion check, which would ask for a return type the author never wrote.

[#fn-rules]
==== Rules


* [[a-helper-parameter-states-its-type]]a helper's parameter MUST have a type that is stated or determined. A parameter only read through a field, or used as a function, names no type by being used, so it is annotated;
* [[a-helper-answers-what-it-declares]]a helper that declares a return type MUST answer with it;
* [[a-recursive-helper-declares-its-return-type]]a recursive helper MUST declare its return type, its result not being readable from a body that names itself;
* [[a-recursive-helper-does-not-reach-an-injected-behavior]]a recursive helper's own body MUST NOT call an injected behavior. It is emitted as a pure static method, and the effect belongs to the behavior that calls it. Applying a function argument that reaches one is unaffected — the effect is then the caller's, declared in the caller's `+depends on+` (<<fn-rules>>).

* A `+let+` with a same-named behavior does not write argument types; the type comes from the behavior. A count mismatch is a compile error. A behavior whose type takes nothing (`+() -> T+`) is implemented by a value, which writes no parameter list.
* A `+let+` written with a parameter list defines a function; one written without defines a value. An empty `+()+` is a compile error — a definition taking nothing is the value form. A helper therefore has at least one parameter.
* A module's `+let+` MUST NOT be named like a data the module declares. A data is written where a value goes — a unit data is one, a newtype is applied to what it wraps, a record is constructed by its name — so the two would be one spelling with two answers. An identifier may be written in any script and case carries no meaning (<<unit-data>>), so the collision is refused where it is declared. A behavior and a same-named `+let+` are not a collision: they are one thing's declaration and implementation.
* The same holds for a name that reaches the value namespace by *import*: `+import lib ( Amount )+` in a module that declares `+data Amount+` is refused at the import line. Where a name comes from does not change how many answers one spelling may have. A binding is not a collision — it is in force over a region and everything outside it is unaffected (<<blocks>>) — and neither is an import a module's own `+let+` of that name shadows, which is that `+let+`.
* A helper (a `+let+` with no same-named behavior) states its own argument types, except where its *body* determines them. A parameter takes the type the body asks of it, at any position it stands in: an operand of an operator against a value of known type (`+let twice (n) = n * 2+`), an argument of a call, the value of a field in a construction, an `+if+` condition, an arm of an `+if+` or a `+match+` beside an arm that answers a value, an element of a collection literal beside another element, the answer of a body whose return type is declared (`+let same (v): Int = v+`), the value of a binding — its written type where it has one, and otherwise whatever the binding's own body determines, so `+let y = x in y * 2+` types `+x+` through `+y+`. A *signature's type variable* is solved at the call it is written in, from the other arguments (`+Set.contains(v, s)+` types `+v+` from `+s+`), from what the call as a whole is asked for (`+List.sum(xs) + 1+` types `+xs+` as `+List<Int>+`), and from the closure it is handed (`+List.map((v) -> v * 2, xs)+` types `+xs+` the same way) — and a combinator's closure is read in a scope its own parameters are in force over, so `+List.map((x) -> x + k, xs)+` over a `+List<Int>+` types `+k+`. The whole body is read, so a position further down it — or a helper declared further down the file — determines the parameter just as well; written order decides only which of several determining positions wins. Call sites are not read — what a helper means is settled by reading the helper, not by reading its callers.
* A *non-recursive* helper MAY carry the type variables a signature left unsettled, where the body decided what the value *is* and left open only what it *holds* — the outermost layer of the type names a type constructor. `+let count (xs) = List.length(xs)+` names the container and not the element, so `+xs+` is a list of something, and the helper is monomorphized at each expansion exactly as a core helper carrying `+'a+` is (<<type-variables>>): `+count([1, 2])+` and `+count(["a"])+` are both written in one module. A type stated outright always wins over one that leaves something open, wherever either is found, so `+let total (xs) = List.sum(xs) + 1+` is `+List<Int>+`. Two readings of one parameter are taken together, and two that disagree about what the value is settle nothing. What the body gets wrong is reported where the disagreement is, as it is for a type written beside the parameter, rather than as a parameter nothing determined. The variables belong to the helper: a library signature is read once and shared by every call site, so `+let sizes (xs, ys) = List.length(xs) + List.length(ys)+` holds two different things although both calls spell the variable `+'a+`, while `+let sameHead (xs, ys) = List.get(0, xs) == List.get(0, ys)+` holds one, because `+==+` reads the two together.
* A parameter the body leaves open MUST be annotated: `+let id (v) = v+` says nothing about `+v+` at all, and `+let qtys (ls) = List.map((l) -> l.qty, ls)+` reads a field off a value nothing names, which is a structural question a nominal model does not ask (<<union-intersection>>). A *function-typed* parameter MUST be written: neither applying it nor handing it to a combinator that applies it determines its type. A *recursive* helper is lowered to a method rather than inlined (below) and is typed on its declaration, so it writes all of its argument types.
* A helper *may recurse* (self- and mutual recursion). A non-recursive helper is inline-expanded at its call site; a recursive helper is not inlined but lowered to a method — self- and mutual calls become plain method calls. Traversal of self-referential data that `+fold+` cannot write (climbing the manager chain to the root, counting tree depth) is written this way.
* A recursive helper MUST *declare its return type* (above). Without a declaration it is a compile error.
* A recursive helper MUST be *pure*: its own body MUST NOT call an injected behavior (`+depends on+`). It MAY apply a function argument that does — the effect is then the caller's, declared in the caller's `+depends on+` and carried in by the closure (<<blocks>>). An effect touching the outside world is placed on the behavior that calls the helper, or on the function it is handed.
* [[helper-carries-its-termination-guarantee]]Recursion is *total by default*. A recursive helper — or a group of mutually-recursive helpers — MUST terminate, proven by *size-change termination*: each recursive call relates caller parameters to callee arguments, an argument that is a strictly smaller part of a parameter is a strict decrease and one passed through unchanged is non-increasing, and every recursive cycle MUST strictly decrease some parameter. A strictly smaller part comes from a `+match+` on a field or a case (`+match s.manager with | Some b -> depth(b)+` passes the unwrapped `+OrgMember+` — an org chart, or a two-`+Option+`-field binary tree), or from a value handed to a standard-library container combinator's closure over a container that is part of a parameter — a list element (`+List.fold((acc, c) -> acc + depth(c), 0, t.children)+` — an element of `+t.children+` is smaller than `+t+`, so an n-ary `+List<T>+`-children tree is total), or an `+Option+`'s unwrapped `+Some+` payload (`+s.manager |> Option.map(b -> depth(b) + 1)+` — the payload of `+s.manager+` is a smaller `+OrgMember+` than `+s+`, so `+Option.map+` recursion over a manager chain is total, exactly as the equivalent `+match+` is). Either way it bottoms out on the finite self-referential data. This one check covers self-recursion, mutual recursion, and structural lexicographic recursion (different calls decreasing different positions). A helper or group that cannot be shown to terminate is a compile error (E2001). Write `+partial let f ...+` to opt out. Numeric (`+n - 1+`) and index (`+List.get(i, xs)+` with `+i + 1+`) recursion is not structural — Souther has no inductive `+Nat+` — so it MUST be `+partial+`; counting is otherwise written with `+fold+`. There is no way to *claim* totality on non-structural recursion; `+partial+` only disclaims it. The standard library's `+List.foldFrom+` — private, reached through `+List.fold+` — is trusted total.
* `+partial+` *declares that the helper does not carry Souther's termination guarantee*. It does not assert that the helper diverges: a recursion that does terminate and that the analysis cannot prove is written this way too, and so is one the analysis gave up on as too large. What it disclaims is the termination guarantee and nothing else — the helper is type-checked, its `+match+` is checked for exhaustiveness, and its invariants hold as any other's do. A helper written without the word does carry the guarantee.
* *A helper's totality covers everything it reaches.* An unmarked helper MUST NOT reach a `+partial+` one, directly or through any chain of calls; doing so is a compile error (E2001) naming the path. Its caller has to say `+partial+` as well. Every member of a mutually-recursive group holding a `+partial+` member therefore needs the word — each reaches the others. Reading a `+let+` written with no parameter list is reaching it, because the value is substituted where it is named. A behavior's implementing `+let+` is not a helper and publishes no termination guarantee, so this rule is not asked of it: it MAY call a `+partial+` helper. That is not a boundary the walk stops at — it is a declaration the walk is never asked about, and a helper between the behavior and the `+partial+` one still needs the word.
* A `+partial+` helper MUST NOT be written where a value goes. It may be applied; it may not be handed over. A function type (<<fn-value-semantics>>) says nothing about termination, so a `+partial+` helper stored in one leaves the call graph and arrives somewhere the rule above cannot see it. Writing the name in an argument, or anywhere else a function value is expected, is a compile error (E2001). A `+let+` with no parameter list is a value rather than a function and is not covered by this: reading it is reaching it, which the rule above already answers.
* Across a module boundary the answer is the word on the declaration being called. A published helper's body travels as source (<<exposed-values>>), but a reader does not walk it: an imported helper written without `+partial+` promises that nothing it reaches is `+partial+`, because the module that published it was held to the same rule. A jar built under a different boundary version is refused rather than believed.
* An `+example+` is evaluated within a time budget, so a recursion that does not terminate is reported rather than hanging the compiler.
* A local lambda (`+let f = (x) -> ...+`) cannot recurse. Recursion is a property of a named module helper.
* Two `+let+`s MUST NOT be written for one behavior.
* [[an-implementing-let-takes-its-shape-from-the-behavior]]A `+let+` implementing a behavior takes its shape from that behavior: how many parameters it has, what they are, the order the injected ones arrive in, and what it answers with. None of them is written again on the `+let+`, and none of them may disagree.

[[a-composition-has-no-let]]A `+>->+` composition is its own implementation, so no `+let+` may implement one.

A `+let+` may implement a behavior only when both are in the *same module*. A behavior of another module may be imported and called, but its implementation MUST NOT be given by a `+let+` in another module. This is the line that keeps the name-binding rule from conflicting with separate compilation.
* A helper `+let+` may construct data, but its construction set is inferred transitively and MUST be included in the calling behavior's `+constructs+` (<<constructs>>, <<blocks>>) — except for what another module's published body carried in, which that module already stated (<<exposed-values>>). A helper cannot be placed in a `+>->+` stage, because it does not appear in the spec DSL. It may be written in `+exposing+`: what a module offers is not only its list of behaviors.
* A function type (`+(A) -> B+`) is an ordinary type and is written in a type position like any other (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>). What refuses one is the position's requirement, not the grammar: a data field, a newtype's base and a behavior's input and output require an external representation, and a function has none — including a function carried inside a `+List+` or a `+Map+` there (<<what-crosses-the-boundary-has-an-external-representation>>). A behavior output is a sum of business results, and a function is not a business result.
* A `+let+` may return a function (`+let adder (n: Int) = (x) -> x + n+`). An escaping function — a return value or a runtime choice — becomes a closure capturing free variables (<<blocks>>). If only passed or applied on the spot, it is expandable and no closure is needed. A helper's name written where a value goes is the function it names. A binding MAY state its type (`+let p: (Int) -> Bool = ...+`), and that is what settles the parameters of the lambda it binds. Where no type is written they are inferred from how it is applied (a local rule, distinct from global type inference, which the declarative design does not do — a behavior takes its type from the specification and a helper states its own, <<fn-declaration>>).

[#injected-behavior]
=== A behavior with no implementation — outside-world dependency

*If a behavior with only a type has no implementation and declares nothing to depend on*, its implementation is injected from Java.

Two things are written the same way, and the clause tells them apart. A behavior with no `+let+` that declares `+depends on+` takes those dependencies as arguments of a `+let+` (<<depends-on>>), so it is Souther's to implement; with no `+let+` it is one nobody has written (<<unwritten-behavior>>). An injection target is the one that declares nothing to depend on: it is what gets injected, and what a dependency is supplied to is the behavior that calls or composes it. It corresponds to the spec DSL's `+// depends:+` and `+// side effect:+`.

An implementation is either a same-named `+let+` (<<fn-declaration>>) or a right-hand `+>->+` composition (<<composition>>). A `+>->+` composition behavior has no `+let+` but does have an implementation, so it is not subject to injection. What is injected is only one that wrote a type and no `+let+`.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: // depends: clock
behavior now : () -> DateTime

// spec DSL: // depends: memberRepository
behavior findMember : (id: MemberId) -> Member | NoSuchMember | CorruptStoredData
    constructs NoSuchMember, CorruptStoredData
----

Output cases are domain outcomes only. `+NoSuchMember+` (not found) and `+CorruptStoredData+` (a stored value breaks a domain invariant) are business-meaningful results, so they are cases. A *platform failure* such as DB down is not placed here — the Java Binding throws an exception and Souther passes it through (<<java-impl-rules>>).

No keyword is needed. Having no implementation is what "provided from outside" means. The spec DSL also has no word for `+required+`; whether something is a dependency is only noted in a comment. This form keeps the DSL line as is.

`+constructs+` is required on a behavior with no implementation too (<<constructs>>). The declaration reads the same as when implemented in Souther. `+findMember+` creates two failures and does not create `+Member+`; `+Member+` is obtained by passing a value read from outside through the decoder. The factory of <<java-base-class>> distributes from here.

Typical uses: DB query, HTTP call, file I/O, clock, ID generation, message sending. The concrete APIs are not brought into the language core. The distinction between dependency (only reads) and side effect (changes) is documentation of intent and does not affect value composition.

Forgetting to write an implementation is not an error; that behavior simply becomes subject to injection. But a caller MUST write that name in `+depends on+` (<<depends-on>>), and doing so surfaces it in a `+bind+` argument (<<jvm-behavior>>). If there is no caller, it is not caught within the `+.sou+`.

Which behaviors get a `+let+` and which are injected is not decided mechanically from the DSL. The spec DSL's `+// depends:+` is a note, not an obligation, so its absence does not mean an internal implementation. The 1:1 of <<non-functional>> is a declaration correspondence: one DSL behavior line maps to one Souther behavior declaration. It does not decide the implementation form (`+let+` / injection / `+>->+` composition). The model author chooses the form using the DSL's `+// depends:+` and `+// side effect:+` as cues: a note suggests injection, an internal computation a `+let+`, a chain of state transitions a `+>->+`.

[#unwritten-behavior]
=== A behavior Souther is to implement and nobody has

A behavior that declares `+depends on+` and writes no `+let+` is one whose implementation is Souther's and has not been written. It is not an injection target: what it depends on is passed to it by its own `+let+` (<<depends-on>>), so there is nothing for Java to be handed and no base is generated for it (<<java-base-class>>).

[,souther]
----
behavior quote : (cart: Cart) -> Priced
    depends on price
----

This is the state a model written example-first passes through. The rows go down before the body, and until the `+let+` arrives they are recorded rather than evaluated (<<example-pending>>) — the same as the rows of a behavior with no `+let+` and no clause. Waiting is not an error and is not warned about; how many rows are waiting is reported by `+souther examples+`.

Its name exists. Another behavior MAY declare `+depends on+` on it, a module that imports it reads its declaration, and where each behavior's body comes from travels with the module rather than being worked out again from it — a reader with two states to sort three declarations into would take this one for an injection target and tell an importer it may hand an implementation in.

[[nothing-built-rests-on-an-unwritten-behavior]]What is refused is resting on it from something that is built here: a behavior with a `+let+` of its own that names it in `+depends on+`, or a `+>->+` composition that names it as a stage (<<e1627>>). Such a behavior holds it as an injected field and nothing can supply that field. Resting on it from another behavior that is itself unwritten is not refused: that graph is the specification, and the bodies arrive one at a time from the leaves.

[#java-base-class]
=== Java base-class generation

A behavior subject to injection (wrote a type, no `+let+`; <<injected-behavior>>) is generated as an *abstract base class*, and has a `+protected+` factory for each unit data among its output's cases and for each data its `+constructs+` names. The two come from different places on purpose: a unit is told from a decoded pass-through output by its shape alone, while a field-bearing one is not, so only the latter needs the clause to say it (<<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>). Whether it takes one input or several is orthogonal to injection, exactly as for a `+let+`-implemented behavior (<<jvm-behavior>>): a *single* input extends the unary `+Behavior<In, Out>+` (so it still composes with `+>->+`) and inherits its `+apply+`; *any other count* makes it a standalone abstract class with a typed `+apply(A, B, …)+` and no `+Behavior+` supertype. Two or more inputs are more than `+>->+` passes along, and a behavior taking none has nothing to receive, so neither can follow an arrow; both can still be a pipeline's first stage, which receives the pipeline's own arguments (<<sequential-composition>>). A zero-input behavior therefore declares `+apply()+` and is handed nothing — it produces rather than transforms. A Java implementation overrides that typed `+apply+` directly, so several inputs are passed as arguments rather than bundled into a record. A collection parameter keeps its `+java.util.List+` / `+Map+` / `+Set+` (or the runtime `+Option+`) type with its element type, not `+Object+`.

[,souther]
----
behavior findMember : (id: MemberId) -> Member | NoSuchMember | CorruptStoredData
    constructs NoSuchMember, CorruptStoredData
----

generates, conceptually:

[,java]
----
public abstract class FindMember implements Behavior<MemberId, FindMemberResult> {
    protected FindMember() {}
    protected final NoSuchMember      NoSuchMember()      { ... }   // only declared unit cases
    protected final CorruptStoredData CorruptStoredData() { ... }
    // apply(input) is inherited from Behavior (unimplemented). An implementation overrides it.
    // A platform failure such as DB down is not a case but an exception. The implementation throws; Souther passes it through.
}
----

Java `+extends+` this and implements `+apply+` (<<java-example>>). Success values (such as `+Member+`) are assembled by the decoder; failure cases are created by the inherited `+protected+` factory. Because a data's constructor is non-public, the implementation may be placed outside the generated package (in any package). An output case that is not a unit or that carries an invariant gets no factory and is obtained via the decoder.

This abstract base class is *always public*, regardless of whether it is in `+exposing+`, so that a Java implementation can `+extends+` it from any module; a behavior subject to injection is by definition implemented outside the module. `+exposing+` controls the exposure of domain types, but the injection base class is scaffolding for implementation and is exposed independently. Without it the injection example of <<example>> would not hold.

Because the base is public and the implementation overrides `+apply+` from outside the module, the implementation *writes* the behavior's input type and its output type. A type the module keeps to itself cannot be written there, and no raw-typed override stands in for it — the erased signature clashes with the one being overridden. So an injected behavior's input and output MUST be exposed by the module that declares them, whether or not the behavior itself is in `+exposing+` (<<exposed-surface>>).

[#injected-constructs-are-creatable]
When a data listed in `+constructs+` is not exposed with `+exposing+`, it is a compile error (E1305). A non-exposed data has no public decoder, so a Java implementation would have no way to create that value; if exposed, it can be created via the decoder (<<jvm-codec>>). Every entry is a non-unit data (<<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>), so this reaches all of them. A unit case of the output is creatable without being exposed, through the `+protected+` factory the base class carries for it.

What is confined is <<invariant-on-construct>>. An invariant-bearing data is always checked whether it goes through `+__construct+` or the decoder. So even if it can be created from outside, *no unvalidated value can be created*. A unit data has nothing to validate, so nothing is lost by allowing it to be created from outside.

The base is an ordinary JVM abstract class, so any JVM language implements it. Kotlin and Scala `+extends+`/`+:+` it and override `+apply+` as Java does; a collection parameter arrives as `+java.util.List+`/`+Map+`/`+Set+`, which a Kotlin or Scala collection or a Clojure persistent collection satisfies directly. Clojure extends the abstract base with `+proxy+` or `+gen-class+` (not `+reify+`, which is interface-only) and dispatches `+apply+` by arity; because the `+constructs+` factories are `+protected+`, a Clojure implementation reaches them through `+gen-class+`'s `+:exposes-methods+` (a `+proxy+` sees only public methods). The factories stay `+protected+` — that is the construction confinement, not an interop choice.

[#java-impl-rules]
=== Rules for Java implementations

A Java implementation returns a *domain outcome* — a success value (such as `+Member+`) and a declared failure case (such as `+NoSuchMember+` / `+CorruptStoredData+`).

A *platform failure* (DB down, network drop, HTTP failure — anything that is not a domain outcome) is not folded into a case but *MAY be thrown as an exception*. Souther does not make the exception a case but passes it through to the boundary (a generated `+>->+` pipeline or `+apply+` does not swallow the exception). The language (`+.sou+`) has no exceptions (<<out-of-scope>>), but a Java Binding that touches the outside world may throw. The boundary (the Java / framework side) receives and handles the exception — HTTP 503, a retry, a transaction rollback. A VM failure or an implementation bug also escapes as an exception (out of scope for guarantees).

Guidance for the distinction. A *domain outcome* (not found, insufficient stock, a stored value breaks a domain invariant — a business-meaningful result) is a case, appears in a domain type, and is subject to exhaustiveness checking. A *platform failure* (infrastructure unavailable or not responding) is an exception, does not pollute domain types, and is handled at the boundary as a cross-cutting concern. Folding DB-down into a case would make every DB-touching behavior's domain type carry an infrastructure case, and when the DB actually goes down there is no honest case value to return (one would have to fake "insufficient stock" or fabricate an infrastructure case). So the former is a case, the latter an exception.

The exception *type* is agreed between the boundary and the implementation. With jOOQ + Spring, the boundary catches `+org.springframework.dao.DataAccessException+` and maps it to 503. jOOQ's own `+org.jooq.exception.DataAccessException+` is not a subclass of Spring's type, so this assumes Spring's exception translation for jOOQ is in effect (Spring Boot's jOOQ auto-config adds it by default). An implementation that throws a checked exception (such as raw JDBC's `+SQLException+`) wraps it in a non-checked exception of the same `+DataAccessException+` family (e.g. `+DataAccessResourceFailureException+`) — not `+IllegalStateException+` or a bare `+RuntimeException+` (which would pass through a `+DataAccessException+`-typed handler and make an infrastructure failure look like a program bug). The handler catches this failure type narrowly, and an implementation bug (an exception) falls through to a general 500.

`+CorruptStoredData+` (a stored value breaks a domain invariant on decode) stays a domain case. Infrastructure worked and returned data, and that data breaking a domain rule is a statement about the *domain*. Whether it deserves a name is the model author's judgment; this rule forces neither.

[#set-invariant]
=== Set invariants are passed as input data

An invariant that a single object cannot decide (schedule overlap, uniqueness) MUST be stated by passing the set needed for the decision as input data, not as a hidden dependency. The side effect of reading existing applications is placed at the workflow boundary (a behavior with no implementation).

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: behavior checkScheduleOverlap = SameApplicantRequests AND Drafting -> Drafting OR ScheduleOverlap
data ScheduleOverlap

behavior checkScheduleOverlap : (
    sameApplicantRequests: List<TravelRequest>,
    request: Drafting
) -> Drafting | ScheduleOverlap
// neither is created: `Drafting` is the input returned as is, and `ScheduleOverlap` is a unit

// a behavior with no implementation (reads the outside)
behavior readRequestsOfApplicant : (applicant: EmployeeId) -> List<TravelRequest>
----

`+checkScheduleOverlap+` stays a pure judgment; the side effect is on the outer behavior with no implementation. This corresponds to the conclusion of SMDD's DDD trilemma (purity is not layer separation but making explicit which functions have side effects).

[#requirement-propagation]
=== Requirement propagation

Anything referencing a behavior with no implementation inherits its requirement. Requirements accumulate outward, toward the side that wrote the name. If a `+let+` calls a behavior with no implementation, it becomes the requirement of the behavior holding that `+let+`. It accumulates further onto a composition placing that behavior in a stage.

At the destination, whether to write or infer diverges (<<depends-on>>).

* a simple behavior with a `+let+`: *write* in `+depends on+`; receive it as a `+let+` argument
* a `+>->+` composition: *infer*; decided by the union of the stages' requirement sets

[,souther]
----
behavior loadAndConvert = findMember >-> toView // depends on not written
----

This requires the implementation of `+findMember+`. Composing several gives a union. A composition is not made to write it, because it is readable from the stage declarations; the spec DSL also attaches no `+// depends:+` to a composition.

A stage and a call differ in who owns the behavior. A `+>->+` composition *builds* its stages, so a stage's requirements become the composition's and accumulate outward. A `+let+` that calls a behavior *receives* it, so the behavior itself is the caller's requirement and its own requirements stay with it (<<calling-a-behavior>>). One behavior may be reached both ways; which a module writes says which relationship it means.

The call graph is static at every stage, so the requirement set is decided as a concrete set. That no variable enters is what makes this design hold: inference and declaration can be reconciled, and the composition union can be computed, because the set is concrete (<<core-semantics>>). A behavior reaching itself would leave nothing to build first, so it is a compile error (<<e1608>>).

This is unchanged by making a `+let+` higher-order.

[,souther]
----
let applyAll (xs: List<MemberId>, p: (MemberId) -> MemberLabel) = List.map(p, xs)
----

The requirement set of `+applyAll+` is empty. Whatever `+p+` requires is irrelevant, because `+p+` arrives already closed. Requirements are injected as constructor arguments (<<jvm-behavior>>) and enter at the moment `+PreApprove.bind(now)+` builds the implementation. The receiver need not know what it required. `+bind+` is the operation that partially applies that argument.

[#composition]
== behavior composition

[#sequential-composition]
=== Sequential composition `+>->+`

[[a-stage-after-the-first-takes-one-input]]Every stage after the first takes one input: what reaches it is the value the stage before answered with. A behavior taking more is called inside a stage rather than made one.

[[a-pipeline-composes-behaviors]]`+>->+` composes behaviors and nothing else. A decoder and an encoder are the edges the pipeline runs between (<<decoder>>, <<encoder>>), not stages within it.

`+>->+` connects behaviors. Of the previous stage's output cases, those the next stage's input type can receive flow to the next stage; the cases it does not receive are carried to the output (departing the mainline).

[,souther]
----
behavior handle = findMember >-> toResponse
----

The stage following `+>->+` is single-input (a 1-argument behavior). `+>->+` passes one value to the next stage, so the receiver cannot take two or more. To place a multi-argument behavior downstream, call it inline in the implementation or receive it with `+match+` instead of making it a stage.

*The first stage is not restricted.* The first receives the pipeline's own arguments, so any number is fine; the pipeline receives them as is. A behavior subject to injection is a first stage on the same terms as an implemented one (<<java-base-class>>): the pipeline inherits it as a requirement and calls it with those arguments.

[,souther]
----
// spec DSL: behavior reject = AwaitingPreApproval AND RejecterId -> PreApprovalRejected OR NotAuthorized
//           behavior returnToDraft = PreApprovalRejected -> Drafting
//           behavior rejectAndReturn = reject >-> returnToDraft
behavior rejectAndReturn = reject >-> returnToDraft
// rejectAndReturn : (AwaitingPreApproval, EmployeeId) -> Drafting | NotAuthorized
----

The rule of <<type-routing>> requires single input only for the right side; however many the left side takes is irrelevant to routing.

A multi-input pipeline is itself a multi-input behavior, so it cannot be placed downstream of another pipeline. It can be the first stage.

A stage is a behavior, not a `+let+`. `+>->+` is composition on the specification side, and the spec DSL writes it so. A helper `+let+` does not appear in the spec DSL and cannot be a stage.

Decode / encode are not behaviors but edges of the boundary and are not `+>->+` stages. At the boundary, decode first, pass only the successful domain value to the behavior pipeline, and emit the output with encode (<<decoder>>, <<encoder>>). `+>->+` composes only the domain world (behaviors and their sum outputs).

[#type-routing]
=== Type-routing composition rule

[#composition-stages-type-route]
Every stage of a `+>->+` composition MUST be able to receive what the stage before it answers with: of the previous output, the cases the next input accepts flow on and the rest depart, and a stage that accepts none of them routes nothing.

A pipeline carries the *mainline*. Only the mainline is passed to a stage; a case a stage did not receive departs the mainline and is *never passed to the remaining stages* of that pipeline. A departed case is piled onto the output.

Let the previous stage's output sum be `+O_f+` and the next stage's input type `+I_g+`. Of `+O_f+`, cases `+I_g+` can receive flow to the next stage; the remaining cases `+R+` depart the mainline.

[,text]
----
f : A -> B | R           g : B -> C           f >-> g : A -> C | R
f : A -> B | R           g : B -> C | S       f >-> g : A -> C | S | R
f : A -> B1 | B2         g : B1 | B2 -> C     f >-> g : A -> C
----

The same holds for three or more stages: *only the mainline passes to the next stage*.

[,text]
----
f : A -> B | C     g : B -> D     h : C | D -> E
f >-> g >-> h : A -> E | C
----

`+h+` can receive `+C+`, but `+C+` already departed the mainline at `+g+`, so it does not reach `+h+`. What reaches `+h+` is only the `+D+` that `+g+` produced. This is the Railway. "Failure" is not special-cased; an unconsumed case simply departs the mainline, and which case departs is decided by the composition partner (<<unmarked-sum>>).

The output sum is treated as an open error row: `+(C | R) | S+` is flattened and same-named cases folded. If no case of `+O_f+` matches `+I_g+`, composition is impossible (<<e1701>>).

That a case departed the mainline is remembered by the composition, and not forgotten by naming a midpoint.

[,souther]
----
behavior fg = f >-> g // mainline D, departed C
behavior x = fg >-> h // only mainline D passes to h; C stays departed. x : A -> E | C
----

`+fg+` remembers mainline `+D+` and departed `+C+`. In `+fg >-> h+`, only mainline `+D+` passes to `+h+`, and `+C+` stays departed and is carried to the output. So a named-midpoint `+fg >-> h+` is the same `+A -> E | C+` as the one-line `+f >-> g >-> h+`.

`+>->+` is associative. Hoisting a sub-pipeline by name or regrouping does not change the result.

The mainline/departed split is composition plumbing, not a marker on values (<<unmarked-sum>>). The plumbing unwinds at both ends of the pipeline. Viewing `+fg+` alone, its output is the folded sum `+D | C+`, and the external representation is as before. Even if the same type of case appears in both mainline and departed, the plumbing remembers which rail, so they are not mistaken.

When a downstream stage receives only some cases, or you want to branch per case, open branches with `+match+` instead of `+>->+`.

[,text]
----
match submitTravelRequest(request) with
    | Submitted as s   -> ...
    | AwaitingPreApproval as p -> ...
----

[#composition-with-requirements]
=== Composition with outside-world dependencies

A behavior's internal representation is:

[,text]
----
Behavior<Requirements, Input, Output>
----

`+Output+` is the sum of possible business results, with no fourth slot separating failure. Composition rule:

[,text]
----
f : Behavior<R1, A, O_f>
g : Behavior<R2, B, O_g>        // I_g = B receives cases of O_f
f >-> g : Behavior<R1 ∪ R2, A, (O_g) | (sum of O_f minus the consumed part)>
----

`+R1+` and `+R2+` are what the stages declared (<<depends-on>>). The composition's `+R1 ∪ R2+` is inferred by the composition; a composition does not write `+depends on+`. Whether an implementation is internal or Java-side does not affect value composition. `+Requirements+` (dependencies needed) and `+Output+` (the sum of results produced) are composed independently: the former by set union, the latter by consumption / propagation.

`+Output+` consumption / propagation splits into the *mainline* passing downstream and the cases that departed unconsumed. The composition carries this split, so, like the `+R1 ∪ R2+` set union, `+>->+` is associative (<<type-routing>>). The mainline / departed split stays in the composition plumbing and folds back into a sum when it exits as `+Output+`. The internal representation needs no fourth slot for failure.

[#single-case-composition]
=== Composition of single-case output

A behavior with a single-case output (`+-> A+`) can be seen as having no passed-through case (an empty failure row, `+Never+`). If a downstream stage receives `+A+`, it flows through. The user need not wrap it in anything. If `+guard ... else+` returned another case early, that case propagates as `+R+` of <<type-routing>>.

[#declared-composition-output]
=== Declaring and reconciling a composition's output

A `+>->+` composition's output is inferred from its stages (<<composition-with-requirements>>). A spec DSL composition line writes no output (`+behavior placeOrder = validateOrder >-> ...+`), so the default is this inference.

An inferred output *changes silently on a distant change* when a stage is a behavior of another module. If an upstream stage adds a case, the composition output grows silently; if a downstream stage narrows its input, a previously processed case moves to departed (<<type-routing>>) and likewise increases the output. The composition's definition site has nothing to signal this.

[#composition-output-agrees-with-inference]
So a composition *MAY optionally declare its output*. If written, it MUST *exactly match* the inferred output or it is a compile error (E1604). Neither too few (a case appears that is not declared) nor too many (a declared case does not appear) is admitted.

[,souther]
----
behavior placeOrder = validateOrder >-> priceOrder >-> allocateStock -> PlacedOrder | OutOfStock
----

`+-> PlacedOrder | OutOfStock+` is the composition's declaration, not the output of the last stage `+allocateStock+`. Because a stage is a behavior reference (name only), the trailing `+->+` reads unambiguously as the composition's. Writing this line makes an error appear here when an upstream stage adds a case, so it does not grow silently downstream.

Input is not declared. A pipeline is point-free, and the input is decided by the first stage (<<sequential-composition>>). Only the output may be declared. A dependency (`+depends on+`) has the same distant-change risk, but a composition leaves it inferred (<<depends-on>>).

[#exposed-composition-declares-its-output]
For an internal composition this declaration is optional. But for a composition exposed with `+exposing+` it is *required*, written not on the definition line but in the exposing list as `+name : cases+` (<<modules>>). Leaving a public boundary to inference means that when an upstream stage adds a case, it reaches another compilation unit (another module, or a non-exhaustive Java switch or JSON serialization) silently, and the definition site has nothing to signal it. Placing a signature in `+exposing+` makes the composition's definition error (E1604) the moment a case is added, and the author chooses to handle the case or update the signature. If no signature exists, it is E1605. A non-composition behavior writes its type at the definition, so it MUST NOT write a signature in `+exposing+` (doing so is E1605). This division corresponds to.

[#case-propagation]
== behavior case propagation and Decoder error accumulation

A behavior's output sum is treated as an open error row; a case the downstream does not consume in a `+>->+` propagates through to the tail (<<type-routing>>). This is the composition style of the domain world.

Decode-side error accumulation is handled by the peripheral library Raoh. A derived Object Decoder reads each field from the key of the same name and collects all failures of independent fields (Applicative).

[,text]
----
{ "name": ..., "email": ..., "age": ... }
// read name / email / age with each field's derived Decoder and accumulate all independent failures (Raoh)
----

A derived Decoder's field accesses are mutually independent and collect all failures. Accumulated failures aggregate into Raoh's `+Result+` failure (`+Issues+`). In a custom boundary codec (<<custom-codec>>), one access may depend on a preceding result; from that point it becomes sequential (monadic), and a subsequent access is not run if the preceding one fails.

Accumulation (applicative) is inside the decode boundary; case propagation is behavior composition. They belong to different layers. Because decode is not a `+>->+` stage, the two never mix on the same sum (<<sequential-composition>>). Accumulation holds because a Decoder's field accesses are mutually independent (do not depend on a prior value).

[#examples]
== Examples

An `+example+` attaches compile-time-checked test rows to a behavior. It is evaluated as part of compilation: a row that does not hold is a compile error (<<e1905>>), so a stale example cannot survive a build. An `+example+` is not exposed and produces no run-time class.

[,souther]
----
example submitTravelRequest
    | "within the ceiling it submits"
        : (Drafting { applicant = EmployeeId("e-001"), estimatedCost = Amount(50000) })
        -> Submitted
    | "over the ceiling waits for pre-approval"
        : (Drafting { applicant = EmployeeId("e-001"), estimatedCost = Amount(100001) })
        -> AwaitingPreApproval
----

What a row is held to, and what holding a build to it requires:

* [[example-target-is-a-behavior]]the target MUST be a behavior of the module the example is attached to;
* [[example-holds]]an evaluated row's result MUST match what it expects;
* [[example-does-not-reach-unreachable]]a row's evaluation MUST NOT reach an `+unreachable+` (<<unreachable>>): either the row states a combination the model rules out, or the reason written there does not hold;
* [[example-evaluation-stays-within-budget]]a row's evaluation MUST come to a value within the counted budget it is held to — the points it may pass and the depth a recursion may go to;
* [[example-evaluation-stays-within-stack]]and it MUST reach that counted depth limit before the JVM stack runs out, the two being set so the counted one is met first;
* [[example-evaluation-answers]]the work around the counted evaluation — loading a class, decoding a value, comparing two of them — MUST answer within the wait the policy allows. Unlike the two above, this says nothing about the model.

An `+example+` names a target behavior, then lists one or more `+|+`-led rows. A row is an optional name (a string, followed by `+:+`), an input argument tuple, `+->+`, and an expected result — or `+<?>+`, which says the answer is owed and has not been written (<<example-answer-owed>>). `+example+`, `+examples+`, and `+for+` are contextual keywords, not reserved words, so they do not collide with an identifier or the `+example.*+` namespace.

[#example-row-name]
=== A row's name

The string a row is written with is its name, and a name is what says which row is meant from somewhere the row is not written: a report line two runs are compared on, a test that prepares what one row needs.

[[a-row-name-is-unique-within-its-behavior]]A name written on a row MUST be carried by no other row of that behavior, over the module's own source and every `+examples for+` file attached to it (<<example-placement>>). Every row carrying a name more than one row carries is reported, each in the file it is written in (<<e1925>>). Which of them is the one to write differently is not decided here: two rows of one behavior are what they are whichever order a compile was handed its files in.

A string with nothing in it is not a name, and a row written with one is refused where it is written (<<e2304>>). A row written without a name is held to none of this. It can be shown — as which of its behavior's rows in that file it is — and it cannot be named from outside, which is the whole of what writing no name means.

Editing a name renames the row. What was written elsewhere against the old name no longer says anything about this row, the same way a renamed behavior no longer answers to its old name.

[#example-expected]
=== The expected result

The expected side asserts at one of two granularities.

[[example-expected-arm-is-an-output-case]]Where a row expects an arm, that arm MUST be one of the target behavior's output cases.

* A bare type name (`+-> Submitted+`) asserts only the *arm* — which case of the output sum the result is — and ignores its fields.
* A construction (`+-> Rejected { reason = "high_cost" }+`), a newtype application (`+-> Amount(0)+`), or a literal asserts the *whole value* by structural equality.

The cases a row may name are the output's cases whether the output is written as a case list (`+-> Submitted | Rejected+`) or under the sum's own name (`+-> Verdict+`, <<sum-data>>) — a case stands where a value of its sum is expected here as everywhere else. A sum among those cases contributes its own cases, so a row names a leaf. The sum's name is not itself an expected arm: it says which cases are possible, not which one this input yields (<<e1904>>).

Types make totality and the case set exhaustive (<<match>>, <<unmarked-sum>>); an `+example+` fixes the value rule for a representative input — which arm, or which value, a given input yields.

[#example-answer-owed]
=== A row whose answer is owed

A row may be written `+<?>+` where its expected result goes. What it says is that these inputs are worth asking about and nobody has written down what the system answers for them. It is one token, written in this position and nowhere else: `+< ? >+` is not it, and a value position that reads it is a parse error.

[,souther]
----
example submitTravelRequest
    | "over the ceiling"
        : (Drafting { applicant = EmployeeId("e-001"), estimatedCost = Amount(100001) })
        -> <?>
----

Such a row runs like any other and asserts nothing. Its inputs are built, its stand-ins are read, the behavior is applied, and what the behavior answered is held to what it declares of its answer (<<an-implementation-keeps-what-the-behavior-states>>). Two things do not happen, both for the same reason. What the row states is not held to an `+ensures+` (<<a-row-keeps-what-the-behavior-states>>), because it states no answer for a clause to be read against; and the answer is not compared, because there is nothing written to compare it with. So the row says nothing about the value the model yields.

A module whose rows are still owed answers goes on compiling. `+souther examples --generate+` writes the rows it composes with `+<?>+` in this position, so a block is pasted whole and answered a row at a time rather than held out of the file until the last of it is written.

[[every-row-has-its-answer-written]]Every row MUST have its answer written (<<e1934>>). The row is written down and its answer is not, so this is read off the source and nothing about how far a run got bears on it — a behavior with no arms at all owes it, and so does a row at an arm another row already covers.

What such a row does not do is cover. An arm whose only rows are owed answers is an arm nothing states the behavior's answer at, so it is reported too, and under the same rule rather than as an arm no row goes through (<<e1918>>): a row does go through it. Nothing composes a second row for such an arm — the question is already written down, and what is left is an answer no search can supply.

[#example-evaluable]
=== What can carry an example

[#example-target-is-evaluable]
The target MUST be something this module can run: a behavior with a `+let+` implementation, or a `+>->+` composition of them. A helper `+let+` is not one.

[[example-fixtures-are-buildable]]Every fixture a row writes MUST build at the position it supplies. An input builds against its parameter type: a row's operand is compiled as one of this module's own definitions and run in the program the behavior is applied in, so what holds it to that type is the language's own typing, and its invariant is read where its construction runs. The expected result builds as the value the row wrote, which may be a case the behavior does not answer with — that is a disagreement the row reports (<<e1904>>) rather than a fixture that cannot be built. A `+with+` value or a `+fake+` output MUST be a value of the type the dependency answers with (<<example-fakes>>): it *is* that dependency's answer while the row runs, so there is nothing about it for the row to disagree with. The expectation is built before the behavior is applied.

[[fixture-is-written-not-carried]]A fixture MUST be written the way Souther writes the value, not the way a boundary carries it. A derived Decoder reads the representation a value arrives in from outside — a date-time as ISO text over JSON, a temporal out of a JDBC row — and a fixture goes through no Decoder at all: it is written in the source and compiled as source, so what a boundary additionally reads is not a second way to write one. So `+"2026-07-25T09:00"+` is a `+String+` wherever it is written, and a `+DateTime+` is `+DateTime("2026-07-25T09:00")+` (<<temporal-literal>>); a newtype over one takes the value it wraps, `+貸出日(Date("2026-07-25"))+`, and a `+?+` field holds the value itself, so it holds one there too. This is a rule about the form and not about the text, so it holds of a value a helper answered with (<<example-evaluable>>) as much as of one written out: a helper returning a `+String+` where a temporal stands has not written one. A fixture whose position's type does not admit it is refused where the same value written in a body is (<<e1812>>, <<e1317>>), wherever it stands. An expected value is not one the model is given: a row writing `+"2026-07-25T09:00"+` where a `+DateTime+` comes out has written a `+String+`, and that the two are of different types is the disagreement the row reports (<<e1905>>).

[[fixture-names-what-it-supplies]]A fixture the model is given MUST be a value of the position it is written at, and MUST say so itself. This is the language's own rule about types and not one examples have of their own: at a `+data AmountN = Int+`, `+1+` is an `+Int+` and `+AmountN(1)+` is an `+AmountN+` wherever either is written, and a row is one of the places either may be written.

A nominal position takes one of the names it holds. A newtype takes its own name applied to what it wraps, and one wrapped in another takes both — `+DecisionNN(DecisionN(Approved { … }))+`; a record takes its own construction, so a record of the same fields under another name is not one; a position typed by a sum takes any of its cases (<<sum-data>>). A position that is a primitive or a collection takes a value under no name at all, which is the same rule read the other way: an `+AmountN+` is one wherever it is written, so `+AmountN(1)+` at an `+Int+` is refused as `+1+` at an `+AmountN+` is. An optional takes what it holds, or `+None+` (<<optional>>), and `+None+` stands where an optional makes room for it and nowhere else: a position that holds a value takes one, whatever its Decoder would make of a missing one — a unit case's reads nothing and would answer with the case the row never wrote.

Where a helper is applied, what it answers with is what says what it supplied — so a helper answering `+Int+` does not supply an `+AmountN+`, and one answering `+List<Int>+` does not supply a `+List<AmountN>+`. Whether that type was declared or settled from the body is not a difference a reader of the call can see, and no rule here turns on it. The rule reaches inside what it answered with, because a position's element type is part of what the position is.

Where a field is taken, the declaration of the field taken is what says what it supplied: its declared type, and not the value found there. A field declaring a `+List<AmountN>+` supplies one wherever it is read, and an empty one supplies it too — which is the case the value could not have answered, an empty collection having no element to name. Where the value a field is taken off is one a helper answered with, what a helper was declared to answer with is not read, so what says what it supplied is the value it answered with, as it is wherever a helper stands. Which field may be taken is still the declaration's, read from what that value is.

This holds at every position a value stands at — a parameter, a record field, a collection element, a map key, an argument of an applied helper, the value under a newtype — and not only the outermost: `+Order { amount = 1 }+` states no `+AmountN+` at the field that takes one. A `+...+` spread is the one form that states no value of the type being written: it supplies the fields of a value of another type (<<field-spread>>), which is how one record is written from another, and the positions inside it are written the way any other is.

An *expected* value is not one the model is given and is not held to this rule. A row may state what the behavior does not answer with, and reporting that disagreement is what the row is for (<<e1905>>), so an expected value is compared rather than held to its position. A value the model is given that states no value of its position is refused as it is anywhere else the language reads a value of that type.

[[expected-is-built-as-written]]An expected value MUST be built as the value the row wrote, at every position and not only the outermost. The position it stands at says what the text does not — whether `+[ ]+` is a list, a set or a map — and says nothing about what type a value there is: `+Receipt { total = 1 }+` states an `+Int+` at a field declaring an `+AmountN+`, and a behavior answering `+Receipt { total = AmountN(1) }+` did not answer with it. What a construction's parts are is that construction's to say — a newtype's argument is the base it wraps, a field is what its record declares — so a value states its own type wherever it stands. Where the two sides differ by their type rather than by their contents, that is what the row is told, at the position they part (<<e1905>>). A value that states nothing of its own type is refused before anything is compared: `+AmountN("x")+` is <<e1317>> because an `+AmountN+` wraps an `+Int+`, which is what the same construction written in a body is. A newtype's invariant is read where the construction runs, whether what it wraps was written out or answered by a helper, and a row whose expectation breaks one states nothing to compare (<<e1903>>).

What a row wrote is more than the names in it. The form states which collection it is where it says so — `+Set.fromList([ 1 ])+`, `+Set.empty+`, and a helper answering with a `+Set+` state a set, while `+[ 1 ]+` and `+[ ]+` state neither and take the answer's — that a map's keys are distinct, and that a record has a value for every field it has. A row writing a set where a list came out has written a value of another type (<<e1905>>); a construction with no value for a field that is not optional is refused as it is in a body (<<e1005>>), and two entries under one key are `+Map.fromList+`'s to resolve there as anywhere, so the map the row states is the one it resolves to. Leaving a field out states `+None+` and states only that (<<optional>>).

A behavior with a `+let+` implementation is evaluated (<<fn>>), and so is a `+>->+` composition (<<composition>>). A behavior with no `+let+` has nothing to run, so its rows are *recorded* rather than evaluated (<<example-pending>>) — whether Java is to supply the body (<<injected-behavior>>) or Souther is and nobody has (<<unwritten-behavior>>). A target that is not a behavior at all — a helper `+let+` of that name — is <<e1902>>. A behavior's `+depends on+` dependencies are supplied by fakes at the example (<<example-fakes>>).

A composition's row is written like any other. Its arguments are the first stage's (<<sequential-composition>>), and its expected result is any case of the composition's output — including one that departed the main line at an earlier stage (<<type-routing>>), which is the part no stage's own example can state, because the routing is not any stage's rule.

[,souther]
----
behavior winOrder = settleQuote >-> makeOrder -> Order | BelowFloor

example winOrder
    | "meeting the floor wins the order"
        : (Negotiating { floor = Amount(100) }, Amount(200))
        -> Order
    | "below the floor it stops here"
        : (Negotiating { floor = Amount(100) }, Amount(50))
        -> BelowFloor { floor = Amount(100), offered = Amount(50) }
----

An input and an expected value are fixtures: literals, newtype applications, record constructions, and helpers applied to those. A `+?+` field takes a value directly (wrapped in `+Some+`) or `+None+` for empty, the same way a `+let+` body writes an optional (<<optional>>); omitting the field reads as `+None+` too. A row's argument is compiled as this module's code and run, so a fixture that breaks an invariant is rejected (<<e1903>>) — by the construction that carries it, as one in a body is.

A row may also *name a value* (<<fn-declaration>>) rather than write the whole input again, and may spread one to vary a field. A spread copies the fields of the value it names — one another module published as readily as one of this module's own, since a published value crosses closed — and the case is the one the construction writing it names; a name on its own stands for the case the value it denotes constructs. So a fixture in a position typed by a sum says which case it is however it is written.

[,text]
----
let acmeLead = QualifiedLead { companyName = CompanyName("Acme"), budgetFixed = true, ... }

example convert
    | "a fixed budget converts" : (acmeLead) -> Converted
    | "an unfixed budget does not convert" : (QualifiedLead { ...acmeLead, budgetFixed = false }) -> NotConverted
----

What a row may name is a property of the value graph, not of one definition's text: a value is *fixture-evaluable* when its body is a literal, a construction, a spread, `+Set.fromList+` / `+Map.fromList+` over one, `+Map.empty+` / `+Set.empty+`, a helper applied to those, or a reference to another fixture-evaluable value. A field taken off a fixture-evaluable value is one: where `+e+` is fixture-evaluable and its type declares a field `+f+`, `+e.f+` is fixture-evaluable, and a newtype declares `+value+`, so what a newtype wraps is read the same way. The rule is recursive on its own conclusion, so a field taken off a field is one. So a chain of values holds even though each link names the next. A value that reaches itself has no fixture to be, and is reported as the cycle it is.

A row may also *apply a helper* — the rule the row is about — wherever a fixture value is written: an input, an expected result, a `+with+` value, a `+fake+` table's inputs and outputs, a record field, a list element, a map key or value, and an argument of another helper application. The helper is *run*, as the module that compiled it runs it. The value it returns is the value the module's own code produced, standing where the application was written: nothing is read back into another form, because what encloses the application is compiled around it. In an expected value it is the value it answered with wherever it stands, since a helper produced a value of its own type and an expected value is built as what the row wrote (<<expected-is-built-as-written>>). Which case that value is comes from the value rather than from the text. Its arguments are fixtures like any others, so a fixture that breaks the argument type's invariant is <<e1903>>.

[,souther]
----
let taxIncluded (amount: Amount) = Amount(amount.value * 110 / 100)

example bill
    | "billAtTaxInclusivePrice" : (taxIncluded(Amount(100))) -> Bill { total = Amount(110) }
----

Without this a row restates what the rule computes — `+Amount(110)+` where it means "the taxed price of 100" — and a change to the rule leaves a row that is wrong and still passes.

A fixture applies the one instance the call it wrote determines. Where a declaration carries type variables, the arguments the row wrote settle them — `+List.length([1, 2])+` settles `+'a+` as `+Int+` — and the method the row runs is emitted at that instance, as an expansion is monomorphized at a call site (<<type-variables>>). A variable no argument settles is settled as it is in a body, an empty collection carrying no element to name: `+List.isEmpty([ ])+` answers as it answers anywhere it is written.

A function with a parameter that declares no type cannot be applied in a fixture, because the type it takes comes from the call sites the helper is expanded into, and a fixture is not one (<<e1903>>). One whose body produces a function answers no value, and is refused where a block that escapes is refused anywhere (<<e1809>>). How a function is implemented does not enter: a standard-library kernel is applied like any other function a row names, and the method it is applied through is emitted for the row that applies it. `+Map.empty+` and `+Set.empty+` are values and not functions (<<fn-declaration>>), so nothing is applied and nothing has to run: the name is the empty collection.

Building the fixtures, running the helpers they apply, applying the behavior and comparing the result are one evaluation under one time budget, so a row cannot buy more time by applying more helpers.

`+Set.fromList+` and `+Map.fromList+` are admitted because a value is ordinary code, where a list literal is a `+List+` whatever the position declares, while a row writes a set as its elements and a map as its entry pairs.
