They are the forms that notation stands for, so one record serves as both a value and a row. `+Map.empty+` and `+Set.empty+` are admitted for the same reason: they are what a body writes for the empty collection a row writes `+[ ]+`, and a row that could not name them would refuse the spelling the body it is exampling uses.

A collection is written as a list, and its declared type says what the list means: a `+List+` keeps its order and repeats, a `+Set+` drops duplicates on decode (`+labels = [ Label("bug"), Label("bug") ]+` is one label), and a `+Map+` takes its entries as `+(key, value)+` pairs — `+counts = [ ("bug", 2), ("ui", 1) ]+` — the form `+Map.fromList+` takes (<<stdlib-map>>). A temporal takes a written one (`+Date("2026-07-25")+`, `+Time("09:00")+`, `+DateTime("2026-07-25T09:00")+`, `+Instant("2026-07-25T00:00:00Z")+`; <<temporal-literal>>). This is what lets a rule over dates, sets, or maps be fixed by its examples rather than only by a hand-written test at the boundary.

This holds wherever a value is written, not only inside a record: a behavior taking `+(lines: List<Line>)+` is exampled with `+([ Line { quantity = 1 } ])+`, one returning a `+Map+` is expected as its pairs, and a fake's value is written the same way. A collection does not need a data wrapped around it to reach an example. A map keyed by a newtype (<<collections>>) writes its keys as they are constructed (`+[ (ProductId("A-1"), 3) ]+`), and each key goes through that type's invariant like any other fixture.

When a row does not hold, the diagnostic (<<e1905>>) shows what the behavior produced against what the row expects, both encoded through the data's derived Encoder (<<encoder>>) so the two read against each other.

[#example-fakes]
=== Faking a dependency

[[example-dependency-has-a-usable-fake]]Every dependency required to evaluate a row MUST have a usable fake: one supplied, by a `+with+` on the row or a `+fake+` table, and one whose fixture builds as a value of what that dependency answers with.

[[a-stand-in-names-a-behavior]]A `+fake+` and a `+with+` MUST name a behavior in scope (<<e1932>>). Which behavior is settled by resolving the name, as it is wherever a behavior is named (<<qualified-reference>>), so what a table stands in for is decided once and not worked out again from its spelling.

What that behavior is *implemented* by is not required to be anything. A stand-in is a written statement about what a behavior answers, and it stays one when the behavior it names turns out to have a body: a model written example-first passes through a state where the `+let+` has not arrived, and the table beside the rows goes on being read against them afterwards (<<stand-in-and-recorded-row-agree>>). A table nothing depends on and no row records an answer for is built all the same, because it is written.

[[fake-answers-the-inputs-it-is-asked]]A `+fake+` table MUST answer every input it is asked for, by listing it or by carrying a `+_+` default row.

[[fake-table-builds]]Building a `+fake+` table MUST come to a value within the budget and the wait it is held to; where it does not, the table states nothing that was checked.

[[every-row-of-a-fake-table-can-answer]]Every row of a `+fake+` table MUST be one the table's dispatch can answer with. A row stating what an earlier row states is never the first match, and a `+_+` row followed by another `+_+` is never the one a table falls through to; either is a statement the author is making and nothing reads, and is reported at the row that answers nothing, naming the row that answers instead (<<e1926>>). Which of a pair is the dead one is the dispatch's to say and is not the order they are written in: of two rows stating one input the later answers nothing, and of two `+_+` rows the earlier does.

To evaluate a behavior that `+depends on+` an injected dependency (<<depends-on>>), the example supplies a fake for it. Best is to need none: an outside-world value written as an *input* rather than an injected behavior keeps the behavior example-able with no fake (<<purity>>). When a dependency stays injected, a fake stands in, scoped to the example and never shipped (no run-time class is produced).

A *value dependency* — one whose faked result is a constant — is given on the row with `+with+`. Its value is a fixture and is written like one: as a value of the type the dependency answers with (<<example-fixtures-are-buildable>>), so a `+now+` answering `+DateTime+` is stood in for by `+DateTime("…")+` and not by the string a boundary would carry one as (<<fixture-is-written-not-carried>>).

[,text]
----
example preApprove
    | (AwaitingPreApproval { ... }, EmployeeId("boss-1"))
        with now = DateTime("2026-07-20T09:00") -> PreApproved
----

A *function dependency* — one whose result varies with its input — is given by a `+fake+` declaration: an input→output table matched by value equality, defaulting to a `+_+` row or, absent one, a miss error (<<e1909>>). A multi-input injected dependency (<<java-base-class>>) is faked the same way, with the row's inputs matched as a tuple.

[,souther]
----
fake findManager
    | (EmployeeId("e-1")) -> EmployeeId("boss-1")
    | _                   -> EmployeeId("unknown")
----

Each dependency of the target must have a fake — a `+with+` on the row or a `+fake+` table — or it is a compile error (<<e1908>>). A fake, like an example, is written inline or in an attached `+examples for+` file. `+fake+` is a contextual keyword.

The stand-in is local to the module that writes it; the behavior it stands in for need not be. A module declaring a requirement, another importing it and carrying it through its own `+depends on+`, and a `+fake+` where that module's rows are run is one construct, and the fake names the dependency the way a `+depends on+` clause names it: bare where the scope settles it, or qualified through the module that declares it (<<qualified-reference>>).

[,souther]
----
fake catalog.currentPrice
    | (Sku("a-1")) -> Price(10.00m)
    | _            -> Price(0.00m)
----

Writing one does not put a fake into the other module: nothing is added to what that module declares or emits, and no other module sees this table. What the name settles is which behavior is stood in for, so a module declaring a behavior of the same spelling as an imported one has two, and a stand-in written here answers the one it names. A row of the table is held to what the *declaring* module states about that behavior (<<e1929>>), by the check that module emitted.

[[a-behavior-has-at-most-one-stand-in-table]]A behavior MUST be named by at most one `+fake+` block, over the module's own source and every `+examples for+` file attached to it (<<example-placement>>). Two targets name one behavior when resolution denotes one, however either is written. Every block naming a behavior another names is reported, each in the file it is written in (<<e1933>>); which of them is the one to write differently is not decided here, as it is not for two rows carrying one name (<<a-row-name-is-unique-within-its-behavior>>).

The rows of a block are one table and are read in the order they are written, and a module's source and the files attached to it have no order between them. So two blocks are not rows of one table, where two `+example+` blocks are rows of one behavior: what an `+example+` writes is a set of statements, and what a `+fake+` writes is what the dependency answers, decided row by row.

Where more than one block names a behavior, none of them establishes what stands in for it. An example row that would require that stand-in does not run the behavior and states nothing about an answer from it, in the same way as a row whose stand-in table could not be built.

A `+fake+` table is built because it is written. Each block whose target names a behavior is built once, where it is written, whether or not any row reads it and whether or not it is the block that stands in for that behavior — a block naming nothing has no declaration to be built against, and is refused where its name is read (<<e1932>>): a module may write a table nothing depends on and no row records an answer for, and what is wrong with such a table is wrong wherever it is read from. A table with a row that will not build is an error at the fake (<<e1908>>), said once however many rows would have read it; a table that does not finish being built is a warning there (<<e1921>>). A row that would have stood in with a table that will not build does not run, and says nothing of its own about the table.

A composition's dependencies are its stages' (<<composition-with-requirements>>), so a row exampling one supplies a fake for each of those. That holds whichever path the row takes: an input that departs the main line at the first stage never reaches the second, and the second stage's dependency is faked all the same. What a row has to supply is a property of the composition, not of the input this row happens to use. One dependency two stages share is one fake — the composition holds one of it (<<composition-with-requirements>>) — and the diagnostic names the stage that wants it (<<e1908>>).

[#example-pending]
=== A row waits for the implementation it is about

[[stand-in-and-recorded-row-agree]]A stand-in and a recorded row MUST NOT state different answers for one input. Which of the two the model is to be held to is not readable from the text, so neither is named as the right one.

The two are statements about a behavior and need not be written in one module. A row records what a behavior owes where that behavior is declared (<<example-evaluable>>), and a table stands in for it where the rows it is run against are — so a requirement carried across a module boundary has its two statements in two files of two modules, and they are read against each other all the same. Which module reads them is the one that wrote the stand-in: it is what added the second statement, and its own reading already carries the classes of every module it reaches, so both values are built in one execution and compared by the language's own equality (<<equality>>). The report marks the recorded row and points at the stand-in, wherever each is written.

[[stand-in-comparison-completes]]Reading a `+fake+` against the rows recorded for that behavior MUST come to a value, so that what the two state was compared. Being told nothing was found and being told nothing was compared are different answers.

[[an-answer-is-of-the-module-being-evaluated]]What answers a behavior MUST be built against the module the rows are written for, and where that cannot be established the rows MUST NOT be run against it (<<e1927>>). An implementation supplied from outside a compile reads a row's values by the declarations it was built against, and a row handed to one built against another revision is decided by the two builds disagreeing rather than by the model: an invariant narrowed since refuses a value the model admits, a case added to a union arrives where there is no such case, a field renamed is not found under its name. Such a row is undecided rather than failing — everything it can be held to without being run has been, and what it would have decided was not found out.

What is compared is what a value crossing between the two depends on: which cases a union has, what a data holds, what an invariant admits, what a newtype wraps, what a behavior's signature is, which behaviors are left to be injected, and the `+let+`​s a declaration cannot be read without — whichever module published one, since a rule written where a type is declared and called by a reader's invariant is as much a part of what the reader's values are as one written beside it. Both builds' declarations are read the way this compiler reads any published module, so what is compared is what they mean rather than the words they were written with. A name that reaches a declaration is the declaration it reaches — a bare name an `+import+` brought in, a name written under an alias and one written out in full are one name (<<modules>>) — a name a declaration binds is the binding it is, so a binding renamed with its uses admits what it admitted, and a number is the number it is, so `+1.0m+` and `+1.00m+` are one bound. None of that is decided by the comparison: it is what the language already says, and asking it twice is how the two answers come to differ. What a field is called is compared as the word it is, because a decoder reads a value under that word.

Everything else may differ. A `+let+` body, an example, a `+fake+`, a published `+let+` no declaration is read through, an `+import+` line nothing compared is written with, the order two declarations are written in, and how any of it is spaced are not things a value can be read differently by, and a build that differs only in those is not another build for this purpose.

The modules held to are the ones those declarations reach into — read off the names they are written with, not off the import lines, since a type is reachable qualified whether or not it was imported and a module may import what only an unread `+let+` wanted. They are read from what this compile reads declarations of, which is what it generated and what its path carries.

Whether the two agree and whether it can be told are different answers. Classes carrying no declarations, and declarations this compiler cannot read, establish nothing about either build — the answer may well be of exactly this module — so they are reported as what they are rather than as a build that has moved, and the rows are not run either way. Which side could not be read is said with it: what the answer brought is that build's to fix, and what this compile reads is its own path's. This is not the boundary revision (<<a-published-module-agrees-with-this-compiler>>): two builds of two different models carry the same one, and it says which compiler wrote a module rather than which model it was.

A run whose answers are the ones the compile generated has nothing to establish. There is one build, so what applies a behavior is of the module being evaluated by being of this compile of it.

An injected behavior (<<injected-behavior>>) has no `+let+` to apply, so a row naming one is *recorded* rather than evaluated. It is not refused. A model that a system is being migrated onto is injected everywhere at the start, and the rows harvested from the system it replaces are the record of what each behavior will owe; refusing them would mean keeping that record somewhere the compiler cannot read, which is where it stops being checked.

What a row can be held to without a body, it is held to. Its arity, each input built against its parameter type — compiled as this module's code and run, so an input that breaks an invariant is <<e1903>> the day it is written — the expected arm against the output's cases (<<e1904>>), and the expected value built (<<e1903>>). What is left needs something to run.

Evaluation begins by itself. The row is unchanged when the `+let+` arrives; from that compile on it is applied and compared like any other, and a row that stated the wrong answer is <<e1905>> then rather than never.

An implementation supplied to a run is the other way it begins. Where something outside a compile answers the behavior, the row is applied and compared the same way — the same fixtures, the same arm resolution, the same comparison — and a row that stated the wrong answer is <<e1905>> there too. What the implementation answered is held to what the behavior declares of what it answers (<<an-implementation-keeps-what-the-behavior-states>>), which for an injected behavior is where that clause is checked at all. Whether such a run happens is not the model's to say and nothing about the row changes for it: what is recorded is what applied it, and the rows are the same rows either way.

A `+fake+` is not what a pending row wants. A fake stands in for a dependency while some *other* behavior's row runs (<<example-fakes>>); an `+example+` of an injected behavior says what that behavior itself will have to answer. Neither is run from the other, so both belong. An injected behavior declares no `+depends on+` of its own (<<depends-on>>), so a pending row needs no fake at all.

They are read against each other all the same. Where a stand-in and a recorded row answer one input differently, that is <<e1919>>.

Each side is read the way it is read where it is used. What a fake answers for an input is what the fake itself would answer — the first row stating that input, and otherwise the `+_+` row (<<example-fakes>>) — so a table's default is what a recorded row is held against wherever no explicit row states its input; and a table with a row that will not build answers nothing at all, being one no fake can stand in with. A recorded row is read as the evaluator reads it: its arity, its inputs built against their parameter types, and its expectation held against the output's cases (<<e1904>>) and then built. A row that fails any of those states nothing here, because it states nothing there.

A stand-in and an assertion are not the same kind of thing. A recorded row may name a case and say nothing under it (<<example-expected>>); a `+fake+` row and a `+with+` may not, because what they install is a value, and a case with fields is not one. So a `+with lookup = Missing+` where `+Missing+` has fields stands in for nothing (<<e1908>>) and there is nothing for a row to disagree with.

A `+with dep = value+` is read this way only where `+dep+` takes no input. A fake row states its own inputs, so a recorded input picks the row that answers it and nothing is evaluated in between. A `+with+` states none: it is bound to the run of the row it is written on, and what reaches the dependency there is whatever the parent behavior computes and passes. Held against every recorded row it would drop the parent row's own input, and two rows faking two answers for two different inputs — each right about its own — would read as contradicting each other. A dependency taking nothing has one input, `+()+`, so there the `+with+` and the row recorded for it are about the same call. A `+with+` takes precedence over a `+fake+` table while its own row runs, and that settles nothing here: the table remains a statement about the same behavior, written for every other row and every other run.

Neither side is ranked. A model being migrated onto may run against a stand-in while the real answer is still being harvested, which is written the way a mistake is; what is known is that the two disagree, and where each is written. So it is a warning, and it is said at both statements rather than at one of them.

[,souther]
----
behavior findMember : (id: MemberId) -> Found | Missing

example findMember
    | "a member that is registered" : (MemberId("m-1")) -> Found { id = MemberId("m-1") }
    | "a member that is not"        : (MemberId("m-9")) -> Missing { reason = "no such member" }
----

How many rows are waiting is what `+souther examples+` reports; it is not a compile warning, because waiting is the normal state of a model being written and a warning on every one of them says nothing.

[#example-ensures]
=== A written value is held to what the behavior states

A row and a `+fake+` row each write an input and the answer that goes with it, which is what an `+ensures+` relates (<<ensures>>). Where a clause is declared, the values written are held to it.

[[a-row-keeps-what-the-behavior-states]]The values an `+example+` row states MUST keep what the behavior declares of what it answers (<<e1928>>), and [[a-fake-row-keeps-what-the-dependency-states]]the values a `+fake+` row stands in with MUST keep what the faked behavior declares of what it answers (<<e1929>>). A declaration is what says what a behavior answers, and a row stating otherwise records something the model rules out.

[[an-implementation-keeps-what-the-behavior-states]]Where an implementation supplied to a run answers an `+example+` row of an injected behavior, what it answered MUST keep what that behavior declares of what it answers (<<e1930>>). A behavior with a body checks its own answer where it answers; an injected one has none, so what it answers is checked where it enters the domain, and applying it for a row is such an entry.

This is not the policy ADR-0093 declined. Two written statements disagreeing is a disagreement with no right side (<<stand-in-and-recorded-row-agree>>), which is why it is a warning naming both; a written value against a declaration is not two descriptions but a description held to what the model states, and the declaration decides.

It is the declaration's check that decides it, run over the values the row wrote. A row's operands are compiled and applied as the module's own code, so what a row states exists as a value while the module is compiled, and the same check that runs where the behavior answers is applied to it. Nothing reads the clause a second time and decides what it means. This is *compile-time fixture validation* and not a proof: what is established is that these values keep the clause, not that the behavior does — which is <<invariant-discharge>>'s distinction, on the other side of it.

A row waiting for an implementation is held all the same. The values are written whether or not anything applies the behavior, and a record of an answer the model rules out is a wrong record however long it waits (<<example-pending>>).

What is held is decided by what the row wrote, and an arm is part of what it wrote. A bare case name naming a unit case states the whole answer — a unit case has one value, so naming it is writing it — and such a row is held like any other. This is the same reading of an arm as <<ensures>>'s: an arm is a reference to the answer, so `+ensures NotFound -> id > 0+` is decided by a row stating an input and that arm, with no construction written.

Where only one side is written there is no relation to hold. A `+_+` row of a `+fake+` states no input, and a `+with+` supplies an answer for whatever it is asked; what either states is held where the behavior answers.

A table with a refused row answers nothing wherever else it is read. Nothing stands in with it (<<example-fakes>>), and it is not compared with the rows recorded for the behavior (<<stand-in-and-recorded-row-agree>>): that comparison names neither side as the right one, and this has already named one. The two cannot both be said about one pair.

Where a bare case name does not determine a value on its own, what the row states is the case and nothing under it, and the declaration is asked what that alone decides. A rule reading only the inputs is decided: its arm names the answer, and the row has said which case that is. A rule reading `+value+` is not, and stays undecided rather than being decided against a value nobody wrote — it is held where the behavior answers, as everything nothing wrote a value for is. Which rules a case decides is worked out where the declaration's check is emitted, so an arm naming a sum decides for each of the cases it has, and nothing here reads a clause to find out.

[#example-placement]
=== Inline or an attached file

[[examples-file-holds-only-examples]]An `+examples for+` file MUST hold only examples, the fakes they run against and the values their rows name, and MUST NOT declare a name the target module already declares.

[[examples-file-names-a-compiled-module]]An `+examples for <module>+` file MUST name a module among those being compiled.

[[an-attached-files-values-are-for-its-rows]]A value an attached file declares MAY be named by a row, by a `+fake+` and by another value of an attached file, and MUST NOT be named by the model: not by a `+data+`'s `+invariant+`, a `+behavior+`'s `+ensures+`, a `+let+`'s body, or the module's `+exposing+` list.

The base form is written inline, in the same source as the behavior. To keep fixtures out of the model source, examples may instead be written in an attached file that begins with `+examples for <module>+`. It holds the rows, the `+fake+` tables they run against, and the values they name; a data, a behavior, an `+import+` and a `+let+` with parameters belong in the module itself, and writing one there is <<e1906>>. The compiler merges an attached file into its target module; it is not a module of its own and needs no `+import+` (it sees the target module's names directly). Its values join the module the rows join, so a name the module already declares is <<e1906>> too — the rows would read that declaration and the one written here would say nothing. An attached file that names a module not being compiled is <<e1907>>.

The merge makes those values reachable under the same names as the module's own, and the direction they may be reached in is one way. A row is what the file is for, and a row written inline in the model source is a row too, so it names them as freely as one written beside them. The model is the other way: a module that named one would not compile without a file of fixtures, and a clause that named one would travel to an importer under <<a-published-module-carries-what-it-says-it-declares>> spelling a name the jar has no source for — the module's own source is what is published, and an attached file adds nothing to it. Naming one from the model is <<e1626>>. A value the model needs is declared in the model.

[#example-adequacy]
=== What the rows establish

An `+example+` fixes the value rule for one input. Whether a model's rows together say enough about it is a different question, and `+souther examples+` answers it from what evaluating them observed. Nothing here changes what compiles; it reports. And what compiles does not decide whether it reports: a compilation that raised a diagnostic observed whatever it observed, and the rows covered whatever they covered (<<example-report-emission>>).

[[a-line-is-owed-once-wherever-it-is-read]]*What a row is owed for is a point of a line a rule drew, and where the line was read is no part of it.* A point against the line is the line and which of the two it is: a row standing at length 1 answers what `+UserId+` says about a length of 1, so one row anywhere settles it however many coordinates read the line. A point away from the line is the line, which of the two it is, and *what stops the region on the far side* — a row away from the line answers for a region rather than a value, and a region is bounded by the line together with whatever ends it, so two readings owe one row there only where the far end is the same thing. One line can therefore owe more than one row in one of those roles, and a `+guard+` on a name every case of a sum spreads is as many borders as the sum has cases (<<boundary-coordinates>>) whatever it owes.

Which of a line's roles owe a row at all is the rule's (<<a-border-says-why-a-point-is-not-owed>>), so the points are what the rule leaves and never four by counting. What is owed by whom follows the rule that drew the line: one a behavior's own rule drew is that behavior's, a body's comparison being about that body, and one a `+data+` clause drew is the module's, owed once however many behaviors carry the type — what `+UserId+` says is the same wherever it is carried, so no behavior is short of it and the module answers for it under the declaration that wrote it.

These are the questions the dial asks of a model's rows, each answered where the measurement was complete:

* [[every-output-case-is-expected-by-a-row]]where a behavior's output is a sum, some row SHOULD expect each of its cases;
* [[every-input-case-is-used-by-a-row]]where an input position is a sum, some row SHOULD apply the behavior to each of its cases;
* [[every-axis-class-has-a-row]]where the model divides an input position into classes (<<example-partition>>), some row's value there SHOULD fall in each of them. Told apart from the case above it, which the same value can answer: a case is what the type declares and a class is what the model's own rules divide the position into, so a position with no sum at all has classes and a sum bounded by an invariant has fewer classes than cases. One row can close both, which is what makes the pair easy to read as one thing and wrong to report as one;
* [[every-obligation-against-its-line-has-a-row]]some row SHOULD answer every obligation at an `+ON+` or an `+OFF+` point (<<a-line-is-owed-once-wherever-it-is-read>>) — of an `+invariant+`'s bound, of a comparison in an `+ensures+` (<<a-clause-draws-a-line-on-what-it-compares-an-input-against>>), or of a line a fork of a body draws. Those are the two roles against the line (<<a-border-has-four-point-roles>>), and together they are what fixes where the border falls: without the nearer one inside it, nothing shows the border is not a step further in, and without the nearer one outside it, nothing shows it is not a step further out. The two points away from the line are reported always and owed to a build held to reliable domain coverage (<<e1917>>). What answering it takes is what the rule that drew the line is about: a bound and a clause are about the values, so the rows a compile already ran answer them, and a line a fork drew is about a place in a body, so nothing answers it until the rows are run again. A build is told about a line whose own measure could be made at the level it asked for, and about no other.

*The combinations are counted and are not asked for.* How many pairs of two positions' classes the rows sit in is reported (<<example-report-vocabulary>>) and no row is offered for one, because no bar refuses over one: a pair is what the classes of two positions make between them, and the classes are what the model states. A row composed for a pair moves two positions at once, which says nothing about which of the two the answer turned on — so what is composed for is one class, and what a row happens to settle beside it is a fact about the run rather than what the row is for (issue #967). Where the model states a value of a position's own type — a `+let+` of it — a row is written against that value: the position it is about is that value with the one field moved, `+Cond { ...none, f = C }+`, and the difference between the row and what the model already says is the class and nothing else. Every such value is an origin, and each names the one position it is a value of. That two of them go together is a further thing and nothing states it, so no row is written against a pair the file merely declares one after the other; a set of values that do go together is one an author wrote a row with, and those are read whole.

*Evidence is graded, not counted.* How far a row got decides what it proves, and three claims are kept apart rather than collapsed into one:

* *specified* — a row expects this case. Somebody wrote down that the model owes it.
* *observed* — the behavior was seen to answer with this case. A row that expected something else still saw what it saw: a row expecting `+Approved+` that got `+Rejected+` is no evidence for `+Approved+`, and is evidence that `+Rejected+` can happen.
* *verified* — a row expected this case and the behavior produced it.

A pending row (<<example-pending>>) reaches *specified* and no further, which is the honest account of a behavior that has been described and not yet written. A row that failed reaches *observed* — a measure that dropped it would report the case it produced as one nothing produces.

An input position carries the same three, with *executed* in place of *observed*: the behavior was applied to a value of that case, which claims nothing about what came back. Covering every case an input can be is the mechanised half of asking whether the behavior is total (<<match>>).

*What counts as a case to cover.* A position that is a sum — a union, or a named `+data S = A | B+` — has cases to cover, folded to their leaves. A position typed as one data has one, and covering it is not a question any row can fail, so it is not counted. This is narrower than what a row's expected arm is held against (<<e1904>>), which uses the single name to catch a row that wrote the wrong one.

[[a-case-the-rules-refuse-is-not-covered-or-uncovered]]*A case the model's own rules refuse is neither covered nor uncovered.* It is *excluded*: a third answer beside the two, reported and not counted against the model. Nothing can be constructed at such a case (<<e1903>>), so asking for a row at one is asking for work the compiler refuses, and a model whose rules narrow an input would report a coverage it can never reach. The case stays *declared* — the type has it, and that is part of what the model says — and leaves what the rows are held to, so what a report counts is `+declared - excluded+`. Which cases those are is what the declarations reaching the position leave (<<what-a-position-admits>>), read once and read the same way by every measure; a case nothing refuses is counted whatever any body says about it.

[[a-case-a-claim-is-made-about-is-counted-until-the-rules-refuse-it]]*An arm declaring that a case cannot arrive is a claim, and it is held against those rules rather than read into them.* It has three answers and no fourth. Where the rules refuse the case, the claim says what they say: the case is already out of every denominator, and what the claim adds is the author's own words, which the report prints beside it. Where the rules leave the case standing at a position nothing stands between and the caller, the claim is refused where it is written (<<e1326>>). Where nothing settles it — a case behind another fork, or one at a position whose rules could not be read — the case keeps whatever it was owed and the report says the claim was not proven. What removes an obligation is a proof; the absence of one is not, and an exclusion that is both unproven and silent is what leaves an author unable to tell a gap they can fill from one they cannot.

*A measurement says how much of itself it could make.* A row whose case cannot be read from its text — a helper's answer, which names no case (<<example-expected>>) — is counted as unclassified rather than dropped. While there is one, a case with no rows is *undecided* rather than missing, because one of those rows may be covering it. A behavior with no rows at all is not measured: an absence of evidence is not a set of gaps.

*An editor answers from the workspace or not at all.* Analysing one document cannot say what a behavior's rows cover, because they are written across its module's own source and any number of attached files (<<example-placement>>); reading one file would report what another covers as uncovered. So an editor computes this from the workspace compile, and where it has only a single document the measures are unavailable and nothing is reported.

*Not every measure has a code.* Which of these a build is told about is a separate decision from measuring them, and it is made per measure: a case of the signature nothing claims (<<e1913>>, <<e1915>>), a row missing against a border's line (<<e1916>>), a row missing away from one (<<e1917>>), an arm nothing goes through (<<e1918>>) and a class no row is in (<<e1931>>) are warnings a build can ask for — the last two of those only where the build asked for a bar that refuses over them; the rest — how many rows are waiting for a `+let+` and which positions have no finite partition to derive — are reported by `+souther examples+` and never warned about. A position the model draws no line through is a fact about the model rather than a mistake in it, and a row waiting for a `+let+` is the normal state of a model being written. The warnings are off unless a build asks for them, and asking for the arms costs a second run of every row.

*Whether the measurement could be made and whether the rows cover it are two answers.* A report says both. The first is `+complete+` or `+partial+`; the second is `+satisfied+` where every measure the bar it is held to rests on came to an answer and none of them found a gap, `+not_satisfied+` where one did, and `+undetermined+` where a measure that could have found one was not asked for or could not be made. A model the bar can ask nothing of is `+satisfied+`: it was asked what the bar asks and had nothing to answer for. One word for both would call a model adequate on the strength of a measurement that never ran.

Which measures could have found a gap is what the model has and what the bar asks about, and not what the evidence looks like. A behavior whose arms were never asked about, one that has no arm to reach — a `+>->+` composition, whose stages have them and which has none of its own, or a body that forks nowhere a row can be in or out of — and one whose arms could not be measured are all three without arm evidence. The first and the third leave the answer undetermined, because a row somebody writes would have been counted and nobody counted it; the second does not, because no row anybody writes would give that behavior an arm.

*What refuses a build.* A gap a measure came to an answer about and the bar the build asked for asks for.

[[a-bar-is-a-criterion-and-what-it-adds]]*A bar is a domain-coverage criterion and whatever else it refuses over beside it.* The two are independent questions and neither is a stronger answer to the other: how firmly a border's points are asked for is what the syllabus's two criteria answer, and whether a class no row is in is a row somebody owes is not about borders at all. So the bars are named rather than ordered. `+reliable-domain+` is reliable domain coverage and nothing beside it; `+classes+` is that and a class no row is in (<<every-axis-class-has-a-row>>, <<e1931>>). A bar can add only findings about rows the model asks for: what a measure could not read is this compiler's shortfall rather than the model's, and no bar turns one into a gap.

`+classes+` is asked for and is never a default. A model being written has classes no row is in — that is what writing rows is — so a build held to it by default would refuse every model before its rows were finished.

Both ways of asking read the same answer: `+souther compile --adequacy classes --warnings error+` and `+souther examples --adequacy classes --strict+` refuse the same findings, both being held to the same bar. The word means one bar wherever it is written. How many rows are waiting for a `+let+` refuses nothing. A build that failed on one would fail on the record of what an injected behavior owes, which is what <<example-pending>> says a model being migrated onto is made of.

*A report command measures everything, and the bar it is read against is named where every bar is.* `+souther examples+` has no measurement to choose — its output is the report, so everything is measured — and it takes `+--adequacy+`, which is the same word a compile picks a bar with and names the same bar. Left unwritten it is `+reliable-domain+`; the levels that word also spells on a compile (`+off+`, `+witness+`, `+all+`) say how much to measure and so name nothing here, and are refused.

[[strict-decides-the-exit-status-and-nothing-else]]*`+--strict+` decides the exit status of that verdict and nothing else: the report a reader is given is the same whether or not it was written.* It names no bar, and that is what keeps the sentence true — a flag that chose one would change which findings the report marks, and two runs differing only in `+--strict+` would be reports of two different questions. So `+souther examples X+` and `+souther examples --strict X+` write the same output, as do `+souther examples --adequacy classes X+` and the same line with `+--strict+`; only the exit status differs.

*What is measured and what a build is held to are two things it asks for.* `+--adequacy+` names both at once, and the words are presets rather than a single ladder. `+off+` measures nothing, and every measure says so rather than being left out of the report: a measure nobody asked for is not one that found nothing. `+witness+` reads what the rows the compile already ran established, which is every measure that needs no instrumentation — the cases of a signature, the classes a position divides into, and the lines a bound or a clause draws. It composes no values: what proves a row can be written at a line is then the rules' own reading, and a line only a composed value could settle is reported and counted against nobody, which is the account any edge nothing has settled gets. `+all+` adds what the arms and the lines a fork drew take a second, instrumented run to find out, and holds the build to simplified domain coverage. `+reliable-domain+` measures the same as `+all+` and holds the build to reliable domain coverage, which adds the two points away from each line (<<a-border-has-four-point-roles>>, <<e1917>>). The last two differ in the bar and not in the work: the points away from a line come off the same assessment as the points against it, so a fourth level would have said nothing about how much to measure.

[[a-criterion-chooses-point-roles-and-not-what-a-row-is-owed-for]]*A criterion decides which established gaps a build refuses over, and nothing about what an obligation is or whether it is one.* The two names are the syllabus's and they are borrowed for the roles they select — `+ON+` and `+OFF+`, and `+IN+` and `+OUT+` beside them — so what a criterion answers is which of those roles a gap has to be at for the build to be held to it (<<a-bar-is-a-criterion-and-what-it-adds>>). What is owed, and what became of it, are settled before any bar is consulted and are the same under either: the same obligation at the same point is the same gap, and moving the bar moves only what the build does about it. The syllabus asks its items of each border; this asks them of each obligation (<<a-line-is-owed-once-wherever-it-is-read>>), so a model this reports as leaving no point uncovered is one a reader working from the borders would have written more rows for.

*A selector names a subject or the command is refused.* `+souther examples --module+` and `+--behavior+` narrow what is reported, and a name that resolves to no subject is a usage error rather than a report of nothing. A report of nothing says the measurement was made and found no gap, which is what a build reading it is entitled to accept. The two names are resolved together, so names that each resolve and hold nothing between them select nothing; and they are resolved against what the sources declare rather than against what reached the report, so a subject that resolves and then cannot be measured is reported as `+partial+` and is not a selector that named nothing.

*The question is asked of a module, not of a source.* A behavior's rows are written in the module's own source and in any number of attached files (<<example-placement>>), so a source-by-source reading would report what another file covers as uncovered.

[#example-partition]
=== The classes a model already states

The equivalence classes an input is measured at are read off what the model already declares. Nothing is invented: a position the model draws no line through has no classes, and is reported as *not derivable* rather than filled in with values nobody asked for. An `+Int+` with no invariant is not split at zero, because a partition nobody wrote is a rule nobody wrote, and reporting coverage of it reports a gap for failing to test something the model does not say.

A type says the classes. `+Bool+` is two; an optional is `+None+` and `+Some+`; a sum is its cases, folded to their leaves (<<sum-data>>). A record is not a class of its own — it is taken apart field by field, and how deeply a value is nested decides nothing: every path that opens no declaration twice is taken apart to its end. A path ends where taking it further would open a declaration already open on it, because what stands under such a position is what stands under the one it returns to.

[[a-position-may-stand-under-a-refinement]]*A position may exist only under a refinement of another, and a refinement is not a step into the value.* What a case of a sum declares is declared whether or not anything constructs one, so the fields of a case are positions of the input, written under the narrowing they stand beneath: `+query@GlobalQuery.tag+` is the `+tag+` of the same `+query+`, read as the case it turned out to be. The sum itself is still a position and still divides into its cases. A narrowing takes no level of the record structure — `+query@GlobalQuery.limit+` is as far from the parameter as `+query.limit+` is where the record is the parameter — and it puts no position where the case is the whole of a value, a unit case having nothing under it. A case the rules refuse (<<a-case-the-rules-refuse-is-not-covered-or-uncovered>>) has no positions to cover.

What a row has to be for such a position to exist in it is what the path says and nothing beside: a row whose `+query+` is a `+FeedQuery+` stands at none of the positions `+GlobalQuery+` declares, and is a row that was read there rather than one nothing could read. Classes of positions under two cases make no combination, so they are no part of what the pairs count (<<example-report-vocabulary>>) and no row is asked for one.

A name a `+match+` arm binds denotes the scrutinee's position under the case that arm selects, where the arm selects one. So a `+guard+` written inside an arm draws its line on that position, as one written outside the `+match+` draws its line on the scrutinee's.

A threshold says where one class ends and the next begins. A `+guard+` comparing an input against a constant (<<guard>>) divides the position, because both sides of that comparison hold values a row can write, and so does a comparison in an `+ensures+` (<<a-clause-draws-a-line-on-what-it-compares-an-input-against>>). A `+guard+` comparing one input against another divides neither of them and still draws a line (<<a-comparison-of-two-positions-draws-a-line-between-them>>). Thresholds at one position merge into one partition, intersected with what the position admits: an invariant of `+x >= 0+` and a guard of `+x < 10+` divide the position into `+0 <= x < 10+` and `+10 <= x+`, and nothing below zero, because nothing below zero can be constructed. Two rules drawing a line at one value merge into one partition and stay two obligations, since reaching one says nothing about the other.

[[boundary-coordinates]]*What a line is drawn on is a boundary coordinate, which is not always a position — and being ordered is what such a value must be rather than what makes it one (<<a-line-is-drawn-where-the-values-can-carry-one>>).* A coordinate is the content of a location — a parameter, a field chain, a newtype's wrapped value — or the size of a container or a string taken of one: `+List.length+`, `+String.length+`, `+Set.size+`, `+Map.size+`. Which values divide an input space is its own question, and this states the answer rather than borrowing one: a value some other reading of the model can point at is not thereby an axis rows are asked to cover. So `+data Name = String invariant String.length(value) > 0+` draws a line at a length of 1, and a `+guard String.length(t.name.value) > 3+` draws one at 3 with a 4 beside it, exactly as a bound and a guard on a number do. A line is named by the coordinate it is drawn on — `+String.length(t.name) = 1+` — and an invariant and a guard mean the same line when they take the same measure of the same location. A size is a whole number and is never negative, and neither has to be written down: no row is asked for at a length below zero however the rules about it are phrased.

A position whose values no rule measures still has no classes. A `+String+` nothing says anything about is *not derivable* and is named by the position, not by a length nobody wrote a rule about.

A position has one coordinate, and which one is settled by its own type's rules where it has any. A rule reaching the position from the value it sits in states an end on a coordinate and does not say which coordinate the position is measured at: `+data Name = String invariant value >= "m"+` held in a record bounding the length of it is measured on `+Name+`'s order still, and the length bound states an end on a coordinate this position is not measured at. Where the position's own type says nothing, one such rule settles it; where they say where both of its coordinates stop, neither does, and the position is one the model draws no line through.

[[a-line-is-drawn-where-the-values-can-carry-one]]*Which ordered values a line is drawn on is stated, and where none is drawn the position says so.* A line is drawn on an `+Int+`, a `+Decimal+`, a `+Date+`, a `+DateTime+`, a `+Time+`, an `+Instant+`, a `+String+`, an enumeration — ordered by the order its cases are declared in — and a single-value newtype over any of them, which is ordered by the value it wraps (<<primitives>>). A line on an enumeration is named by its cases and never by the place a case takes in the declaration, and one on a `+String+` by the string. No line is drawn on a position that is one case of an enumeration, or a union of some of them: it is comparable on its sum's order and ranges over less than it, so a line drawn from the sum's places would divide values the position does not hold. Such a position is named as one a rule was written about that this could not draw, which is a fact about the compiler and not about the model. That is told apart from the other answer because they ask for different work: *not derivable* says the model draws no line at this position, and *not read* says it draws one this could not read (<<example-report-vocabulary>>).

The value beside a line is a separate question from the line. It is asked for only where the values have a smallest step. A whole number, a date, a date-time, a time of day, a moment and an enumeration have a neighbour — a `+DateTime+` and a `+Time+` are held to the second (<<a-local-temporal-is-held-to-the-second>>) and an `+Instant+` to the nanosecond (<<an-instant-carries-what-a-timestamp-said>>), which is the step each moves in — and a `+Decimal+` and a `+String+` have none: no smallest step is named for a decimal, and naming the string just above another means choosing a character this language does not name either. Inventing one would test a rule the model never stated. A line whose neighbour does not exist is still a line and is still owed a row at the value itself.

A rule is read the same way wherever it is written. An invariant's bound, a `+guard+`'s comparison and a comparison in an `+ensures+` draw lines on the same values, so a position bounded at a date and a position compared against one are measured alike; a rule any of them writes that this could not read is named as unread, whichever wrote it.

[[a-clause-draws-a-line-on-what-it-compares-an-input-against]]*A comparison in an `+ensures+` draws a line, and one a row can be written at.* `+ensures asked = NotFound -> id.value > 0+` says the behavior may not answer `+NotFound+` at or below zero and may above it, which is a value the stated relation changes at. Both sides hold values a row can write, so the value and its neighbour are owed a row, exactly as a `+guard+`'s line is. What differs is what meeting it takes, and the two lines are not measuring the same thing. A `+guard+`'s is about control flow: the comparison is a place in a body, and meeting the line takes getting it to answer. A clause states a relation the behavior is held to, and its line is covered by the input the relation changes at — so a row writing the value has met it, which is what an invariant's bound is met by as well, and it is why a clause draws its lines whether or not anything implements the behavior. Whether some evaluation of the clause reached that comparison is a separate question and is not what a boundary measures.

Only what a rule requires, and only about an input. A conjunct is part of what the rule asks of an answer, so the relation changes where it does; a disjunct is not, so `+id.value > 0 || urgent+` draws nothing at zero — the rule is satisfied wherever the other side is, whatever the comparison comes to. A comparison reading `+value+` states where the relation changes on the answer, and no row can be written there: what a row chooses is what the behavior is applied to.

A rule comparing one input against another draws a line between the two, the way a `+guard+` doing so does (<<a-comparison-of-two-positions-draws-a-line-between-them>>), and that line is met the same way this one's others are: by a row putting one count in both positions. It owes all four of its points, which are the four of a border on the difference the two fall apart by (<<a-relational-border-is-a-border-on-the-difference>>).

A comparison is read wherever in a condition it is written. `+kind == Domestic && cost <= 100000+` draws a line at 100000 as much as the comparison written on its own does: which position the model divides is not a question about the shape of the condition around it.

What the condition does decide is which arm stands as evidence that the comparison was evaluated. `+&&+` and `+||+` evaluate left to right and stop as soon as the answer is settled, so under `+A && B+` reaching either arm shows `+A+` ran and only the arm where the whole condition held shows `+B+` did; under `+A || B+` it is the arm where it did not hold. A condition mixing the two leaves neither arm separating the rows that reached a comparison from the rows that did not. A line no arm can witness on the side a row would have to be written is still a line the rows are owed, and is reported as one nothing measured rather than as one nothing drew.

[[what-a-position-admits]]*What a position admits is not what its type admits.* A field's type says which values exist of that type; the record holding it may state a rule relating that field to another, and what the position admits is that rule read at this field. `+data WorkInterval = { startsAt: MinuteOfDay, endsAt: MinuteOfDay } invariant startsAt < endsAt+` over minutes of a day running to 1440 leaves `+startsAt+` stopping at 1439 and `+endsAt+` starting at 1: a `+startsAt+` of 1440 would want an `+endsAt+` the day has no room for, and no row can be written at it.

[[a-clause-places-an-edge-where-it-reaches-one-coordinate]]*Which coordinates a clause reaches decides what it may do; the declaration it is written on does not.* A clause bounding one boundary coordinate against a constant, without relying on another coordinate, places an edge there wherever it is written: `+data Tally = { n: Int } invariant n >= 1+` places one at 1 exactly as `+data Count = Int invariant value >= 1+` does, and `+data Bag = { xs: List<Int> } invariant List.length(xs) >= 1+` places one at a length of 1. A clause relating coordinates takes an existing edge in and places none — `+startsAt < endsAt+`, and `+List.length(xs) >= minLines+` as much — because which values of one are on which side depends on the other, and a rule between two fields is not a partition of one of them (<<what-a-position-admits>>).

Placing an edge and taking one in are separate, and a position with no edge placed on it has no line however far its range was taken in. `+data R = { a: Int, b: Int } invariant a < b invariant b <= 10+` leaves `+a+` running to 9 and draws no line through it, because that 9 is where `+b+` stops and not something any clause said about `+a+`; `+b+` has a line at 10.

A clause reaches a position from wherever it governs it: the coordinate's own type and the names wrapped round it, the record the position is a field of, the declarations under that record, and the names wrapped round any of them. `+data NonEmptyBag = Bag invariant List.length(value.xs) >= 1+` places the edge `+Bag+` would have placed by stating the same rule, and names `+NonEmptyBag+`. A name wrapped round a value is not a step of the coordinate's path, so the coordinate is the `+xs+` a reader of a `+NonEmptyBag+` sees, and it is the same coordinate whether the name is worn at a parameter, at a field of a record, or several deep. A line is named by the declaration the clause is written on.

A name wrapped round a record is a governing declaration like any other, so what its clauses do is what any clause does: place an edge where one reaches a single coordinate, and take an existing edge in where one relates coordinates. `+data Wrapped = Base invariant value.a < value.b+` places no edge and still leaves `+a+` stopping where the room `+b+` needs begins. Where a record's own clause is the tighter of two, the line is the record's: `+data Moved = { n: Count } invariant n.value >= 5+` over a `+Count+` of at least 1 draws its line at 5 and names `+Moved+`. Where two clauses place an end at one value, both name it. Only a clause relating coordinates is named beside a line rather than as one, since it moved an end another clause placed — and it is named by the declaration whose clause is holding that end, which need not be the value the position sits in: a `+Held+` holding a `+Wrapped+` whose clause relates two of its fields reports the edge as taken in by `+Wrapped+`. Each end is named on its own, and a relation that reaches nothing the coordinate had not already passed names neither.

[[a-comparison-of-two-positions-draws-a-line-between-them]]*An ordering comparison of two terms draws a line, and it is not a partition of either position.* A `+guard+` comparing one position against another — `+charge.value > ceiling.value+` — puts its line where the two hold the same value, and so does a comparison in an `+ensures+`: `+ensures ordered = Ok -> from.value < to.value+` puts one at `+from = to+`. That is a line and not a class: which values of one position are on which side depends on the other, so neither position is divided, and the two answers are separate. The position is reported as one nothing divides, and the line is one the rows are owed (<<every-obligation-against-its-line-has-a-row>>). An equality is not one of these: `+a == b+` puts a whole arm on the line, and that arm is a row the arms are already asked for (<<every-arm-has-a-row>>).

Which ordered values such a line can be drawn on is the same list as anywhere else (<<a-line-is-drawn-where-the-values-can-carry-one>>), and it is what the two sides must share rather than a type. Two operands compare only when they are of one type, and the values a line is drawn on are wider than that in one direction and narrower in another: `+Charge+` against `+Ceiling+` is two types whose values are ordered alike and has a line, while two positions each declared as one case of an enumeration compare on their sum's order and range over less than it, so they have none. Both sides must be terms a row can be written at; where one is something taken of them (`+charge > ceiling + 1000+`) the line is at no value either position has, and it is reported as a comparison nothing was read from.

Whether a row can be written on such a line is whether the two positions can hold one value *at once*, and the ranges their own rules leave them do not say. A value in both ranges is one each of them admits on its own, and what refuses the pair need not be in either range: `+data Pair = { a: Int, b: Int } invariant a < b+` leaves `+a+` and `+b+` running over each other everywhere while the line `+a = b+` holds nothing, and a rule the ranges could not take in — a disequality leaving a hole, a pattern leaving one string — refuses values the range says are there. So such a line is settled by a witness: a row already on it, or a value that went through the decoder, which is what reads every rule of a value at once. Where nothing settles it the line is reported and not counted, the same account any other edge nothing promises gets. A line at a value of one position keeps its own account, where the rules that bound the position are the rules that drew the line.

Failing to compose a row settles nothing either way. A line the rules prove stays counted whatever a search made of it, and a tool with no way to write the value says so about itself rather than about the model.

[[a-relational-border-is-a-border-on-the-difference]]*A line between two positions answers for the same four roles as any other border, and they are the four of a border on the difference the two terms fall apart by.* A point of it is a pair standing exactly so many steps apart and a side of it is a pair standing further apart than that, so `+guard a < b+` over whole numbers is at its `+OFF+` point where the two are equal, at its `+ON+` point where `+a+` is `+b+` less one, and has its `+IN+` point under that and its `+OUT+` point over the line:

....
guard a <  b    ON  a = b - 1   OFF a = b       IN  a < b - 1   OUT a > b
guard a <= b    ON  a = b       OFF a = b + 1   IN  a < b       OUT a > b + 1
....

Read as the line against itself with no step, the pair one step inside the border fell into the side beside it — a row at the `+ON+` point counted as the point away from the border, and the point against it reported as one nothing owed. Where the carrier names no value one step from the line the two points against it are not named, which is the same answer a border at a place gives for the same reason.

What is read of such a rule is what a comparison of numbers states — an ordering between two fields, or between a field and a constant. A rule of another shape leaves the position where its type left it, and that edge is then not one anything promises: the rule that could not be read refuses a value at the edge as readily as one past it. Such an edge is reported and not counted, and no build is refused over it (<<example-adequacy>>) — the same account a combination nothing has settled gets. A concrete value accepted at the edge settles it, being a value that went through the decoder: an `+example+` row written at it does, and so does a candidate the compiler composed and the decoder took. Failing to compose one settles nothing — it leaves the writability unknown, the same as never having tried — so an edge becomes counted when a witness is found and is never dropped for want of one.

An invariant's bound is a boundary and not a partition. Everything outside it is refused at construction (<<e1903>>), so there is no class on the far side to cover — what remains is an edge worth a row, and the two points out there are excluded rather than left unsaid (<<a-border-says-why-a-point-is-not-owed>>). Both sides of a `+guard+`'s line do have values, so it wants the line and its neighbour, and the neighbour only where the type has one: `+cost <= 100000+` asks for 100001 on an `+Int+`, and asks for nothing beside it on a `+Decimal+`, which has no next value.

A rule that drew a line is kept per line. The same value can be a boundary of an invariant and of two separate `+guard+`s, and reaching it through one says nothing about the others, so one value can be several obligations. A rule that took a line in is not a second line: the bound and the record that narrowed it are one boundary, named by both, because neither of them wants a row of its own.

[[what-is-read-off-a-body]]A class the rules refuse is *excluded*, as a case of the signature is (<<a-case-the-rules-refuse-is-not-covered-or-uncovered>>), and it is one reading that says so — for this position, for the combinations this position takes part in, and for the rows a tool offers to fill what nothing covers. One universe, so that the three cannot come to disagree about what there was to cover.

What a body declares cannot arrive is read beside that reading and never into it (<<a-case-a-claim-is-made-about-is-counted-until-the-rules-refuse-it>>). Only what the body states without anything having to be decided is read:

* the body is followed to the first fork through what a `+let+` binds;
* a function written where a call takes one is not entered. Evaluating that position makes the function, and what it matches on when the call applies it is about its own parameters rather than about this behavior's inputs;
* where that fork is a `+match+` on an input position, each case whose arm answers nothing (<<an-arm-that-answers-nothing-is-not-an-arm>>) is a case that arm declares cannot arrive at that position;
* nothing below a fork is read. An arm of one `+match+` holding a `+match+` on a second input says what that input cannot be *given the first one*, which is not a fact about the second input and is not something a class of it can carry;
* nothing is read off a condition. Which values satisfy `+somePredicate(f)+` is a question with a solver behind it, and an answer taken on faith here removes a case the author should have been asked for.

So claims that could be read are missed, and that is the direction to miss in: a claim nothing read leaves the class in, and a class left in is one the report asks for a row at, which tells the author something true about their model. It is safe here because a claim takes nothing away on its own — read as a reason to drop a class, the same miss would be a class taken out with nothing having asked whether it can arrive, and that tells the author nothing at all.

[#example-branch-coverage]
=== Which arms of the body the rows go through

Where a behavior has a body, `+souther examples+` also reports which of its arms the rows run through. An arm is one branch of an `+if+` or a `+guard+` (<<if>>), one arm of a `+match+` (<<match>>), or the success branch or one departure of an attempted construction (<<attempted-construction>>). Cases written together on one arm are one arm: they are one run of code, and a row takes it or does not.

One more the dial asks, needing the arms measured:

* [[every-arm-has-a-row]]some row that states what it expects SHOULD go through each arm of an `+if+`, a `+guard+` or an attempted construction. A row whose answer is owed (<<example-answer-owed>>) goes through an arm without saying what the behavior answers there, so it does not answer this; an arm whose only rows are those is reported as a row owing its answer (<<every-row-has-its-answer-written>>) rather than as an arm nothing reaches.

The line such a fork draws is asked for as well, and it is asked for wherever a rule draws a border rather than here (<<every-obligation-against-its-line-has-a-row>>): a `+guard+` comparing one position against another draws its line where the two are equal (<<a-comparison-of-two-positions-draws-a-line-between-them>>), exactly as an `+invariant+` and an `+ensures+` draw theirs. For a guard the arms do not stand in for it: both arms of `+charge > ceiling+` are reached by rows nowhere near the line, and a row on it takes the same arm as one well below it.

This is *branch-arm* coverage and nothing wider. Going through both arms of two nested conditions is four arms and says nothing about whether their combinations were tried, so covering every arm is a lower bound on covering what a body can do rather than the same thing.

An `+unreachable+` (<<unreachable>>) is not an arm. Reaching one is already <<e1911>>, so counting it would leave every correct model permanently one arm short. An invariant clause is not one either: it is a property of a type rather than a fork in this body, and whether the rows reach its edges is <<example-adequacy>>'s question, asked another way.

[[an-arm-that-answers-nothing-is-not-an-arm]]More precisely, an arm is not an arm where evaluating it cannot answer a value. That is the whole of what an arm is made of and not the shape it is written in: an arm whose every path ends in an `+unreachable+` answers as little as one written as a bare `+unreachable+`, and so does a value one of whose parts is an `+unreachable+`. An arm that has one path answering a value is an arm, whatever else is beside that path and whether or not a row has been written for it — what a body has to cover is a property of the body, and rows do not move it.

[[an-arm-for-a-case-the-rules-refuse-is-not-an-arm]]Nor is an arm an arm where no value can reach it. A `+match+` arm is written for one or more cases of what it matches on, and where the rules reaching that position refuse every one of them (<<what-a-position-admits>>) there is no value to take the arm: `+data Active = Flag invariant value /= Off+` leaves no `+Off+` to arrive, so the arm written for one is owed no row. Read wherever the `+match+` is, and not only at the first fork — a refusal is about the position and holds under however many arms the fork sits inside, which is the opposite of what a case being *admitted* says, since that says nothing about whether the fork is reached at all.

[[a-fork-nothing-reaches-has-no-arms]]Nor is an arm an arm where nothing reaches the fork it belongs to. A row cannot get past something that aborts, so a fork written after one — a `+match+` under a binding whose value is an `+unreachable+`, anything at all inside an arm that answers nothing — is a fork no row takes: the row that would take it reaches the `+unreachable+` first and is <<e1911>>, which states nothing. Such arms are as much not arms as the one that aborts, and for the same reason. This reaches the whole body, so a body that cannot answer at all has no arms and draws no line for a row to be at.

What a call answers is not read. A non-recursive helper is already inlined into the body that uses it, so its arms are here; a recursive one is a shared method, and an arm whose only content is a call to a helper that never answers is counted as an arm. That is a gap in the measure and is stated rather than worked around, because reading through a call means deciding what a call does.

A function written where a call takes one is made there and run elsewhere. Evaluating that position produces the function; its body runs when the call applies it, on arguments the position does not have — a step that answers `+unreachable+` for an element it may never be handed does not stop the expression around it from answering. The arms inside such a function are arms, and the rows that run it go through them.

*The measurement is opted into by writing a row.* A behavior that no row names is not reported as having arms nothing reaches, whether or not another behavior's rows run through it. A row about `+A+` that calls `+B+` did go through `+B+`'s arm and that arm counts as reached; what does not follow is that `+B+`'s author has been asked anything.

*A row that did not finish contributes nothing.* What such a row went through before its budget ran out (<<e1910>>) is some of what it would have gone through, and it is dropped rather than counted.

[[an-arm-is-answered-for-on-its-own]]*Each arm is answered for on its own.* An arm is one of three things: a row goes through it, no row does and every row was read, or no row was seen to and a reading that could have been holding one did not run to the end. A row through an arm went through it whatever became of the rows beside it, so a reading that stopped leaves the arms it lit alight and the arms it did not light open. Only the second is a gap, and it is the only one <<every-arm-has-a-row>> is asked of; the third is named as the question it is, because an author told to write a row for it may be told to write one they have written. The three make up what the count holds, so the difference between the two numbers a report prints is walkable arm by arm.

[[an-arm-is-named-by-what-tells-it-from-the-others]]*An arm is named to a machine by what tells it from the others.* What a reader is shown of an arm is a word and a place, and neither tells two of them apart: a body with two `+guard+`s writes two arms called `+else+`, and a fork whose caller supplies the rule is one arm per rule handed in — at one place, under one word, where the helper is written in the module that calls it. So the document publishes the arm's identity wherever it names one, in the account and on the finding alike, and a consumer joins the two on it (`+obligationId+`).

[[an-arm-whose-rule-nobody-worked-out-is-not-counted]]*An arm standing for a number of rules nobody could work out is not counted.* A fork whose declaration says its caller decides is one arm per rule a caller hands in (<<a-block-is-not-a-value>>), and where which rule reached it could not be worked out, one place can be as many arms as there are rules reaching it: a row through it may or may not be a row through this one. Such an arm is outside both numbers, and the fork is named under them. Held inside the count, what a row is owed for would be a predicate the sentence over the denominator does not hold of.

*A boundary a guard drew is met by reaching the comparison, not by writing the value.* A threshold read off a `+guard+` is a boundary to reach (<<example-adequacy>>), and a row can carry exactly that value and never get to the guard because an earlier branch went the other way. Such a boundary counts as met only where a row both wrote the value and went through one of the two arms of the `+if+` whose condition compares it. That is a fact about the rule that drew the border and holds of all four of its points (<<a-border-has-four-point-roles>>): a row well inside a guard's line that never reached the guard says as little about it as a row on the line that did not. A boundary an invariant drew is met by writing the value: nothing outside the bound can be constructed, so the edge is the whole of what there is to reach.

[#example-report-vocabulary]
=== The words the report uses

`+souther examples+` writes its own words, and a reader who wants to know what one of them means has nowhere to look it up unless it is written down. A diagnostic carries a code and a code names a rule; a line of this report carries neither, so its vocabulary is fixed here.

Most of it is borrowed rather than coined. Deriving partitions from a model's declarations, drawing a line where a rule places one and asking for a row at the line and at the value beside it is domain testing, which has a syllabus and a body of practice behind it (ISTQB CTAL-TA v4.0 §3.1.1, generalising equivalence partitioning and boundary value analysis). Where this report means what that vocabulary already means, it says it in that vocabulary — a reader who practises the technique should not have to translate, and a word this compiler invented for something with a published name is a word nothing can be looked up in.

[[the-report-claims-no-coverage-criterion]]What the report does not do is name a criterion as satisfied. The syllabus defines two — simplified domain coverage and reliable domain coverage — and either one, printed as a verdict, would be a single word standing for a whole report, which ADR-0089 refuses. So the report says which coverage items a border owes and which of them a row is at, and a reader working to one of those criteria decides for themselves whether it adds up. That is the honest reading while some coverage items cannot be produced at all. A point beside a border is derived only where the carrier names the neighbouring value, and a `+Decimal+` names none: the syllabus asks for the closest value "according to the given precision" and this language carries no precision for a decimal. Such a point is not counted as one a row is owed, because no row could be written at it — an item that cannot exist is not a gap.

Which is a separate decision from what a build is told about. A row against the line is what simplified domain coverage asks for (<<e1916>>) and a row away from it is one of the two items reliable domain coverage adds (<<e1917>>); which of them a build refuses over is the bar's and is said once (<<a-criterion-chooses-point-roles-and-not-what-a-row-is-owed-for>>). Both come off one measurement of one border, so what a build is held to is a reading of it rather than a second measure made to other rules.

*What the rows establish about a case.* `+specified+` — a row expects this case; `+observed+` — the behavior was seen to answer with it; `+verified+` — a row expected it and the behavior produced it. An input position carries `+executed+` where an output carries `+observed+` (<<example-adequacy>>).

*What the rows reach of the model's own distinctions.* A class of a position is an *equivalence partition* of it, and a line some rule drew between two of them is a *border*. `+axes+` is how many positions the model divides at all; `+equivalence partitions+` is how many of those positions' partitions some row is in. `+not derivable+` is a position the model draws no line through, which is a fact about the model and not a gap in the rows (<<example-partition>>).

Named for what it counts, which is not what the syllabus calls an `+IN+` point. An `+IN+` point is a value inside a partition and away from *one border of it*, so it is keyed on a border and this count is keyed on a class. A row on a line is in one of the two partitions it parts and counts here; whether that partition also has a row away from the line is what the border's `+IN+` point says, and neither number answers for the other. The two are different measures of different things and both are reported: this one is equivalence partitioning's item, one row per class, and the four keyed on a border are domain testing's.

*What a border owes.* `+borders+` is how many lines some rule drew, each named by the coordinate it was drawn on (<<boundary-coordinates>>). A border is *closed* where the values on the line satisfy the rule that drew it and *open* where they do not, which is what tells a rule written `+<=+` from one written `+<+`.

*What a behavior owes.* `+obligations+` is how many rows the behavior's own rules ask for, and how many of them some row answers — the obligations <<a-line-is-owed-once-wherever-it-is-read>> defines, counted rather than defined again here. The two numbers are two questions and neither is the other multiplied — a border is where a row can be written, and an obligation is the work. The count is over the obligations whose coverage was measured and whose writability is known, the way the border block's items are, at the coarser grain. What the rules refuse to owe a row at is no obligation and is said under the block by the reading it is a point of, not counted beside these.

*What the declarations owe.* The same word again, under the module's declarations: the line a `+data+` clause drew is the module's obligation and no behavior's (<<a-line-is-owed-once-wherever-it-is-read>>), so it is counted there and no behavior is short of it. A model whose every rule is a declaration's shows borders in its blocks and its obligations under the declarations; the two counts do not overlap, and neither is a part of the other.

[[every-obligation-in-the-count-is-named-or-met]]*Every obligation a block counts is met, or is named under that block.* A count is over the obligations whose readings came to an answer and which something has shown a row can be written at, and each of them is in one of three states: a row stands at it, no row does and the readings ran to the end, or no row was seen and a reading that could have been holding one did not. The first is what the count's numerator is. The second is a gap, and it is marked `+!+` where the build is held to a criterion that refuses over it and `+·+` where it is not — which is what the mark says, and it says nothing about the gap. The third is marked `+?+`: it is no finding, nothing refuses over it, and it is named because a difference between the two numbers that nothing accounts for is a number a reader cannot act on. An obligation the count leaves out is said under the block as well, with the reason it is out — nothing was read against it, or nothing has shown a row can be written there, and both may hold of one.
