[[an-obligations-explanation-names-each-distinct-reason-once]]*An obligation marked `+?+` MUST name each distinct reason its readings met once, however many readings met that reason.* A line is read at every coordinate that carries the type, each reading meets its own reasons, and two readings stopped by the same thing are one thing to tell an author. Said once per reading, the sentence counts the paths a fact arrived by rather than the facts, and a reader is left counting repetitions of a clause that identifies nothing. The reasons are said in the order written down for them (<<a-kind-of-reason-is-published-in-one-written-order>>) and not in the order the readings were walked in. And the sentence MUST NOT say which reading met which reason: the readings are named under the mark, one to a line, and pairing a reading with a reason is a word about a reading rather than about the line.

[[a-kind-of-reason-is-published-in-one-written-order]]*Reasons a report says together MUST be said in an order somebody decided, and it is one of two.* A plurality of reasons that arrives with no order of its own — the reasons the readings of a point met, the budgets a search ran out of, the grounds something is writable on — MUST be said in an order written down for that kind of reason, and each such kind MUST have one such order. Where the reasons arrive in an order the model already has, that order is what is published and a report MUST NOT put them in another: the reasons a question stands are the parts of the rule that raised it, in the order they were written, and a reader may act on the first of them being the first the author wrote.

What is refused either way is the third thing: an order that came from the order a reading was walked in, or from the order this compiler's constants happen to be declared in. Neither is a decision about what a reader is shown, and both move when somebody tidies something a reader cannot see. So two consequences a reader may hold this compiler to. Rearranging a declaration inside this compiler is not a change to what a report says. And two runs over one model write one sentence, however many readings met a reason and in whatever order they arrived.

A written order says nothing else. No reason outranks another and none of those sequences is a ranking: what a reader does with two reasons is both of them, and what the order is for is that the same two reasons come out the same way twice. An order the model has says one thing more, which is that the author wrote this part first.

[[an-array-said-once-per-fact-is-ordered-by-what-is-written]]*An array a document writes one entry per fact in MUST be said in an order over what it writes of each, and that order MUST tell two entries apart wherever a reader can.* The rule above is about a plurality of a kind, where a word is said or is not and an order over the kind settles the sequence. Some of what a document says is counted per fact instead — the facts a verdict is open on, what a module could not read — and there the same word may be said more than once, so an order over the kind leaves those to whatever the entries were handed over in. What such an array is ordered by is what the document writes of an entry and nothing that went into deciding it: a key that left out something a reader is shown would put two entries a reader can tell apart in an order nobody decided, and one that read something a reader is not shown would order by it. Two entries the key cannot tell apart are then two entries a reader cannot either, and which of them comes first is not a question about the document.

Nothing is folded to get there. Two facts a document calls by the same word are two entries, because the array's unit is the fact and a reader counting it counts facts; folded on what they are printed as, the array would count the kinds, which is what an array of the first sort already does.

What left the measurement short of an answer without being a reading's own is said where that measurement stopped, and is not repeated under each obligation. A row nothing read at all bears on every line the module has, so an obligation whose readings met nothing themselves is marked and says no reason — which is the mark saying that what is open here is open everywhere, rather than a reason having gone missing.

A gap is marked per obligation, and the readings of the line are said under the mark, each as the coordinate it was read at and what a row there has to do in that coordinate's terms. The mark says which of the four points it is and which rule drew the line, and no more: writing where a point is takes a quantity, and a quantity belongs to the coordinate that read the line, so a mark that named one would be naming a place the row is not owed at — and one written from the level alone comes out true and unreadable, since the `+ON+` point of `+from < to+` is a distance of −1, which is no value any position holds. Where the line has more readings than the report has room for, it says the first few in the order their sentences sort and how many it left out; the document carries every reading, and where the point is is published there as identity rather than as words (`+obligationId+`).

A line one of the module's declarations owns is the exception the author makes: it is on something they wrote — `+String.length(value)+` — so it is said with that word, under the declaration that owes it.

[[a-border-has-four-point-roles]]*A border has a point at each place its rule leaves one, and answers for all four roles.* Two questions and not one. Which points it has is the rule's — the value it wrote, the nearest value on each side whose class differs, and the run either way — and what a row is owed at each of them is the rule's too: no row at all, one row at a value, or a row in each region the readings bound differently on the far side (<<a-line-is-owed-once-wherever-it-is-read>>). Which of the four each point is follows from where it is and from whether the rule holds there, so the four words are the classes of the points and not the keys they are held under: a role can have two points and a role can have none, and a border says which of the four it has no point in and why (<<a-role-with-no-point-is-said-and-not-left-out>>). What a border adds is where a row may stand: it is a reading of the line, and a line read at several coordinates is several borders owing what the line owes. The four roles are the ones the syllabus names: an `+ON+` point is inside the partition the border bounds and closest to it, an `+OFF+` point is outside it and closest to it, an `+IN+` point is inside it and away from it, and an `+OUT+` point is outside it and away from it. The same value can be a different point of the border beside it, which is why none of them is a property of the value. A closed border is at its own `+ON+` point with an `+OFF+` point one step over, and an open border is at its own `+OFF+` point with the `+ON+` point one step back; the two sides run from those points outwards:

....
guard n <= 100    ON  n = 100    OFF n = 101    IN  n < 100    OUT n > 101
guard n <  100    ON  n =  99    OFF n = 100    IN  n <  99    OUT n > 100
....

[[a-role-may-have-a-point-on-each-side-of-the-line]]A role is what a point is and not which point it is, and a border can have two points in one role. Where a border has them is where the values on both sides of the line it drew fall in another class than the line's own value: a rule that names a value — `+x == 10+`, `+x /= 10+` — parts that value from every other one, so each neighbour crosses and each is a point of its own. A rule that orders the values has one neighbour that crosses and one that does not, and the second is a row away from the line rather than a row against it.

....
guard n == 10     ON  n = 10     OFF n = 9   OFF n = 11    OUT n < 9    OUT n > 11
guard n /= 10     OFF n = 10     ON  n = 9   ON  n = 11    IN  n < 9    IN  n > 11
....

A row at one of them says nothing about the other: an `+x == 10+` written as `+x <= 10+` is caught by the 9 and not by the 11, and one written as `+x >= 10+` the other way about. So they are two coverage items, and a row that legitimately stands at both — which no row here can, they are two values — would answer both without making them one.

Which of the four a point is follows from where it is and from whether the rule holds there, and from nothing else. `+ON+` and `+OFF+` are the values against the line, `+IN+` and `+OUT+` the runs away from it, and which of each pair a point is, is whether a row there satisfies the rule that drew the line. So the same three places are the equality's `+ON+`, `+OFF+`, `+OFF+` and the disequality's `+OFF+`, `+ON+`, `+ON+`, and neither rule needs a role the other does not have.

[[a-point-away-from-a-line-lies-in-the-partition-that-line-bounds]]`+ON+` and `+OFF+` ask for a place. `+IN+` and `+OUT+` ask for a *partition* — the one the border bounds and the one it keeps out — less the value against the line, so a row anywhere in that partition other than at the line is at the point. Which is not the same as everywhere past the line, and the difference only shows where a position has more than one line through it:

....
guard n > 10                      IN  11 < n              OUT n < 10
guard n > 10 ; guard n > 20       IN  11 < n <= 20        OUT n < 10
....

With the second line written, a row at thirty is in a third partition and answers for neither point of the border at ten. Read as everything past the line it answered for the `+IN+` point, and a model whose rows reach the middle partition only at its edge had nothing said about it: the count of equivalence partitions reports the partition as reached, which it is, and the point that tells a row inside from a row on the line was already discharged by a row that is in neither.

What the report writes for a point is the relation and what it is against, whole — a value alone would name a witness of a partition as though it were the partition.

* [[a-border-says-which-point-it-owes]]a point the report names MUST say which of the four it is. A border can owe a row at all of them, two of the values differ by one step, and a report naming neither role leaves an author to work the closed-border rule out for themselves — which is the reading this vocabulary exists to hand over rather than ask for.
* [[a-border-says-which-side-a-point-is-on-where-the-role-does-not]]and where the border has two points in that role (<<a-role-may-have-a-point-on-each-side-of-the-line>>), it MUST say which side of the line the point is on. The role alone prints the same sentence twice and asks for a row without saying which value either time. The side is the line's own and is the same at every coordinate it was read at, so it is said where the point is named; where on the quantity the point falls takes a coordinate's words and is said under it, as every other word with a quantity in it is.

[[a-border-says-why-a-point-is-not-owed]]Which of its points a border owes a row at is the rule's to say, and it is not always all of them — and a point it owes none at MUST say which of two things settled that. Left out, they read as the report being short of items the technique asks for, and neither is a shortfall:

* the rules leave no value there. An invariant leaves nothing outside its bound, so its `+OFF+` and `+OUT+` points are *excluded* — the same reading a case its rules refuse gets (<<a-case-the-rules-refuse-is-not-covered-or-uncovered>>), one step further in — and a side the rules leave one value wide has no `+IN+` point for the same reason;
* the carrier names no neighbouring value, so the point one step away is not named at all. A closed border there has only its `+ON+` point and an open one only its `+OFF+` point — the missing one is decided by which side the cut falls on and not by a rule that decimals lose their `+OFF+` points. This is the item that cannot exist rather than one nobody has got to (<<the-report-claims-no-coverage-criterion>>).

What kind of rule drew the line settles which points a border has and never whether a row is owed at one it has. A rule that names a value has a point outside it on each side, so it declines neither of them; where one of those values is outside what the rules leave, or is one the carrier cannot name, that point is answered by the first or the second of these like any other.

* [[a-role-with-no-point-is-said-and-not-left-out]]and a border with no point in one of the four roles MUST say which role and why, in words that do not read as a row being owed. The technique has four words and a border can have no point in one of them: what a rule that names a value keeps is that value alone, so there is nowhere in that class away from the line — `+x == 10+` has no `+IN+` point and `+x /= 10+` no `+OUT+` one. That is not a point the rules refuse, which is an item the model settled and counted out; it is a word for something the line does not have. Said in the same sentence, a reader cannot tell an item that was discharged from one that never existed; said in neither, they are told the four words are four and shown three.

A strict bound on a `+Decimal+` is further along again: its cut is a value the type refuses, and this draws no border there at all rather than drawing one and owing a row at it.

*What combinations the rows reach.* `+combination+` is a line of its own, and `+pairs+` under it counts combinations across two positions, as counts rather than a ratio (<<example-partition>>). This is combinatorial testing (ISTQB CTAL-TA v4.0 §3.1.2) and a different technique from the one the lines above are about — printed at the end of the partition line it sat beside the border counts, where a reader could add the two into a total neither is part of.

*What a case behind an `+unreachable+` is told.* `+excluded+` is a case the model's own rules refuse, counted out of what the rows are held to and printed with whatever the body wrote about it (<<a-case-the-rules-refuse-is-not-covered-or-uncovered>>). `+declared unreachable ... and nothing here proves it+` is the other answer: a case a body says cannot arrive that nothing settled, which is still counted and still owed a row. The gap it names may be one no row can fill — a row reaching the point is <<e1911>> — and saying so is what the words are for, since an exclusion that is both unproven and silent leaves an author unable to tell that gap from one they can close.

*Whether a measure has a number.* Three states, and the report's own word for each. `+complete+` — the measure was made and everything it reads was readable, so a gap is a gap. `+partial+` — it was made and something it reads was not, so it shows what it saw and what it could not decide; a line under a partial measure reads `+undecided+`, which is that measure's account of one thing rather than a state of its own. `+unavailable+` — there is no number.

*Why a measure has none.* `+unavailable+` alone does not say, and the two words that follow it ask opposite things of a reader:

* [[a-measure-says-why-it-has-no-number]]a measure that has no number MUST say why, and one that has a number MUST NOT.

`+not applicable+` — there is nothing here for the measure to be about, and no row could change that: a `+>->+` composition and a behavior with no `+let+` have no arms, and a position that is not a sum has no cases to cover. `+not measured+` — the measure has something to be about and has no number: either it was never started, or it was started and could not be finished, and the reason says which. The two are one word because they ask the same thing of an author, who has something to do either way; they are two reasons because what to do is not the same, and a measurement that was started and could not be finished says what it went without as well (<<a-measure-weaker-than-complete-says-what-made-it-so>>).

That difference is the one worth reading. `+not applicable+` asks nothing of an author. `+not measured+` says what to do: write a row, ask for the arms, or find out why the run could not read them. Rows that nothing could read are neither — a source that would not evaluate may hold the row being asked for, so what it was carrying is undecided rather than absent.

Which reason it is, is what a reader acts on, so the words are held to the state and not to the kind of thing the state is:

* [[a-reason-is-said-for-the-state-and-not-for-its-kind]]a reason MUST be said for the state the measure is in, and never for the kind of reason that state is one of. A kind can hold more than one state and gain another later, and a sentence written for the kind is a sentence about whichever of them was in mind when it was written — which every other state of that kind is then given. A behavior whose signature could not be read and one whose signature was read and whose input was not are one kind and two states, and the second was told the first's sentence: an author reading it looks in the declaration for a name that resolved.
* [[two-reasons-that-send-a-reader-elsewhere-are-said-apart]]and two states MUST NOT share a sentence where they send a reader to a different cause, a different action, or a different place in the model. Which states share one is decided by what a reader does about them and not by how alike they look: the same words for two of them are as much a loss as no words at all, and different words for one of them are a difference a reader goes looking for and does not find.

*What made a measure weaker than complete.* A measure that shows a number and cannot be trusted for it, and a measure that was started and could not be finished, both went without something, and the report says what:

* [[a-measure-weaker-than-complete-says-what-made-it-so]]a measure reported `+partial+` MUST say what weakened it, and a measure reported `+unavailable+` because it could not be finished MUST say the same. A measure reported `+complete+` MUST NOT, and neither MUST one that has nothing to be about or that nobody asked for.

Which is why a report can say `+partial+` at all. The numbers alone read the same either way — a behavior one of whose rules this compiler could not read shows the line it did draw and the same coverage counts a model read to the end gets — so a measure weaker than complete that could not say why left a reader with a word and nowhere to take it. Each thing that weakened it is one word, and what the word is about is named where the document already names that thing: the rule, the position, the row.

Every kind, once. Two rules this compiler could not read are two facts and one word, and a list that wrote the word twice would be counting the paths a fact arrived by rather than saying anything a reader can act on.

*A measure's reason is the measure's own.* It is not worked out from the numbers beside it, from how many rows a behavior has, or from whether the behavior is injected. Those correlate with the reason and are not it.

*The JSON form carries the same three.* Every measure emits `+status+`, emits `+reason+` exactly where `+status+` is `+unavailable+`, and emits `+weakening+` exactly where something weakened it.

The three are what makes four words enough. `+not measured+` with no `+weakening+` is a measurement nobody asked for; the same with one is a measurement that was asked for, started, and could not be finished — which is a different thing to know and was, for a while, a thing only this compiler knew. What the report calls a state and what this compiler holds one to be need not be the same count, and are not: a projection that keeps the difference in the document beside the word loses nothing.

*A measure with no number writes no number.* Where a measure has no number it writes none — not a nought, not an empty set. The two used to be one shape, and a reader that reached past `+status+` to the counts got an answer from a measurement nobody made.

*Nor does a measure write a conclusion it could not draw.* A number and a claim over it are two things, and a measure that has the first may still lack the second. Which of them a measure has is asked of each thing it counts rather than of the measure as a whole: a run some of whose rows could not be read has arms it went through, arms it certainly does not reach, and arms nobody can say either way, and the three are told apart where they are counted (<<example-branch-coverage>>). Answered once for the measure, a claim over the whole of it was withheld the moment anything at all weakened the measure, and every arm the run certainly does not reach went unsaid under a number whose difference nothing accounted for.

What this is not is a rule that a field beside a measure waits on that measure. What the model states is written whatever anybody measured: the size of a combination space, the relation a border's point asks a row to stand in. So is what a different body of evidence settles — whether a row can be written at a point is answered by a row already there, by a value built through the module's own decoders, or by the rules proving the point inhabited, and only the first is anything a coverage measure reads. Such a field is written beside a measurement that has no value, and the two are consistent: they answer different questions. Which evidence supplies a field is the question to ask of it, not which object it is written in.

`+schemaVersion+` is raised when a field is removed or renamed, and not when one is added: a document written before a field existed is still a document of this version. A word added to an enumerated field is read the same way, for the same reason — no document written before it carried it. That holds one way only. Every object in the schema is closed, so an older copy of it refuses a document carrying a field added since; a reader that has to accept newer documents wants the newer schema rather than a higher number.

*What the rows could not be observed doing.* Beside the measures, the report carries the reasons the rows were not observed — what happened, and what it happened to: a behavior, a source, the module, or a position in a value. A row that did not come back, a source none of whose rows were evaluated, a run whose instrumentation could not be tied back to the body. The JSON writes them as `+incompleteness+`.

A reason about a source is written in the two forms apart, and this is the one place in the report where they differ:

* [[a-reason-about-a-source-names-the-file]]in the form a person reads, a reason about a source MUST name that source the way the same run's diagnostics name it. A run over several files is where such a reason is worth reading, and one that cannot say which of them it could not read says nothing an author can act on;
* [[the-json-carries-what-identifies-a-source]]in the JSON, a reason about a source MUST carry what identifies that source to whoever handed it over — the position in the list a build passed, the document URI an editor is keyed on — unchanged.

The two are apart because the second is not a name. What a file is called is the shortest thing that tells this run's files apart, so it is a fact about the set in front of a reader rather than about any one of them, and it is neither stable across runs nor a key. A document that carried it in place of the identity would say what a person should be shown and stop saying which source it is about.

Which leaves a document that cannot be read on its own, since nothing in it says what a position in a list was:

* [[the-json-explains-the-identities-it-carries]]every source identity the JSON writes anywhere MUST have an entry in `+sources+` saying what that source is called, under the name the same run's diagnostics give it. This is about the identities the document carries and not about the sources the compile was handed: a source the report says nothing about needs no entry, and a document listing every input would be saying a second thing.

A reason's subject is one place an identity is written and never the only one — a position points into a source too. The rule is written over what the document carries rather than over the fields it is carried in, because a field that comes to carry one is the case a list of fields does not cover.

This is not the whole account of what an assessment went without, and an empty list does not say that everything was read. What stopped a measure from reading what was observed is said by that measure instead: a combination count that stopped at its own limit, a value too large to be read back. Those carry no entry here, and a report may say `+partial+` with this list empty — what it may not do is say `+partial+` and leave the measure's own `+weakening+` empty as well (<<a-measure-weaker-than-complete-says-what-made-it-so>>). The division is which half of the work did not happen — the rows not observed, or the observations not read — and `+status+` is what a reader asks about completeness either way.

These are not the compilation's diagnostics restated. A diagnostic says what is wrong with the source and where; one of these says which evidence the assessment went without, which is a fact about the measurement. Most of them will sit beside a diagnostic that accounts for it — a source that would not evaluate is a source something was wrong with — and one of them never does: the runtime not being reachable is a fact about the host that no source can be corrected for. Neither substitutes for the other: a reader wanting to know what to fix reads the diagnostics, and one wanting to know how far the report's own numbers can be trusted reads these.

[#example-report-emission]
=== When the report is written

What the command answers with, what it says went wrong, and whether it succeeded are three results, decided apart:

* [[a-diagnostic-does-not-withhold-the-report]]a diagnostic MUST NOT on its own withhold the report. The rows covered what they covered, and that is the question this command was asked;
* [[no-subject-leaves-no-report]]where nothing the compilation produced can be measured — no module formed at all — there is no subject for a measure to be about, and no report is written;
* [[an-error-still-refuses-the-command]]an error MUST still be reported, and MUST still leave a non-zero exit status. `+--strict+` decides the exit status of a compilation that raised none (<<example-adequacy>>).

This is what makes the reasons above reportable. All but one of them arrive with a diagnostic beside them, so a report written only for a compilation that raised none could carry almost nothing that field is for — the exception being the runtime not being reachable, which no source is at fault for.

A reader that took an empty stdout for a refused command reads the exit status instead: a failing invocation may write a report and a non-zero status together.

[#control-flow]
== Control structures

[#let]
=== let

[,souther]
----
let normalized = String.trim(input)
let counted: Map<String, Int> =
    List.fold((acc, k) -> Map.updateOrInsert(k, 1, n -> n + 1, acc), Map.empty, keys)
----

Reassignment is forbidden. A `+let+` is placed inside a block expression (<<block-expression>>). What follows `+let x = v+` is the scope of that binding, nested at desugaring time.

A `+let+` at the top level is a definition (<<fn-declaration>>); a `+let+` inside a block is a binding. Which one a `+let+` is follows from where it is written, not from how it is spelled, and the two are typed from different places. A definition takes its parameter types from its own body and its call sites are not read (<<fn-rules>>). A binding has no declaration to read, so a lambda bound here takes its parameter types from the applications in the body that binds it. A block holds no parameter list — `+let f (x) = …+` is written at the top level — so the two forms never meet in one place.

A binding may carry its type, `+let x: T = v+`. The type is optional — a binding otherwise takes the type of its value — and writing it does two things: the declared type is pushed into the value, so a value only its context can type (an empty `+[]+`, `+Map.empty+`, `+Set.empty+`, including one produced by a `+fold+` over such a seed) takes the declared element type instead of staying undetermined (<<algebraic-types>>); and the value is checked against it, so a declared type the value does not have is a compile error rather than an ignored comment. This is the same declaration-into-body direction a helper's declared return type has (<<fn-declaration>>) — it is a declaration, not an extension of inference. As with a helper's return, `+T+` is an ordinary type, a function type included: `+let p: (Int) -> Bool = (x) -> ...+` settles the parameters of the lambda it binds, and is how a lambda whose body names no type for them is given one (<<fn-rules>>). A tuple destructure (`+let (a, b) = t+`) takes no annotation.

[#if]
=== if

[,text]
----
if condition then expression1 else expression2
----

Both branches are written. An `+if+` MUST have its `+else+`, so no `+if+` is left one-armed and an `+else+` closes the nearest `+then+` that is still open: `+if a then if b then c else d else e+` gives `+d+` to the inner `+if+` and `+e+` to the outer one. A branch taken only when the condition fails is written `+guard ... else+`, whose other arm is the rest of the body (<<guard>>).

`+if+` is an expression. If both branch types agree, its type is that type; if they are different data, it is a sum of both (`+if c then Submitted{...} else AwaitingPreApproval{...}+` is `+Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval+`). A mismatch between primitives is a compile error.

The condition is a `+Bool+`, with one exception: a construction followed by `+as <name>+`, whose invariant decides the branch and whose value the name carries into the success branch (<<attempted-construction>>).

The widening reaches inside a collection as well, since a collection is element-covariant (<<collections>>): branches giving lists of different cases give a list of both, the same direction `{plus}{plus}` takes on its elements (<<stdlib-list>>). It descends `+List+`, `+Set+`, `+Map+`, `+Option+` and tuples, so an empty collection whose element type nothing has fixed yet takes it from the other branch at any depth, not only at the top. Where the context did fix it — a declared return or field type reaches both branches (<<collections>>) — the two branches merge by the widening like any other pair. `+match+` arms merge by this rule too.

[,text]
----
if total >= 100000 then [HighValueTravel { threshold = threshold }] else [ClientCost]   // List<HighValueTravel | ClientCost>
----

[#match]
=== match

[,text]
----
match request with
    | Drafting as d             -> ...
    | Submitted as s            -> ...
    | AwaitingPreApproval as p  -> ...
    | PreApproved as a          -> ...
----

Place `+with+` after the scrutinee and list cases one per `+|+`. The leading `+|+` of the first case is optional. Braces are not used. Souther is layout-independent, so only `+|+`-separated cases hold without layout.

The one place layout decides anything is which match an arm belongs to. A `+match+` written in an arm's body is ended by a `+|+` that reaches back to the enclosing match's arm column; a `+|+` indented past it is the inner match's own arm.

[,text]
----
match settlement with
    | FixedSettlement { budgetRange } ->
        match budgetRange with
            | RangeBudget { ceiling } -> ...      // an arm of the inner match
            | UndecidedBudget          -> ...
    | HourlySettlement { unitPrice } -> ...              // back to the outer match
----

Nothing else reads indentation, and a misplaced arm cannot silently change meaning: a case is a named data (<<sum-data>>), and a name belongs to one data, so an arm that lands on the wrong match is a compile error naming the case and the sum. Where the two matches are on one line, the columns cannot separate them — write the inner one in a block (`+-> { match … }+`), which ends it explicitly.

A case value is bound with `+as+` (the `+s+` in `+| Submitted as s+` is Submitted, read as `+s.submittedAt+`). `+as+` binds the whole matched value. A bare identifier beside the case name is not a second spelling of it: `+| Submitted s+` is a compile error naming the `+as+` form to write instead. A case is a reference to a named data (<<sum-data>>), so it carries no payload to bind positionally; the one case that does is Option's `+Some+`, and the positional form is written only there (below).

Fields may be bound directly with a destructuring pattern, the inverse of construction. It uses the same `+{ }+`, `+,+`, `+=+` as construction `+Member { id = x }+`, and `+{ id }+` is shorthand for binding to a same-named variable (paired with <<record-literal>>). Write only the fields used. Destructuring is possible only for a single named case; an or-pattern (below) binds to a sum type and has no fields. It may be combined with `+as+`: `+| Member { id } as whole+` binds both fields and the whole.

[,text]
----
match c with
    | EmailContact { email = e } -> ...   // e is the email field
    | Member { id, mail }        -> ...   // { f } binds f to a same-named variable
----

A newtype case is opened with the constructor form `+X(v)+`, the inverse of construction `+X(v)+` (<<newtype>>). `+v+` binds the wrapped value. Nesting it reaches through a newtype over a newtype in one pattern — `+| Activated(EmailAddress(s)) -> s+` binds `+s+` to the base `+String+`, so `+.value.value+` is not written; the shorter `+| Activated(a) -> ...+` binds `+a+` to the one wrapped layer (`+EmailAddress+`). Each written name MUST be the newtype opened at its layer: `+Activated(Source(s))+` is a compile error when `+Activated+` wraps `+EmailAddress+`, not `+Source+`. The single-layer accessor `+.value+` (<<newtype>>) stays for reading one layer inline; the pattern is for reaching through several. The same form opens a newtype where a name is bound (<<binding-patterns>>); what `+match+` adds is the arm for a value that may be one of several cases.

[,text]
----
match m with
    | Activated(EmailAddress(s)) -> s          // s is the base String, two layers opened
    | Unactivated(a)             -> a.value    // a is EmailAddress, one layer opened
----

[#primitive-arm]
An arm may name a primitive type. A behavior or a standard-library function that answers a value or a reason there is none returns a union with a primitive as a member (<<union-member>>) — a model's own `+-> Int | NoAnswer+`, and the core's `+Int.divide+` / `+Int.truncatingRemainder+` returning `+Int | DivisionByZero+` (<<stdlib-int>>), `+Decimal.divide+` returning `+Decimal | DivisionByZero+` (<<stdlib-decimal>>), `+String.toInt+` / `+String.toDecimal+` returning `+Int | NotANumber+` / `+Decimal | NotANumber+` (<<stdlib-string>>) — and the arm that takes the value names that primitive. Nothing wraps it: the matched value is the number itself, so `+as+` binds it as it binds any other case.

[,text]
----
match Int.divide(i.numerator, i.denominator) with
    | DivisionByZero -> 0
    | Int as n       -> n
----

`+| Int(n)+` is rejected: the parens open a newtype (above) and a primitive is not one, so the message names the `+as+` form instead. `+| n -> n+` is not a fallthrough either — Souther has no `+_+` wildcard, every arm names a case, and `+n+` is looked up as a type name and not found.

Where the model has already ruled the failure out — a `+require+` (<<attempted-construction>>) or the divisor type's own invariant keeps the divisor away from zero — the union need not arise: the operator `+/+` divides, and its abort on zero (<<stdlib-int>>) cannot be reached. The arm is for the divisor the model admits as an input.

To route multiple cases to the same processing, list `+|+` within a case, `+| A | B -> ...+` (an or-pattern). It writes "these states are treated the same" for a state-machine sum in one line. The case-separator `+|+` and the or-pattern `+|+` are the same symbol, but `+->+` ends a case's pattern part, so they do not conflict.

[,text]
----
match request with
    | Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval as x -> ...   // x is TravelRequest (the sum type)
    | Drafting as d    -> ...
    | PreApproved as a -> ...
----

An or-pattern's binding differs from a single case. A single case's `+as+` binds to that case's type (the `+s+` in `+| Submitted as s+` is `+Submitted+`), but an or-pattern's `+as+` binds to *the scrutinee's sum type*, because no single case type fits all alternatives. So an or-pattern binding cannot read case-specific fields; carry it as the sum or open it again with match. Exhaustiveness counts cases one by one; a case received by two cases is a duplicate error.

The `+Some+` of `+Option+` (<<algebraic-types>>) extracts the wrapped value by *positional binding*. The `+v+` in `+| Some v+` is bound to the contents, not to `+Some+`. `+Some+` is the only built-in case with a payload, so it reaches the contents by positional binding rather than `+as+` (which binds the whole value elsewhere). `+| Some as v+` cannot be written. A `+?+` field and the return of `+List.get+` / `+Map.get+` are extracted this way.

When the contents are a newtype, the constructor-destructuring form nests inside the positional binding: `+| Some(EmployeeId(v)) -> v+` opens the wrapped `+EmployeeId+` and binds `+v+` to its base value, so the `+.value+` on the positional binding is not written. The first written name MUST be the element type — `+Some+` wraps `+EmployeeId+`, so `+Some(EmailAddress(v))+` is a compile error — and each further name opens one more layer, the same rule as `+X(Y(s))+` on a user case. This nesting is allowed only in a pattern; `+Some(...)+` is still not a construction form (<<algebraic-types>>). The short `+| Some v+` (bind the whole contents) stays.

[,souther]
----
data TravelCompleted =
    { preApprover: EmployeeId?
    }

let approverLabel (c: TravelCompleted) =
    match c.preApprover with
        | Some(EmployeeId(v)) -> v // v is the base value, no .value
        | None -> "None"
----

[#match-covers-every-case]
A match on sum data MUST be exhaustive.

Four more hold of the arms themselves:

* [[a-match-subject-is-a-sum]]what a `+match+` is written on MUST be a sum. There is nothing for arms to tell apart otherwise;
* [[a-match-has-arms]]a `+match+` MUST have at least one arm;
* [[a-match-arm-names-a-case-of-the-subject]]an arm MUST name a case of that sum;
* [[no-two-arms-match-one-case]]no two arms may match one case, a second one for it being unreachable.

[[branches-agree-on-a-type]]The arms MUST agree on a type: a `+match+` answers with one value, so what its arms produce is one thing. The same holds of an `+if+`, whose two branches are arms of the same kind.

[[a-pattern-opens-what-the-value-is]]A pattern that opens a value with `+(...)+` MUST be opening something that has one to open, and the type it names MUST be what is inside — a newtype's wrapped value, an optional's payload. This holds wherever the pattern is written, in a `+match+` arm or in a binding (<<binding-patterns>>).

[[an-or-pattern-binds-the-sum-and-opens-nothing]]An or-pattern binds the sum and opens nothing. It says these cases are treated the same (<<match>>), and what they carry is not the same, so there is nothing shared for it to name. Exhaustiveness is decided over what a value of the subject can be, which for a sum whose case is a sum is the leaves under it and not the cases it listed. Such a case is transparent as a value (<<sum-data>>), so an arm may name the inner sum (`+OwnCost+` of `+data CostBearer = OwnCost | ClientCost+` being further `+Reimbursement | Advance | CorporateCard+`) or name one of the leaves under it, and the two are the same kind of arm: one answers for the three, the other for one of them. An arm may name any type every value of which the subject can be, whether or not the subject's declaration reaches it; naming the subject itself is therefore an arm, and it answers for every value, so no arm may follow it. Two arms may not answer for one value, and a match is exhaustive when no value of the subject is left unanswered. [[an-arms-alternatives-each-add-what-it-answers-for]]An arm's alternatives each add to what it answers for. Writing one that answers for nothing another of them did not — the same name twice, or a case and something that already covers it — is refused, since the line was written for a reason that did not happen. Read of one other alternative at a time: an alternative that no single other covers is written down, even where the rest of them together answer for all of it. What a `+match+` is told it did not answer for is said in the model's own names, as cases that cover it and share no value with each other: taken in the order the subject states its cases, one all of whose values are still unnamed is named, and one only partly so is opened and what is inside it named the same way. Where two cases of one subject both cover what is missing — a value may be a case of more than one declaration (<<sum-data>>) — the first is named and the second is opened, so the names read as arms that could be written. This is how the report reads and not what the check decided over.

[#unreachable]
==== An arm may say its combination cannot arise

A table indexed by two sums has a cell for every pair, and some of those pairs the model does not admit. `+unreachable "reason"+` is what such an arm answers: it produces no value, and reaching it aborts.

[[unreachable-states-its-reason]]`+unreachable+` MUST state why the point cannot be reached, written as a string. The reason is what a reader is shown when a row gets there (<<e1911>>), and an `+unreachable+` with nothing to say leaves them the position alone.

[,text]
----
match age with
    | UnderThirty ->
        match period with
            | UnderOneYear      -> BenefitDays(90)
            | TenToTwentyYears  -> BenefitDays(180)
            | TwentyYearsOrMore ->
                unreachable "twenty insured years cannot precede the age of thirty"
    ...
----

This is not a proof. The compiler does not decide that the point cannot be reached — it could not, since what rules the pair out is a fact about the world or a check some caller already made, and neither is written in the types this rule sees. What the form does is state that premise where it is relied on, and make its failure an abort rather than a number a report goes on to print.

The rules it obeys:

* The reason is a string literal and MUST be written. It is read without running the model — by a reader, by the diagnostic an `+example+` gets (<<e1911>>), and by the abort's message — so it is not an expression that builds one.
* Its type is `+Never+` (<<algebraic-types>>), which fits whatever the position expects. An arm answering it contributes no case to what the `+match+` answers: the type is the one the other arms agree on.
* [[unreachable-stands-where-a-type-is-stated]]The position it stands in MUST state what it holds, or a branch beside it MUST — a field, a declared return, an annotated binding, an arm beside one that answers a value. Written where nothing does (`+let n = unreachable "..."+`, `+1 + unreachable "..."+`), it is <<e1307>>: the value it stands for is never made, but the code around it is still compiled, and nothing there says what shape that code reads.
* Exhaustiveness counts the arm like any other. Nothing here relaxes <<e1201>>, and there is still no `+_+`.
* Reaching it *aborts*, as an invariant violation does (<<invariant-abort>>) and for the same reason: what failed is the model, not the business. It is not catchable, and a boundary maps it to a 500, apart from a business failure's 400.
* It is not written in an `+invariant+` (<<invariant-expressions>>): an invariant answers whether the value being built holds, on every path through it.

[[unreachable-is-for-what-the-model-rules-out]]What it is not for is a case the model can produce. `+| InsufficientFunds -> unreachable "the caller checked the balance"+` states an assumption no type carries, and if the caller ever does not check, the rule aborts where it should have answered. Where the combination is one the model admits as input, the arm answers a value or the input types change; `+unreachable+` is for what the model rules out.

*Where that can be read, it is checked, and a case the model admits is <<e1326>>.* An arm of the first `+match+` a behavior's body reaches, on one of its own input positions, is the end where it can: nothing stands between the fork and the caller, so what may arrive there is what the declarations reaching that position leave (<<what-a-position-admits>>). A case they leave is a case a caller can supply, and an arm declaring it cannot arrive is a premise the model's own signature denies.

Nothing further is proven and nothing further is refused. A case behind a second `+match+`, behind a `+guard+`, or at a position whose rules this compiler could not read is one nothing here settles — a table indexed by two sums says what it says about *pairs*, and neither axis states anything about the other — so the arm stands and what is not settled is said rather than decided (<<a-case-a-claim-is-made-about-is-counted-until-the-rules-refuse-it>>). Every such arm is read, wherever in the body it is written; what is not read is an arm of a `+match+` on something that is not an input position at all, which is a claim about a value the model's rules say nothing about.

[[a-branch-the-rules-make-dead-is-said-so]]The other direction is a branch the model's own rules make dead, and it is *said*. Where the conditions on the way to a branch cannot all hold, no value the model admits arrives at it: no row can be written through it, and the arm asks for work nobody can do.

[,text]
----
guard a.value < 5000 else Free
guard a.value < 6000 else Free      // nothing under 5000 is 6000 or more
----

Reported as a warning (<<e1327>>) and not a refusal. Nothing about such a program is ill-formed — it runs, and the branch simply never runs — and there is no way to write "I know" at a `+guard+`'s departure the way `+unreachable+` says it at a `+match+` arm. Making it a refusal would leave an author no way out.

The rules it obeys:

* Only a proof reports. What is read of a path is an over-approximation of the values that can arrive, so a branch disjoint from it is disjoint from the values themselves; a branch the reading cannot settle keeps its rows and is said nothing about. The absence of a proof is not a proof.
* Nothing here proves that a branch *is* reached. A reading that found no contradiction found none — which is not the same as a value existing — so what would be needed to refute an author's claim is a value, a run that went through, or a rule that states it settles the question, and none of those is this.
* What takes an obligation away and what reports a defect read one answer and act on the one arm of it. A branch left unsettled keeps its rows and is silent; the two never disagree about which branches those are.
* It is reported to the module whose source wrote the fork. A call splices another module's branches into this body, and an argument handed to them can leave one of their arms unreachable *here* while it is alive wherever else that module is used — which is a fact about the call, and a branch the author of this module cannot take out.

[#guard]
=== guard

[,text]
----
guard amount <= balance
    else InsufficientBalance
----

`+guard+` is used inside the implementation (`+let+`) of a behavior whose output has a departure case. If the condition is false, it returns the `+else+` value and ends on the spot. The `+else+` value is one of the output sum's cases (just data). The implementation only places the value of the case it wants to return as the last expression. No special wrapping.

`+guard+` is sugar for `+if+`. The following two mean the same.

[,text]
----
guard <condition> else <value>
<rest of the body>
----

[,text]
----
if <condition> then <rest of the body> else <value>
----

Desugaring is done at parse time; later stages (type check, construction-authority check, backend) see only `+if+` or the attempted form below. That both branches may have different types (<<if>>) is what makes this desugaring hold.

[#attempted-construction]
=== An attempted construction

[,text]
----
guard DomainName(d) as name else BadDomain
if DomainName(d) as name then [ name ] else []
----

[#attempt-subject-is-a-construction]
Where the condition is a construction followed by `+as <name>+`, that construction's invariant decides
the branch. `+as+` names the value a construction builds, so what it is written on MUST be one; a
condition that is anything else states no invariant for the branch to be decided by.

The invariant is run — the type's own and every invariant a `+...+` spread brought in
(<<invariant-mvp>>, <<field-spread>>). Holding, the value is built and `+<name>+` names it in the
success branch; failing, the `+else+` value is taken and no value is built. The invariant runs once.

A construction is either spelling — a newtype's `+T(v)+` or a record literal `+T { ... }+`
(<<record-literal>>). A behavior that reports every business failure rather than the first one it meets
builds the reasons as a list and attempts the case that carries them, so "at least one reason" is that
case's own invariant rather than a guard written beside it.

[,text]
----
data NotConverted = { reason: List<BlockedReason> }
    invariant List.length(reason) >= 1

if NotConverted { reason = blockers(lead, enquiry) } as blocked
    then blocked
    else Converted { ... }
----

The name is in scope in the success branch only. *Nothing carries the failure.* There is no value for
it — no `+Option+`, no `+Result+`, no reason — and the name a business failure gets is the one written
in `+else+`, which MAY be a value naming nothing (`+else []+`).

`+guard+` here is the same sugar for `+if+` (<<guard>>), so the `+if+` form is the base one and a
helper `+let+`, which has no departure case to name, reaches it too.

[#attempt-departures]
==== Departing per clause

The `+else+` MAY instead answer each invariant clause the type names (<<invariant-clause-name>>), so a
behavior that departs differently per rule states the rules once rather than restating them as guards.

[,text]
----
guard QuoteLines(List.map(r -> toLine(r), lines)) as ls else
    | nonEmpty       -> NoLines
    | uniqueProducts -> DuplicateProduct
----

What an arm answers is which rule failed. The name a business failure gets is still the one the arm
writes, and no value crosses from the failure into the arm.

Which arm is taken is decided by the clause that failed, so the order the arms are written in has no
effect; which clause failed is decided by the order the clauses are declared in (<<invariant-mvp>>).

The arms MUST cover what can fail, so that no failure reaches a branch that is not there:

* [[attempt-answers-every-failing-clause]]every named clause of the type MUST have an arm;
* [[attempt-arm-names-a-declared-clause]]an arm MUST name a clause the type has;
* [[attempt-answers-each-clause-once]]no clause may be answered twice — the arm is chosen by the clause
  that failed, so a second arm for it could never be reached;
* [[attempt-answers-unnamed-clauses]]`+| _ -> …+` MUST be written when the type has a clause carrying no
  name — it answers the clauses that cannot be named;
* [[attempt-catch-all-has-clauses-to-answer]]and MUST NOT be written when the type has none, there being
  nothing left for it to answer.

[[attempt-arms-answer-an-attempt]]Arms of this shape answer for an attempted construction, so there MUST
be one for them to answer for. The `+else+` of a condition that is not an attempt takes one value.

Adding an unnamed clause to a type whose clauses are all named is therefore a compile error at every
mapping site, which is what adding a rule to a published type should be: today it shows up only as more
aborts and more decode failures.

The `+else <value>+` form keeps its meaning — one value for any failure — so naming a clause does not
disturb a site that does not care which rule broke.

A construction inside an argument is an ordinary construction and aborts on violation
(<<violation-destination>>); what is attempted is the outer construction alone.

An attempt builds a value on its success branch, so the enclosing behavior's `+constructs+` MUST name
the type (<<constructs>>).

[#attempt-subject-declares-an-invariant]
The attempted type MUST carry an invariant, its own or one a spread brought in: without one the
`+else+` value could never be reached, so writing it is a compile error rather than a branch that is
not one.

Writing `+T(v)+` on its own still aborts (<<violation-destination>>). This is the form a writer
chooses at the site, and it is what lets a rule be stated once — the rule lives in the type's
`+invariant+`, and a guard restating it can drift from it. Across a module boundary the restatement
cannot be written at all: a helper `+let+` an invariant names is not exposed (<<exposed-surface>>), and a
behavior cannot be called from an invariant (<<invariant-expressions>>). The attempt names the type,
which the declaring module does expose.

Newtype arithmetic (<<newtype-arithmetic>>) re-wraps rather than constructs, so it is not what `+as+`
takes; write the construction out (`+T(a.value - b.value)+`).

[#block-expression]
=== Block expressions

`+{ ... }+` is an expression. Place `+let+`s and `+guard+`s, then put the result expression last.

[,souther]
----
let preApprove (request, approverId) = {
    guard approverId == request.applicant.managerId else NotAuthorized

    PreApproved { ...request, preApprovedAt = now(), preApprover = approverId }
}
----

If only the result expression is placed, no block is needed. `+let isNonNegative (v: Int) = v >= 0+` and `+let isNonNegative (v: Int) = { v >= 0 }+` are the same.

A block is an expression, so it may be written anywhere an expression may (`+if c then { let x = f(a) g(x) } else h+`). Its contents desugar per the rules of <<let>> and <<guard>> and appear in the AST as bindings and `+if+`. A bare `+{+` resolves to a block expression; a record literal is preceded by a type name (<<record-literal>>), so there is no confusion.

[#purity]
== Purity

Souther code *does not touch the outside world directly*. The following are forbidden.

Mutable variables, field assignment, static mutable fields, random, current time, environment variables, files, network, threads, exceptions, reflection.

An outside-world dependency is declared as a behavior with no implementation, and its implementation is injected from the Java side (<<injected-behavior>>). A `+let+` may receive one as an argument and call it, so a `+let+`'s result may depend on the outside world. But the way it touches must go through a call to the injected thing, and that fact is written in `+depends on+` (<<depends-on>>). This corresponds to the conclusion of SMDD's DDD trilemma — purity is not layer separation but making explicit which functions have side effects (<<set-invariant>>). *Souther made this "explicit" literal.* Left to inference, which functions have side effects cannot be read from the declarations.

*"The requirement set is empty" is stronger than this sense of purity.* This chapter's sense is "does not touch directly", which every `+let+` satisfies. An empty requirement set means receiving nothing injected, which only some `+let+`s satisfy. What may be called from an invariant is the latter (<<invariant-expressions>>). To avoid conflating the two, the latter is henceforth called "empty requirement set" and not "pure".

[#stdlib]
== Standard library


[[an-empty-collections-element-type-is-determined]]An operation seeded with an empty collection MUST have its element or accumulator type determined where it is written — by the binding it feeds, by the field or output position it lands in, or by a non-empty seed. What an empty collection holds does not follow from it.

[#reserved-namespace]
=== Reserved namespace

[[core-privileges-stay-in-the-core]]Some forms are the core's and not a model's: `+intrinsic+`, `+private+`, a type variable, and an optional written anywhere but on a data field. A module outside the reserved `+souther+` namespace MUST NOT write them — the core is what stands between the JVM and a model, and a model that reaches for its forms is writing the layer below the one it is in.

The standard library is bundled with the compiler and referenced by short qualifiers — `+List+` (list), `+Map+` (map), `+Set+` (set), `+String+` (string), `+Bool+` (boolean), `+Int+` / `+Decimal+` (numbers), `+Date+` / `+DateTime+` (temporal), `+Option+` (optional). Every function is called through this qualifier: `+List.map(f, xs)+`, `+String.trim(s)+`, `+List.length(xs)+`. There is no automatic import — names are not delivered implicitly. What may be written bare is operators (`+==+` `+<+` `+{plus}{plus}+`, etc.), `+if+` / `+match+`, literals, and the current module's helpers. Argument order follows the convention of <<pipe>> (primary subject last).

A standard-library function is therefore never a bare name and never crosses what a module writes bare. What a module writes bare in a value position is one of: a binding in force, one of its own `+let+`s, a data of its own written as a value, a behavior it can reach, a name an import let it write without its qualifier, a name the language gives (`+None+`), or a case of a data core declares (`+HALF_UP+`; <<stdlib-decimal>>). One spelling means one of these — a `+let+` may not be named like a data (<<fn-rules>>), a name the language gives may not be taken, and a binding in force wins over a declaration.

A qualifier is the only spelling that reaches its library, so a *type* MUST NOT be named like one: `+data List = String+` is a compile error, as `+module souther.foo+` is. In expression position the qualifier still yields to a binding — a parameter or a `+let+` named `+Map+` makes `+Map.empty+` that binding's `+empty+` field — because a binding in force wins over everything.

To call a library name bare, import it explicitly: writing `+import List (map, filter )+` lets you write `+map(f, xs)+` unqualified thereafter. Qualified access always works, and `+import+` is only sugar to elide the qualifier. Importing the same bare name from two modules is a compile error, so qualify one of them (e.g. `+String.length+` and `+List.length+` used together in one module; <<imports>>).

Qualifiers such as `+List+` denote a module only in expression position, distinct from the type-position `+List<T>+` (angle brackets), which does not conflict. `+length+` / `+get+` span `+String+` and `+List+`, and `+List+` and `+Map+`, but they become distinct names `+String.length+` / `+List.length+`, `+List.get+` / `+Map.get+`, so they are not overloads but each module's own function.

The entity the short qualifier denotes is placed in the reserved namespaces `+souther.string+` / `+souther.list+` / `+souther.map+` / `+souther.set+` / `+souther.bool+` / `+souther.int+` / `+souther.decimal+` / `+souther.date+` / `+souther.datetime+` / `+souther.option+`. The `+souther+` namespace is reserved, and a user module MUST NOT name itself under it — `+module souther.foo+` is a compile error. Only the reserved namespace holds core's privileges (generics, recursion, intrinsics), and this is the line preventing a user from granting them to itself (the boundedness of <<algebraic-types>>). A name that merely starts with the string `+souther+`, such as `+southernmost+`, is an ordinary user name, not under this namespace.

[#type-variables]
==== Type variables

A core function may write a type variable `+'a+` in a type position — a parameter, a return value, `+List<'a>+`, `+Map<String, 'a>+`, or a function type `+('a) -> 'b+`. No declaration list (`+<A>+`) is written; a `+'a+` appearing in a signature is implicitly universally quantified. Because identifiers do not distinguish case (<<unit-data>>), a type variable is marked by a leading apostrophe rather than by a lowercase name.

Only core in the reserved namespace may write a type variable; a user module writing `+'a+` is a compile error. The user model stays bounded (<<algebraic-types>>). When a non-recursive core helper has `+'a+`, it is monomorphized by inline expansion at the call site — `+'a+` is resolved to each call's concrete type, and no polymorphic method is generated. An `+example+` row that applies one is not an exception: the row settles the variables from what it wrote and the method emitted for it is at that instance (<<example-evaluable>>), so two instances are two methods and two rows settling one instance share the one.

Writing one and carrying one are different things. A user module's non-recursive helper may *carry* a variable its body left unsettled and is monomorphized the same way (<<fn-rules>>); what it may not do is write one. The variable is inferred, never spelled, and appears in no declaration a reader of the module sees.

A single application of a function instantiates each variable its declared signature wrote once, and every occurrence of that variable — in its parameter types and in its result — denotes that one instantiated type. Two applications of one function instantiate separately. This holds however the application is compiled: a call to a `+core+` primitive stands and a call to a Souther-bodied function is expanded, and neither decides anything the other does not. So `+let has (xs, y) = List.contains(y, xs)+` types `+y+` as what `+xs+` holds, because `+List.contains+` wrote one variable for its element and its list.

[#intrinsics]
==== intrinsics

A primitive is declared as a core function with a body of `+= intrinsic "key"+`. `+key+` names the JVM operation the backend emits and is *prefixed per type* to make it unique (`+"string.trim"+`, separating `+list.length+` and `+string.length+`).

[,souther]
----
let trim (s: String): String = intrinsic "string.trim"
----

An intrinsic MUST declare its return type (it cannot be inferred from `+intrinsic+`). A call is type-checked against the declared signature — that is, `+trim+` is "a built-in function whose signature is placed in core", and this declaration, not a hardcoded compiler table, decides its arguments and return value — and the backend emits the bytecode for `+key+`. It is neither inlined nor lowered. Only the reserved namespace may write it. A user cannot call the JVM directly and only calls blessed functions (<<asymmetric-interop>>).

A core module may also write `+private let+`. A `+private+` declaration is part of the library's implementation and not of its surface: it is reachable from the library's own modules and from nowhere else, so a module outside the reserved namespace that writes its name is told the library has no such member — the same answer a misspelling gets, because from where the caller stands they are the same thing. `+private+` is a core privilege like `+intrinsic+`; writing it in a user module is a syntax error, and there would be nothing for it to do there, since everything a user module declares is published (<<modules>>). `+List.foldFrom+`, which `+List.fold+` supplies the starting index for, is one.

*Every* standard-library function is declared in a core module this way, whether its body is Souther (self-hosted, like `+List.map+` over `+foldFrom+`) or `+intrinsic "key"+` (a JVM kernel). The declaration is the single source of truth for the signature — there is no compiler table of signatures, and a function the compiler can call but a declaration does not describe does not exist. What stays in the compiler is what the *type language cannot say*, as a side condition on a declared signature rather than in place of one:

* the operators (`{plus}` `+-+` `+*+` `+/+` `+==+` `+<+` `+<=+` `+>+` `+>=+`) are syntax, and the arithmetic ones carry the closed-newtype rules (<<newtype>>) a plain function does not;
* [[what-is-ordered-by-is-an-ordered-value]]"the element is an ordered value" — Souther has no `+comparable+` type class, so `+List.sort+` / `+max+` / `+min+` declare `+List<'a>+` and the constraint on `+'a+` is checked in the compiler. `+sortBy+` states it of what its key function answers with, which is the value being ordered by;
* [[what-is-summed-is-a-number]]"the element is a number" — `+List.sum+` / `+product+` declare `+List<'a>+` for the same reason, and the constraint that `+'a+` is `+Int+` or `+Decimal+`, the two types arithmetic is defined for, is checked in the compiler.

Neither is a type mismatch, and a diagnostic about one does not read as one: the argument has the type its parameter takes, and what it does not have is a capability the signature had no way to ask for.

Because a signature is a declaration and nothing else, a standard-library function is a function value like any other: `+List.map(String.trim, xs)+` and `+let f = String.length+` are ordinary (<<blocks>>). A value the library offers is declared the same way, with no parameter list — `+let empty : Map<'k, 'a> = intrinsic "map.empty"+` — and is written `+Map.empty+`.

[#pipe]
=== The value pipe and argument order

To thread a value through functions, write `+|>+` left to right. `+e |> f(a)+` is `+f(a, e)+` — the left side enters the *last argument* of the right-hand call. A parenthesis-less function name `+e |> f+` is `+f(e)+`. `+|>+` binds most loosely and left-associatively, so `+a |> f |> g+` reads as `+g(f(a))+`.

[,text]
----
input.raw
    |> split(",")                          // split(",", input.raw)
    |> map(piece -> piece |> trim |> lowercase)
    |> filter(piece -> String.length(piece) >= 1)
    |> sort                                // sort(...)
----

Standard library arguments put *the primary subject (collection, string, Map) last, functions, separators, and keys first*: `+List.map(f, xs)+`, `+List.filter(p, xs)+`, `+List.fold(step, seed, xs)+`, `+String.split(sep, s)+`, `+Map.get(key, m)+`. So the left value drops into the last argument and the pipe flows top to bottom. A `+fold+`'s folding block stays in `+(acc, x)+` order.

The right side admits only a function call or a function name; `+e |> 3+` is a compile error. A bare field getter is not a pipe target either — write `+e.field+` for a single value, or use the getter inside a call (`+xs |> List.map(.field)+`), not `+e |> .field+` (<<blocks>>). `+|>+` is expanded to a call at parse time, so no later stage sees `+|>+` — the same pure syntactic sugar as `+guard+` expanding to `+if+`. It is distinct from the `+>->+` that composes behavior stages (<<composition>>); that connects behaviors, this threads expression values.

[#stdlib-string]
=== String

[,text]
----
length   isEmpty  trim     lowercase  uppercase  contains  startsWith  endsWith
slice    append  reverse  repeat     padLeft    padRight  lines
split    join  replace    words      fromInt   concat       matches
characters  codePoints  toInt  fromDecimal  toDecimal
----

`+append(a, b)+` joins two strings in their written order; the `{plus}{plus}` operator is the common form, `+append(a, b)+` equals `+a {plus}{plus} b+` (<<stdlib-list>>).

[#string-code-points]
Every length, index and range in this module counts *Unicode code points*. It is not the UTF-16 code unit a JVM `+String+` is stored in — that is the backend's representation and says nothing a domain wants — and it is not the grapheme cluster a reader sees as one character. `+髙+` and `+𠮷+` are one code point each; a base letter followed by a combining accent is two, and an emoji joined with zero-width joiners is several. So `+String.length("𠮷")+` is `+1+`, and an invariant written `+String.length(value) <= 20+` admits twenty code points — not twenty columns on a printed form, and not twenty UTF-16 units. A derived decoder carries the same bound to the boundary in the same unit, so a value the model admits is not rejected on the way in (<<decoder-error>>).

What the choice buys is that the operations agree with one another and that none of them can answer with half a surrogate pair:

[,text]
----
String.length(s) == List.length(String.characters(s))
String.length(s) == List.length(String.codePoints(s))
String.slice(0, String.length(s), s) == s
----

Grapheme-cluster counts and display widths depend on Unicode segmentation and on where the text is shown; they are a different question from how long a stored value is, and would be a differently named function.

[#string-canonical]
**Text that arrives from outside is canonicalized to NFC.** Unicode calls `+が+` written as one code point and as か followed by a combining mark *canonically equivalent* — the same text — and a comparison by code units calls them different. Left alone they would be two values: two `+Map+` keys, two `+Set+` members, `+==+` false, and a length that depends on which keyboard produced them. There are two places text arrives, and both canonicalize: a derived decoder, at its string leaf and therefore before every constraint chained after it (<<decoder-error>>), and a string literal in a source file, when the compiler reads it. So a pattern written in one form matches a value that arrived in the other, whichever form each was typed in.

What is canonical is the *value*; nothing about comparison changes. NFC and not NFKC — compatibility folding turns `+①+` into `+1+` and a half-width kana into a full-width one, which is a claim about the text that a stored name does not want. A variation sequence (`+葛+` followed by U+E0101) is stable under every normalization form and stays two code points, so this is not a way to make a name count as its characters look; that is the grapheme-cluster question above. A decoder therefore transforms as well as validates, and what it answers is not always byte-for-byte what arrived — anything needing the bytes as sent keeps them outside the decoder.

`+matches(pattern, s)+` is a whole-string regular-expression match (Java `+Pattern+` flavour, anchored: the *entire* string must match), returning a plain `+Bool+`. It is what states a format-constrained value as an invariant — `+data PostalCode = String invariant String.matches("[0-9]{3}-[0-9]{4}", value)+`. The `+pattern+` MUST evaluate to a string at compile time: it is validated there (compiled once to confirm it is well-formed), so a malformed regex is a compile error rather than a runtime exception. A string literal evaluates to one, and so does a `{plus}{plus}` of literals and of values — a `+let+` with no parameter list (<<fn-declaration>>) — which is how types whose formats share a part hold that part once instead of spelling it out in each of them:

[,souther]
----
let sitePrefix = "001"

data SiteId = String
    invariant String.matches(sitePrefix ++ "[0-9A-Za-z]{12}", value)
----

What is validated is the string the whole expression evaluates to, not the pieces it is written in. At run time the pattern is compiled on first use and cached, not recompiled on each call. A regex backslash class is written with a doubled backslash (`+"\\d{3}"+`), since `+\d+` is not an escape a string literal reads and a lone backslash before `+d+` is refused (<<string-literal>>). A pattern a run-time value takes part in is therefore not accepted (a future `+Regex+`/`+fromString+` would carry that, returning a case for an invalid pattern); a pattern the compiler can read is what the invariant needs. Regex lives in core rather than a package, since Souther has no package layer. A pathological pattern can backtrack; keep invariant patterns simple.

The middle row is the rest of what Souther's types allow, which have no `+Char+`, `+Maybe+` or `+Float+`. `+split(sep, s)+` splits by a separator into `+List<String>+`, keeping empty pieces (`+split(",", "a,,b")+` is `+["a", "", "b"]+`). `+words(s)+` splits by runs of whitespace, dropping empty pieces. `+join(sep, xs)+` joins with a separator. `+replace(target, replacement, s)+` replaces every occurrence of `+target+`. `+fromInt(n)+` makes an `+Int+` a decimal string. `+concat(xs)+` joins a `+List<String>+` with no separator (equal to `+join("", xs)+`). The primary subject string is last (<<pipe>>). There is no `+Char+` type and no character-mapping operation.

`+isEmpty(s)+` is `+length(s) == 0+`, the sibling of `+List.isEmpty+` / `+Map.isEmpty+` / `+Set.isEmpty+`. It belongs in a predicate — `+filter(piece -> not(String.isEmpty(piece)))+` — rather than in a newtype invariant: a derived decoder recognises `+String.length(value) >= 1+` and reports a violation as `+too_short+` with its bound, while the helper's inlined form leaves it the generic `+invariant_violation+` (<<decoder-error>>).

`+reverse+` / `+repeat+` / `+lines+` / `+padLeft+` / `+padRight+` are the rest of what Souther's types allow. `+reverse+` turns the code points around, so a character outside the basic plane survives it. `+repeat(n, s)+` joins `+n+` copies (nothing for `+n+` of 0 or less; a count no string could hold *aborts* rather than quietly giving fewer copies than asked for, as an `+Int+` overflow does — <<stdlib-int>>). `+lines(s)+` breaks on newlines, accepting both `+\n+` and `+\r\n+`, and keeps empty pieces — a trailing newline leaves an empty last line. `+padLeft(width, pad, s)+` / `+padRight+` widen `+s+` to `+width+` code points with copies of `+pad+` and leave a string already that wide alone. `+pad+` is repeated and cut to the code points still needed, so it need not be one character and the width is exact: `+String.length(padLeft(width, pad, s))+` is `+Int.max(width, String.length(s))+` for any non-empty `+pad+`. An empty `+pad+` fills nothing and gives `+s+` back, the one input that equation does not hold for. Padding is what *builds* a fixed-width identifier — a bay number becomes `+"03"+` with `+padLeft(2, "0", fromInt(bay))+` — so a code whose shape a newtype invariant states can be produced in the domain and not only checked there.

`+fromDecimal(d)+` renders a `+Decimal+` in plain notation, never in exponent form, keeping the scale the value carries: `+1000.00m+` is `+"1000.00"+`. Scale is incidental to the number (<<primitives>>) but not to how it is written, so a caller wanting a fixed number of places sets it first with `+Decimal.round(scale, mode, d)+` and names the rounding there. Grouping separators and a currency symbol are presentation and stay outside the domain. `+toDecimal(s)+` parses back, returning `+Decimal | NotANumber+` — the sibling of `+toInt+`, and a compiler built-in for the same reason.

`+slice(fromInclusive, toExclusive, s)+` takes the code points from `+fromInclusive+` up to but not including `+toExclusive+`. Half-open is the rule for *extracting* a range, as `+Inclusive+` in a name is the rule for *generating* one (`+List.rangeInclusive+`, <<stdlib-list>>): two slices that meet at an index join back into the whole, and `+slice(0, String.length(s), s)+` is the string. An index the string has not got, or a `+toExclusive+` before `+fromInclusive+`, *aborts*.

The bottom row takes a string apart into the units it is made of, without a `+Char+` type — a character is a one-code-point `+String+`. `+characters(s)+` gives one code point per element (`+characters("a12")+` is `+["a", "1", "2"]+`), so `+List.fold+` can walk them; `+codePoints(s)+` gives the same split as the integers. Both are total, so the empty string gives the empty list. There is no "first code point" function: a caller who wants one writes `+List.get(0, String.codePoints(s))+` and takes the absence from `+List.get+`, where every other absence in the library comes from. `+toInt(s)+` returns `+Int | NotANumber+`: a named case, since `+Maybe+` has no surface form — the same case-return the `+/+` operator and `+Int.divide+` draw between abort and case. `+toInt("007")+` is `+7+` (leading zeros kept, unlike a numeric parse-and-reformat); `+toInt("12x")+` is `+NotANumber+`, and the parsed value is taken out with `+| Int as n+` (<<primitive-arm>>). `+toInt+` is a compiler built-in rather than a core declaration (<<intrinsics>>); `+characters+` / `+codePoints+` are ordinary intrinsics.

Together these make a checksum (Luhn, EAN, a mod-10 check digit) a plain `+let+` over `+List.fold+` in a behavior: validate the digits with `+matches("[0-9]+", value)+` in the type's invariant, read each digit as its code point less that of `+"0"+` over `+String.codePoints+` (total, because the invariant proved every character is a digit), and take the remainder with `+Int.floorMod+`. A checksum is *not* an invariant — it folds, and an invariant may not recurse (<<invariant-expressions>>) — so it lives in a behavior, but a short one.

[#stdlib-int]
=== Int

[,text]
----
add  subtract  multiply  divide  truncatingRemainder  compare  floorMod
abs  min  max  clamp
----

`+Int+` is signed 64-bit (<<primitives>>). If an operation exceeds that range it *aborts* — it does not wrap. Overflow is a model bug, not a business result, so like an invariant violation it throws `+ConstraintViolation+` (<<algebraic-types>>, <<violation-destination>>, <<jvm-abort>>).

Division by zero returns `+Int | DivisionByZero+`. This is a possible input on the business flow (a divisor of 0), so it is returned as a case rather than aborting. Overflow is not a second such case: `+divide+` *aborts* on the one pair whose quotient no `+Int+` holds, exactly as `+/+` does, because the case is about a divisor the model admits and not about a result that will not fit. The arm that takes the quotient names the primitive and binds it with `+as+` — `+| Int as n+` (<<primitive-arm>>).

The four operations may also be written with the operators `{plus}` `+-+` `+*+` `+/+` (the symbol table of <<primitives>>). `+/+` is truncating division and *aborts* on division by zero (like overflow). To receive `+Int | DivisionByZero+` as a case, use the functions `+divide+` / `+truncatingRemainder+` — operator = casual and aborting, function = safe with a case. All three take the dividend first and the divisor second: a binary arithmetic operation keeps its operands in the order the mathematics writes them, and the pipe's subject-last rule is for the unary ones (<<pipe>>).

`+abs+` / `+min+` / `+max+` / `+clamp+` are ordinary core `+let+`s over `+<+` and `+-+`. `+abs(n)+` negates with the `+-+` operator, so the smallest `+Int+` — the one value with no positive counterpart — aborts on overflow like any other overflow here; it is what turns a signed difference into a distance (`+Int.abs(Date.daysBetween(a, b))+` says "how far apart" without the caller choosing an argument order to dodge the sign). `+min+` / `+max+` are the two-value form the comparison operators imply, distinct from `+List.min+` / `+List.max+`, which take a list and answer an optional because a list may be empty. `+clamp(lo, hi, n)+` holds a value inside a range, the subject last as the pipe convention wants.

`+floorMod+` and `+truncatingRemainder+` are the two remainders, and the names say which is which because languages disagree on what a bare `+mod+` or `+remainder+` means for a negative operand. `+floorMod(dividend, divisor)+` is the remainder of a *floored* division, so the result takes the sign of the divisor: `+Int.floorMod(-7, 3)+` is `+2+`. `+truncatingRemainder(dividend, divisor)+` is the remainder of a division whose quotient is truncated toward zero, so the result takes the sign of the dividend: `+Int.truncatingRemainder(-7, 3)+` is `+-1+`.

They also differ in what a zero divisor does, and that difference is *not* in the names. `+truncatingRemainder+` returns `+Int | DivisionByZero+` like `+divide+`. `+floorMod+` *aborts* like `+/+`, so its result is a plain `+Int+` that reads in an invariant — `+data Box = Int invariant Int.floorMod(value, 12) == 0+`. That makes `+floorMod+` the one standard-library function whose failure is not in its type; it is a stated exception, not a pattern to copy (ADR-0095).
