Use `+truncatingRemainder+` where a zero divisor must be a business case.

[#stdlib-decimal]
=== Decimal

[,text]
----
add  subtract  multiply  divide  compare  fromInt  toInt  round
abs  min  max  clamp
----

Division makes the rounding scale and mode explicit as arguments. `+Decimal.divide(dividend, divisor, 2, HALF_UP)+` rounds HALF_UP at 2 decimal places. The mode is an ordinary value of the data core declares:

[,souther]
----
data RoundingMode = HALF_UP | HALF_EVEN | HALF_DOWN | UP | DOWN | CEILING | FLOOR
----

A mode argument is evaluated like any other, so a policy can be stated once and passed by name, chosen by an `+if+` or a `+match+`, or computed by a helper, and the three operations taking one are ordinary functions that can be handed over by name (<<blocks>>). The cases are unit data and follow the unit-data rules (<<unit-data>>): written bare, shadowable by a binding. The type provides no codec, so it cannot appear where one is required — a field of it, an example fixture over it — and this is intentional: a rounding policy is a computation's input, not a value that crosses a serialization boundary.

The cases correspond to `+java.math.RoundingMode+`. `+Decimal.divide(dividend, divisor)+` without scale and mode cannot be written — to surface rounding as a domain decision, the same position as "scale is incidental" in <<primitives>>. Division by zero returns `+Decimal | DivisionByZero+`, opened by `+| Decimal as d+` (<<primitive-arm>>).

[[a-scale-is-used-as-the-number-written]]*A scale is used as the number written.* It is never narrowed, wrapped, or replaced by another scale. Rounding at a scale other than the one written would be an answer to a question nobody asked, and one nothing in the model could tell from the answer to the question that was.

A scale is an `+Int+`, which is signed 64-bit (<<primitives>>), and the scales these operations run at are the signed 32-bit numbers — `+-2147483648+` through `+2147483647+`. A scale outside those *aborts*, as an `+Int+` overflow does (<<stdlib-int>>, <<jvm-abort>>).

The number is written here rather than left to whatever performs the arithmetic. A `+Decimal+` is a `+java.math.BigDecimal+` (<<primitives>>), so what a scale can be is already settled by what a `+Decimal+` is, and leaving it unsaid would not have made it any less settled — it would only have meant that a model's meaning was read off an implementation. Two things read this range, the check that says what a quotient lies between (<<invariant-discharge-arithmetic>>) and the run time that divides, and a rule read off one of them would be a rule the other need not hold to.

A scale the run time receives exactly may still name a division whose result it cannot hold. That aborts too, and it is a different failure: the first is about the scale, the second about the value the operation would have had to build. Both reach a boundary as `+ConstraintViolation+` and neither is a business result. The same holds of `+round+` and of the four operators — a sum, a difference or a product whose scale runs off the end of what a `+Decimal+` holds aborts where it is computed.

[[a-division-that-does-not-run-needs-no-scale]]*A zero divisor is answered before the scale is.* `+Decimal.divide(a, 0m, scale, mode)+` is `+DivisionByZero+` whatever the scale, including one the run time could not have divided at: a division that does not run needs no scale to run at. This is about which of two conditions decides the answer and not about which arguments are evaluated — all four are, as at any call, and only `+&&+` and `+||+` decide which of their operands run (<<a-condition-stops-when-its-answer-is-settled>>).

`+Int+` and `+Decimal+` meet only where the conversion is written. `+Decimal.fromInt(n)+` widens an `+Int+`; it is exact, so nothing has to be stated. `+Decimal.toInt(mode, d)+` narrows back, and because that drops a fraction it names the rounding at the call, as `+divide+` does. There is no implicit widening: an arithmetic operator takes two operands of one type (<<primitives>>), so a rate applied to an amount in yen reads `+Decimal.fromInt(netAmount) * taxRate+` — the conversion sits where the value stops being an amount. A `+Decimal+` too large for an `+Int+` aborts, as an `+Int+` overflow does (<<primitives>>).

`+Decimal.round(scale, mode, d)+` sets the number of decimal places, naming the rounding at the call as `+divide+` and `+toInt+` do, and holding its scale to what the run time takes as `+divide+` does (<<a-scale-is-used-as-the-number-written>>). Unlike `+toInt+` the result stays a `+Decimal+`, so it is how a value is prepared for `+String.fromDecimal+` (<<stdlib-string>>), which keeps whatever scale the value carries: a rate of `+0.10m+` times `+100.0m+` is `+10.000m+`, and `+round(0, HALF_UP, d)+` is what makes it read as `+"10"+`.

`+round+` and `+toInt+` each work on one `+Decimal+`, so the subject comes last and the rounding policy leads — `+d |> Decimal.round(2, HALF_UP)+`. `+divide+` is a binary operation, so its two operands come first and the policy follows them. The `+(scale, mode)+` pair therefore sits in front of the subject in one and behind the operands in the other; that is the two rules meeting, not an inconsistency (<<pipe>>).

`+abs+` / `+min+` / `+max+` / `+clamp+` are the same `+Basics+` arithmetic `+Int+` carries (<<stdlib-int>>), on the same reasoning: `+<+` and `+-+` are defined for `+Decimal+`, so these are ordinary core `+let+`s. `+abs+` compares against `+0.0m+`, so a value whose scale differs still answers by numeric value, as `+compare+` does. `+clamp+` answers one of the three values written, never a re-scaled copy.

The four operations may also be written with the operators `{plus}` `+-+` `+*+` `+/+`. Each of them aborts where the value it would answer is one no `+Decimal+` holds (<<a-scale-is-used-as-the-number-written>>) — a product's scale is the sum of its factors' scales, so that is where the end of the range is reached first. The operator `+/+` divides with a default rounding — significant digits (about 29) and HALF_UP — so `+10m / 3m+` is `+3.3333…+` and does not abort. Division by zero *aborts*. To make rounding an explicit domain decision, or to receive division by zero as a case, use the function `+Decimal.divide(dividend, divisor, scale, mode)+`.

[#stdlib-list]
=== List

==== Construction

A list is made by a literal or a guard comprehension.

[,text]
----
[e1, e2, ...]         // literal. One or more elements
[<element> | <condition>, ...]     // guard comprehension. One element if all conditions hold, empty otherwise
----

An empty literal `+[]+` may also be written. It has no element type of its own; its type is decided by context — the counterpart of `{plus}{plus}`, the other branch of an `+if+` / `+match+`, the seed a `+fold+` grows, or a `+List<T>+` a field or return value requires. An empty list does not depend on elements at runtime, so this is always sound. To transcribe a DSL bullet list, a guard comprehension (empty if the condition is false) is natural.

`{plus}{plus}` is concatenation. Both sides MUST be two lists or two strings: two lists give a list, two strings give a string (`+"a" {plus}{plus} "b"+` is `+"ab"+`, the operator form of `+String.append+`; <<stdlib-string>>). A mixed pair (a list and a string, or either with a non-list non-string) is a type error.

The element type is folded if the same, and widened to a sum of cases if different data (the same direction as <<sum-data>>).

[,text]
----
[HighValueTravel { threshold = threshold }] ++ [ClientCost]      // List<HighValueTravel | ClientCost>
----

Guard comprehension and `{plus}{plus}` exist to transcribe conditions the spec DSL listed as bullets.

[,text]
----
// the spec DSL (chapter 10) lists reasons as bullets:
//   reason 1: estimatedCost is at least 100,000 yen / reason 2: applicant is Staff / reason 3: cost borne by the other party
   [HighValueTravel { threshold = threshold } | estimatedCost.value >= threshold.value]
++ [InsufficientAuthority { value = applicant.rank } | isStaff(applicant)]
++ [ClientCost                 | isClientBorne(costBearer)]
----

Each reason makes a 0- or 1-element list and concatenates. *One DSL bullet line becomes one Souther line.* The result is `+List<HighValueTravel | InsufficientAuthority | ClientCost>+`, matching the three cases of `+PreApprovalReason+`. A unit data is constructed by name alone (<<unit-data>>), so `+ClientCost+` is written directly.

Note that `+applicant.rank == Staff+` cannot be written as a condition. `+==+` works only between the same type (<<primitives>>), and `+Rank+` and its case `+Staff+` are different types. Case judgment is done with `+match+`, and to make it a predicate wrap it in a helper `+let+` (the `+isStaff+` above). Ordering (`+>=+`) exists only for `+Int+` / `+String+` / `+Decimal+` and the temporals, so compare `+.value+`, not `+Amount+` (a newtype) directly.

==== Operations

[,text]
----
map  filter  fold  foldRight  all  any  length  get  mapIndexed
reverse  contains  sum  product  append  concat  flatMap  isEmpty  sort  rangeInclusive
distinct  distinctBy  allDistinctBy  partition  groupBy  indexBy  filterMap  max  min  find  sortBy
take  drop  zipShortest  unzip
----

All are called through the qualifier `+List+` (<<stdlib>>). `+List.get+` returns `+Option<T>+`. `+List.map+` / `+List.filter+` / `+List.fold+` / `+List.all+` / `+List.any+` / `+List.mapIndexed+` take a block (<<blocks>>). Import them as `+import List (map, all )+` to write unqualified thereafter.

`+rangeInclusive(from, to)+` is the consecutive integers from `+from+` to `+to+`, both ends included; `+from+` above `+to+` gives the empty list, and a span longer than a list can hold *aborts* before the walk starts rather than filling memory until it dies. Whether a bare `+range+` closes at `+to+` or stops before it is a thing languages disagree on, so the name carries the answer — the same reason `+zipShortest+` carries its own, and the counterpart of the half-open rule for *extracting* a range (`+String.slice+`, <<stdlib-string>>). It is the one list that is neither written out nor read from outside, so a walk over positions — the levels of a bay, the digits of a code — has something to walk. Like `+reverse+` and `+sort+` it is a primitive: building it from `+fold+` would need a list to fold over in the first place.

`+flatMap(f, xs)+` maps each element to a list and joins the results in one pass; writing it as `+map+` then `+concat+` builds an intermediate `+List<List<'b>>+`, which is why the shape is otherwise hand-rolled as a fold with `{plus}{plus}` in the step.

`+foldRight(step, seed, xs)+` walks from the end. Its step takes the element first and the accumulator second — `+('a, 'acc) -> 'acc+` — the shape a right fold has and the opposite of `+fold+`'s. It is implemented as the left fold over the reversed list with the step's arguments swapped, which *is* the right fold rather than an approximation of one: Souther is strict and a list is finite, so the two orders of combination give the same value. Time is O(n), and it holds one reversed copy, so extra space is O(n) too. `+fold+` is the left one and carries no `+Left+` in its name: `+Map+` and `+Set+` publish no first and no last element, so `+fold+` is the name they can share, and only `+List+` has a direction for a second name to be about (<<stdlib-map>>, <<stdlib-set>>).

`+zipShortest(xs, ys)+` pairs two lists element by element and `+unzip(ps)+` takes the pairs apart again. A length mismatch truncates to the shorter list rather than aborting, and the name says so because languages disagree — Elm's `+map2+` truncates, F#'s `+List.zip+` raises. Souther has tuples, so a two-list map is written `+zipShortest(xs, ys) |> map(f)+`. The tuples stay inside the behavior — a tuple has no external form (<<tuple>>).

[,text]
----
import List ( map, all )

map(x -> fixLine(x), xs)
all(r -> isMinor(r), reasons)
----

If an injected thing is called from within a block, its requirement appears in the enclosing behavior's `+depends on+` (<<depends-on>>).

None of these is a compiler primitive. `+List.fold+` is a recursive `+let+` in `+souther.list+`: it walks the list with `+List.get+`, threads an accumulator, and takes its step as a function value (<<blocks>>). Its tail-recursive self-call is compiled to a loop, so it reads as a loop would (<<stdlib>>). `+map+` / `+filter+` / `+all+` / `+any+` are `+let+`s derived from it, expanded at the call site: `+map+` / `+filter+` seed with an empty list `+[]+` and grow it, `+all+` / `+any+` fold a `+Bool+`. `+mapIndexed(f, xs)+` is `+map+` that also hands `+f+` the element's 0-based index: `+fold+` carries no index, so it threads one in a pair accumulator `+(i, ys)+` and drops the counter at the end — the caller writes a weighted sum or positional transform (`+mapIndexed((i, c) -> w(i) * d(c)) |> sum+`) without hand-rolling that `+(i, acc)+` fold. `+List.length+` / `+String.length+` and `+List.get+` / `+Map.get+` are separate module functions, reached by their qualifier.

In the lower row, `+append(a, b)+` joins two lists and `+concat(xss)+` flattens a list of them — the same split `+String.append+` and `+String.concat+` make (<<stdlib-string>>), with `{plus}{plus}` as the common form of the first. `+contains+` folds with `+==+` so it works for comparable elements, and `+isEmpty+` looks at length. `+contains+` is the word `+Set.contains+` and `+String.contains+` use, because it is the same question; `+Map+` asks it as `+containsKey+`, which says which half of an entry is being looked for. `+sort+`, `+reverse+`, `+sum+` and `+product+` are primitives. Self-language sorting needs, besides comparison, a place to hold intermediate results, which `+fold+` does not give; `+sort+` sorts by the element's natural order.

`+sum(xs)+` / `+product(xs)+` answer the total and the running product of a list of numbers. The element MUST be `+Int+` or `+Decimal+` — the two types arithmetic is defined for (<<stdlib-int>>, <<stdlib-decimal>>) — and the answer has the element's type. A newtype over one of them is not a numeric element: it declares no addition and no zero of its own, so a list of them is summed by mapping to the wrapped value first (`+sum(map(h -> h.value, hours))+`). They are primitives rather than folds because a fold written in the language would have to write its seed, and a written seed is `+0+` or `+0.0m+` — one of the two types, never both. Over the empty list the answer is that seed (`+sum([]) == 0+`, `+product([]) == 1+`, as in Elm), and which of the two comes from the position the call is written in — the field it fills, the annotated binding it feeds, the declared output it returns. A `+?+` field asks for the value it wraps rather than for an optional (<<optional>>), so it says which as a plain field does. Written where nothing states a type, it is a compile error rather than a default: choosing `+Int+` there would make a numeric-default rule out of one library function, and the annotation says what the domain meant. Written where the position states some other type, it is a type mismatch — nothing about the element is unknown, and what is wrong is that a sum does not go there. Elements MUST be an ordered value — one of the ordered types (`+Int+` / `+String+` / `+Decimal+` / `+Date+` / `+Time+` / `+DateTime+` / `+Instant+`; <<primitives>>), an enumeration, ordered by its declaration (<<primitives>>), or a single-value newtype over any of those, which is ordered by the value it wraps (<<primitives>>) and carries that ordering on the JVM (<<jvm-product>>). Passing a list of product data to `+sort+` is a compile error (a product has no ordering, so it is rejected up front rather than at runtime; to sort them, map to an ordered field first with `+List.map+`, or order by that field with `+sortBy+`).

The third row adds more list operations. `+distinct+` keeps the first occurrence of each element and drops later duplicates, folding a `+Set+` of what it has seen; `+partition+` splits into `+(kept, rest)+` by a predicate; `+groupBy+` buckets elements into a `+Map<'k, List<'a>>+` by a key; `+indexBy(key, xs)+` keys them one entry each into a `+Map<'k, 'a>+`, which is how a list of rows read from outside becomes something a caller looks up by key instead of scanning per lookup — two elements with the same key collapse and the later wins, as `+Map.fromList+` does, so state the key's uniqueness with `+allDistinctBy+` when it matters. `+filterMap(f, xs)+` maps and drops in one pass: `+f+` answers with an optional and only what is there joins the result, which is how an optional field becomes a plain list (`+filterMap(i -> i.assignee, issues)+`) without a stand-in value to filter on afterwards. Its step is the one stdlib signature that writes an optional as a type, `+('a) -> 'b?+` (<<algebraic-types>>). These five are self-language `+let+`s that grow their result from within `+List.fold+`. `+max+` / `+min+` return the largest / smallest as an `+Option+` — `+None+` on an empty list — and compare by natural order, so like `+sort+` the element MUST be ordered; `+find+` returns the first element satisfying a predicate as an `+Option+`. `+sortBy(key, xs)+` orders by a projected ordered key rather than the element itself: unlike `+sort+`, whose comparison is built in, its key is a runtime function value, so the sort carries an actual `+Fn+`. It is stable, so ordering by two keys is a sort by the secondary key followed by one by the primary; a key MUST be a single ordered value, not a tuple. `+max+` / `+min+` / `+find+` / `+sortBy+` are primitives (they step outside `+fold+`); there is no `+head+` — a domain reads the first element with `+List.get(0, xs)+`. `+distinct+`, `+distinctBy(key, xs)+` and `+allDistinctBy(key, xs)+` are one family under one word. `+distinctBy+` keeps the first element of each key rather than of each element; `+allDistinctBy+` holds when nothing would be dropped — the "this projection is a unique id" invariant, written directly instead of comparing a `+distinct+` of the projected keys against the length by hand. Deriving from `+fold+`, they may stand in an invariant, as `+distinct+` itself may.

`+take(n, xs)+` keeps the first `+n+` elements and `+drop(n, xs)+` skips them — the top-N of a ranking and the rest of it. Both clamp instead of failing: a count of 0 or less takes nothing / drops nothing, and a count past the end takes the whole list / leaves it empty. Like `+mapIndexed+` they thread a pair accumulator `+(i, ys)+` through `+List.fold+` and keep the elements whose index falls on the wanted side of `+n+`.

[#stdlib-map]
=== Map

A `+Map+` is immutable and string-keyed (<<collections>>). The accessors read; the building operations return a fresh map.

[,text]
----
get  containsKey  keys  values                   // read
empty  singleton  insert  remove  isEmpty  size  // build
toList  fromList                                 // convert to / from entry tuples
fold  mapValues  filterEntries                   // higher-order
updateIfPresent  updateOrInsert                  // higher-order, by key
union  intersection  difference                  // key algebra
----

`+get+` returns `+Option<V>+`. `+empty+` is the empty map: like the empty list `+[]+`, its key and value types are fixed by context (a field or return position, a written binding type, the other branch of an `+if+` / `+match+`, or the first `+insert+`), so it is a *value* and not a function — written `+Map.empty+`, never `+Map.empty()+` (<<fn-declaration>>). `+singleton(k, v)+` is a one-entry map; `+insert(k, v, m)+` adds an entry or overwrites the existing one; `+remove(k, m)+` drops one, leaving `+m+` unchanged when the key is absent. Because a `+Map+` is immutable, `+insert+` / `+remove+` return a new map rather than mutating. `+size+` is the entry count and `+isEmpty+` whether there are none. The map operated on is last (<<pipe>>), so `+m |> Map.insert(k, v)+` reads left to right.

`+toList(m)+` is the entries as a `+List<(String, V)>+` — a list of key/value tuples in the map's iteration order — and `+fromList(entries)+` builds a map back (a later entry wins on a duplicate key). Iteration order is not specified by the language. It is not the order the entries were inserted in and is not to be read as one: no particular order is part of a `+Map+`'s semantics, and two maps that are equal are not guaranteed to iterate in the same order, so a program must not depend on the order it gets. A boundary map's members *are* specified: they are written in ascending order of their rendered keys (<<collections>>), which is not `+toList+`'s order — what is written out there is keyed by the rendered string and not by `+K+`, and a map that has been through an `+insert+` and one that has only been decoded are not walked alike. The tuple type in these signatures is allowed because a signature is not a codec boundary (<<tuple>>).

These are the operations that take a step function. `+Map.fold(step, seed, m)+` is layered on `+List.fold+` over the entries (`+toList+`) rather than walking the map itself, and the entries combine in the map's own iteration order, which is the unspecified one above. Its step is `+(acc, key, value)+`. It is `+fold+` and not `+foldLeft+` because a map publishes no first and no last entry: there is no left here for a right to be the counterpart of, and `+List+` is where that distinction exists (<<stdlib-list>>).

`+mapValues(f, m)+` keeps the keys and rewrites the values, growing a fresh map through `+fold+`; `+filterEntries(keep, m)+` keeps the entries its predicate holds for, the same way. Both steps see the key and the value — `+(key, value)+` — and the names say which half of an entry each operation is about, since a bare `+map+` or `+filter+` on a map leaves a reader to guess whether the keys, the values or the entries are what the step answers for. Without `+filterEntries+` a caller goes out through `+toList+`, filters as a list, and comes back through `+fromList+`, leaving the map type behind for two steps and re-hashing every survivor.

`+updateIfPresent(key, f, m)+` rewrites the value under one key with `+f+` and does nothing when the key is absent — which the name says and a bare `+update+` would not. Its step is `+('a) -> 'a+`: `+Option+` is not surface-writable, so a step cannot return one — insert a new key with `+insert+` and drop one with `+remove+`. `+updateOrInsert(key, default, f, m)+` collapses insert-if-absent / modify-if-present into one call, named as the two branches in the order they are written: it applies `+f+` to a present key's value and inserts `+default+` for an absent one. Like `+updateIfPresent+` it keeps `+Option+` internal — the absent branch takes a plain value. Typical use is accumulation, e.g. counting `+updateOrInsert(k, 1, n -> n {plus} 1, m)+`.

`+union+` / `+intersection+` / `+difference+` are the key algebra, named as `+Set+`'s are (<<stdlib-set>>) and as the set operations they are. All three answer with entries taken from `+a+`: `+union(a, b)+` adds `+b+`'s entries under keys `+a+` does not have, so `+a+` wins a collision; `+intersection(a, b)+` keeps `+a+`'s entries whose key is also in `+b+`; `+difference(a, b)+` keeps `+a+`'s entries whose key is not. Being binary operations they keep their operands in the written order and take no subject last, so `+a |> Map.difference(b)+` is not the way to write `+a+` minus `+b+` (<<pipe>>). Values from `+b+` are never merged into `+a+`'s — combining two values under one key is a domain decision, so it is written with `+fold+` and `+updateOrInsert+` where that rule lives.

[#stdlib-option]
=== Option

`+List.get+` / `+Map.get+` / `+List.find+` and a `+T?+` field all yield an `+Option+` (<<algebraic-types>>). Before this module the only way to read one was a two-arm `+match+`, which ends the value pipe (<<pipe>>); these functions let the optional flow through `+|>+` instead.

[,text]
----
map  withDefault
----

`+withDefault(default, opt)+` reads the option: the wrapped value when present, `+default+` when absent. `+map(f, opt)+` transforms a present value with `+f+` and leaves an absent one absent, so its result is again an `+Option+`; `+f+` is a plain `+('a) -> 'b+` and never sees the absent case. Both take the option last (<<pipe>>), so it threads through the pipe: `+Map.get(k, m) |> Option.map(v -> v.value) |> Option.withDefault("None")+`.

`+Option+` stays non-surface: the caller never writes `+Option<T>+` nor builds a `+Some+` — the optional is inferred and unnamed as it flows here, and `+map+`'s result stays inferred as it reaches `+withDefault+`. There is no `+andThen+`: its step would have to return an `+Option+`, which no user function can build under that rule (insert-if-present style folding stays with `+Map.updateOrInsert+` / `+update+`, which keep `+Option+` internal). For the same reason `+map+` cannot be self-hosted — a body cannot construct a `+Some+` — so it is a compiler built-in, while `+withDefault+`, which only reads, is an ordinary helper.

[#stdlib-set]
=== Set

A `+Set<'a>+` is unordered with no duplicates, compared by value equality (<<collections>>). It is immutable, so the building operations return a fresh set.

[,text]
----
empty  singleton  insert  remove  contains       // build / query
union  intersection  difference                   // algebra
isEmpty  size  toList  fromList                   // convert
fold  map  filter  partition                      // higher-order
----

`+empty+` is the empty set — like `+[]+` and `+Map.empty+`, its element type is fixed by context (a field or return position, a written binding type, the other branch of an `+if+` / `+match+`, or the first `+insert+`), so it is a *value* and not a function — written `+Set.empty+`, never `+Set.empty()+`. `+singleton(v)+` is a one-element set; `+insert(v, s)+` adds `+v+`; `+remove(v, s)+` drops it (leaving `+s+` unchanged when absent); `+contains(v, s)+` tests membership. `+union+` / `+intersection+` / `+difference(a, b)+` are the set algebra (`+difference+` is `+a+` minus `+b+`). Being binary operations they keep their operands in the written order and take no subject last (<<pipe>>). `+toList(s)+` lists the members in an order the language does not specify and `+fromList(xs)+` builds a set, dropping duplicates. That order is not the order the members were inserted in and is not to be read as one: no particular order is part of a `+Set+`'s semantics, and two sets that are equal are not guaranteed to list in the same order, so a program must not depend on the order it gets. The set operated on is last (<<pipe>>), so `+s |> Set.insert(x)+` reads left to right.

`+fold+` / `+map+` / `+filter+` / `+partition+` are the operations that take a step function. As with `+Map+`, a `+Set+` adds no loop of its own: each one goes out through `+toList+`, uses the `+List+` combinator, and comes back through `+fromList+`, so `+List.fold+` stays the single primitive. `+map+` may shrink the set — two elements that land on the same image collapse, since a set holds no duplicates — and `+partition(keep, s)+` answers the pair `+(kept, rest)+`, the shape `+List.partition+` gives. `+fold+` runs over `+toList+`'s order, which is unspecified, so a step whose result depends on that order is a mistake the compiler cannot catch: combine with something associative and commutative.

A `+Set+` field decodes from a JSON array and deduplicates it, and encodes back to an array whose members are in ascending order of their own external representation (<<collections>>). That is *not* `+toList+`'s order: the boundary's is specified and a `+Set+`'s own is not, and only what is written has a representation to be ordered by — a `+Set+` inside a behavior body may hold a tuple, which has none (<<tuple>>). The input array's order has no semantic significance and is not guaranteed to be preserved.

Which member a `+Set+` holds, where two equal elements are *written* differently, is not specified: equality decides that they are one member (<<equality>>) and the set keeps one of them, and nothing fixes which. It does not reach the boundary — a `+Decimal+` is written as its amount, so `+1.0+` and `+1.00+` are one member and one number written (<<primitives>>) — but `+toList+` hands back the one the set kept, and a program that reads a scale off it is reading something the language does not decide.

[#stdlib-bool]
=== Bool

[,text]
----
not
----

`+not+` is the *first self-host function* placed in `+souther.bool+`. It is not a primitive but written in Souther as `+let not (b: Bool) = if b then false else true+`, called qualified as `+Bool.not+` (bare via `+import Bool (not )+`). Being non-recursive, it is inline-expanded at the call site and no dedicated class is generated. Negation is written with this `+not+` — there is no prefix `+!+` operator (redundant, since `+not+` exists).

[#stdlib-date]
=== Date

[,text]
----
addDays  addMonths  addYears  daysBetween  year  month  day  fromParts
----

[#temporal-literal]
A temporal is written as its type applied to the text: `+Date("2026-07-25")+`, `+Time("09:00")+`, `+DateTime("2026-07-25T09:00")+`, `+Instant("2026-07-25T00:00:00Z")+`. The argument MUST be a written string: the compiler parses it where it stands, so malformed text fails the build rather than a run, and an `+example+` fixture — which holds only written values (<<example-evaluable>>) — can carry a temporal at all.

`+Time+` and `+DateTime+` are written to the second, and text carrying a fraction of one is <<e1322>> — they hold none, so admitting the text would drop part of it (<<a-local-temporal-is-held-to-the-second>>). `+Instant+` is written in UTC, the `+Z+` form. Text carrying a numeric offset is <<e1322>> — not because the offset is wrong, since `+09:30:00+09:00+` and `+00:30:00Z+` are one moment and either determines it, but because a written value is written the way the value is written back (<<fixture-is-written-not-carried>>) and an `+Instant+` is written in UTC. An offset is a *spelling*, not a zone: a zone is a place with rules about when its offset changes, and neither is a thing this language names. A boundary reads the offset form and answers the same moment (<<an-instant-carries-what-a-timestamp-said>>).

[[a-leap-second-is-no-moment]]Text naming a leap second — a `+:60+` second — MUST be refused, where it is written and where it arrives. The language has no moment for one, and the reading that would otherwise be taken is the second before it, which is a *different* moment than the text says. This is the one place a written temporal is refused for what it means rather than for its form, and it is refused for the reason everything else in this section is: a value nothing downstream can tell from the value that was sent is worse than no value.

A newtype over a temporal takes the temporal it wraps: `+data 貸出日 = Date+` is built `+貸出日(Date("2026-07-25"))+`, in a fixture as in a model body. The string alone is a `+String+` there as anywhere (<<fixture-is-written-not-carried>>).

This is the only form for *writing* a temporal out. It is not the only way to *reach* one: `+fromParts+` below builds a `+Date+` from three Ints a model already holds, and answers a case where they name no day.

`+Date+` is a local calendar date (<<primitives>>). Calendar arithmetic is pure and belongs in the domain, not behind an injected behavior (<<injected-behavior>>) — only reading the *current* date needs the outside world. `+addDays(n, d)+` / `+addMonths(n, d)+` / `+addYears(n, d)+` shift `+d+` by `+n+` units (`+n+` may be negative), following `+java.time.LocalDate+`, which clamps the end of a month (`+addMonths(1, 2026-01-31)+` is `+2026-02-28+`). `+daysBetween(from, to)+` is the whole days from `+from+` to `+to+`, negative when `+to+` precedes `+from+`. The date operated on is last (<<pipe>>), so `+d |> Date.addDays(3)+` reads left to right.

[[a-shift-off-the-end-of-a-temporal-aborts]]A shift that leaves the range a temporal can hold MUST *abort*, as an `+Int+` overflow does (<<stdlib-int>>): it is a model bug and not a business result, so it throws `+ConstraintViolation+` and does not wrap, clamp, or answer a case (<<jvm-abort>>). A year past what a date holds and a count too large to add are one abort and not two, because what a caller can do about either is the same. This is the arithmetic and not <<a-temporal-built-from-parts-is-refused-where-the-parts-name-none>>: parts are a question about whether three numbers name a day, and a shift is a computation that ran out of room.

`+year(d)+` / `+month(d)+` / `+day(d)+` read a date's parts as `+Int+`s — the month 1 to 12, the day 1 to 31. `+year+` / `+month+` / `+day+` all answer `+Int+`: `+addMonths+` counts in months, and a named month would have to be converted back before adding one. There is no `+dayOfWeek+`: neither language answers that with a bare number, so it needs a named seven-case type.

A month boundary follows from `+day+` and belongs in the domain:

[,souther]
----
let monthStart (d: Date): Date = Date.addDays(1 - Date.day(d), d)
let endOfMonth (d: Date): Date = Date.addDays(0 - 1, Date.addMonths(1, monthStart(d)))
----

`+fromParts(year, month, day)+` reads three Ints as a day, answering `+Date | NotADate+`. It is the inverse of `+year+` / `+month+` / `+day+` and normalises nothing (<<a-temporal-built-from-parts-is-refused-where-the-parts-name-none>>), so a model holding a year and a month it took in as `+Int+` can say the date they name, and is told when they name none:

[,souther]
----
data 診療年月 =
    { 年: Int
    , 月: Int
    }
    invariant 月 >= 1 && 月 <= 12

let 時効内か (m: 診療年月, 申請日: Date): Bool =
    match Date.fromParts(m.年, m.月, 1) with
        | Date as 診療月の初日 -> 申請日 < Date.addYears(2, Date.addMonths(1, 診療月の初日))
        | NotADate -> false
----

One case covers both a day the calendar does not have and a year a date cannot hold, as `+NotANumber+` covers both text that is no number and a number too large to hold (<<stdlib-string>>): what a caller does with either is the same.

`+NotADate+` and `+NotATime+` are written in a `+match+` arm and nowhere else, as `+DivisionByZero+` and `+NotANumber+` are (<<algebraic-types>>). They are the library's names for the library's own refusals, so a `+let+` that wants to pass one of them on to its caller names a case of its own and answers that.

[#stdlib-time]
=== Time

[,text]
----
fromParts  hour  minute  second
----

`+Time+` is a time of day to the second, with no date and no zone (<<primitives>>) — what a model holds when it names an opening hour, where writing a `+DateTime+` would invent a day the domain does not have. `+hour+` / `+minute+` / `+second+` read the parts as Ints, and `+fromParts(hour, minute, second)+` reads three back into a `+Time+`, answering `+NotATime+` for hour 24, minute 60 and the leap second.

There is no time arithmetic. Adding to a time of day has to say what happens at midnight — whether 23:30 plus an hour is 00:30 or is nothing — and that is a decision about a domain rather than about a clock. A model that holds the day as well adds on the `+DateTime+`, where the day is there to carry the answer. A model that holds only a time of day says what crossing midnight means as a rule of its own — telling it to hold a `+DateTime+` instead would have it invent a date, which is the thing a `+Time+` exists to avoid.

[#stdlib-datetime]
=== DateTime

[,text]
----
addMinutes  addHours  addDays  minutesBetween  toDate  toTime  fromDateAndTime
----

`+DateTime+` is a local date-time with no zone (<<primitives>>); the zone stays a boundary concern. `+addMinutes+` / `+addHours+` / `+addDays+` shift by their unit; `+minutesBetween(from, to)+` is the whole minutes between (negative when `+to+` precedes `+from+`). As with `+Date+`, the date-time operated on is last (<<pipe>>).

`+toDate+` / `+toTime+` / `+fromDateAndTime+` are the three that say a `+DateTime+` is a `+Date+` and a `+Time+` (<<a-date-time-is-a-date-and-a-time>>). `+fromDateAndTime(d, t)+` cannot fail and has no case to write an arm for: whatever could have been wrong about the parts was refused where the `+Date+` and the `+Time+` were built. Its name is not `+fromParts+`, which the other two temporals use for the Ints they read — a `+DateTime+` comes apart two ways, into a date and a time or into six numbers, so `+fromParts+` would leave a reader to work out which it takes.

[#stdlib-instant]
=== Instant

[,text]
----
----

`+Instant+` has no operations, and the empty list above is not a section waiting to be filled: it has no parts to read without a zone, so it has none to be built from either, and the two directions are absent together (<<an-instant-carries-what-a-timestamp-said>>). What the missing zone rules out is the *calendar*. An operation that stays on the timeline — the elapsed time between two, a shift along it — needs no zone, and none is here because nothing has asked for one.

A model still holds one, compares two (<<primitives>>), keys a `+Map+` by one (<<collections>>) and writes one as a literal (<<temporal-literal>>). To read one as a local date-time it declares a behavior with no implementation and the Java side supplies the zone, the way the current time is supplied (<<injected-behavior>>):

[,souther]
----
behavior 地方日時 : (at: Instant) -> DateTime
----

Decoders and Encoders do not appear in Souther's syntax. Derived from a data's shape, they are generated by the ClassFile backend as Raoh Decoders / Encoders (<<codec-generation>>, <<encoder>>). Primitive parsing, error accumulation, discriminator dispatch, and encoding are handled by Raoh combinators; there is no facade on the souther-runtime side. A custom boundary codec (<<custom-codec>>) uses Raoh directly from the Java side.

[#jvm-output]
== JVM output

A declaration is emitted as classes and methods, and a class file has structural limits a declaration can be written past. What the compiler emits MUST fit inside them, since past any of the three the class cannot be loaded at all:

* [[generated-methods-fit-jvm-parameter-slots]]a generated method MUST fit the JVM argument-slot limit, which is what bounds how many fields a `+data+` may declare and how many parameters and dependencies a `+behavior+` may take;
* [[generated-methods-fit-jvm-code-size]]a generated method's code MUST fit the JVM code-size limit;
* [[generated-classes-fit-jvm-constant-pool]]a generated class's constant pool MUST fit what a class file can address and write out.

Two more come from names rather than sizes:

* [[no-two-declarations-become-one-class]]no two declarations of a module may emit one class. A behavior capitalizes into a class name (<<jvm-behavior>>), a union output becomes a sealed interface (<<jvm-sum>>), and a member joining a union from outside becomes a bridge case (<<bridge-case>>) — three ways to arrive at a name, and a model may already have declared it;
* [[a-field-does-not-collide-with-an-object-method]]a field MUST NOT be named after a no-argument method of `+Object+`, whose accessor the generated class already has (<<jvm-product>>).

The first is decided at the declaration, before anything is emitted. The other two are not known until the code is written, and are reported as the definition being emitted when the limit was met.

[#target-jdk]
=== Target JDK

The artifacts target Java 25 or later, which is also what the compiler is built and run on.

A name reaches the class file as it was written. What a class file may hold is an unqualified name, which admits a Unicode character generally and excludes `+.+`, `+;+`, `+[+` and `+/+` (and `+<+` and `+>+` in a method name) — none of which spells a Souther name (<<identifier>>). So a model written in Japanese is emitted in Japanese, and nothing is escaped or romanized.

What a class file holds and what Java source can write are two different limits, and neither contains the other. Souther's alphabet and Java's identifier rules do not coincide: each admits characters the other does not, and each is read against its own Unicode version. A name outside what Java writes is still emitted and still loads; what it costs is that Java code cannot name it — and Java names a generated type where it implements an injected behavior or calls a decoder (<<java-example>>). A model whose types are reached from Java is spelled in characters Java can write, which is the author's decision at the boundary and not a rule the compiler enforces on every name in the language.

The class-file version of the generated `+.class+` is fixed at Java 25 (major 69). It MUST NOT be left to the default of the JDK running the backend: pinning is what makes the floor a decision rather than a property of whichever JDK ran the build. souther-runtime is on the artifact side and is compiled to the same target.

[#jvm-nullness]
=== Nullness

No generated value is ever null. Absence is `+Option+` (<<optional>>), a failure is a case of the output union (<<unmarked-sum>>), and a collection holds neither a null key nor a null value — so no accessor, factory, codec or `+apply+` hands one back.

Every generated class carries JSpecify's `+@NullMarked+`, and souther-runtime carries it on its own packages, so a consumer's compiler knows that too. It matters most where a compiler acts on it: Kotlin reads the annotation off the class file and types what it names as non-null, where without it the type is a platform type and a null check is neither required nor possible. The annotation is read by name, so the JSpecify jar need not be on the consumer's classpath.

A record's component accessor is the exception. Kotlin types it from the record component and reaches it without applying the class's marking, so a component read would be a platform type again while every other member of the same class is not. Each accessor therefore says `+@NonNull+` on its return type itself, at every position of it — the `+List+` and the element it holds — and the `+Record+` component repeats it, for a reader that goes through reflection rather than the accessor. A primitive return (an `+Int+` accessor returns `+long+`) states nothing, having no nullness to state.

It rides on each class rather than on a `+package-info+` of the module's package. That package is where a consumer's own `+package-info.java+` sits, and a generated class of the same name would lose to that declaration — the marking would then reach only the modules that own no Java of their own.

A generated method whose descriptor names a generic type carries the `+Signature+` for it, so the type argument survives to a reader. `+__construct+` returns `+Result<T, String>+`; a raw `+Result+` would be a platform type again on the Kotlin side, where the class carrying it says everything else is not.

The few places that do admit a null are annotated one by one — a `+java.util.Map+` implementation's `+get+`, which returns null for an absent key because that interface says so.

[#jvm-product]
=== Product data

Generated as a record: a final class extending `+java.lang.Record+` whose `+Record+` attribute names its fields as components, in declaration order, with a non-public canonical constructor. Fields taken in by a `+...+` spread are laid flatly in the generated class.

[,text]
----
public final class Submitted extends java.lang.Record {
    private final Employee applicant;         // from ...TravelRequestCommon
    private final Amount estimatedCost;       // from ...TravelRequestCommon
    // ...
    private final LocalDateTime submittedAt;

    Submitted(...) { ... }                    // canonical, package-private

    public Employee applicant() { ... }       // one accessor per component
    public String toString() { ... }          // Submitted[applicant=..., ...]
}
----

Being a record is what a reader acts on without being told anything about Souther. javac deconstructs the value in a record pattern, and does so for a case of a sum in an exhaustive `+switch+` — the same way it reads the runtime's `+Ok+` / `+Err+`, which are records too. Kotlin reads each component as a property, so `+issue.id.value+` is written instead of `+issue.id().value()+`; without the `+Record+` attribute Kotlin resolves the backing field instead and reports that it cannot be accessed, naming a field where the accessor was meant.

Every data gets a public read accessor per field (<<field-visibility>>), so an exposed one is readable from another module and from Java; because module = package, package-private cannot read across the boundary. A data the module keeps to itself has accessors too — a component is read through its accessor, and the generated code of the module reads a field the same way — but its class is package-private, so nothing outside can call them. The constructor stays non-public either way, so a value can be read but not constructed.

`+toString+` prints the record form, `+Submitted[applicant=..., estimatedCost=...]+`. It is generated rather than inherited: `+java.lang.Record+` declares it abstract, so a class without one would throw `+AbstractMethodError+` when anything printed the value. `+equals+` / `+hashCode+` are generated over the components as well, and compare a `+Decimal+` by value rather than by scale (<<primitives>>) — a record's shape does not dictate what equality means.

A field MUST NOT be named after a no-argument method of `+Object+` (`+toString+`, `+hashCode+`, `+getClass+`, `+clone+`, `+finalize+`, `+notify+`, `+notifyAll+`, `+wait+`): the accessor would collide with a method the class already has. It is a compile error where the field is written, as a record component of that name is in Java. `+equals+` is not among them — `+equals()+` and `+equals(Object)+` are different methods — and is allowed, as Java allows it as a component name.

A framework that constructs records reflectively — Jackson, given a generated type as a request body — can reach the canonical constructor and build a value without passing the invariant `+__construct+` runs. Construction authority is governed by declaration rather than by visibility, and the same value was already reachable through `+setAccessible+`; what is guaranteed is what a decoder admits (<<decoder>>), not what reflection cannot forge.

A single-value newtype over an ordered type implements `+Comparable+` against itself, comparing the value it wraps. That is what `+sort+` / `+max+` / `+min+` / `+sortBy+` compare by, and it makes the type usable as a `+TreeMap+` key or a `+Collections.sort+` element from Java. Where it wraps an enumeration the comparison reads the sum's declaration order, the case values having no `+Comparable+` of their own (<<primitives>>) — so the wrapper is where a Java reader gets one. A newtype over an unordered type (`+Bool+`) and a product data get no `+Comparable+`.

[#jvm-collection-equality]
=== A collection's equality, seen from Java

A generated data, a newtype and a tuple compare with their own kind and nothing else, so their `+equals+` is the language's value equality (<<equality>>) and a Java reader sees exactly what Souther means.

A `+List+`, a `+Set+` and a `+Map+` implement the JDK interfaces of those names, and a foreign implementation may be on the other side of an `+equals+`. A `+List+` therefore keeps the `+List+` contract — elements compared by their own `+equals+` — because a list that compared them by value equality would be equal to a JDK list that was not equal back. The language's question is answered by a separate pair of methods the runtime calls.

Reading one is not comparing two. `+Map.get+`, `+Map.containsKey+` and `+Set.member+` read the collection they are given by the index it carries: every collection Souther builds is indexed by value equality, and so is the `+Set+` a field decodes into. A `+Map+` a field decodes into is a JDK map, and a boundary map's key is a `+String+`, a temporal, an enumeration or a newtype over one of those (<<collections>>) — for each of those the two agree. A `+java.util.Map+` handed in from Java with some other key is answered by its own index. Comparing two collections normalizes both first, since Java's equality is finer and a foreign collection may hold two elements the language calls one.

A `+Set+` and a `+Map+` cannot do both. Which element a set holds and which key a map finds is decided by the hash the trie is indexed by, and there is one index, not two: `+contains+` and `+get+` answer the language's question, and `+equals+` and `+hashCode+` follow them rather than the elements' own. Against a foreign `+java.util.Set+` holding the same `+Decimal+` at another scale, `+equals+` is therefore not symmetric. Only Java interop can build that pair — a set Souther built is compared with a set Souther built — and the alternative would be a set whose `+contains+` found an element and whose hash said it was not there.

[#jvm-sum]
=== Sum data

Generated as a sealed interface and final implementation classes. If a case is a named data, its final record class implements the sealed interface. A unit data is generated as a record with no components, holding its one value in a shared instance — so a Java `+switch+` deconstructs it as `+case Approved()+` next to the product cases (<<jvm-product>>).

A field every case spreads (<<sum-data>>) is declared on the sealed interface as an abstract accessor, which each case record's accessor of the same name implements. Java reads it off the sum the same way Souther does, without a `+switch+`.

A case may itself be a sum (<<sum-data>>), and then its interface extends the outer one. Only that one step is emitted: interface inheritance carries the rest, so a leaf is a value of every sum above it without being named in any of them. The relation has to exist because the rest of the language already reads it that way — the checker folds a nested sum to its leaves to judge a case, and the derived codec dispatches over those same leaves, so a decoder hands back a leaf where the outer sum is what was asked for.

[#jvm-codec]
=== Decoder / Encoder

An exposed data gets a per-source acquisition API. What is returned is a Raoh Decoder / Encoder (`+Decoder<I, T>+` is `+I -> Result<T>+`).

[,text]
----
public static Decoder<Map<String,Object>, TravelRequest> decoder()      // Map (raoh-core; a newtype is Decoder<Object,T>)
public static Decoder<JsonNode, TravelRequest> jsonDecoder()            // JSON (raoh-json)
public static Decoder<Record, TravelRequest> recordDecoder()            // DB row (raoh-jooq)
public static Encoder<TravelRequest, Map<String,Object>> encoder()      // object/sum -> Map (JSON via valueToTree at the boundary)
// newtype encodes to a bare scalar. e.g. MemberId.encoder() : Encoder<MemberId, String>
----

The concrete implementation is generated directly as bytecode by the ClassFile backend from Raoh combinators (<<codec-generation>>). Nothing in souther-runtime calls Raoh — the dependency is the generated code's — and souther-runtime declares it, because that is where a project and everything downstream of it resolve it from without being asked to name a version. `+jsonDecoder()+` / `+recordDecoder()+` are generated only when the data's shape supports it (<<codec-generation>>; JSON for a shape without temporal, `+Record+` for a flat shape). `+decoder()+` (bare values / Map) is always generated.

[#jvm-behavior]
=== behavior

A fn- or `+>->+`-implemented behavior is generated as a public interface named after the behavior (first letter capitalized), with the implementation in a `+<BehaviorName>$Impl+` class. Java declares the behavior by the interface name and never names the implementation. A single-input behavior's interface extends `+Behavior<In, Out>+` (so it composes with `+>->+` and its `+apply+` is typed by inheritance); an interface for any other input count is standalone and declares the typed `+apply+` directly — `+apply(A, B, …)+` for several inputs, `+apply()+` for none. The interface carries a static factory: `+of()+` for a behavior with an empty requirement set, or `+bind(...)+` taking one implementation per requirement.

[,text]
----
var handle = Handle.bind(new JdbcFindMember(dataSource));   // Handle is the interface; bind builds Handle$Impl
----

An injected behavior (a `+behavior+` with no `+let+`) is instead an abstract class the Java implementation extends (<<java-base-class>>), not an interface.

The generated names are derived from the behavior name (first letter capitalized). Because a data name already starts with a capital, a behavior `+quote+` becomes a type `+Quote+`, colliding with a `+data Quote+` in the same module. This is a compile error; rename one to distinguish them.

[#jvm-construction-privacy]
=== Preventing exposure of construction authority

A data's constructor is non-public (package-private): nothing reaches the fields without the invariant. The checked entry `+__construct+` is public for an exposed data and non-public otherwise, so a construction declared in another module (<<closed-construction>>) has the path its declaration names, and a data a module keeps to itself has none. The Java implementation of a behavior with no implementation extends the generated abstract base class (<<java-base-class>>) and constructs failure cases via the base's `+protected+` factories; it may be placed outside the generated package.

What is guaranteed to Java is that no unvalidated value can be created — not that no value can be created. `+decoder()+` is public API (<<jvm-codec>>) and `+__construct+` is public for an exposed data, so Java may build one at any time; the invariant is checked on both paths (<<invariant-on-construct>>), a decode violation becoming a `+Result+` failure. A unit data ignores input and decodes (<<codec-generation>>), so its `+decoder()+` lets anyone construct it — but a unit data has nothing to validate.

Reflection, Unsafe, and bytecode manipulation are out of scope for guarantees.

[#jvm-abort]
=== Abort on invariant violation

An invariant violation inside the domain (<<algebraic-types>>, <<violation-destination>>) appears in generated code as throwing `+souther.runtime.ConstraintViolation+`. It carries the broken data name and the invariant's location.

Souther code cannot catch this. There is no catching syntax, so it cannot control business flow (<<out-of-scope>>). The boundary Java can catch it, but handles it as distinct from a business failure. A business failure arrives as an output case (<<unmarked-sum>>) and maps to 400; an abort is a model bug and maps to 500.

A decode violation is not this; it returns as a Raoh `+Result+` failure (<<decoder-error>>).

[#jvm-anonymous-union]
=== A behavior output's anonymous union

When a behavior's output is an anonymous union (`+-> Member | NoSuchMember+`), a sealed interface representing that union is generated. Its name is the behavior's class name (first letter capitalized; <<jvm-behavior>>) plus `+Result+`, i.e. `+<BehaviorName>Result+`; `+permits+` lists the union's leaf cases, and each case data implements it. An anonymous union has no common supertype (<<asymmetric-interop>>), so this lets Java receive it as a single return type and exhaustively check with `+switch+`.

[,java]
----
// when findMember is -> Member | NoSuchMember
sealed interface FindMemberResult permits Member, NoSuchMember {}
// Member and NoSuchMember implement it; the injected behavior's abstract base FindMember implements Behavior<MemberId, FindMemberResult>
----

There are two cases where it is not generated. If the output is a named sum data, that is itself a sealed interface (<<jvm-sum>>) and is the return type. If the output is a single case, that case type is the return type.

One data type may appear in the output of multiple behaviors, so it may implement several `+<BehaviorName>Result+` at once. The output of a `+>->+` composition behavior (the folded sum of mainline and departed; <<type-routing>>) is also an anonymous union, generated by the same rule from the composition name.

`+<BehaviorName>Result+` takes a class name in this module, so it is subject to the rule every other name this module emits is subject to (<<bridge-case>>): a `+data BillResult+` beside a `+behavior bill+` whose output is a union is a compile error, and so is a `+behavior billResult+` beside it. Two result unions cannot collide with each other, their behaviors having already been refused for capitalizing into one class (<<jvm-behavior>>).

[#union-encoder]
The generated type carries an `+encoder()+` (<<jvm-codec>>) that dispatches its members by the discriminator `+"type"+`, like a sum over the same leaves (<<sum-discrimination>>). It has to: a member's own encoder writes no discriminator — only the union it sits in knows which member it is — so a behavior answering `+-> Member | NoSuchMember+` would otherwise put out a form nothing could tell apart. What each member writes is what <<sum-discrimination>> says a case of that shape writes. A member that is a product lays its fields flatly beside `+"type"+`; a unit writes `+"type"+` alone; a member whose own representation is not an object — a primitive, a newtype — goes under `+"value"+` beside it. A union every one of whose members is a unit data is an enumeration and travels as the member's name, a bare string, exactly as the named sum over those leaves does. A member reaching the union through a bridge case is read out of it first, so the bridge case appears in no external representation and still carries no codec of its own. A member declaring a field named `+"type"+` is refused, as a sum's case is (<<discriminator-field>>).

[,text]
----
// when bill is -> Issued | UnknownSku, and lengthOf is -> Int | NotFound
Issued { amount = 200 }  ->  {"amount":200,"type":"Issued"}
UnknownSku               ->  {"type":"UnknownSku"}
4                        ->  {"type":"Int","value":4}
----

There is no matching decode. Nothing that crosses a behavior's input is an anonymous union (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>), so nothing reads one back. A boundary that wants a case back reads it with that member's own decoder — from the object itself where the member laid its fields flatly, and from what is under `+"value"+` where it did not, since a newtype and a primitive read their representation bare (<<newtype>>).

[#bridge-case]
A case class receives the unions it belongs to when *its own* module is generated. A member this module declared therefore carries the interface itself. A primitive cannot — `+Int+` is `+java.lang.Long+`, the JDK's class — and neither can an imported type, whose class the module that declared it has already emitted; giving it an interface from here would make a module's bytecode a function of who imports it, the mirror of what ADR-0024 refuses. Such a member joins the union through a *bridge case*: a record of one component, `+value+`, that this module emits and that implements the union in the member's stead.

[,java]
----
// when owed is -> Yen | NothingOwed, with Yen imported from `up`
sealed interface OwedResult permits NothingOwed, YenCase {}
public record YenCase(up.Yen value) implements OwedResult {}
----

The bridge case is named `+<MemberName>Case+`, and there is one per member per module: it implements every `+<BehaviorName>Result+` of this module the member belongs to. That is the rule a local case class already follows — what differs between the two is not what membership means but which module owns a class it can be put on.

|===
| Member | Where its result-union membership sits

| a data this module declares | the case class itself
| a primitive, or an imported data | a bridge case this module emits
|===

A bridge case takes a class name in this module, so it is subject to the rule every other name this module emits is subject to: no two of them may be one class. `+YenCase+` is a name a model may well have declared, and so are `+IntCase+` and `+DateCase+`, so the collision is reported where it happens rather than the names reserved — a name nothing collides with stays available. Three ways to collide: with a data this module declares, with the class a behavior capitalizes into (<<jvm-behavior>>), and with another bridge case, which is two types of one spelling from two modules, members of two different unions of this module. Within one union the member-name rule (<<union-member>>) has already refused them.

A bridge case has no codec. Belonging to a union does not change a member's own external representation, so a consumer that has switched to a bridge case takes the value out and uses the codec the member itself carries (<<jvm-codec>>). A consumer that wants the answer as the union writes it asks the union instead (<<union-encoder>>), which is where the discriminator no member writes on itself comes from.

The bridge case is a JVM form. It is not a Souther type, it is not written in a `+match+` arm or an `+example+` row, and it does not appear in an external representation. What each side sees:

|===
| Where | What a value of the union is

| Souther source, a `+match+` binding, an `+example+` row | the member's own value
| the external representation | the member's own contents, decided by its Souther type, under the union's discriminator (<<union-encoder>>)
| a Java `+switch+` and the `+apply+` that returned it | the case class, or the bridge case for a member this module could not put the interface on
|===

The two forms meet at exactly two places. A behavior's return puts the Souther value it answers with into its declared union's JVM form; a call takes it back out before the result is used. Both walk the union's members, which are known where they are emitted, so neither asks a value whether it happens to be wrapped — and because everything between the two is a Souther value, nothing is ever put into a bridge case twice. Reading a value out and putting it back gives the value that was there.

The model author does not write this type name in Souther source; they only write `+-> Member | NoSuchMember+`. A Java consumer declares the behavior by its interface name (<<jvm-behavior>>) and calls `+apply+`, whose return type is `+<BehaviorName>Result+` by inheritance, then matches the cases with `+switch+` — so the consumer never writes the `+Result+` name either. It surfaces explicitly only in an injected behavior's Java implementation, which writes it as the `+apply+` return type (<<java-example>>).

[#compiler-pipeline]
== Compiler structure

[[source-nesting-is-bounded]]A source MUST nest no deeper than the compiler reads. The limit is stated as the compiler's rather than as any one pass's, and it is reached while reading, so it is reported at the token that reached it — including by the formatter, which reads sources it cannot compile. Breaking the nesting into named parts is how a text past it is written instead.

[[source-structural-complexity-is-bounded]]A definition MUST have a structural complexity of 320 or less. Its complexity is counted over what the source wrote:

* a name or a literal is one;
* a construct is one more than the deepest thing it holds;
* an application of a helper that is substituted at the call (<<fn-rules>>) is its argument count more than the deepest of what it holds, since each argument is bound where the body is put; an application that is not substituted — a behavior, a recursive helper, a function a binding holds — is a construct like any other;
* a name that reaches a value counts that value's complexity where the value is substituted (<<fn-value-semantics>>).

A block is counted in the steps its statements take. Everything a statement is followed by is written inside what that statement introduced, so a block of statements and an expression written that deep are the same structure said two ways, and what a statement holds is counted from where that statement stands rather than from the block's end.

A step is not always a binding. A `+guard+` introduces no name and is one step, since what follows it is written inside the case it settles; an ordinary `+let+` is one; a `+let+` written with a pattern is one per binding that pattern introduces.

A pattern that is a name costs nothing beyond the name it binds. Any other pattern takes a value apart, and binds the value and each part taken out of it: a tuple binds the value and each of its elements, a record the value and one per field it names, a case the value and whatever its inner pattern binds, each of those counted the same way through a pattern written inside one.

Two things follow from that, and they are different numbers. What comes after the pattern is written inside one level per binding, since each part is named before the next is: that is what a `+let+` written with a pattern takes as steps. Taking a part out of the value is one level more, on the part rather than on what comes after — so a pattern on its own is one deeper than it binds, and a statement written with one encloses what follows it in the bindings alone. A definition's parameters, and a lambda's, are patterns with the body written after them, and cost the bindings and that one more.

This is a separate limit from the one above, and neither implies the other. Nesting is bounded as the source is read; complexity is bounded over what the source says, which the reading does not settle: a module writes its declarations flat and substitution composes them, so a source that nests barely at all can be arbitrarily complex. It is reported at the construct that exceeded it — the block, the definition, or the value whose substitution composed it — and never as a nesting to flatten, since naming parts is what composes complexity rather than what reduces it.

The limit is a property of the language, not of a compiler's internal representation. What a compiler builds from a definition, and how deep that is, is its own choice; what a definition may say is this.

A compiler states the runtime it holds this bound on. Every phase descends what it builds by recursion, so what a definition may say and what it takes to walk one are the same question asked from two sides, and a stack left to whatever a platform defaults to would make a source compile in one place and not another. This compiler is supported on a 4 MB thread stack, against a source at the bound needing about a megabyte. The `+souther+` binary sets it for itself and this compiler's tests run with it; a host that launches the language server, or the compiler as a library, is the one that gives it that stack, and this is what it has to give. The number is this implementation's and moves with it; the bound above does not.

[[running-out-of-room-is-reported]]A compiler that runs out of room MUST report it rather than fail. Every phase descends what it builds by recursion, and running out of stack is not a diagnostic, so left alone it reaches the author as a trace and takes an editor's session with it. No position is claimed: where the stack ended is not a fact about the source.

This is not either of the limits above said again. Both of those are reached at a place in the source and reported there; this is reached wherever a walk happened to end, and on the stated runtime a source that passed both should not reach it at all. Reaching it is therefore a report about the compiler or about the stack it was given, and it says so rather than asking the author to change anything.

[,text]
----
Source -> Lexer -> Parser -> AST
  -> Resolve (what every written name denotes)
  -> Derive (add decoder/encoder to each resolved data from its shape)
  -> Lower (behavior / let bodies: helper inlining, desugaring)
  -> Type Check -> Core IR (typed: the checker produces it as it types a body)
       construction-authority check / requirement-set check and composition inference / exhaustiveness check / invariant type check
  -> ClassFile Backend -> .class (direct bytecode generation)
----

Behavior and `+let+` bodies are lowered — helper inlining and surface desugaring happen once here — and then typed. The type checker produces the Core IR as it types a body: every Core node carries the type decided for it, and the backend emits from that rather than inferring types a second time. A `+data+`'s `+invariant+` and a behavior's `+ensures+` are produced the same way and by the same checker, so what a construction is refused by and what an answer is held to are decided once and read by every backend. Data definitions and the decoders / encoders derived onto them are otherwise not lowered, and a decoder's and an encoder's expressions are typed where the backend emits them; there is no separate Domain IR / Decoder IR / Encoder IR. Type checking verifies construction authority, requirement sets, exhaustiveness, and invariants together: the surface checks read the AST, and the body check reads the lowered form. The backend generates `+.class+` with the Class-File API (`+java.lang.classfile+`) and does not go through javac.

[#ast-tracking]
== Information tracked by the AST and backend

Apart from the typed Core IR the type checker produces for behavior and `+let+` bodies, for a `+data+`'s `+invariant+` and for a behavior's `+ensures+` (<<compiler-pipeline>>), the compiler keeps no separate intermediate representation; the following is held as the AST and the type-check results.

[#ast-nodes]
=== AST definition nodes

[,text]
----
Module  Data(ProductData)  SumData  UnitData  Include  Field  Invariant
SpecBehavior  PipeBehavior  FnDef  Import
FieldInit  Expression  Case  Block
----

Behavior definition nodes are two, corresponding to the two right-hand sides of <<behavior>>. `+SpecBehavior+` holds input type, output type, `+constructs+`, `+depends on+`, and its `+ensures+` clauses, with no body. If a same-named `+FnDef+` exists it is the implementation; otherwise it is subject to Java injection (<<injected-behavior>>). `+PipeBehavior+` holds the sequence of stage names and no `+ensures+` slot. It is itself the implementation, so it takes no `+FnDef+` and is not injected; its type is computed from the stages (<<type-routing>>). Composition vs. simple is distinguished by these two nodes, so later stages need not re-read the right-hand side to tell them apart.

`+FnDef+` holds argument names and a body. If a same-named `+SpecBehavior+` exists the type comes from it; otherwise it is a helper `+let+` and holds its own argument types, except for a parameter its body determines (<<fn-declaration>>).

A `+SumData+` case holds only a reference to a named data (<<sum-data>>). A `+...+` spread inherits taken-in fields and invariants flatly. A Decoder and an Encoder are not forms of the AST: one does not appear in Souther's syntax (<<decoder>>), so no parse writes one. `+DecoderDef+` / `+EncoderDef+` and the nodes only they hold (`+Construct+`, `+DecRef+`, `+RawExpr+`) are added to a data definition by derivation from its shape, once the names in it are resolved (<<decoder>>, <<encoder>>). `+guard+` and `+let+` are desugared, so they do not appear in the AST; they appear as an `+if+` and as block bindings (<<guard>>, <<block-expression>>).

[#ast-behavior-info]
=== Information tracked about a behavior

[,text]
----
input type  output type (sum of possible business results)  requirement set  construction authority (constructs)
----

The output type is a single type or a sum. `+SpecBehavior+` takes these four from the declaration — the requirement set is also taken from `+depends on+` and reconciled with the set inferred from the `+let+` (<<depends-on>>). `+PipeBehavior+` computes input and output types from the stage sequence (<<type-routing>>) and infers the requirement set as the union over stages (<<composition-with-requirements>>).

The body is not here. A behavior has no body; the `+FnDef+` holds it (<<behavior>>, <<ast-nodes>>). The backend combines this behavior-side information with the `+FnDef+`'s body, lowered to Core (<<compiler-pipeline>>), to generate bytecode.

[#compile-errors]
== Compile errors

A code (e.g. `+E1301+`) is the stable, locale-independent identity of an error; the title, message, and hints are prose shown in the selected locale. The catalogue below lists each code with the message and hint it states; the header, the quoted line and the caret described next are left out of the quotations.

An error is rendered from structured data, not a frozen string. The default (human) form is a titled report: a header naming the error and the source location, the offending line quoted with a caret under the token, found-vs-expected type blocks for a type mismatch, and any hints. An error that concerns more than one place — the two branches of a disagreeing `+if+`, the two sides of a failed composition — points at each with its own caret and label. A compile stops at the first error, except where a pass finds several independent ones at once: every failing `+example+` row is reported with its own position and reason, one report after another. `+--format json+` emits each diagnostic as a JSON object (code, source region, message, hints) for tools and agents, one per line; `+--lang+` selects the language the messages are written in — English unless `+--lang+` or `+SOUTHER_LANG+` names another, with Japanese available; the machine's locale never selects it, and the code is the same string in every language. The quoted line and caret are shown for a single-file compile.

[#e1001-removed]
=== (retired)

E1001 is retired. Construction is guarded by the declaration and not by visibility: what a behavior may build is what `+constructs+` names, so building outside that authority is E1002 and naming more than is built is E1006. On the JVM the factory a data generates is `+protected+`, so there is nothing for a direct construction to call (<<jvm-construction-privacy>>).

[#e1002]
=== Insufficient construction authority

[,text]
----
Behavior `changeEmail` constructs `Member` but does not declare `constructs Member`.
Hint: Add `constructs Member` to `changeEmail`, or stop constructing Member here.
----

This check works only when `+constructs+` is *written* (<<constructs>>). If written, the declaration MUST exactly match the construction set, and constructing something omitted from the declaration is E1002. If omitted, it is inferred and this check does not run. The subjects are both what the behavior constructs directly and what a called helper `+let+` constructs transitively (<<blocks>>).

[#e1006]
=== Over-declaration of construction authority

[,text]
----
Behavior `checkCeiling` declares `constructs Amount` but never builds Amount; it passes an existing value through.
Hint: Remove `Amount` from the `constructs` clause.
----

If `+constructs+` is written, it MUST NOT list something not constructed. Paired with E1002 (too few), it keeps the declaration exactly matching the construction set, so the declaration correctly reads "creates new / passes through" (<<constructs>>). Same position as the `+depends on+` over-declaration E1603.

[#e1026]
=== A unit data named in constructs

[,text]
----
Behavior `find` names `Missing` in `constructs`, but a unit data is not part of a construction set.
Hint: Remove `Missing` from the `constructs` clause.
----

A unit data is constructed and carries no construction authority, so it is in no behavior's construction set and is named in no clause (<<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>). Distinct from E1006, which says the body never built the data: here the body may well build it, and the entry is still wrong. Keeping the two apart is what lets E1006 go on meaning "declared but never built".

[#e1003-removed]
=== (retired)

E1003 is retired. It required a violation case on a behavior constructing an invariant-bearing data, but a violation inside the domain aborts, so no slot is needed in the output (<<algebraic-types>>, <<violation-destination>>).

[#e1004]
=== Spread field-name collision

[,text]
----
Field `submittedAt` from `...TravelRequestCommon` conflicts with a field of `Submitted`.
----

[#e1005]
=== Missing field in a construction

Reported where a construction finishes with a field of the constructed type left without a value (<<construction-supplies-every-field>>). A field may be written out, filled from a binding of its name, or supplied by a spread. The hint follows how the construction was written: nothing was spread, or something was and what it copies from does not declare the field, or a sum was and the field is outside the part its cases share.

Nothing was spread, so the field had to be written and was not:

[,text]
----
Construction of `PreApproved` is missing field `preApprover`.
Hint: Give `preApprover` a value: write `preApprover = …`, or write `preApprover` alone to fill
it from a binding of that name.
----

Something was spread, and what it copies from does not declare the field:

[,text]
----
Construction of `PreApproved` is missing field `preApprover`.
Hint: Supply `preApprover` explicitly; the spread does not provide it.
----

A spread of a sum copies the part every case of it spreads (<<sum-data>>), so a field outside that part is missing however plainly the cases declare it:

[,text]
----
Construction of `PreApproved` is missing field `preApprover`.
Hint: `preApprover` is not in the part every case of `Request` spreads, so the spread does not
provide it. Supply it explicitly, or open `Request` with `match` and read it from the case that
has it.
----

[#e1007]
=== A newtype wraps something with no external representation

[,text]
----
A newtype takes the external representation of what it wraps, and a tuple has none. Wrap a named
data instead.
----

Reported where a `+data X = Y+` names a `+Y+` carrying no external representation of its own (<<newtype-wraps-an-external-representation>>) — a tuple or a function type. A newtype's decoder and encoder are derived from the wrapped type's (<<newtype>>), so there would be nothing to derive them from.

[#e1008]
=== A data is written with an empty body

[,text]
----
data `Rank` has an empty body, which names a type with one value — that is a unit data, and it is
written without a body.
----

Reported at a `+data+` whose body is written and holds nothing (<<unit-data-is-written-without-a-body>>). The declaration means what a unit data means, and a unit data has one spelling.

[#e1009]
=== A newtype wraps an optional

[,text]
----
`Approver` wraps `EmployeeId?`, but a newtype wraps one value and takes its representation, so there
is nothing for it to be when the value is absent.
----

Reported at a `+data X = Y?+` (<<newtype-wraps-a-value-that-is-always-there>>). The absence belongs to the field that holds the newtype — `+approver: Approver?+` — where a reader can see it; inside the newtype it would be a value that is sometimes not one.

[#e1010]
=== A case declares the discriminator as a field

[,text]
----
`Manager` is a case of `Rank` and declares a field `type`, which is the discriminator its sum writes
beside the fields.
----

Reported at the field (<<a-case-does-not-declare-the-discriminator-field>>). A product case lays its fields where the tag goes, so the two would want one key and whichever was written second would be what a reader got back.

[#e1011]
=== A name is declared twice

[,text]
----
data `Amount` is already defined.
----

Reported at the second declaration (<<a-declaration-is-made-once>>): a `+let+`, a data, a field of one data, an entry written twice in one clause list.

[#e1012]
=== A `let` and a data share a spelling

[,text]
----
`let Amount` and data `Amount` are one name where a value is written.
----

Reported where a `+let+` and a data of one module carry one name (<<a-spelling-answers-one-thing-where-a-value-is-written>>). A data is written where a value goes, so the two would be one spelling with two answers. A behavior and its implementing `+let+` are not this: they are one thing's declaration and its body.

[#e1013]
=== A data cannot be constructed at all

[,text]
----
data `OrgMember` cannot be constructed: it needs a value of itself, with nothing to bottom out.
