data `Discount` cannot be constructed: its rules cannot all hold, so no value satisfies them.

data `Pair` cannot be constructed: `value` must hold more values that differ than there are of what
it holds, which is at most 1.

data `Window` cannot be constructed: the rules leave `value` nothing to hold.
----

Reported at the declaration (<<a-data-can-be-constructed>>). One code for every way a data comes to have no value, and the sentence says which of them was shown. Self-reference is what an org chart or a tree is made of, and it bottoms out at an optional, or at a collection the rules on it let be empty (<<self-referential-data>>); with neither on the cycle there is no first value.

[#e1014]
=== A construction writes a field the type does not have

[,text]
----
`preApprover` is not a field of `Submitted`.
----

Reported at the field (<<a-construction-names-fields-of-its-type>>).

[#e1015]
=== A spread has no product to copy

[,text]
----
A spread `...request` copies the fields the cases of `TravelRequest` share, and they share none: no
data is spread by every case.
----

Reported at the spread (<<a-spread-copies-a-product>>). A spread is nominal (<<field-spread>>): what it copies is the data every case spread, and a sum whose cases share no such data has nothing for it to name.

[#e1016]
=== A spread provides a field with the wrong type

[,text]
----
The spread provides `estimatedCost` as Int, but `PreApproved` needs Amount.
----

Reported at the spread (<<a-spread-supplies-what-the-fields-need>>). Fields are copied by name, and a name is not a type: two fields spelled alike and typed differently are two fields.

[#e1017]
=== A construction is written where one may not be

[,text]
----
`Amount` is a type, so `Amount(...)` constructs one, and a construction cannot be written here.
----

Reported at the construction (<<a-construction-is-written-where-one-may-be>>). An `+invariant+` states a condition over a value it is given (<<invariant-expressions>>); building one inside it would run a check inside a check.

[#e1018]
=== A construction names something that is not a data

[,text]
----
I cannot construct `submit` here.
----

Reported at the name (<<a-construction-names-a-data>>).

[#e1019]
=== A binding takes a built-in's name

[,text]
----
`unreachable` is a built-in value and cannot be used as a binding name — it would shadow the
built-in; choose another name.
----

Reported at the binding (<<a-binding-does-not-shadow-a-builtin>>). Shadowing a model's own name is ordinary and harmless — the outer one is still there to read — and a built-in has no declaration to read instead.

[#e1020]
=== A sum names a case that is not declared

[,text]
----
Unknown case `Withdrawn` in sum `TravelRequest`.
----

Reported at the case (<<a-sum-case-is-a-declared-data>>).

[#e1021]
=== A sum contains itself

[,text]
----
Sum `TravelRequest` contains itself through `Submitted`. A sum's cases are the values it can be, so
one of them cannot be the sum itself.
----

Reported at the declaration (<<a-sum-does-not-contain-itself>>), naming the chain that closes. Self-reference through a *field* is how a tree is written and is unaffected (<<self-referential-data>>); this is about the cases.

[#e1022]
=== A value reaches itself

[,text]
----
Value `ceiling` reaches itself: ceiling -> limit -> ceiling.
----

Reported wherever the cycle closes (<<a-value-does-not-reach-itself>>). A value with no parameter list is substituted where it is named, so a cycle among them has nothing to substitute first.

[#e1023]
=== A name is not in scope

[,text]
----
I cannot find a value named `estimatdCost` in scope.
----

Reported at the name (<<a-name-is-in-scope>>), with the nearest name in scope suggested. A value, a type and a behavior each say which of the three was being looked for, because that is what narrows where to look.

[#e1024]
=== A name is held as a value and is not one

[,text]
----
`submit` is a behavior, and cannot be held as a value here.
----

Reported at the name (<<a-name-held-as-a-value-is-a-value>>). A behavior, a module qualifier and a type are names of other things; writing one where a value goes names the thing rather than a value of it.

[#e1025]
=== A standard-library function is written bare

[,text]
----
`map` is a standard-library function and must be called qualified, as `List.map`.
----

Reported at the name (<<a-standard-library-function-is-called-qualified>>). The qualifier is what reaches the library, and it is also what tells `+List.length+` from `+String.length+`.

[#e1101]
=== Invariant type mismatch

[,text]
----
An invariant expression must have type Bool, but this is String.
----

[#e1102]
=== An invariant is declared on a type with nothing to observe

[,text]
----
`Staff` is a unit data, so it has no field for this invariant to observe and nothing it could
reject. Drop the clause, or give `Staff` the field the constraint is about.
----

Reported where an `+invariant+` is written on a unit data (<<invariant-needs-a-value-to-constrain>>). A unit data has one value, so a clause over it is true of that value or true of nothing, and neither is a constraint.

[#e1103]
=== Two invariant clauses share a name

[,text]
----
`Amount` declares two invariant clauses named `inYen`. A name is what an attempted construction and
a boundary issue call one rule, so two rules cannot share it.
----

Reported at the second clause of a name (<<invariant-clause-names-are-distinct>>). An attempted construction answers a clause by name (<<attempt-departures>>) and a decode failure reports one by name; neither can say which of two was meant.

[#e1104]
=== An invariant clause is named `_`

[,text]
----
`_` cannot name an invariant clause of `Amount`.
Hint: Give the clause a name of its own, or leave it unnamed — an unnamed clause is enforced and
answered by `| _ -> ...`.
----

Reported where a clause is written `+invariant _ = …+` (<<invariant-clause-name-is-not-underscore>>). `+| _ -> …+` answers the clauses carrying no name, so a clause holding that name as its own could never be the one it answered.

[#e1105]
=== An invariant constructs a data

[,text]
----
The invariant of `QuoteLines` constructs `Line`, but an invariant may not construct a data — it
observes the value being built, it does not build one.
----

Reported at the construction (<<an-invariant-observes-and-does-not-build>>), naming the clause it is written in or reached from. Building inside a check would run that type's own check inside this one, so whether this value holds would depend on whether another one did.

[#e1106]
=== An invariant does not answer on every path

[,text]
----
The invariant of `Amount` reaches the `partial` helper `spin`, which carries no termination
guarantee; an invariant is checked on every construction.
----

Reported at the clause (<<an-invariant-answers-on-every-path>>). An invariant runs at every construction and at every boundary, so a path through it that answers nothing — an `+unreachable+`, or a `+partial+` helper that may not stop — is a check that may not come back.

[#e1107]
=== An invariant is declared where no construction would owe it

[,text]
----
`Stage` is a sum, and nothing constructs one — a value of `Stage` is written as one of its cases —
so this invariant would be owed by no construction and checked at no boundary.
----

Reported at the clause (<<an-invariant-is-declared-where-a-construction-owes-it>>). Write the rule on the case it is about, or on a newtype over the sum, which is built and carries its own check. Refused rather than kept as a clause with no effect: a sum is lowered into a declaration with no place to put one, so a clause allowed through here is dropped along with anything written inside it, and a reading that asks which rules a position has cannot tell it from a declaration nobody wrote a rule on.

[#e1201]
=== Non-exhaustive match

[,text]
----
This `match` on TravelRequest does not cover every case.
Hint: Add a case for: PreApproved
----

[#e1202]
=== `match` is written on something that is not a sum

[,text]
----
`match` needs a sum-typed value, but got Int.
----

Reported at the subject (<<a-match-subject-is-a-sum>>). A primitive is told apart by comparing it, not by naming its cases (<<primitive-arm>>).

[#e1203]
=== A `match` arm names a case the subject does not have

[,text]
----
`Drafting` is a case of `TravelRequest`, so this arm belongs to a match over `TravelRequest`.
----

Reported at the arm (<<a-match-arm-names-a-case-of-the-subject>>). Where the case belongs to another sum the message says which, since an arm in the wrong `+match+` reads exactly like a misspelling and is not one. Where a nested `+match+` has swallowed the arms that follow it on the line, it says that instead, that being how the arm arrived under the wrong subject.

[#e1204]
=== Two arms match one case

[,text]
----
`Submitted` is matched by more than one case.
----

Reported at the second arm (<<no-two-arms-match-one-case>>). The first one answers it, so this one cannot be reached.

[#e1205]
=== A `match` has no arms

[,text]
----
This `match` has no cases.
----

Reported at the `+match+` (<<a-match-has-arms>>).

[#e1206]
=== A pattern opens something that has nothing to open

[,text]
----
`Amount` wraps `Int`, not `String`.
----

Reported at the pattern (<<a-pattern-opens-what-the-value-is>>), in a `+match+` arm or in a binding. Three ways to be wrong about it: what is opened is not a newtype and holds no wrapped value, the type named inside is not what is wrapped, or the case has no payload to open at all.

[#e1207]
=== An or-pattern opens a value

[,text]
----
An or-pattern binds the sum type, so it cannot open a case's value with `(...)`.
----

Reported at the pattern (<<an-or-pattern-binds-the-sum-and-opens-nothing>>). What an or-pattern says is that these cases are handled the same way, and what they carry is what differs between them.

[#e1208]
=== The arms of a `match` disagree on a type

[,text]
----
The match branches disagree: Submitted vs Int.
----

Reported at the arm the arms stopped joining at (<<branches-agree-on-a-type>>). A `+match+` answers with one value, so its arms produce one type; a union of what they produce is written by declaring it.

An `+if+` names both of its branches, there being two of them and each with a type of its own. An `+else+` answering per clause (<<attempt-departures>>) has more, and joins them as a list joins its elements — so it is reported as a list is, at the arm the join was refused at, with the join of the branches before it stated rather than pointed at:

[,text]
----
This branch produces Int. The preceding branches produce Blue | Red.
----

[#e1209]
=== An arm's alternative adds nothing to it

[,text]
----
`Station` adds nothing to this arm: `OnceKind` also answers for every value it does.
----

Reported at the arm (<<an-arms-alternatives-each-add-what-it-answers-for>>). A case named twice is reported as that and not as one covering the other, since it is the more particular of the two: `This arm names `Station` twice, and the second says nothing the first did not.`

[#e1301]
=== Use of null

[,text]
----
`null` is not part of the language. Use an optional field with `?`.
----

[#e1302-removed]
=== (retired)

E1302 is retired. `+.sou+` has no `+throw+` and no `+catch+` to reject: the rule that a failure is answered as a case of the output rather than raised is carried by the grammar, and a Java method named in an expression names nothing the language knows, so it is reported as a name that is not in scope (<<out-of-scope>>, <<e1023>>).

[#e1303]
=== Writing an option case

Reported where `+Some+` or `+None+` is written outside the one place an optional is made — a `+?+` field being given a value, where a plain value is wrapped and `+None+` is the empty one (<<optional>>). The two names fail for different reasons and are reported apart.

`+Some(...)+` is never a call, wherever it is written, because the wrap at the field is implicit:

[,text]
----
`Some(...)` is not a call: a value written into a `?` field is wrapped for you,
and that is the only place an optional is made.
Hint: Where a `?` field is being given a value, write the value on its own.
Elsewhere an optional is only read — a `?` field, `Map.get`, `List.find` — never made.
----

`+None+` is a value, and the error is that this position is not asking for one:

[,text]
----
`None` is the empty value of a `?` field, and nothing here is asking for one.
Hint: Where the model owns the absence, make it a case of its own sum. Absence of that
kind does not reach `List.filterMap`, whose step has to answer an optional: use
`List.flatMap` over a step answering a list of nought or one.
----

A pattern is unaffected by either: `+| Some v+` matches an optional the model was given.

[#e1307]
=== unreachable where the position states no type

[,text]
----
Nothing here says what this position holds, and `unreachable` answers no value to say it
with.
Hint: Write it where the type is stated — an arm beside one that answers a value, a
field, a declared return, an annotated binding.
----

Reported where an `+unreachable+` (<<unreachable>>) stands in a position that states no type and has no branch beside it that does — `+let n = unreachable "..."+`, or an arithmetic operand. No value is made there, but the code that would read one is still compiled, and it has no shape to read.

[#e1308]
=== `?` is written on a sum

[,text]
----
`?` marks a single type optional, but it follows a sum of 3 cases.
----

Reported where `+?+` follows a sum rather than one type (<<optional-marks-one-type>>). What a sum is missing is a case of its own; marking the whole optional says the absence belongs to whoever reads the field rather than to the model (<<optional>>).

[#e1310]
=== `unreachable` states no reason

[,text]
----
`unreachable` states why the point cannot be reached, as a string: `unreachable "twenty insured
years cannot precede the age of thirty"`.
----

Reported at an `+unreachable+` written with nothing after it (<<unreachable-states-its-reason>>). The reason is what a reader is shown when a row reaches the point (<<e1911>>), and it is the only thing the declaration says that the position does not.

[#e1311]
=== A value that cannot cross the boundary is written where one has to

[,text]
----
parameter `range` is a tuple; a tuple has no external representation and cannot cross the boundary,
so a behavior's input must be a named data.
----

Reported wherever a value with no external representation is written where one crosses (<<what-crosses-the-boundary-has-an-external-representation>>): a behavior's parameter or output, a data's field, or a type a codec would be derived from. Which value had none is what the message carries (<<what-has-no-external-representation>>), and the position it was written in is what the author changes.

[#e1312]
=== A behavior's parameter is written as an anonymous union

[,text]
----
parameter `outcome` has an anonymous union type `Allocated | OutOfStock`; a behavior's parameter must
name a single type — declare `data ... = Allocated | OutOfStock` and take that name.
----

Reported at the behavior's parameter (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>). An anonymous union is the shape of one behavior's answer and introduces no name (<<union-member>>); an input is a type the caller names, so it takes a declared one. A helper's parameter is not a boundary and takes one (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>).

[#e1313]
=== An optional stands in a behavior's boundary

[,text]
----
The output of `findMember` carries an optional (`Member?`).
----

Reported at the parameter, or at the behavior for its output (<<an-optional-does-not-stand-in-a-boundary>>). The message names the optional it found, which is the whole of the boundary when it is written as one and a part of it when a collection carries it — so what changes is the position that optional stands in, which is not always the behavior's own type. Absence at a boundary is part of the model's vocabulary: put it on the `+?+` field of the data that carries it, whose own decoder reads it, or name it as a case of a sum so the caller matches on a name rather than unwrapping. Where the whole output is the optional, that sum is written directly — `+-> Member | NotFound+`; a behavior's parameter takes a declared name instead (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>).

[#e1314]
=== A Map that crosses the boundary has a key that cannot be written

[,text]
----
`inventory` crosses the boundary, so the Map it holds must be keyed by String, a temporal, an
enumeration, or a newtype over one of those.
----

Reported at the field, parameter or output holding the Map (<<a-map-that-crosses-is-keyed-by-a-text-key>>). The boundary writes a key as text and reads it back the same way, so a key with no text form is one nothing outside can address.

[#e1315]
=== A collection would have to compare something that cannot be compared

[,text]
----
This collection would have to compare (Int) -> Int as its key, and a function has no value to
compare.
----

Reported at the collection (<<a-collection-member-supports-equality>>). A `+Set+` asks whether two elements are the same and a `+Map+` whether two keys are; the message says which of the two asked, because that is what the author is holding.

[#e1316]
=== A collection type is written without its arguments

[,text]
----
`List` needs a type argument, e.g. `List<Int>`.
----

Reported at the type (<<a-generic-type-is-given-its-arguments>>). The bare qualifier names the module whose functions reach the collection (<<stdlib>>), which is why writing it where a type goes is not a shorthand for anything.

[#e1317]
=== A value does not have the type its position requires

[,text]
----
Field `estimatedCost` expects Amount, but got Int.
----

Reported at the value (<<a-value-has-the-type-its-position-requires>>). The rule most of the others in this range are a special case of, and what a diagnostic says where no narrower one applies; the positions it covers are the type positions (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>).

[#e1318]
=== A list's elements do not all have one type

[,text]
----
This element has type Int. The preceding elements have type Amount.
Hint: Make every element the same type — widen them to a sum they are cases of, or convert the one that differs.
----

Reported at the element the elements stopped joining at (<<a-lists-elements-have-one-type>>). A list is `+List<T>+` for a single `+T+`; a sequence of several types is a list of a sum they are cases of. What that element conflicts with is the join of the elements before it. That is one element's type only where one element has been read, which is a fact about how long the list is and not about where the type came from — so it is stated rather than pointed at, and `+[1, "x", 2]+` and `+[1, 2, "x"]+` are reported the one way.

`{plus}{plus}` reaches the same rule from two written operands rather than from an accumulator, so each list is pointed at and the message names neither type:

[,text]
----
`++` joins two lists into one, so the elements of both must have one type.
----

[#e1319]
=== An operator is given types it is not defined for

[,text]
----
`++` joins two lists or two strings, but the operands are Int and String.
----

Reported at the operator or at the operand it is about (<<an-operator-takes-the-types-it-is-defined-for>>) — at the operator where the rule is about the pair, as comparing two unrelated types is.

[#e1320]
=== A tuple pattern does not match its tuple

[,text]
----
This pattern binds 2 name(s), but the tuple has 3 element(s).
----

Reported at the pattern (<<a-tuple-pattern-matches-its-tuple>>).

[#e1321]
=== A field is read off a value that does not have it

[,text]
----
I cannot read a field `submittedAt` on `TravelRequest`: a sum has no fields of its own, its cases
do, and which case this is is not known until it is opened.
----

Reported at the read (<<a-field-read-is-declared-by-the-value>>). A sum exposes a field only where every case spreads the data declaring it (<<sum-data>>), so the message says which of the two it is: no case has the field, or each case declares its own and nothing is shared.

[#e1322]
=== A temporal value is not written as a literal

[,text]
----
`2026-13-01` is not a Date. Write `Date("2026-07-01")` / `Time("09:00")` /
`DateTime("2026-07-01T09:00")` / `Instant("2026-07-01T09:00:00Z")`.

`2026-07-01T09:30:45.123` is finer than a second, and a `DateTime` holds no fraction of one. Write
it to the second, or use `Instant` where the sub-second reading is the point.

`2026-07-01T09:30:45+09:00` carries an offset. It names the same moment
`Instant("2026-07-01T00:30:45Z")` names, and that is how an `Instant` is written. A boundary reads
either.

`2026-06-30T23:59:60Z` names a leap second. The language has no moment for one, and reading it as
the second before would answer a different moment than the text says.
----

Reported at the value (<<a-temporal-value-is-written-as-a-literal>>). The text is spelled out so it can be read at compile time, which is what lets malformed text be refused here instead of at run time. The same code covers three more refusals, and they are not one kind of thing. A fraction of a second, and a leap second, are text saying something the type cannot hold — the first a reading finer than the type keeps (<<a-local-temporal-is-held-to-the-second>>), the second a moment the language does not have (<<a-leap-second-is-no-moment>>). A numeric offset is neither: it names exactly the moment the `+Z+` form of it names, and is refused only because a written value is written the way the value is written back (<<temporal-literal>>). A boundary reads the offset form and refuses the other two.

This code is about *writing* a temporal. Parts that name no moment are not this: they are values rather than text, so `+Date.fromParts+` answers `+NotADate+` at the position that built them (<<a-temporal-built-from-parts-is-refused-where-the-parts-name-none>>).

[#e1323]
=== A `String.matches` pattern is not a literal regular expression

[,text]
----
The pattern of `String.matches` must evaluate to a string at compile time, so it can be validated
there: write a string literal.
----

Reported at the pattern (<<a-matches-pattern-is-a-literal-regular-expression>>). A pattern computed at run time could not be checked at all, so writing it out is what makes the check possible; the same code says when what was written is not a valid expression.

[#e1324]
=== Newtype arithmetic does not follow the units

[,text]
----
`Amount` and `Rate` are newtypes over Int, and adding them would answer with neither.
----

Reported at the operator (<<newtype-arithmetic-follows-the-units>>). Which rule was broken — same-newtype addition, a product's units, a quotient's, a scalar, a reciprocal — is what an author needs and is not readable from the operand types, so the refusal names its own rule rather than reporting a type mismatch.

[#e1325]
=== A type the language declares stands in a behavior's boundary

[,text]
----
`divide` answers `DivisionByZero`, which the language declares rather than this model.
----

Reported at the parameter, or at the behavior for its output (<<a-boundary-carries-the-models-own-vocabulary>>). `+DivisionByZero+` and `+NotANumber+` say what `+Int.divide+` and `+String.toInt+` can answer, `+RoundingMode+` says what `+Decimal.round+` takes, and `+Raw+` is reserved; each is the language's account of one of its own operations. A behavior that has the same thing to report declares the case — `+data Undivided+`, answered as `+-> Int | Undivided+` — so that what the behavior publishes stays its own even when the language's account of itself changes. Reported wherever the name stands in what crosses, at any depth and as a member of an output union.

[#e1326]
=== An `unreachable` at a case the model admits

[,text]
----
`Off` can arrive at `f`, so this point is reachable.
Hint: `Off` is a case of `Flag`, and no rule refuses it here.
----

Reported at an `+unreachable+` in an arm of the first `+match+` a body reaches, on one of the behavior's own input positions, whose case the declarations reaching that position leave standing (<<unreachable-is-for-what-the-model-rules-out>>). Nothing stands between that fork and the caller, so the case is one a caller can supply and the point is one a row reaches — which is <<e1911>> for whoever writes it and an abort for whoever runs the model.

The diagnostic names three things, because acting on it takes all three: where the claim is written, which case at which position it is about, and the declaration that says the case can arrive. The answer is the one the rule above states — the arm answers a value, or that declaration changes.

Read from the body as it is emitted, so a claim written in a `+let+` this body called is judged here as well: what the claim says about this behavior's input is what it says wherever it was written.

[#e1327]
=== A branch the model's own rules make dead

[,text]
----
Nothing the model admits arrives at this branch, so no row can be written through it.
Hint: The conditions on the way here cannot all hold: line 11 holding, line 12 failing.
Hint: Take the branch out, or widen what reaches it.
----

Reported at the branch, as a warning (<<a-branch-the-rules-make-dead-is-said-so>>). What makes it dead is said with it: the conditions on the way and which way each went, the position whose values stop short of what the branch is written for, or the cases the rules refuse where the arm matches on them.

The same reading is what takes the branch's rows out of what the model is measured against, so a report never asks for a row through a branch it has already said nothing reaches. Where the reading cannot settle a branch, both halves leave it alone: the rows stay owed and nothing is said.

[#e1401-removed]
=== (retired)

E1401 is retired. It was raised where a name applied to arguments resolved to nothing, which is what E1023 says (<<e1023>>), and nothing established that such a name was on the JVM. `+java.time.LocalDate.now+`, which this section used as its example, is read from a root `+java+` that names no qualifier and resolves to nothing, so it was reported as an unknown name and not as this. That arbitrary Java calls are out of scope is a design decision and stays one (<<no-arbitrary-jvm-calls>>); it was never a thing a compile could find.

[#e1402]
=== A core form is written outside the core

[,text]
----
`intrinsic` is a core privilege: only a module in the reserved `souther` namespace declares one.
----

Reported where a module outside the reserved `+souther+` namespace writes `+intrinsic+`, `+private+`, a type variable, or an optional anywhere but on a data field (<<core-privileges-stay-in-the-core>>). Each is a way of writing the layer below the model, and one code says which one it was.

[#e1501]
=== Cyclic import

[,text]
----
Cyclic module dependency detected.
----

A module depends on every module it imports and on every module it names in a qualified reference (<<qualified-reference>>), so the cycle is reported whichever of the two closes it.

[#e1502]
=== A declaration takes a name the core has

[,text]
----
module `List` uses a name reserved for the standard-library qualifier `List` (as in `List.…` /
`import List ( … )`); pick another module name.
----

Reported where a module or a data takes a name the core holds (<<a-core-name-is-not-taken>>) — the reserved `+souther+` namespace, or a standard-library qualifier. The qualifier is the only spelling that reaches the library, so a declaration of that name does not shadow it in one place, it hides it everywhere.

[#e1503]
=== A module is declared in two places

[,text]
----
Module `example.employee` is compiled here and is also on the path.
----

Reported when two sources declare one module, or a compiled source declares one that is also on the path (<<a-module-is-declared-in-one-place>>). Which of the two an importer would read is not decided by the text, so neither is chosen.

[#e1504]
=== A module a compile reaches is not there

[,text]
----
Module `example.travel` needs `example.employee`, which is not on the path either.
----

Reported where a module a compile reaches is neither compiled here nor on the path (<<every-module-reached-is-on-the-path>>) — imported, written qualified, or needed by a module that is. A module the path does carry and this compiler cannot read is not this: it is on the path, this compile has heard of it, and what is wrong is the artifact (<<e1509>>).

[#e1505-removed]
=== A module on the path was compiled by another Souther

A module read from compiled output still has to have been built by a Souther that agrees with this one (<<a-published-module-agrees-with-this-compiler>>), and it is still refused where it was not. What changed is where the refusal is said. It is one of the ways an artifact cannot be read back, so it is reported as <<e1509>> with the disagreement said under it — a rule the publishing project broke, rather than the rule the importing author is shown as theirs.

[#e1506]
=== A name reached across a boundary is not declared there

[,text]
----
`Amount` is not defined in `example.employee`.
----

Reported where a name written on an `+import+` list or after a module qualifier is not one that module declares (<<a-reached-name-is-declared-by-its-module>>). The nearest name it does declare is suggested. Separate from <<e1507>>: this is a mistake about what is there, not about what is published.

[#e1507]
=== A name reached across a boundary is not exposed

[,text]
----
`Amount` is declared in `example.employee` but not exposed by it.
----

Reported where the name exists and the module keeps it (<<a-reached-name-is-exposed-by-its-module>>). What a module publishes is its own decision (<<exposed-surface>>), so the move is to expose it there rather than to reach past the decision from here.

[#e1508]
=== An imported name denotes more than one thing

[,text]
----
`Amount` is imported from both `example.employee` and `example.billing`.
----

Reported where a name an `+import+` brings in has more than one answer here (<<an-imported-name-denotes-one-thing>>): exposed by two modules, colliding with this module's own declaration, or an alias already in use. Reaching one of them qualified is what says which, and is why the answer is not to pick one for the author.

[#e1509]
=== A module on the path cannot be read back

[,text]
----
Module `example.employee` is on the class path, and this compiler cannot read what it published.
  It was built by Souther 0.4.0, which does not agree with this compiler about what an importing
  module may reach.
  Rebuild `example.employee` with this compiler, or compile against the version that built it.
----

Reported where a module the class path carries cannot be read back into the surface this compiler understands (<<a-published-module-can-be-read-back>>). What went wrong inside the artifact is said under the report: a class carrying metadata this compiler cannot read, a boundary revision that does not agree (<<a-published-module-agrees-with-this-compiler>>), declarations that name a different module from the one they were found under, a declaration whose class is not there (<<a-published-module-carries-what-it-says-it-declares>>), published text this compiler does not parse, an `+import+` line of the published surface it cannot read.

Said where the module was reached from — the `+import+` line in this project that arrives at it, and every other line that does, since a dependency two files import is reached by both. The artifact's own lines are not this compile's to quote, and a report carrying one sends an author to a place in a file they do not have.

One report and one code, whichever failure it was, because the author reading it has one thing to do about any of them. The rules broken are the publishing project's; naming one of them as the rule this author is in breach of hands them somebody else's obligation. A module refused here is still a module this compile has heard of, so an importer is not also told there is no such thing.

[#e1601-removed]
=== (retired)

E1601 is retired. A requirement is reconciled between the `+depends on+` clause and the body, so what it once covered is reported as the disagreement it is: a dependency the body uses and the clause omits is E1602, a clause naming what the body never calls is E1603, and a clause naming something that is no dependency at all is E1607.

[#e1801-removed]
=== (retired)

The `+error+` declaration and E1801 are retired. A failure is ordinary data, and whether it departs the mainline is decided by composition, so there is no distinction between a success position and a failure position (<<unmarked-sum>>, <<behavior>>, <<composition>>).

[#e1609]
=== `exposing` names something this module does not declare

[,text]
----
`exposing` names `Amount`, which is imported into this module, not defined here; `exposing` lists a
module's own definitions and does not re-export imported names.
----

Reported where `+exposing+` names something this module does not declare (<<exposing-lists-this-modules-own-definitions>>), whether that is nothing at all or a name it imported. Re-export is the second of those and is refused on purpose: one declaration behind two surfaces leaves a reader two modules to ask about it.

[#e1610]
=== `exposing` names a derived codec

[,text]
----
`exposing` is type-granular: a data's `decoder`/`encoder` are always public once the data is
exposed. Write `Amount`, not `Amount.decoder`.
----

Reported where `+exposing+` names a data's `+decoder+` or `+encoder+` (<<exposing-is-type-granular>>). They are how the type crosses a boundary, so a type that crosses one carries them; there is no state in which the data is published and they are not.

[#e1611]
=== An exposed signature names a type the module keeps

[,text]
----
Behavior `submit` is exposed and its input names `Draft`, which this module does not expose.
----

Reported where an exposed behavior's input or output, or an exposed data's field, names a type this module keeps to itself (<<an-exposed-signature-names-only-exposed-types>>). A caller would be handed a value of a type it cannot name. An injection target says it differently, because there the caller is Java.

[#e1612-removed]
=== (retired)

E1612 is retired. It refused a behavior with no `+let+` that wrote a `+depends on+` clause, on the reading that a behavior with no `+let+` is an injection target. That reading answered two questions with one fact: whether Java supplies the body, and whether the body has been written yet. The clause tells them apart — a behavior that declares `+depends on+` takes those dependencies as arguments of a `+let+`, so it is Souther's to implement, and with no `+let+` it is one nobody has written (<<unwritten-behavior>>). What is refused now is resting on such a behavior from something that is built here (<<e1627>>).

[#e1613]
=== A union member cannot be named in an arm

[,text]
----
`(Int, Int)` cannot be a union member: a member is a type a `match` arm can name — one of the
primitives, or a data type declared here or imported.
----

Reported at the member (<<a-union-member-is-nameable-in-an-arm>>). A union is read by matching on it, so a member an arm cannot write is a case nothing can handle. The same code says when one spelling stands for two members' types, which leaves an arm naming one of them and no way to say which.

[#e1614]
=== A `let` implements a composition

[,text]
----
`let placeOrder` cannot implement the composition `behavior placeOrder`, which is already its own
implementation.
----

Reported at the `+let+` (<<a-composition-has-no-let>>). A `+>->+` is the body: its stages are what runs, and a second body would be a second answer to what the behavior does.

[#e1615]
=== An implementing `let` disagrees with its behavior

[,text]
----
`let submit` parameter `now` should be `clock`: what `depends on` names becomes the trailing
parameters, in declared order.
----

Reported at the `+let+` (<<an-implementing-let-takes-its-shape-from-the-behavior>>). The behavior states the shape — how many parameters, what they are, the order the injected ones arrive in, what it answers with — so the `+let+` writes none of them again, and the same code says whichever of the four was written or was wrong.

[#e1616]
=== An `ensures` clause does not name the answer

Reported at the rule when it refers to the answer neither through an arm nor through `+value+` (<<an-ensures-clause-names-the-answer>>).

[#e1617]
=== An `ensures` clause does not name a parameter

Reported at the clause when an arm expression names no parameter (<<an-ensures-clause-names-a-parameter>>).

[#e1618]
=== A behavior with `ensures` has a parameter named `value`

Reported at the parameter (<<a-behavior-with-a-clause-has-no-parameter-named-value>>).

[#e1619]
=== An `ensures` arm is not an output case

Reported at the arm name (<<an-ensures-arm-is-an-output-case>>).

[#e1620]
=== An `ensures` clause over a sum names no arm

Reported at the clause (<<an-ensures-clause-over-a-sum-names-arms>>).

[#e1621]
=== An `ensures` clause over one type names an arm

Reported at the clause (<<an-ensures-clause-over-a-single-type-names-no-arm>>).

[#e1622]
=== Two `ensures` clauses share one name

Reported at the second name (<<ensures-clause-names-are-distinct>>).

[#e1623]
=== `_` names an `ensures` clause

Reported at the name (<<an-ensures-clause-name-is-not-underscore>>).

[#e1624]
=== A composition carries `ensures`

Reported at the clause (<<a-composition-carries-no-ensures>>).

[#e1625-removed]
=== (retired)

E1625 is retired. It refused a case named twice under one arrow, on the reading that a test selecting one branch would leave the second naming unread. What a clause states is a conjunction — every rule whose guard holds applies — so the second naming states what the first did, which is redundant and not ambiguous. The two collapse to one rule (<<ensures>>).

[#e1626]
=== The model names a value an attached file declares

[,text]
----
`floor` is declared in an attached `examples for` file, which holds the rows, the fakes
they run against and the values those rows name. A declaration of the model cannot name
one: the module would not compile without that file, and a clause carrying the name
travels to an importer that has no source for it.
----

Emitted where a declaration of the model reaches a value an attached file declares (<<an-attached-files-values-are-for-its-rows>>). Two things follow from naming one and either is enough: the module no longer compiles from its own source, because the name it writes is declared in a file of fixtures; and a `+data+`'s `+invariant+` or a `+behavior+`'s `+ensures+` is published as the text that was written (<<a-published-module-carries-what-it-says-it-declares>>), so an importer reads a clause spelling a name it has no source for.

Said where the name is written, once for each occurrence, and the module is not emitted. The `+exposing+` list is reported at the module header, because a name in it is written nowhere else.

What is not reported is the other direction. A row names such a value wherever the row is written — beside the values in the attached file, or inline in the model source — because the values join the module the rows join and a row is what the file is for. A `+fake+` names one as a row does, and one attached value names another. An attached value naming the model's own `+let+` is a fixture reading the model, which is what a fixture is.

The fix is to declare the value in the module's own source. A value the model is written against is part of the model, and the attached file goes on reaching it from there.

[#e1627]
=== Something built here rests on a behavior nobody has written

[,text]
----
`quote` is built here and rests on `price`, which declares what it depends on and has no
`let`, so Souther is the one to implement it and nobody has.
----

Reported where the name is written — in a `+depends on+` clause, or as a `+>->+` stage (<<unwritten-behavior>>). The behavior naming it has a body, so it is built here and holds the named one as an injected field, and nothing can supply that field: Java has no base to extend, because the model says the body is Souther's, and Souther has not written it. Declaring the unwritten behavior is not an error, and neither is resting on it from another behavior that is itself unwritten.

[#e1701]
=== behavior composition type mismatch

[,text]
----
These behaviors cannot be composed: the left behavior's output is not accepted by the
right behavior's input.
Hint: Make the left output a case the right behavior accepts, or handle the extra cases
before this stage.
----

[#e1602]
=== Missing depends on declaration

[,text]
----
`let preApprove` calls `now`, which has no implementation, but `behavior preApprove`
does not declare `depends on now`.
Hint: Add `depends on now` to `behavior preApprove`.
----

[#e1603]
=== Over-declaration of depends on

[,text]
----
`behavior preApprove` declares `depends on numbering`, but `let preApprove` never calls it.
Hint: Remove `numbering` from the `depends on` clause.
----

It requires declaration and implementation to match, and does not admit an over-declaration erring on the safe side (<<depends-on>>).

[#e1604]
=== Composition output declaration and inference mismatch

[,text]
----
behavior placeOrder declares its output as PlacedOrder | OutOfStock, but the pipeline
produces PlacedOrder | OutOfStock | BeingReturned.
Hint: Update the declared output, or handle the extra case before the output.
----

A composition with a written declaration requires exact agreement with the inferred output (<<declared-composition-output>>). An internal composition with none written stays inferred and this check does not run. A public composition requires the declaration, reconciling the signature placed in `+exposing+` with inference (<<declared-composition-output>>, E1605).

[#e1605]
=== Missing / misplaced output signature of a public composition

[,text]
----
Exposed composition `placeOrder` must declare its output in `exposing`.
Hint: Write `exposing ( placeOrder : PlacedOrder | OutOfStock )`.
----

A `+>->+` composition exposed with `+exposing+` requires an output signature (<<declared-composition-output>>). Its absence is this error. Writing a signature in `+exposing+` for a non-composition behavior is also E1605, since the type is fixed at the definition.

[#e1606]
=== A declared sum's case is imported

[,text]
----
`OutOfStock` is declared in `inventory`, so it cannot be a case of `ShipmentResult`.
Hint: A sum's cases are declared with it: the generated sum is a sealed interface, and
only its own module can implement it. Consume `OutOfStock` at the boundary, or re-express
it as a case of this module.
----

A `+data S = A | B+` declares a type this module owns and publishes. Its cases are its vocabulary, and they are written with it. A case from an imported module cannot be one of them: the generated sum is a sealed interface, the class the other module emitted cannot implement it, and the case would be permitted without being a member — no value satisfies the type and an exhaustive `+switch+` over it has no arms.

An anonymous output union takes an imported member (<<union-member>>), and the difference is not the JVM. A named sum introduces a type name: it is written in a parameter, in a field, in another sum. An anonymous union introduces none — it is the shape of one behavior's answer and nobody names it. Taking another context's type into a shape is answering with the value that context owns; taking it into a declared sum is making it a word of this module's own vocabulary, and that is what a bounded context does not do.

The move for a sum is to translate: match the imported result and return a case of this module. A module boundary is where another context's vocabulary is restated in this one's.

[,souther]
----
// allocate is imported and OutOfStock is its departure; CannotAllocate is this module's answer to it
behavior allocateAndShip : (number: PartNumber) -> ShipmentOrder | CannotShip | CannotAllocate
    depends on instructShipment
    constructs CannotAllocate

let allocateAndShip (number, instructShipment) =
    match allocate(number) with
        | Allocated as allocation -> instructShipment(allocation)
        | OutOfStock as shortfall -> CannotAllocate { number = shortfall.number }
----

`+instructShipment+` is a behavior of this module and has a requirement of its own, so it is named in `+depends on+` and arrives bound (<<fn-rules>>): a composition builds its stages and their requirements accumulate into it, while a caller receives what it calls. An imported behavior is reached either way: it is called by name when its requirement set is empty, and named in `+depends on+` when the caller must supply what it requires.

[#e1607]
=== depends on does not name a dependency

[,text]
----
`behavior ship` declares `depends on allocate`, but `allocate` depends on nothing, so
there is nothing to inject.
Hint: Remove `allocate` from `depends on` and call it directly; a behavior that depends
on nothing is reached by name.
----

`+depends on+` names a behavior whose requirement set is not empty, declared here or imported (<<depends-on>>). A behavior that depends on nothing is E1607 — call it instead (<<calling-a-behavior>>) — and so is a `+>->+` composition, whose requirements are inferred from its stages rather than written, and so is a name that is no behavior in scope. All three are reported at the clause, where the name was written.

[#e1608]
=== A behavior reaches itself

[,text]
----
`behavior ship` reaches itself: ship -> allocate -> ship.
Hint: A behavior does not recurse, through calls, `depends on` or `>->` stages. Write the
recursion as a helper `let`, where it is proven total.
----

The cycle is reported wherever it closes, and the edges may be calls, `+depends on+`, `+>->+` stages, or a mixture of them (<<calling-a-behavior>>). A `+depends on+` cycle leaves nothing to build first, and a call cycle does not terminate. Recursion belongs to a named module helper, where it is proven total (<<fn-rules>>).

[#e1305]
=== An injected behavior constructs an uncreatable data

[,text]
----
Injected behavior `findMember` declares `constructs StoredData`, but StoredData is
not exposed, so Java cannot build it.
Hint: Expose StoredData.
----

Emitted when an unexposed data is listed in the `+constructs+` of an injection-subject behavior (implemented from Java; <<java-base-class>>), because Java would have no way to create that value.

Exposing it is the whole of the hint. Making it a unit was the other way out while a unit could be named here; a unit is in no construction set now (<<constructs-excludes-unit-data>>), so taking that advice would answer this diagnostic with E1026 on the same entry. A unit case of the output needs neither: the base class carries its factory whatever `+exposing+` says.

[#e1702]
=== A pipeline stage takes more than one input

[,text]
----
`convert` takes 2 inputs, so it cannot follow `>->` in `placeOrder`. Every stage after the first
takes one input: call it inline, or give it a data holding both.
----

Reported at the stage (<<a-stage-after-the-first-takes-one-input>>). What reaches a stage is the one value the stage before answered with, so there is nothing to fill a second parameter from.

[#e1703]
=== A pipeline names a boundary edge as a stage

[,text]
----
decode/encode are boundary edges, not pipeline stages; `>->` composes behaviors only.
----

Reported at the stage (<<a-pipeline-composes-behaviors>>). The decoder and the encoder are where a pipeline starts and ends (<<decoder>>, <<encoder>>) and are not steps within it.

[#e1802]
=== Something is applied to the wrong number of arguments

[,text]
----
Helper `let depth` takes 2 argument(s), but is called with 1.
----

Reported at the application (<<an-application-gives-the-arguments-it-takes>>). It covers a helper, a newtype applied to more than the one value it wraps, a lambda, and a block whose parameter list does not match the position it was written in.

[#e1803]
=== Something that is not a function is applied

[,text]
----
`unreachable` is a name the language gives, not a function, so it cannot be applied to arguments.
----

Reported at the application (<<what-is-applied-is-a-function>>).

[#e1804]
=== A function is written where a value goes, or the other way round

[,text]
----
Argument 2 of `List.fold` is `seed`, which does not take a function. The function goes to argument 1
(`step`).
----

Reported at the argument (<<a-function-goes-where-a-function-is-taken>>). Which argument takes the function belongs to the function being called, so the message names the one that does rather than describing the shape.

[#e1805]
=== A block does not answer what its position takes

[,text]
----
A fold's block must return the accumulator type Amount, but got Int.
----

Reported at the expression that supplies the block's result (<<a-block-answers-the-type-its-position-takes>>): a `+fold+`'s accumulator, a predicate's `+Bool+`, the element type of what is being grown. A block's own position is where its parameters start, and the value being refused came from its body, so the report is at the body's answer — past any `+let+` written above it, which holds what it binds and not what the block answered. An `+if+` or a `+match+` the body ends in is that answer: its type is the one its arms join to, so no single arm supplied it.

[#e1806]
=== An argument does not have the type its parameter takes

[,text]
----
`List.map`'s element type Amount is not acceptable to the function, which takes Int.
----

Reported at the argument (<<an-argument-has-the-type-its-parameter-takes>>). Where the argument is a `+fold+`'s step and the step is written as a block, it is reported at the expression that supplies the step's result, as <<e1805>> is. Where the step is a function declared elsewhere, nothing at this call made the value it answered with, and the argument is as far as the source goes.

[#e1807]
=== A function binding would need two types

[,text]
----
The function `f` is applied with different argument types: Int vs String.
----

Reported at the binding (<<a-function-binding-has-one-type>>). Applying it at two argument types, or giving it from two branches that produce different function types, leaves the binding with no single type; Souther has no generics to give it one (<<type-variables>>).

[#e1808]
=== A function binding's type cannot be read

[,text]
----
Cannot infer the type of the function `f`: write its type (`let f: (Int) -> Bool = ...`), or apply
it in this scope so its parameter types follow.
----

Reported at the binding (<<a-function-bindings-type-is-known>>).

[#e1809]
=== A block is returned or stored

[,text]
----
A block is not a value: it may be passed as an argument or bound to a `let` and applied, but it
cannot be returned or stored in a field.
----

Reported where a block leaves the scope it was written in (<<a-block-is-not-a-value>>). A function has no external representation (<<external-representation>>), so a field holding one could not be decoded or encoded and an output holding one could not cross out.

[#e1810]
=== An annotation on a function binding is not a function type

[,text]
----
The binding `step` holds a function, and its annotation is not a function type.
----

Reported at the annotation (<<an-annotation-on-a-function-binding-is-a-function-type>>). A function type in that annotation is admitted, as it is in every type position a representation is not required at (<<a-type-is-written-in-a-type-position>>); what this reports is an annotation that describes something else.

[#e1811]
=== A helper parameter has no type

[,text]
----
Helper `let total` parameter `line` is only read through a field, which names no type; annotate it
with its type.
----

Reported at the parameter (<<a-helper-parameter-states-its-type>>). Reading a field off a parameter, or applying it, does not name a type — several types have a field of that name — so the annotation is what says which.

[#e1812]
=== A helper does not answer what it declares

[,text]
----
Helper `let total` declares it returns Amount, but its body is Int.
----

Reported at the helper (<<a-helper-answers-what-it-declares>>).

[#e1813]
=== A recursive helper declares no return type

[,text]
----
Recursive helper `let depth` must declare its return type (`let depth (...) : <type> = ...`),
because its result cannot be inferred from a body that names itself.
----

Reported at the helper (<<a-recursive-helper-declares-its-return-type>>).

[#e1814]
=== A recursive helper reaches an injected behavior

[,text]
----
Recursive helper `let depth` is pure and cannot call the injected behavior `now` — put the effect in
the behavior that calls this helper.
----

Reported at the call (<<a-recursive-helper-does-not-reach-an-injected-behavior>>). The helper is emitted as a pure static method (<<fn-rules>>), which is what lets its termination be proven and what keeps the effect where a reader of the behavior can see it. A function argument that reaches an effect is not this: the effect is the caller's and is declared there.

[#e1815]
=== An empty collection's type cannot be read where it is written

[,text]
----
This empty collection's element type cannot be inferred here.
Hint: Annotate the binding it feeds (`let x: <type> = ...`), place the fold directly in a typed
position — the field or behavior output it feeds — or seed it with a non-empty value.
----

Reported at the operation (<<an-empty-collections-element-type-is-determined>>). What an empty collection holds does not follow from the collection, so it follows from the position it is written in or from nothing at all.

[#e1816]
=== What is ordered by is not an ordered value

[,text]
----
`sort` needs an ordered element, but the element is Member.
----

Reported at the call (<<what-is-ordered-by-is-an-ordered-value>>). Not a type mismatch: `+sort+`, `+max+`, `+min+` declare `+List<'a>+` and the argument fits, and `+sortBy+`'s key function fits its declared signature too. What is missing is a capability the signature had no way to ask for, Souther having no `+comparable+` type class (<<stdlib>>), so the compiler asks it beside the signature. For `+sortBy+` it is asked of what the key answers with, that being the value being ordered by.

[#e1817]
=== What is summed is not a number

[,text]
----
`sum` needs an Int or a Decimal element, but the element is String.
----

Reported at the call (<<what-is-summed-is-a-number>>). The same shape as <<e1816>> and a different obligation: `+sum+` and `+product+` declare `+List<'a>+`, and what `+'a+` has to be is one of the two types arithmetic is defined for.

[#e1818]
=== A behavior is called from somewhere that does not reach one

[,text]
----
`allocate` is a behavior, and it cannot be called from here.
Hint: Call it from the behavior's own `let` and pass the result in, or name it in `depends on`.
----

Reported at the call (<<a-helper-does-not-call-a-behavior>>). A helper is expanded into whatever calls it and travels to an importing module as source, so what it may name is what travels with it; a `+>->+` composition is composed with a behavior rather than calling one. Not <<e1023>>, which is a name that resolves to nothing; this one resolves, to a behavior.

[#e1901]
=== Example target is not a behavior

[,text]
----
`noSuchThing` is not a behavior in this module, so this example has no target.
----

Emitted when an `+example+` names a target that is not a behavior of the module (<<examples>>).

[#e1902]
=== Example target is not evaluable

[,text]
----
`taxIncluded` cannot be evaluated as an example.
Hint: An example runs a behavior with a `let` body, or a `>->` composition of them; here
it is a helper `let`, and this module declares no behavior of that name.
----

Emitted when an `+example+` names something this module declares that is not a behavior — a helper `+let+` (<<fn-declaration>>). Being injected is not a reason: an injected behavior is a behavior, and its rows are recorded (<<example-pending>>). Neither is a `+depends on+` dependency, which is supplied by a fake (<<example-fakes>>), nor a `+>->+` composition, which is evaluated by running its stages.

[#e1903]
=== Example input cannot be built

[,text]
----
Input #1 of `submitTravelRequest` could not be built: value must be >= 0
----

Emitted when a fixture of the row itself did not produce a value when it ran: a wrong arity, a value that breaks the type's invariant, an operation that answers no value for what it was given. What a row writes is compiled as this module's code, so a value that does not state a value of its position at all is refused where the same value written in a body is (<<e1812>>, <<e1317>>, <<fixture-is-written-not-carried>>, <<fixture-names-what-it-supplies>>) and never reaches here. The expected result is built before the behavior is applied, so a row whose expectation does not produce a value is reported here rather than as a mismatch (<<e1905>>) against a value nothing built. A fixture standing in for a dependency is that dependency's, and is <<e1908>>.

[#e1904]
=== Expected arm is not a result case

[,text]
----
`Drafting` is not one of the result cases of `submitTravelRequest`.
Hint: Expected one of: Submitted, AwaitingPreApproval
----

Emitted when a row's expected arm is not one of the target behavior's output cases (<<example-expected>>). An expectation whose case is not written — a helper's answer — has no arm to hold against them and is compared as the value it is; a row expecting the wrong case is then <<e1905>>.

[#e1905]
=== Example does not hold

[,text]
----
This example does not hold.
found:    Submitted { submittedAt = "2026-07-20T09:00" }
expected: AwaitingPreApproval { submittedAt = "2026-07-20T09:00" }
----

Emitted when a row's evaluated result does not match the expected arm or value. Every failing row of a build is reported. Both sides are shown as values, encoded through the data's derived Encoder (<<encoder>>) — the arm name alone does not say what went wrong when a row fails inside one arm. A row that asserts only the arm (a bare case name, <<example-expected>>) shows just that name.

[#e1906]
=== An examples file contains non-example declarations

[,text]
----
An `examples for` file holds examples, the fakes they run against and the values they
name. A data, a behavior, an import, and a `let` with parameters belong in the module
itself.
----

Emitted when a file that begins with `+examples for+` holds something other than an example, a `+fake+` table, or a value its rows name — and when a value it declares carries a name the target module already declares (<<example-placement>>). The second is reported on the attached file, where the declaration is written.

[#e1907]
=== Examples file names an absent module

[,text]
----
`examples for example.nope` names a module that is not being compiled.
----

Emitted when an attached `+examples for <module>+` file names a target module that is not among the compiled modules (<<example-placement>>).

[#e1908]
=== A dependency has no fake

[,text]
----
`preApprove` depends on `now`, but no fake was supplied for it.
Hint: Add `with now = ...` on the row, or a `fake now` table.
----

Emitted when an example targets a behavior whose `+depends on+` dependency has neither a `+with+` on the row nor a `+fake+` declaration (<<example-fakes>>). A dependency that *is* faked but whose fake cannot be built — a `+with+` value, or a row of its `+fake+` table — is reported under the same code and says that instead, naming the dependency once: the fake is supplied, and what failed is the fixture.

Not being a value of what the dependency answers with is one of the ways it fails, and is said here rather than at the row: a stand-in is the dependency's answer while the row runs, so a value of another type is not an answer the row can disagree with (<<e1905>>). It is refused before the behavior is applied, so no row reaches the model holding one.

The two are quoted where each is written. A `+with+` is written on a row, so the row it is written on is where its value not building is said; a `+fake+` table is written on its own, so that is where a row of it not building is said — once for the table, whether or not any row reads it (<<example-fakes>>).

[#e1909]
=== A fake has no output for an input

[,text]
----
A fake had no output for an input: `findManager` has no output for EmployeeId("e-9")
----

Emitted when a `+fake+` table is asked for an input it does not list and has no `+_+` default row (<<example-fakes>>).

[#e1910]
=== An example exceeded its evaluation budget

[,text]
----
The evaluation of this row did not finish within its budget of 100000000 steps.
Hint: What the counting shows is that the evaluation did not finish, not that the model
does not: a `partial` recursion that never stops reaches this, and so does one that would
have stopped shortly after. If the model does finish and needs more, raise
`souther.example.step.limit`; otherwise bound the loop or make the recursion structural.
----

Emitted when a row's evaluation exceeds the budget the example runner holds it to: the number of counted points the evaluated code may pass, or the depth a recursion it reaches may go to. Both are counted by the generated code as it runs, so the reading is a property of what the row does and answers the same on every machine.

What this establishes is that the evaluation did not finish within the budget, and not that the model does not terminate. Those are different statements and a finite budget cannot reach the second: a recursion that never stops exceeds the budget, and so does one that would have stopped a hundred steps later. The usual cause is a `+partial+` declaration the row reaches, which publishes no termination guarantee (<<fn-rules>>) — but a model that does terminate and needs more than the budget allows reaches it too, and raising the setting is then the answer. A helper carrying the termination guarantee is not exempt: it is bounded, not free.

The guarantee is about this diagnostic and not about compilation as a whole. Whether a row is reported here does not depend on how fast the host is, how loaded it is, what the JVM has compiled, or what any row before it did. Whether the compiler manages to answer at all is a separate question: work that is not counted — loading a class, decoding a value through a derived decoder, comparing two of them — still takes time, and an evaluation that stops answering within the wait it is given is reported as <<e1923>>. That work is bounded by what the counted work produced, since a value only gets large by being built, but it is bounded rather than counted, so it is measured in time.

Two other things end an evaluation, and neither is decided by what the model says. Where it stops answering, that is <<e1923>>; where the JVM stack runs out before the counted depth limit is reached, <<e1924>>.

[#e1911]
=== An example reached a point declared unreachable

[,text]
----
This row reached a point the model declares cannot arise: twenty insured years cannot
precede the age of thirty (42:17)
Hint: Either the row states a combination the model rules out, or the reason written at
that `unreachable` does not hold.
----

Emitted when a row's evaluation reaches an `+unreachable+` (<<unreachable>>). The reason the model wrote is shown with the line and column it was written at, because the row and the declaration are usually in different rules; the diagnostic's own place is the row's. It is not a failed comparison: the row produced no answer to compare, and what it found is a premise that does not hold.

[#e1913]
=== No row expects a case the signature declares

[,text]
----
No row expects `Waiting`, so nothing says `submit` owes that answer.
Hint: Write a row whose expected value is a `Waiting`, or drop the case from the
signature.
----

A warning, off unless the build asks for it (<<example-adequacy>>). Emitted at the behavior's declaration when its output is a sum and no row expects one of its cases. Only where the measurement was complete: a row whose expected case could not be read leaves the question undecided, and telling an author to write a row they may already have written is worse than saying nothing.

[#e1915]
=== No row uses a case an input declares

[,text]
----
No row applies `submit` to a `Overseas`, so input #1 has a case nothing was tried with.
----

The same warning for an input position that is a sum. Covering every case an input can be is the mechanised half of asking whether the behavior is total (<<match>>).

Not warned about for a case the model's own rules refuse (<<a-case-the-rules-refuse-is-not-covered-or-uncovered>>): the row it asks for could not be built (<<e1903>>), so an author told to write one has been handed work their compiler refuses.

[#e1916]
=== No row is at a boundary

[,text]
----
No row is at the ON point of the line the `guard` draws.
Hint: A row at the nearest value the border keeps in is what shows the border does not fall a step further in.
Hint: Read at submit/cost, a row there is = 100.
----

A warning, and one that needs the arms measured where the line was drawn by a fork of the body (<<example-branch-coverage>>): meeting such a line takes reaching the comparison, not writing the value. A line an invariant drew is decided without that. The rule that drew it is named, because the same value can be a border of several and reaching it through one leaves the others unmet.

Said of the two points against the line and of neither of the two away from it (<<a-border-has-four-point-roles>>). Those two are what simplified domain coverage asks for and what a row on the line shows that no other row does; the points inside and outside are the two items reliable domain coverage adds, and a build held to that criterion is told about them under <<e1917>>. Which of the two criteria a build is held to is what it asked for (<<example-adequacy>>), and the report still names neither as satisfied.

Which of the two it is, in the word the report gives it (<<a-border-says-which-point-it-owes>>). One finding is told on two surfaces, and a reader handed this vocabulary by the report was working it out again here. `+ON+` and `+OFF+` are the technique's terms rather than English words for it, so they are the same in every catalog and what a catalog holds is the sentence around them. The rule is named as the one that drew the border and not as one drawing its line here: only one of the two points carries the value the rule was written with, so the other would be told the line is where it is not. The hint follows the point and is said in the same words — what a row at the nearest value the border keeps in shows, and what one at the nearest value it keeps out shows. Not which side of the line the value falls on: which of the two points carries the line's own value turns on the border being closed or open (<<a-border-has-four-point-roles>>), so a hint keyed on that would be a second reading of one finding under a sentence that has just named the role. What tells `+<=+` from `+<+` is the border being closed or open, which the report says of the border.

The fork is named by the construct it was written as — an `+if+`, a `+guard+`, or a guard comprehension (<<stdlib-list>>) — and where it is written is pointed at rather than said, a place being something only a reader's own files give a name to. All three lower to one form before anything runs, so the word comes from where the source was read and not from the shape it was lowered to.

[#e1917]
=== No row is at a point away from a boundary

[,text]
----
No row is at an IN point away from the line the `guard` draws.
Hint: A row well inside is what shows the border, and not something else about the value, is
what divides the two.
Hint: Read at submit/cost, a row there is in submit/cost < 100.
----

[[every-obligation-away-from-its-line-has-a-row]]A warning, and only to a build held to reliable domain coverage. The same assessment of the same line answers this and <<e1916>>; what differs is which of the four points a build is asked for (<<a-border-has-four-point-roles>>). A build that asked for simplified domain coverage is not told this, and the points are still reported.

Told apart from <<e1916>> by a code of its own rather than by the role in the sentence. One code covering both would be a build asking to be held to the first criterion and refused over the second's items.

[#e1918]
=== No row goes through an arm

[,text]
----
No row goes through the `else` arm in `submit`.
Hint: Either a row is missing for what this arm answers, or nothing reaches it — which an
`unreachable` states.
----

A warning, needing the arms measured (<<example-branch-coverage>>). Quoted at the `+if+`, `+match+` or attempted construction the arm belongs to rather than at the arm's own body: the fork is what the author wrote, and a body is what lowering rewrites.

A behavior no row names is not warned about, whether or not another behavior's rows run through it. An arm that answers nothing is not an arm and is not warned about (<<an-arm-that-answers-nothing-is-not-an-arm>>); the hint names `+unreachable+` because stating that nothing reaches an arm is what answers this warning where no row can.

What the way through is called comes from the construct as well as from the way. An `+if+` has a `+then+` and an `+else+` to quote; a `+guard+` has the `+else+` the author wrote and, on the other side, the rest of the block, which is named as that rather than as a `+then+` nobody wrote; a guard comprehension has neither word, and its two ways are the element it yields and the empty list it yields instead.

[#e1919]
=== A stand-in and a recorded row answer one input differently

[,text]
----
This row and the `fake findMember` row state different answers for the same input.
Hint: The row says Found { id = "m-1" }; the fake says Missing { why = "no such member" }.
----

A warning, and not one the adequacy dial turns on (<<example-adequacy>>): nothing is counted and no behavior is applied, and what it costs is building the fixtures on both sides and comparing them. Emitted when a `+fake+` row, or a `+with+` for a dependency that takes no input, answers an input for which an `+example+` row records a different answer (<<example-pending>>). Which of the two the model is to be held to is not readable from the text, so neither is named as the right one.

One disagreement is one warning. It is quoted at the recorded row and carries a second region at the stand-in, which names its own file when the two are in different sources (<<example-placement>>). It is reported at both of those files, so an author reading either is told; where the caret sits is where a reader starts, and says nothing about which of the two the model is to be held to.

Where a recorded row names a case and nothing under it (<<example-expected>>), the comparison is on the case, and the case of the other side is the one its value turned out to be — read from the value, resolved to the type this module knows it under. Which case a value is comes from the value and not from the text that produced it, so a fake row answering through a helper states the case that helper returned.

A row whose fixture does not finish is read within its own budget and states nothing; the rows beside it are unaffected, and what happened is said where that row is evaluated (<<e1910>>).

A row whose arity is wrong, whose inputs or expectation will not build, or whose expectation is not a case the behavior answers with, states nothing to compare, and each is said as the error it is (<<e1903>>, <<e1904>>).

[#e1920]
=== A fake and the rows recorded for one behavior were not compared

[,text]
----
Could not compare this fake with the rows recorded for `find` — building the table spent
its budget of 100000000 steps.
----

A warning, quoted where the `+fake+` names the behavior it stands in for. Emitted when reading a `+fake+` table against the rows recorded for that behavior (<<example-pending>>) does not come to a value, so what the two state was never compared. Which of three things stopped it is said: the table spent its counted budget, it reached the recursion-depth limit, or the evaluation stopped answering. The first two are decided by what the table states and read the same on every machine; the third is not a statement about the model at all. Being told nothing was found and being told nothing was compared are different answers, and an empty list of disagreements is what the first one looks like.

[#e1921]
=== A fake's table was not built in time

[,text]
----
Building the `fake find` table spent its budget of 100000000 steps, so nothing it states
was checked.
----

A warning, quoted where the `+fake+` names the behavior it stands in for. Emitted when building the table where it is written (<<example-fakes>>) does not come to a value, with the same three reasons <<e1920>> distinguishes. A warning rather than an error: what stopped the build says the table was not read, not that it is wrong.

[#e1922]
=== An import is never used under the name it was given

[,text]
----
`Employee` is imported but never used under this name.
Hint: Take it off the import list. What another module declares is reachable qualified
whether or not it is imported, so an entry here earns its place only by being written bare
somewhere below.
----

A warning, at the entry on the list rather than at the import as a whole (<<imports-are-used>>). What an import list buys is the bare name; an entry no bare use reads buys nothing and still says the module depends on that name. A qualified reach does not keep an entry: it reads the same whether or not the name is listed (<<imports>>), so a name reached only that way is on the list for nothing. An editor is told the entry is unnecessary rather than merely told about it, since what to do with it is to delete it.

[#e1923]
=== An example evaluation did not answer

[,text]
----
The evaluation of this row did not answer within 60000ms and was given up on.
Hint: This is the compiler failing to decide, not the model failing to terminate: what did
not come back was not counted, so nothing here says the row would not have held. The wait is
over the compiler's own work — a class it loaded, a value it decoded, a module read from a
jar — and not over what an implementation supplied from Java spends answering.
----

Emitted when an evaluation stops answering and the wait the policy allows runs out. Not <<e1910>>: what did not come back was not held to a counted budget, so nothing was shown about whether the model terminates. Counted points are placed in the code this compile generates and in the classes it loads from the module path, so what this reaches is the work the compiler does around them — loading a class, decoding a value, comparing two of them — or the compiler itself.

The wait is over the compiler's own time. Where a row is run against an implementation supplied from Java, that implementation answers out of the caller's world — a transaction, an HTTP call, a test runner — and what it spends there is the caller's to bound, so it is not counted here and this is never a report about it. A row that a supplied implementation never returns from is not given up on by the compiler at all.

[#e1924]
=== An example evaluation ran out of stack

[,text]
----
The evaluation of this row ran out of JVM stack (recursion overflowed the stack) before
reaching its recursion-depth limit of 10000.
Hint: The depth limit is what is meant to stop a recursion, and the stack got there first.
Lower the depth limit, give the evaluation a larger stack, or rewrite the helper as tail
recursion so it loops instead of adding frames.
----

Emitted when the JVM stack runs out before the counted recursion-depth limit is reached. Not <<e1910>> either: how many frames a stack holds depends on how large they are, which is decided by the helper and by whether the JVM has compiled it — so this says nothing about whether the recursion terminates. The depth limit and the stack an evaluation is given are set so the counted limit is reached first; this says they are set wrong for this model.

[#e1925]
=== A name is on more than one row of one behavior

[,text]
----
`over the ceiling` names more than one row of `submit`, so nothing outside this file can
say which row it means.
----

Emitted at every row of one behavior carrying a name another of its rows carries, in the file that row is written in (<<a-row-name-is-unique-within-its-behavior>>). Read over the module's own source and the `+examples for+` files attached to it together, since a behavior's rows may be written across them (<<example-placement>>).

Both rows are reported and neither is named as the duplicate. Which source a row is in comes from the order a compile was handed its files, which a build and a command line decide differently; a rule naming one of the two would say different things about one model depending on how it was compiled.

Rows written with no name are not compared with each other: a row without a name is not addressable from outside, so there is nothing for two of them to disagree about (<<example-row-name>>).

[#e1926]
=== A row of a fake's table can never answer

[,text]
----
An earlier row of `fake findMember` states these arguments, so that is what the table
answers with and this row answers nothing.
----

Emitted at a row of a `+fake+` table that the table's dispatch can never return, naming the row it returns instead (<<every-row-of-a-fake-table-can-answer>>). Two rows are written and never reached: one stating what an earlier row states, since the first match is what answers; and a `+_+` row with another `+_+` after it, since the last of them is what a table falls through to. The two point in opposite directions — the earlier explicit row answers, the later `+_+` does — so the row that answers is quoted rather than left to be read off the order.

An error, and not a measurement: which row answers is decided by the table's own rule rather than by anything a run finds out, so a row that cannot answer is refused the way an arm no `+match+` can reach is (<<e1204>>) rather than reported as something a build may carry.

A `+_+` row and an explicit row do not shadow each other, whichever order they are written in: an explicit row answers where it is asked for what it states, and the `+_+` row answers everywhere else. A second block written for one behavior is not this either: what the dispatch of one table can answer with is one question, and how many blocks name a behavior is another, said where those blocks are (<<e1933>>).

[#e1927]
=== What answers a behavior is not of the model being evaluated

[,text]
----
What answers `findTodo` was built against another revision of `todo`: `Title` is
declared differently there.
----

Emitted at an `+example+` whose behavior is answered by an implementation supplied from outside the compile, where nothing can establish that what answers it was built against this model (<<an-answer-is-of-the-module-being-evaluated>>). The rows are not run against it: what a row's values are is decided by this model and what reads them is decided by whatever that implementation was compiled against, so a row handed over would be decided by the two disagreeing rather than by the model — and that lands on a comparison, which is read as a statement about the model.

Said once for the behavior in each source its rows are written in, however many rows those are: one answer and one model disagreeing is one fact, and the rows did not stop for reasons of their own.
