Not once for the behavior outright, because a diagnostic is said where it can be quoted — a behavior whose rows are written in the module and in an attached `+examples for+` file has rows in two files, and a reader of the second one would otherwise be shown rows that stopped and nothing saying why. Each row is recorded as undecided, with its inputs and its expectation already built and held to their invariants.

Two things are reported under this code, and they are not the same finding. Where both sides were read and one declares something else, the declaration is named and the fix is to build again. Where the declarations could not be read at all — classes carrying none, or declarations this compiler cannot read back (<<a-published-module-can-be-read-back>>) — nothing has been established about either build, and saying a build was stale would be saying it on evidence nobody has.

[#e1928]
=== An example row states values the behavior's clause rules out

[,text]
----
The values this row states do not keep what `findTodo` declares of what it answers:
ensures not held on todo.findTodo: asked, answering Todo
----

Emitted at an `+example+` row whose input and stated answer do not keep an `+ensures+` of the behavior it is written for (<<a-row-keeps-what-the-behavior-states>>). The stated answer is the value the row wrote, or the case its arm names where it wrote no value. What decides it is the declaration's own check, applied to the values the row wrote (<<example-ensures>>), so what the message carries is what that check said — the clause that was not kept, the case that clause's rule was written for, and the case the answer turned out to be. An arm may name a case that has cases of its own, and an answer is one of those, so the two are said apart; where they are the same case it is said once.

A refusal and not a warning. A stand-in and a row disagreeing is two descriptions with no right side (<<e1919>>); this is a description held to what the model states, and a row that records an answer the model rules out is a record of something that will not happen.

Said before anything applies the behavior, so a row waiting for an implementation is told the same day it is written. A row whose expectation is a bare case name is held to what that case decides on its own (<<example-ensures>>).

[#e1929]
=== A fake row stands in with values the dependency's clause rules out

[,text]
----
The values this row stands in with do not keep what `lookup` declares of what it
answers: ensures not held on order.lookup: asked, answering Found
----

Emitted at a row of a `+fake+` table whose input and output do not keep an `+ensures+` of the behavior the table stands in for (<<a-fake-row-keeps-what-the-dependency-states>>). The same rule as <<e1928>> at the other place a model writes both sides of a relation down, and told apart from it because what an author does about the two is not the same: one is what a behavior owes, the other what stands in for it while some other behavior's row runs.

A `+_+` row states no input, so there is nothing here to relate its answer to; what it answers is held at the crossing into generated code, like any other answer arriving from outside.

A table with such a row is not one to stand in with, as a table that will not build is not (<<example-fakes>>). A row reaching it stops without a fake and says nothing of its own: running it would put the rest of the behavior in a state the model rules out, and what the row then reported would be about a run that cannot happen.

Neither is it compared with the rows recorded for the behavior (<<e1919>>). That comparison is written for two descriptions with nothing to choose between them, and this is a description held to a declaration — reported together they would say of one pair both that the fake is the side that is wrong and that neither side is.

[#e1930]
=== An implementation answers a row with a value the behavior's clause rules out

[,text]
----
The implementation supplied for `findTodo` answered this row's input with a value that
does not keep what `findTodo` declares of what it answers:
ensures not held on todo.findTodo: asked, answering Todo
----

Emitted at an `+example+` row of an injected behavior, where an implementation supplied to the run answered the row's input with a value that does not keep an `+ensures+` of that behavior (<<an-implementation-keeps-what-the-behavior-states>>). The check that decides it is the emitted one, the same one a crossing into generated code invokes.

Told apart from <<e1928>> by which text is wrong. That one is a row recording something the model rules out; this is code outside the model doing something the model says it will not, and what an author does about the two is not the same.

Only an injected behavior reaches it. A behavior with a body checks its own answer where it answers, so by the time a row sees that answer it has already been held to the declaration, and asking again would be a clause checked twice.

Said before the row's own comparison. What the answer disagrees with here is the model, and a row told only that it expected one value and saw another would send its author to look at the row.

[#e1931]
=== No row is in a class the model divides a position into

[,text]
----
No row is in the class `C` at grade in `ship`.
Hint: Write a row whose grade is a value the model puts in `C`.
----

A warning, and only where the build asked for a bar that refuses over it (<<a-bar-is-a-criterion-and-what-it-adds>>). The classes are the ones the model already states (<<example-partition>>); a position the model draws no line through has none and is never warned about here.

Told apart from <<e1915>> by what is missing. That one is a case the type declares and no row applies the behavior to; this is a class the model's rules divide the position into and no row's value falls in. The same `+C+` can be both, and one row closes both — which is why they are two findings rather than one: a position with no sum has classes, and a sum an invariant narrows has fewer classes than cases.

Not raised for a class a measurement could not place a row in. A row whose value at the position could not be read leaves the class undecided rather than uncovered, and an author told to write a row may already have written one.

[#e1932]
=== A stand-in does not name a behavior

[,text]
----
`quotes` is no behavior in scope, so there is nothing for this to stand in for.
Hint: Declare it here or import it.
----

Emitted where a `+fake+` or a `+with+` names something no behavior in scope answers to (<<example-fakes>>). The name is read as a behavior is read anywhere else, so a dependency another module declares is admitted, and a behavior of that spelling declared here is not mistaken for it (<<qualified-reference>>).

Told apart from <<e1908>> by which side is missing. There a row wants a dependency and nothing supplies it; here something was supplied and it names nothing to supply. The fix differs, so the two do not share a code.

What the named behavior is implemented by is not asked. A stand-in is a statement about what that behavior answers, and a table written for one that has a body states what it states (<<example-pending>>).

[#e1933]
=== More than one stand-in table for one behavior

[,text]
----
More than one `fake` block stands in for `findManager`, so none of them does.
Hint: write the rows of every `fake findManager` block as one block
----

Emitted at every `+fake+` block naming a behavior another block of the module also names (<<example-fakes>>), each in the file its block is written in. Two targets name one behavior when resolution denotes one, however either is written.

None of them is preferred. The rows of a block are matched in the order they are written, and the blocks themselves are written across a module's own source and the files attached to it, which have no order between them — so there is no table two blocks make and no reading under which one of them is the one that answers. Which of them is the one to write differently is not the language's to answer, as it is not for two rows carrying one name (<<e1925>>).

Told apart from <<e1908>>, where a dependency has no stand-in at all. A row requiring a behavior reported here is not told one is missing, nor that a table has no output for its input (<<e1909>>): it is told neither, because what is wrong is what the module wrote rather than anything the row states.

[#e1934]
=== A row is written with its answer still owed

[,text]
----
Row `over the ceiling` of `submit` is written with its answer still to be written.
Hint: Replace the row's `<?>` with what the system answers for those inputs.
----

Emitted at every row written `+<?>+` (<<example-answer-owed>>), and at every arm whose only rows are those. A warning, and one every bar refuses over: the row is written down and its answer is not, so nothing about how much of the model could be measured bears on it.

Read off the source rather than off what a run came to. A row's answer is owed whether or not the behavior has arms, whether or not another row covers the arm this one goes through, and whether or not anything could be measured about the rows at all — so none of those can take the finding away.

Told apart from <<e1918>>, which is an arm no row goes through. A row does go through the arm reported here; what it is short of is the answer, and writing a second row for it would answer nothing.

[#e2001]
=== A helper does not carry the termination guarantee it claims

[,text]
----
Recursive helper `let depth` is not structurally recursive: it passes no argument that is
a strictly smaller part of a parameter. Recurse on a part obtained by `match` (a field or
a case), count with `fold`, or mark the helper `partial`.
----

Emitted when a helper's recursion is not shown to terminate by size-change analysis. A group of mutually recursive helpers is reported together, and a recursion too large for the analysis says so instead of guessing; `+partial+` is the opt-out in every case (<<fn-rules>>).

The same code says the two other ways the guarantee fails. One is reaching a declaration that does not carry it, reported at the helper's own name and naming the path — one report per helper, so a module needing the word in several places is told about all of them in one build.

[,text]
----
`let depth` does not carry the termination guarantee it claims: it reaches the `partial`
helper `spin` (depth -> measure -> spin). Mark `depth` `partial` as well, or stop
reaching it.
----

The other is writing a `+partial+` helper where a value goes, which takes it out of reach of the walk above.

[,text]
----
The `partial` helper `spin` is written where a value goes. A function type carries no
termination guarantee, so a `partial` helper may be applied but not handed over.
----

[#e2010]
=== A construction violates its invariant

[,text]
----
Constructing `Amount` here violates its invariant: the value being built is one the
invariant rejects. Fix the value.
----

Emitted when the check proves the invariant false at a construction (<<invariant-discharge>>). What is said of it depends on what proved it. The message above is for a violation the values reach on their own — a value written out, an arithmetic result, a name given one of those, or what an input's own type guarantees of it — which fails wherever the construction is written. An invariant is the conjunction of its clauses, so one clause failing that way is the whole invariant failing that way. Where the values alone do not settle it and what else is known where it stands does, the message says that instead:

[,text]
----
Constructing `Amount` here violates its invariant on a reachable path: under what is
assumed where it stands, the value being built is one the invariant rejects. Fix the
value, or guard the path so the violating case cannot reach here.
----

Neither names what decided it: the check knows the values alone did not, and not which of the things known there did.

The secondary regions identify only the invariant clauses shown to reject the value wherever it is built. A clause the check could settle neither way is a clause nothing known there establishes, which is what <<e2011>> reports; it is not presented here as one the value fails.

Where the paths reaching a construction fail different clauses, every path violates the invariant and no clause is one every path fails. The message then names no clause — there is none to name — and the regions identify the clauses a path here fails, saying that of them and not that the value fails them.

[#e2011]
=== A construction is not shown to satisfy its invariant

[,text]
----
model.sou:7:5
7|     invariant charged <= limit
       ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
Nothing known here establishes this clause.

Constructing `Amount` here may violate its invariant: nothing known here establishes it.
Hint: If inputs for which the construction fails are ones the behavior accepts, that failure
is a business case: guard the construction, or attempt it with `... as name else ...`, which
states the invariant check once. If those inputs are not admitted by the model, the relation
belongs to a data — declare it as an `invariant` on the input that holds these values,
introducing such a data where none exists.
----

The message says what the check found and the hint says what the model has left to say, and the two are not the same sentence. What was found is that nothing known where the construction stands establishes the clause — not that a guard is missing. What is known there is what the input types declare, what a `+...+` spread brought in, and what the conditions on the way there add; a guard is one of those and naming it alone read as a report about guards.

The hint states the modelling question, which has two answers and three spellings. Where the inputs the construction fails for are ones the behavior takes, the failure is a business one and is written as a branch — by a guard, or by attempting the construction (<<attempted-construction>>), which is the same answer with the invariant written once instead of twice. Where they are not inputs the model admits, the relation belongs to a data that owns it, which need not already exist: a relation between two parameters belongs to no input, so writing it means introducing the input that holds them both. There is deliberately no third answer that leaves the construction as it is and accepts the abort — an invariant check is the safety net for a model bug (<<violation-destination>>), not a way to record an unanswered modelling question in the source.

The secondary regions identify the invariant clauses nothing there established, at the declarations that write them. Every one of them, in the order they are declared, and however many there are.

What a clause is called and where it is written are separate things a report may carry, and neither is the other's fallback. Of the clauses a report is about, the message names the ones an author wrote a name on (<<invariant-declaration>>), and the regions point at the ones this compile has the source of — which is every clause but those of a module read off the module path, whose declarations arrive as text reassembled from what the module carries. A clause with a name and no source to quote is named and not pointed at; a clause with a source and no name is pointed at and said to be "its invariant". The same holds of <<e2010>>.

Which clauses a report is about is a third thing, and the message and the regions need not be about the same ones: they say different things, and what may truthfully be said of a clause is what decides which of them says it. E2010 where the paths fail different clauses is that case.

A warning, and the one diagnostic in this chapter that says nothing about whether the model is wrong. E2010 is the check having proved the invariant false; this is the check having proved nothing either way, so what was always going to happen still happens — the invariant is tested when the value is built, and a violation aborts there (<<violation-destination>>). A model that does establish the invariant, by an argument the check cannot follow, is told this too, and is not thereby wrong.

What to do about it is therefore a modelling decision and not a mechanical repair, and it is the decision the hint states. Where the behavior admits inputs for which the construction can violate the invariant, that failure is a business one: guard the construction, which puts the reason where the check can read it, or attempt it, which lets the invariant itself decide the branch and writes the rule once. Where the behavior admits no such input, the relation is part of the data that owns those values — declared as an `+invariant+` on it, tested once at that data's boundary and assumed everywhere the value reaches afterwards (<<invariant-discharge>>) — and where no data holds them, writing the relation means introducing the one that does.

Leaving the construction as written leaves the warning unanswered. The run-time test still stands there, as it stands under every construction (<<violation-destination>>), and it is the safety net for a model bug rather than a third answer: nothing an author writes turns this warning into an accepted abort, and there is no notation for doing so.

An attempted construction raises no warning, which is not that third answer either. What this reports is a possible abort, and an attempt takes its `+else+` value instead of aborting (<<attempted-construction>>) — the failure becomes a business branch the author names, which is the first answer written once rather than twice.

[#e2012]
=== An attempted construction that cannot be read

Two codes, for the two ways the form does not read at all (<<attempted-construction>>). E2012 is `+as+` naming something that does not build a value:

[,text]
----
`as` names the value a construction builds, and this is not a construction.
Hint: Write the construction whose invariant decides the branch, or drop the `as` and give
a condition.
----

E2013 is the form reading and having nothing to decide, since a type declaring no invariant refuses no value:

[,text]
----
`Amount` declares no invariant, so an attempted construction of it always succeeds and the
`else` value is never reached.
Hint: Construct `Amount` directly, or give it the invariant that decides the branch.
----

[#e2013]
=== An attempted type declares no invariant

[,text]
----
`Rank` declares no invariant, so an attempted construction of it always succeeds and the `else`
value is never reached.
Hint: Construct `Rank` directly, or give it the invariant that decides the branch.
----

Emitted when the type attempted declares no invariant, its own or one a spread brought in (<<attempt-subject-declares-an-invariant>>). The `+else+` is written to be reached, and here nothing can reach it: the branch is not one, so writing it is an error rather than dead code that reads as a decision.

[#e2014]
=== A per-clause departure and the clauses it answers for

Six codes, for the ways a departure answering one invariant clause at a time does not line up with the clauses the type declares (<<attempt-departures>>, <<invariant-clause-name>>). E2014 answers a clause by a name the type does not declare:

[,text]
----
`Amount` has no invariant clause named `nonNegative`.
Hint: The clauses that can be answered by name are: inYen. A clause is named where it is
declared (`invariant <name> = ...`); one declared without a name is answered by `| _ -> ...`.
----

E2015 leaves a clause that can fail with no arm to reach. A failure reaches one arm, so a clause with none is a failure with nowhere to go:

[,text]
----
These clauses of `Amount` have no arm: inYen. A failure reaches one arm, so every clause
that can fail needs one.
Hint: Answer each of them, or give the `else` one value, which answers any failure.
----

E2016 is the same for the clauses that have no name to be answered by:

[,text]
----
`Amount` has invariant clauses declared without a name, and nothing here answers them.
Hint: Add `| _ -> ...` for them, or name them where they are declared.
----

E2017 is the other direction — the arm that answers the unnamed clauses where there are none:

[,text]
----
Every invariant clause of `Amount` is named and answered, so `| _ -> ...` answers nothing.
Hint: Drop it. It would come back the moment `Amount` declares a clause without a name.
----

E2018 is arms with nothing to answer for, the departure having no attempted construction above it:

[,text]
----
These arms answer one invariant clause each, and there is no attempted construction here
for them to answer for.
Hint: Attempt the construction (`guard T(v) as x else ...`), or give the `else` one value.
----

E2019 is one clause answered twice:

[,text]
----
The clause `inYen` is answered twice. An arm is chosen by the clause that failed, so a
second arm for it could never be reached.
----

[#e2015]
=== A clause that can fail has no arm

[,text]
----
These clauses of `Amount` have no arm: inYen. A failure reaches one arm, so every clause that can
fail needs one.
Hint: Answer each of them, or give the `else` one value, which answers any failure.
----

Emitted when a named clause of the attempted type has no arm answering it (<<attempt-answers-every-failing-clause>>). All the unanswered clauses are named in one report: which of them a build meets first is not a property of the text, and telling an author about one at a time would make adding a clause a sequence of compiles.

[#e2016]
=== Unnamed clauses are left unanswered

[,text]
----
`Amount` has invariant clauses declared without a name, and nothing here answers them.
Hint: Add `| _ -> ...` for them, or name them where they are declared.
----

Emitted when the attempted type has a clause carrying no name and the arms do not write `+| _ -> …+` (<<attempt-answers-unnamed-clauses>>). A clause with no name cannot be answered by name, so `+| _+` is the only arm that can hold it; the other move is to name the clause where it is declared (<<invariant-clause-name>>).

[#e2017]
=== A catch-all arm has nothing to answer

[,text]
----
Every invariant clause of `Amount` is named and answered, so `| _ -> ...` answers nothing.
Hint: Drop it. It would come back the moment `Amount` declares a clause without a name.
----

Emitted when `+| _ -> …+` is written and the type has no clause carrying a name (<<attempt-catch-all-has-clauses-to-answer>>). It is worth saying rather than allowing as harmless: an arm that answers nothing reads as cover the mapping does not have, and the day a clause is added without a name it would silently start carrying it.

[#e2018]
=== Arms answer for no attempted construction

[,text]
----
These arms answer one invariant clause each, and there is no attempted construction here for them to
answer for.
Hint: Attempt the construction (`guard T(v) as x else ...`), or give the `else` one value.
----

Emitted when per-clause arms are written on a condition that is not an attempted construction (<<attempt-arms-answer-an-attempt>>). What an arm names is a clause of the type being built, and where nothing is being built there are no clauses for them to name.

[#e2019]
=== A clause is answered twice

[,text]
----
The clause `inYen` is answered twice. An arm is chosen by the clause that failed, so a second arm for
it could never be reached.
----

Emitted when two arms name the same clause (<<attempt-answers-each-clause-once>>). Which arm is taken is decided by the clause that failed and not by the order the arms are written in (<<attempt-departures>>), so the second one is unreachable rather than a fallback.

[#e2101]
=== A generated method exceeds the JVM parameter-slot limit

[,text]
----
`data Wide` needs 256 JVM parameter slots, but the constructor generated for it takes at
most 254. An `Int` field is carried as a `long` and takes two slots; every other field
takes one.
Hint: Split `Wide` into smaller data and hold them as fields.
----

A JVM method signature holds 255 argument slots, and an instance method spends one of them on the receiver. A declaration is emitted as methods that take what it declares — a `+data+`'s fields become the constructor's arguments, a `+behavior+`'s parameters become `+apply+`'s, its dependencies become its implementation constructor's, and a recursive helper's parameters become a static method's — so past a certain width the class cannot be loaded at all. This is decided before anything is emitted, at the declaration.

What the width is depends on which of them it is, because the emitted form is not the same. A `+data+`'s fields keep their runtime types, so an `+Int+` field takes two slots and every other field one, and the limit falls at 254 slots. A `+behavior+`'s parameters and dependencies are references whatever they were declared as, so each takes one and the limit falls at 254 of them. A recursive helper is emitted as a static method, which spends nothing on a receiver, so its limit is 255 parameters.

[#e2102]
=== A generated method exceeds the JVM code-size limit

[,text]
----
The `apply` method generated for `f` is 88025 bytes of code, but a JVM method holds at
most 65535.
Hint: Split the work into another behavior, which is emitted as a class of its own, or
move the table out of the source. Naming parts with `let` does not divide it: a
non-recursive helper is inlined before it is emitted.
----

How much code a definition becomes is not known until it is written, so unlike E2101 this is not decided at the declaration; it is reported as the definition that was being emitted when the class file writer refused. The message names the generated method, because one declaration becomes several — a `+data+` is emitted as a value class, a decoder and an encoder, and a `+behavior+` as an implementation and an interface — and which of them grew is not readable from the declaration.

[#e2103]
=== A generated class exceeds the JVM constant-pool limit

[,text]
----
The class generated for `f` refers to constant-pool entry 80031, and a class file
addresses at most 65535. The pool holds every name, type and literal the class refers to.
Hint: Move the table out of the source, or split the work into another behavior, whose
constants belong to a class of its own.
----

As E2102, for the other thing a class file runs out of. The pool belongs to the class rather than to any one method of it, so this is reported as the definition the class was generated for and names no method.

The pool is run out of two ways and the message says which: referring to an entry the class file cannot address, which names the entry that was wanted, and holding more entries than it can write out, which names how many there are. They are one code because the author's move is the same.

[#e2104]
=== A source nests deeper than the compiler reads

[,text]
----
This nests deeper than I read. Break it into parts that have names, so each one is read on its own
and says what it is: a binding for a value, a declaration for a type. What those parts add up to is
bounded too, and far above this.
----

Reported where reading a source descends past the depth the compiler holds itself to (<<source-nesting-is-bounded>>). It is reached while reading, so it is reported wherever the reading is done, including by the formatter, which reads sources it cannot compile, and at the token that reached it.

Naming the parts is the answer to this one, and what the parts add up to is a separate limit — <<e2107>>, which is far above this one and counted differently. What a pass builds from a source may be deeper than the source was; this limit says nothing about that.

[#e2107]
=== A definition is more complex than the compiler holds

[,text]
----
This block takes {steps} structural steps, and a definition holds at most {holds}. Everything a
statement is followed by is written inside what that statement introduced, so each step is a level —
a `guard` binds nothing and is one all the same.
----

[,text]
----
`let {definition}` is {costs} levels of structure, and a definition holds at most {holds}.
----

[,text]
----
Substituting `{value}` here takes this past {holds} levels of structure, which is what a definition
holds. A value is substituted where it is named, so what it is worth is added to what names it.
----

Reported where a definition says more than the compiler holds (<<source-structural-complexity-is-bounded>>), at whichever construct exceeded it: the block, the definition as written, or the value whose substitution composed it. The three are one limit counted one way, and which of them is reported is where the count first passed it.

Naming parts with `+let+` is not the answer to this one — it is what a block and a substitution are made of, and both are counted. What reduces it is writing less structure: a behavior of its own, which is a definition of its own and counted on its own.

[#e2108]
=== The compiler ran out of room

[,text]
----
I ran out of room working on this. What a source may nest and what a definition may say are both
bounded, and a source past either is refused with the place it happened, so this is not a report
about the source: on the stack this compiler is supported on, something it built went deeper than
anything it accepted should. Either the stack it was given is smaller than that, or this is a shape
the bounds do not hold yet.
----

Reported where a walk exhausted the stack (<<running-out-of-room-is-reported>>). It carries no position and asks for nothing: a source within <<source-nesting-is-bounded>> and <<source-structural-complexity-is-bounded>>, read on the runtime the compiler states, does not reach it.

[#e2105]
=== Two declarations become one class

[,text]
----
behaviors `bill` and `Bill` both capitalize to the same JVM class `Bill`; rename one so their class
names differ.
----

Reported at the second of the two (<<no-two-declarations-become-one-class>>). A declaration reaches a class name three ways — a behavior capitalizes (<<jvm-behavior>>), a union output becomes a sealed interface (<<jvm-sum>>), a foreign union member becomes a bridge case (<<bridge-case>>) — and a model may already have declared something of that name. Names are not reserved ahead of time: one nothing collides with stays available.

[#e2106]
=== A field is named after a method of `Object`

[,text]
----
`hashCode` cannot be a field of `Member`: the generated class reads its fields through accessors of
the same name, and `hashCode()` is already a method every class has.
----

Reported at the field (<<a-field-does-not-collide-with-an-object-method>>). `+equals+` is not among them: `+equals()+` and `+equals(Object)+` are different methods, and Java allows it as a component name.

[#e2201]
=== A written codec does not agree with its type

[,text]
----
The decoder for `Amount` must construct `Amount`, but constructs `Level`.
----

Reported where a hand-written codec is not a codec for the type it is declared on (<<a-custom-codec-agrees-with-its-type>>): a decoder constructing something else, an encoder missing a case of its sum, an element encoder against a different element type, a source-shaped encoder given another shape. A derived codec agrees because it was read off the type; a written one is held to the same agreement.

[#e2301]
=== A declaration is not written in its form

[,text]
----
A behavior signature uses `:` before its parameters: `behavior submit : (...) -> ...`.
----

Reported where a declaration does not read (<<declaration-syntax>>): the module header, a top-level `+data+`, `+behavior+` or `+let+`, a behavior's signature, a `+depends on+` clause. The message names the form that was expected rather than the token that was found, since what a reader needs is the shape and not the position.

[#e2302]
=== An expression is not written in its form

[,text]
----
The right side of `|>` must be a function call or a function name.
----

Reported where an expression does not read (<<expression-syntax>>), including a block that ends in no expression: a block's value is its last expression, and one holding only `+let+` and `+guard+` lines states nothing.

[#e2303]
=== A pattern is not written in its form

[,text]
----
A case value is bound with `as`: write `| Submitted as s`, not `| Submitted s`. Only `Some` carries
a payload to bind positionally.
----

Reported where a pattern does not read (<<pattern-syntax>>). The forms differ by what is being destructured — a case binds with `+as+`, an optional binds its payload positionally, a record destructures its fields directly — so the message names the form for the thing at hand.

[#e2304]
=== An example is not written in its form

[,text]
----
An `example` needs at least one `|` row.
----

Reported where an `+example+`, a `+fake+` or an `+examples for+` file does not read (<<examples-syntax>>, <<examples>>).

[#e2305]
=== A literal is not written in its form

[,text]
----
A fractional literal is a Decimal and needs the `m` suffix (write `1.5m`); Souther has no
floating-point type.
----

Reported where a literal is not written in the form its type takes (<<literal-syntax>>). Most of it is answered while the text is read into tokens, before anything is parsed: a string not closed before the end of the line, a backslash written before something that is not an escape (<<string-literal>>), a fractional literal with no `+m+`. One is answered after, where a literal was expected and what stands there cannot be one.

[#e2306]
=== The text is not made of tokens

[,text]
----
Unexpected character `@`.
----

Reported while reading the text into tokens (<<the-source-is-made-of-tokens>>), before anything is parsed. Unlike the syntax codes above it says nothing about where in the language the text was, nothing having been read yet: a character that begins nothing, and a `+'+` with no name after it, are the two ways to reach it.

[#e2307]
=== An anonymous union is written in a narrow type position

[,text]
----
Field `ticket` is written with an anonymous union, which is not a form a field's type is written
in. Declare a `data` over the cases and give the field that name.
----

Reported where a `+|+` follows the type of a data field, a type argument or a tuple's member (<<an-anonymous-union-is-not-written-in-a-narrow-type-position>>). None of the three reads the form, so it is recognized at the `+|+` rather than left to the delimiter the reading stopped at — the author reached for a choice, and a report about a missing `+}+` is not about what they wrote. A behavior's parameter is the other position that refuses one, and it reaches <<e1312>>: it reads the form and the boundary refuses what was read (<<a-parameter-names-one-type>>). Everywhere else a whole type is read an anonymous union stands, a helper's signature included (<<union-intersection>>).

[#e2308]
=== An optional is written inside another type

[,text]
----
`?` marks a data field's own optional and is not written inside another type. Write `Option<T>`
for the type, or put the `?` on the field that holds it.
----

Reported where a `+?+` follows a type argument or a tuple's member (<<an-optional-is-not-written-inside-another-type>>). Distinct from <<e1402>>, which refuses a `+?+` a user model writes outside a field and which the core may write: this position takes none from anyone, so `+List<T?>+` is refused in the shipped core too.

[#example]
== Example: the business-trip application model

An example transcribing the spec DSL (`+chapter10/types-and-states.dsl+` and others) to Souther. Each definition is annotated with its corresponding spec DSL in a comment.

[,text]
----
module example.businesstrip exposing (
    TravelRequest,
    submitTravelRequest,
    preApprove
)

// --- value types (a simple value + a constraint comment in the spec DSL → invariant) ---
// data Amount = Integer // at least 0, in yen
data Amount = Int
    invariant value >= 0

// data EmployeeId = String // not empty
// newtype (the data X = Y form). The derived Decoder reads from bare text and checks the invariant.
// Normalization (trim, etc.), if needed, is done in a boundary-side custom Decoder.
data EmployeeId = String
    invariant String.length(value) > 0

// --- a nested sum with unit cases and a newtype ---
// data Rank = Manager OR Staff / data Manager = Level / data Level = Integer // 1..5
data Rank = Manager | Staff
data Level = Int
    invariant value >= 1 && value <= 5
data Manager = Level       // a newtype over Level
data Staff

// data CostBearer = OwnCost OR ClientCost / data OwnCost = Reimbursement OR Advance OR CorporateCard
data CostBearer = OwnCost | ClientCost
data OwnCost     = Reimbursement | Advance | CorporateCard
data Reimbursement
data Advance
data CorporateCard
data ClientCost

// --- product (role name ≠ type name) ---
// data Employee = EmployeeId AND Rank AND ManagerId
data Employee =
    { id: EmployeeId
    , rank: Rank
    , managerId: EmployeeId
    }

data TravelRequestCommon =
    { applicant: Employee
    , estimatedCost: Amount
    , destination: String
    , purpose: String
    , startsOn: Date
    , endsOn: Date
    }

// --- sum of states (case = named data, common items taken in flatly by a ... spread) ---
// data TravelRequest = Drafting OR Submitted OR AwaitingPreApproval OR PreApproved
// The derived Decoder reads the discriminator "type", dispatching by tag = case name ("Drafting", etc.).
// To read by a DB "status" or an English tag ("draft", etc.), use a boundary-side custom Decoder.
data TravelRequest =
    Drafting | Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval | PreApproved

data Drafting = { ...TravelRequestCommon }
data Submitted = { ...TravelRequestCommon, submittedAt: DateTime }

data PreApprovalReason = HighValueTravel | InsufficientAuthority | ClientCost
data HighValueTravel = { threshold: Amount }
// data InsufficientAuthority = Rank (a newtype)
data InsufficientAuthority = Rank
data ClientCost

data AwaitingPreApproval =
    { ...TravelRequestCommon
    , submittedAt: DateTime
    , preApprovalReasons: List<PreApprovalReason>
    }

data PreApproved =
    { ...TravelRequestCommon
    , submittedAt: DateTime
    , preApprovedAt: DateTime
    , preApprover: EmployeeId
    }

// NotAuthorized is just data (a failure is ordinary data; no error declaration)
data NotAuthorized

// --- behavior specifications (the spec DSL's behaviors line up as is) ---

// depends: clock → no implementation. Injected from Java
behavior now : () -> DateTime

// behavior decidePreApproval = EstimatedCost AND Applicant AND CostBearer -> PreApprovalReasons
behavior decidePreApproval : (
    estimatedCost: Amount,
    applicant:     Employee,
    costBearer:    CostBearer
) -> List<PreApprovalReason>
    constructs HighValueTravel, InsufficientAuthority

// behavior submitTravelRequest = Drafting -> Submitted OR AwaitingPreApproval (both success → bare sum)
behavior submitTravelRequest : (request: Drafting) -> Submitted | AwaitingPreApproval
    constructs Submitted, AwaitingPreApproval

// behavior preApprove = AwaitingPreApproval AND ApproverId -> PreApproved OR NotAuthorized (a failure is a case too)
// depends: clock → write it in depends on
behavior preApprove : (
    request:    AwaitingPreApproval,
    approverId: EmployeeId
) -> PreApproved | NotAuthorized
    depends on now
    constructs PreApproved

// --- implementations ---

let decidePreApproval (estimatedCost, applicant, costBearer) = { ... }

let submitTravelRequest (request) = { ... }

// precondition: approverId equals the applicant's managerId
// depends on now appears as the last argument
let preApprove (request, approverId, now) = {
    guard approverId == request.applicant.managerId
        else NotAuthorized

    PreApproved { ...request, preApprovedAt = now(), preApprover = approverId }
}
----

The type of `+preApprove+`:

[,text]
----
preApprove :
    Behavior<{now}, (AwaitingPreApproval, EmployeeId), PreApproved | NotAuthorized>
----

All three of `+Behavior+` come from the declaration: `+{now}+` is `+depends on+`, the input is the argument list, the output is the right of `+->+`. The `+let+` has 3 arguments because it is pre-injection; when `+PreApprove.bind(clock)+` fixes the last argument, it becomes the declared 2-argument type (<<depends-on>>).

`+...request+` copies the common items and `+submittedAt+` of `+AwaitingPreApproval+` by name, ignores `+preApprovalReasons+` (not in `+PreApproved+`), and adds `+preApprovedAt+` and `+preApprover+` to construct.

[#java-example]
== Java-side implementation example

[,java]
----
// extends the abstract base FindMember generated for a behavior with no implementation (not implements).
public final class JdbcFindMember extends FindMember {
    private final DataSource dataSource;

    public JdbcFindMember(DataSource dataSource) {
        this.dataSource = dataSource;
    }

    @Override
    public FindMemberResult apply(MemberId id) {   // the generated sealed union: Member | NoSuchMember | CorruptStoredData
        try (var connection = dataSource.getConnection();
             var statement = connection.prepareStatement(
                 "select id, email, display_name from member where id = ?")) {

            // value extraction goes through the encoder. MemberId is a newtype, so encode is a bare String.
            statement.setString(1, MemberId.encoder().encode(id));

            try (var resultSet = statement.executeQuery()) {
                if (!resultSet.next()) {
                    return NoSuchMember();                            // inherited protected factory
                }
                var raw = Map.<String, Object>of(
                    "id",   resultSet.getString("id"),
                    "Mail", resultSet.getString("email"),
                    "displayName", resultSet.getString("display_name"));

                // decode is a Raoh Result<Member>. An invariant violation maps to the CorruptStoredData case
                return switch (Member.decoder().decode(raw, Path.ROOT)) {
                    case Ok<Member> ok   -> ok.value();              // Member (mainline)
                    case Err<Member> err -> CorruptStoredData();          // inherited protected factory
                };
            }
        } catch (SQLException e) {
            // A platform failure is not folded into a case but thrown. Souther passes it through; the boundary handles it (503, etc.).
            // SQLException is checked, so wrap it in a non-checked exception of the DataAccessException family the boundary catches
            // (not IllegalStateException or a bare RuntimeException — those pass through the handler and make infrastructure failure look like a bug).
            throw new DataAccessResourceFailureException("member lookup failed", e);
        }
    }
}
----

Binding to a composition behavior:

[,java]
----
var handle = Handle.bind(new JdbcFindMember(dataSource));
var result = handle.apply(rawInput);
----

[#future-work]
== Future extensions

The language core is implemented. The following are future extensions — distinct from what is permanently absent (<<out-of-scope>>).

* A full formal grammar (BNF). The key delimiters and precedence are stated ahead in <<delimiters>>, and where each written form is given is <<grammar>>.
* Incremental compilation.

[#non-functional]
== Non-functional requirements

* compile errors are rendered from structured data — a code, a source region, a message, and hints; the human form quotes the source with a caret, a JSON form (`+--format json+`) is available for tools, and messages are localized, the language being selected as <<compile-errors>> states
* the generated `+.class+` passes standard verification and is usable straightforwardly from the Java version <<target-jdk>> names
* each compiler stage is independently testable
* nothing in souther-runtime calls Raoh; the dependency is the generated code's, and souther-runtime declares it so that a project holding generated code resolves it without naming it (<<codec-generation>>)
* the language core does not depend on the Java class model
* the requirement set is tracked statically; how it is reconciled and inferred is <<requirement-propagation>>
* generated code exposes no unvalidated value, on the terms <<jvm-construction-privacy>> states
* one spec DSL definition maps to one Souther definition with no intervening translation

[#core-semantics]
== Core semantics

Souther tracks two things about a data.

[,text]
----
which values are that data
which expressions have the authority to construct that data
----

It tracks three things about a behavior.

[,text]
----
input type   output type (sum of possible business results)   the set of required dependencies
----

A behavior conceptually has the type:

[,text]
----
Behavior<Requirements, Input, Output>
----

There is no fourth slot separating failure. A failure is one of the cases of Output, and whether it departs the mainline is decided by composition. `+>->+` simultaneously performs output-case routing (flowing cases the downstream consumes, propagating the rest) and requirement-set composition.

[,text]
----
f : Behavior<R1, A, O_f>
g : Behavior<R2, B, O_g>
f >-> g : Behavior<R1 ∪ R2, A, (O_g) | (sum of O_f minus the consumed part)>
----

This rule is Souther's central composition semantics. Output corresponds to the spec DSL's `+-> Out OR ...+`, and the requirement set to `+// depends+` / `+// side effect+`. What the author states explicitly is input, output (a bare sum), dependencies (`+depends on+`), and construction authority (`+constructs+`). On the same line as the spec DSL writing `+// depends:+` at a leaf but not on a composition, only a composition is left to inference (<<depends-on>>, <<composition-with-requirements>>).

`+Requirements+` is an argument fixed by injection, not a dynamically handled effect. So on the `+let+` side it appears as an ordinary argument, and `+bind+` partially applies it (<<depends-on>>). `+Behavior<R, I, O>+` can be read as `+(I, R) -> O+` closed over `+R+`. That is why no variable enters the requirement set, and that keeps the whole system simple.

Souther maps a specification model to an implementation model with minimal translation by promoting the value constraints, construction paths, and outside-world dependencies the spec DSL left in comments to invariants, confinement of construction paths, and `+depends on+` with injection.
